[p. v]

## Vorrede — Preface

Even in earlier studies in the field of palaeontology I had arrived at the insight, and had occasionally hinted at it briefly, that every earth-historical age not only brought forth its particular genera of animals and plants, as has long been known, but also manifests itself, in these types of the most various descent, through certain concordant formations, concordant biological characters and organ-structures, that is, through a definite organic architectural style. In this a kind of spirit of the age is expressed, a law by whose aid one can establish the age of origin of all living beings endowed with such peculiarities that still reach into later times or into the present world, even where no fossil remains from that age of their emergence have yet been found, as an evident proof of their earliest existence. One may therefore expect to obtain information also about the age in which the human race arose, if one compares its form and organs with earlier biological temporal characters. This book serves the provisional and wholly general [p. vi] application of this palaeontologically won insight to the high earth-historical age of the human stock and its evolution. But there is more.

In the course of the years, without especially seeking it, I came upon many legendary notions and statements about primordial men and their environment which, seen from this side, suddenly took on a remarkable gleam of life. This soon led me to regard myths, legends, cosmogonies and in part also fairy tales with legendary cores from an earth-historical and human-historical point of view, and to compare them with natural-historical facts, theories and possibilities. The result of such comparisons lies before the reader here. I set them down selectively and without completeness, as they appeared to me, not necessarily as scientific propositions. The whole is an attempt, on a path not yet systematically trodden, so far as I can see, to unite the strange with the long familiar, the natural-historical with the legendary; hence also the word “study” in the subtitle. I am fully aware of the hazard that lies in presenting so many-sided a world of thought to the outside world, and I know only too well the objections the specialist entertains; but I hope to be able later to grasp and to ground more securely the natural-historical law and the connections on which the account of the high age of the human[p. vii] stock rests.

Apart from the matter-of-fact expositions, the book is something thoroughly personal, and its lines of thought join together into a world-picture. It is therefore, like everything lived through, a confession, and is meant to be one. Every section, even the driest, is one through its outlooks. The sources lie clearly in the open, and where they are of foreign origin they are faithfully indicated. But in the last resort everything is rooted in an empathy that has come to living intuition, and it does not claim to want to displace sober scientific research from the place where it belongs by its nature. Nevertheless another truth is to be reached, by other means and on another path, which I try to ground epistemologically and natural-historically; in doing so the work of others will be amply used and drawn upon. But with the natural history of the legends, which we should like to unveil in order to make them fruitful for the history of the earth and of humanity, it is not done alone. Science as such, be it even the simplest description of an object, can, as a will to pure knowledge, have only metaphysical sense — or it has none at all. And a problem such as [p. viii] ours cannot be grasped at all, in its whole extent and its whole depth, without a conscious metaphysics. At the same time, therefore, as follows necessarily from the context, I should like also to show that a bridge from the outwardly natural-historical to the inwardly metaphysical can be built without difficulty, if one entrusts oneself to a way of viewing that has its great exemplars among naturalists too, but that in our days is on the whole not customary and seems to many impossible, if not reprehensible. If others, however, to whom knowledge and study of the legends is a scientific profession, soon follow it on a better road, as I venture to hope from some signs, then what I have brought about here laboriously and full of wanderings, while I saw it, over two decades, going ever more distinctly alongside my specialist scientific work, will soon cast a fuller light on a life that now still lies buried under the debris of millennia and of many prejudices and school opinions.

München-Solln, Anfang 1924.

[p. ix]

[p. x]

[p. 1]

## Einführung — Introduction

Two ways there are to lift the veil from Nature; / One leads into the Nothing, the other to Life: / To a hardened heart and a dry understanding / There appears a turning wheel upon an endless band. / Yet if you draw near in reverence and with fresh heart, / She radiates living sense to you in everlasting chastity.

[p. 2]

[p. 3]

### Theorie und Wissenschaft — Theory and Science

New paths of knowledge are beheld, not conceptually demonstrated. They prove themselves passable when, walking on them, one sees things meaningfully in a new arrangement. Then one speaks of new facts that one has found; and from there new paths open up again. So we remain eternal wanderers. Those who themselves seek new paths are “the dreamers with the great faith.” We are all believers, but each from a different natal hour of his spiritual life, under a different star at the zenith, with a different chord and with different strength. So it may come about that in new thoughts we never find ourselves wholly in common, and that in old ones that have become common we cannot remain standing together. But the goal we all divine, like a promise lying within us.

Faith, when it proceeds from the individual, is in this form unique and solitary. If it is strong, it transmits its vibrations to those who of themselves do not believe, awakens in them their chords, their melodies. “The danger feared by science — says a sensitive interpreter of Bachofen, who was also a solitary — that every unqualified person may pass off his fantasy as objective intuition and an arbitrary chaos take the place of methodical research, is much smaller than one believes. The community here exercises a constant correction, for only what is truly significant can be of lasting effect. The merely subjective collapses in on itself.” If it were otherwise, science could not exist at all; for it has no other means of its preservation than just this one indicated. It lives from faith — or it merely vegetates and accumulates, or dies. One could not yet today reckon with atoms and quanta or speak of the evolutionary history of life, if one did not believe in it and had not first had a theory, before one found new, more deeply reaching facts for it.

The ancients, or at least the Platonic minds, called theory what we may today describe as ingenious inward vision and absorption [p. 4] in a subject treated with earnest devotion, and the immediate certainty springing from it — the beholding of God, the seeing of the inner connections and of the unity of the world of appearances, of existence, in a part of it. But what we today call a theory in scientific usage is a knowledge supported by external facts and built up empirically and intellectualistically. Yet even such a knowledge has never been possible without a greater or lesser degree of Platonic theoria. The ingeniously grasped image of the idea is always the intuitive, life-giving original; the grounding and the scientific mantle, the garment in which the idea afterwards appears also to the rest, is what is brought in from outside, what depends on the material, on the circumstances of the time, on the scientific manner of expression, on previous education and schooling.

The Platonic idea is no abstraction from a few things and processes seen from without, but a beheld reality in the most literal sense of the word; not an emptied formula-image in our intellect, such as in natural science is the concept of species and genus abstracted from a number of individuals; but the idea in the Platonic conception is the inner reality lying in things and living beings, by virtue of which they are and become. But not in themselves are and become, but for our contemplating mind are and become. In the transcendent sphere of the ideas the connection and the one essence of nature for all its seeming individual parts stands there immediately certain to the beholding mind, and for this reason it sees outer nature as a parable, as images of the idea. For him who grasps the living essence of a natural-historical genus, and thus its idea in the Platonic sense, species and individuals are only symbols. The empirical world of natural science is a symbolic world of images of the immediately and livingly operative ideas.

The beheld idea must therefore, in order to become scientifically and intellectually intelligible, be set forth in objective images and lines of thought. Thus arises a theory in the ordinary sense, a doctrine. A doctrine or theory of this kind, serviceable in the struggle of thoughts and illuminating depths, is distinguished from an unscientific opining and guessing by the fact that the investigator’s [p. 5] mind from which it sprang tests it against the sober empirical facts and is ready to alter it consistently as soon as this seems to him necessary. Only theories growing up on such soil, and doctrines that change, signify an inner gain for science in general. They are not merely knowledge pure and simple, but work as heuristic theses, fertilize research through the controversy of thought that promptly sets in, lead to a new grouping of facts and phenomena, hence to new facts themselves, and are beneficial for the spirit and the gaining of material in science, also in that they sharpen and widen the sense most of all in those who combat them, if these lack the same immediate certainty of beholding the fundamental idea. Structures of thought of this kind in the science of the post-Renaissance age were, for instance, the Galilean doctrine, the Newtonian grasp of the principle of gravitation, the Goethean theory of color, the Kantian critique of knowledge, Mayer’s law of the conservation of energy, the Darwinian theory of descent, and perhaps the World-Ice Doctrine (Hörbiger’s Welteislehre).

Not always are new thoughts born at one stroke and in one head only; simultaneously or earlier they appear in one or many persons, in this or that form, here or there, until the man comes who through a single inward vision helps them to life and kindles the storm. As soon as the idea is born as theory or article of faith, it is thereby also cast into a definite narrow form, it has stepped out of the free realm of the spirit into the confinement of physical or psychical reality and is now burdened with all the faults and errors to which every human work is exposed. The genuine ring is lost. Hence it proves nothing against the inner worth and the justification of an ingenious discovery if its grounding in concrete images of fact taken from the environment is opposed by the specialists on every side.

In organic nature, age by age, new types come and old ones pass away. These new types, once present, enter into many variations and fulfil the circle of their existence in a longer or shorter evolution, in order then to vanish and make room for new, different ones, or to carry on a insignificant, rarely once more rising, rapidly declining stunted existence [p. 6]. But one cannot say that the new ones issue directly from the old; yet it does give the impression as if the one had to die out so that the others might thrive. Something analogous happens in the emergence of cultures and peoples and races, and also in the emergence of new living thoughts. They do not issue directly from one another; only a secret connection exists in the coming and going, just as in the evolution of living beings. New thoughts breaking forth also fulfil their time and then no longer continue directly, when they have fulfilled their task. For this reason in science no thought has ever yet been built on endlessly, but each fulfils its circle and dies again. But in this dying there seems to lie, as in a healthy soul, at the same time the power for the becoming of a new one.

Structures of thought, theories, articles of faith, insofar as they are the expression of beheld ideas, remain essentially, during the whole duration of their existence, what they were at their birth, even if afterwards they are taken up by others and altered in their form; often, however, new ideas also make use of the garment of the older ones. It is the same with myths, legends and religious truths. All this is indeed, so far as it is not pure products of the intellect and therefore ephemeral formations, of one essence and the expression of inviolable certainties. They all well up, even when they were beheld in a single head, from a mood of the time, a spirit of the age, which is often borne by only a few and becomes recognizable to them; they well up from it as the forms of the organic world from the womb of nature, which certainly does not remain eternally the same, but also has its spirit of the age. If, according to a saying of Nietzsche’s, every doctrine is in vain that does not express the urge of an age felt by the best, then conversely every doctrine emerging in this way is also always immediately certain to a number of minds, as soon as it has once been uttered. Then the denial of details or their condemnation by the sure, the all-too-sure, counts for nothing. It has opened doors and permitted a glance into the distance, which those growing up will follow, even if they outstrip details and the formulation of the doctrine. But once it has exhausted its circle and revealed the new meaning of the long-familiar things, then it falls [p. 7] from the tree of life, is perhaps tenaciously held fast also by those then old who grew great with it, until it sinks with them into the grave. So it goes with us and with all our doctrines.

Each of the great scientific perspectives — I say deliberately not truths — has brought something essential and lasting, even if it was refuted or long remained unnoticed and really or apparently vanished again. Democritus’s atomic theory was forgotten along with the cultural sphere in which it arose, when it came back in Western physics in a new form and under another world-picture. It has once more created values in this quite different context and will again — we are evidently on the way to it — return into metaphysics, from which it came; something else will take its place. Mayer’s law of the conservation of energy was rejected by the specialist science; then it dominated mechanics. The values of knowledge created by it remain, but another takes its place. Helmholtz’s notion of the gradual contraction of the earth, though without planetary correctness, was able to lead geology so far that it clearly came to know the geophysical preconditions which created the spiritual soil for a more mature doctrine. Cuvier’s doctrine of the catastrophes of the earth had to yield to the doctrine of v. Hoff and Lyell of the summation of the smallest geophysical effects, when the spirit of the age had become ripe for the mechanistic worldview and required another way of viewing; just as the Darwinian doctrine of descent, the genuine child of the utilitarian view of life, which has also passed devastatingly over our people in the last century. All these doctrines have fulfilled their great mission, although they were so much rejected. They taught us to see with new eyes sharpened for the small. Now they are being displaced by new thoughts and visions which certainly may also be false in a thousand details and yet true in the beheld idea, and are so, as much and as little as all earlier ones and all still to come. For “truth” they all bring only insofar as they are ingeniously conceived out of a fullness of partial knowledge and, like a spark penetrating everything, were once lived through by their creators and re-lived by others.

[p. 8] Two activities of the mind are at work together: ingenious contemplation and daily craftsmanship. Both must be joined with each other in the person, if he who calls himself an investigator is not to be an unsuccessful visionary or a deadly pedant. The one has developed more this side, the other more that side of his mind. Our great ones are those in whom both sounded harmoniously together. They have shown their science new paths, have raised for their contemporaries and posterity visions into new spheres, where the same old facts suddenly became new facts and — for their age — also new truths; whereby they made it possible for others to go on living spiritually.

Characteristic of everything spiritually deep and fruitful is thus, on the one hand, its having been won from within, and on the other, that the grounding, the elaboration into a concrete scientific theory, carries errors, faults, contradictions within it. The specialist, who can be as unpleasantly limited as he can be ingenious, will therefore in any case be right at first against a new thought, whether he rejects it, or acknowledges its value wholly or in part and enters into it. That lies in the nature of thinking and beholding, and we cannot act otherwise scientifically, all of us, whether we see it or not.

Characteristic of an intuition of ideas that deepens science is also this, that its outward formation into a doctrine, its demonstration, must be made ad hoc. A suitable presentation, a suitable grouping of concrete material of knowledge is sought, which for him who has seized the idea is basically a side matter. But he must erect such an edifice of theory in order to make himself intelligible to his surroundings and his contemporaries. From this it is clear that in a theory, a new doctrine, what matters is not so much the details of the presentation and grounding as rather that the beheld idea as such be communicated and placed at the disposal of science for searching use. Whoever believes that in the absolute sense a doctrine could be right or wrong has touched only the surface of the world and of existence. In the objective sense, true without qualification are only the archetypes of things perceived by inner seeing, the “ideas” in Plato’s sense. As soon as they are translated into the concrete, their truth and their unconditional being are [p. 9] gone; they are clothed in a doctrine, in a system, and about this one can quarrel endlessly.

It is as with a Gothic cathedral which we behold from afar towering over the houses: its total form makes a great impression on us and gives us immediately the idea of what Gothic willing, Gothic beholding, Gothic earnestness is. We go nearer, quite close, and now discover a multitude of faults of art and beauty and material in the work: individual ashlars are set the wrong way; many ornaments are crudely executed and do not agree with one another; here and there an arch or a window is Romanesque instead of Gothic; or the row of pillars is displaced; or parts are inserted which originally belong to another building and are of necessity used here as well. We are in many ways disappointed and step back again. But with the same overwhelming impression, with the same certainty and truthfulness of its total construction, it towers again over the roofs, and we surrender ourselves to what it truly conveys to us, now after the critical examination with fuller conviction, and behold with the spirit of the master, instead of scolding with the stonemason over the misplaced ashlar.

In this duality between idea and life the spiritual existence of thinking men moves. Not only that of one’s own personality, not only its existence between willing and being able in moral and intellectual respect, of which the figure of the Apostle Paul is so tragic an example; but also in our scientific willing and ought this torment of the double being meets us, which — we feel it clearly — alone hinders us from attaining truth. Its dissolution would be truth. But no one has yet succeeded, by the means of conscious thinking, in leading that duality of representing and experiencing over into the unity of one’s own immediate being. So we glide to and fro: out of the realm of immediate experience, where we are for the moment one with what is known, over into the world of concepts and of multiplicity, into the thought-forms of subject and object, of space and time, of cause and effect, where separation and error reign.

Man, if you would only “know,” how far you remain from the light! / Who has true knowledge, he beholds and — knows it not.

[p. 10] All science goes in its quiet goal, consciously or unconsciously for the individual, toward removing for consciousness that duality in which inorganic nature confronts us ever again as force and matter, organic nature as soul and body, history as meaning and course. We have various mighty and superficial attempts to master this duality in thinking and representing and to arrive in our intellect at the inner unity of all existence which all of us feel, believe in, and which truly is.

All philosophy, from the oldest times until today, is consecrated to this wish to arrive at the inner unity of things and of ourselves; it has no other sense and no other source. Even the sham philosophy of the decades just past, “scientific monism,” had solely and alone this willing, yet it did not keep the goal in view. For instead of setting out the duality of our picture of existence and our feeling of existence in a scientifically intelligible way, and taking it as a fact insurmountable for waking thought, but illuminating it anew, it merely denied it away and decreed it away, was thus dogmatic and in consequence fanatical, but not philosophically scientific. And if in the end its loudest and most childlike herald in the struggle, Ernst Haeckel, speaks of ensouled atoms, because otherwise no notion of the stuff, of matter, arises for him, this is an irony of the history of the mind which once again points the intellect as such to the limits drawn for it once and for all. But they might have learned that from Kant already.

Schopenhauer’s whole endeavor too, sprung from the most genuine ground and touched as if blasphemously if one were to set it beside monism and the “scientific worldview” of the second half of the nineteenth century — Schopenhauer’s whole endeavor too is again and again directed at the intelligible dissolution of the dualism of all existence grasped by thought: The will to existence is alone what is, the intellect only the reflecting addition, whereby the will becomes aware of itself and ripe for reversal, for turning away from individual existence. But when Schopenhauer at the end of his life extols that he himself was still permitted to become acquainted with the world-overcoming and [p. 11] existence-denying wisdom of the Indian Vedas, there comes to light in this most inward confession at the same time the feeling of redemption that pervaded so deep a spirit when a way was shown to him to grow out of the duality, and thereby the rent condition, of the feeling of existence in his intellectual world.

The way thither was always and will in all the future be only one: living religion. Not philosophical-scientific thinking, to say nothing of profane science; not the running and pressing after an external truth — what is truth? the close of every epoch will keep calling out to us, in order to get no answer from us. With natural science we again stand distinctly at such a close of an epoch. The dualism in our picture of the world and of existence is the same as ever before, and not solved scientifically-philosophically. When we raise the arm in order to perform an activity, we notice well enough that the act of will of raising and the function of the arm occurring with it are somewhere a unity; we experience it by feeling as a unity. But as soon as we want to understand the whole act according to the concept of cause and effect, we understand nothing more. For now the unhappy duality comes again, which runs through our whole scientific knowing, so far as it is not immediate experience. Therefore through the thought-principle of cause and effect we come to no living grasp of the essence of things, but only to formulas and laws, however practically usable they may be; yet they are themselves practically usable, say in the building of machines, only if beforehand the idea of a machine and its effect had been grasped by inward vision. The deeper need of knowledge is not satisfied by the attempt at a presentation of events under the image of cause and effect, and formulas are not knowledge in the sense of a deeper truth. So again and again, for the linking of things and phenomena according to the principle of causality, even in the simplest truly scientific work of knowledge, inward vision, the experience of unity, must be added as the principle that alone creates immediate certainty.

With this everything seeks of itself the way to a metaphysics. Some return to their inherited form of faith, in our cultural sphere therefore to their church; others turn [p. 12] to Indian wisdom or to theosophy and anthroposophy; still others seek to fuse both and to feel the deeper unity in them. Still others, who think more soberly, seek an occult science or astrology. He who has most of the scientific cast of mind raises his physics and chemistry into the realm of atomic research; and physics and astronomy let a notion relativizing all concepts of the environment take the place of the old naively mechanical world-picture. Phylogenetic research on animals and plants returns from the realistic descriptions of species and the formalistic pedigree-images to a consciously idealistic morphology, and we here seek a metaphysics of the evolution of man and animals in the skeleton of earth history. Whatever we say and think and mean — we all basically seek religion, insofar as we mean truth and fervently will it.

The science of the post-Renaissance age and that of the materialistically oriented last century wanted this too; as much as the religious mysticism of the late Middle Ages. But the science of the last centuries was concerned with an outward ordering of the world-picture; its aim was to present the mechanism of outward happening as such; better said: to draw up a world-picture according to the concept of mechanism. Under this aspect it saw, and had to see, its own facts. Last in the nineteenth century, however, it almost forgot that this mechanistic world-picture was a dogmatic demand, an attempt of the spirit willed a limine, to overcome the dualism in representation or at least to make it harmless for scientific consideration. Now it held this mechanistic world-picture, so far as its presentation succeeded at all, mistakenly for a result, instead of a postulate posited from the start, and founded upon it anew a worldview giving itself out as if religious, thus made the attempt to utilize it ethically. Now we become aware again that truth cannot be found in this way, because from our science we can extract by way of worldview only what we and our ancestors once put into it, when ingenious intuition of ideas gave birth to it. The invested capital has remained the same. We have let it work and pocketed the abundantly accruing interest as [p. 13] natural-historical descriptions, discoveries, practical results, and consumed it for living, often in order thereby to become poorer in soul. Now that we reflect again upon the capital, we find it again as the same, unchanged in essence. And we ask anew: What is truth?

This self-reflection always leads back to the primal source, to religion. There, in the surrender of the ego, man experiences the unity that he seeks in vain, by the means of everyday understanding, behind the duality of the world as represented—that is, divided into subject and object and reflected in his consciousness. The inwardly experienced unity is faith, is immediate experience, since it is not mediated by the senses; is science pure and simple; is truth. Schooled by the outward scientific empiricism of the last centuries into ascetic, austerely knowing thought, the spirit of our research has become ripe for a new inwardness, for a new positing of a postulate, of an axiom grasped through inner vision, such as the postulate and elaboration of a mechanistic world-picture once was.

The new axiom—one recognizes it clearly, even though, so far as I can see, it has not yet been pronounced in natural research, although it is sought here and there, groping—will be the attempt at a scientific world-picture in which outward happening appears as the image of what is essentially real and effective. We are arriving at a symbolic apprehension of the happening around us, of nature and of our own existence. Of course the methods will thereby become different. The old research into facts will remain; it has proved its worth in its own realm, and without it our intellect will be able to create nothing. But there will be added an inner seeing, which flows from self-exploration and from the knowledge of the “inner side of nature.” This inner side is ourselves. Man’s essence is the measure and the essence of things; his becoming is also the becoming of the surrounding world that he knows, and from his essence flows the knowledge of things, as much from within in contemplation as from without in the empiricism of the senses.

What is to come is nothing new, only something purified, clarified, fermented. For what we now need and seek once more was already there. Our great minds of the preceding [p. 14] centuries, as far back as idealist philosophy and philosophy of nature, possessed it and shine across to us. But they possessed it in another way: they were not inhibited and schooled by the renunciatory thinking and research of our critical science, which soberly strives for the mechanistic world-picture. So they became fruitless; the thread, grown brittle, snapped. Then came the age of materialism, after the decline of the idealist ideas of freedom, unity and universal brotherhood of the late eighteenth and early nineteenth centuries. In science and in popular life Western materialism moved into the foreground. Now one “upswing” chased the next: growing wealth; strengthening in foreign policy; the creation of a public opinion by a press; a soulless caste system not rooted in the people; antagonisms from which revolutionary socialism was born; parliamentarianism; Darwinism; industrialization of labor, including scientific labor; in general the mechanization of existence amid the highest triumphs of the technical in every field. Alongside, sighing deeply from time to time and calling for the buried soul of our people, German intellectual life in particular went its outwardly brilliant, inwardly torn way. Around the turn of the century “philosophy” awoke again. It began with the rediscovery of Goethe’s spirit. People sought again, and sought with presentiment; they no longer knew everything, and the riddles of the world, just now solved with such a flourish of trumpets, began to become unsolved problems again. The mystery in existence stirred anew, faith.

Then came the monstrous suffering and dying. And now, in spite of every outward appearance to the contrary, soul and spirit are free again and are yearning for—religion. We are beginning to tie together again the old threads, torn off in the upswing, far, far back into the past, perhaps toward a new great flight of the spirit. It ought to succeed in uniting the outward empiricism of science with the seer’s inner vision into a deepened symbolic world-picture, and in throwing a bridge between the unfermented East and the fully matured West.

In this state of cognition, which is setting a riper, higher fruit, our [p. 15] Faustian German natural research finds itself, to my feeling, at present, no matter how many of its bearers know or admit it. It leads over to religion.

The evolution of the spirit is like a spiral course; it seems to return to the same point, yet on a higher level, and from there has a view of the same things, yet in running through a higher orbit. Thus we find certain parallels in the spiritual development of peoples or cultures and in our own development. From this we can draw certain guidelines for what is coming; only we must not equate what is similar without looking closely. Eduard Schwartz shows<sup>1</sup> now in the “Charakterköpfe aus der antiken Literatur” how Epicurus in his late philosophy works up the old natural-philosophical teachings and insights of, say, a Democritus into a formal foundation of his doctrinal edifice and his late-age worldview, how, however, it is not his concern to prove the scientific correctness or incorrectness of such a doctrine. With Democritus it arose, as Schwartz says, formerly from the impossibility of transferring the one, unbecome and imperishable being onto the infinite manifold of the visible world that comes to be and passes away; he therefore saw in his doctrine the happy solution of a contradiction that threatened to make every scientific knowledge of the real world impossible. Epicurus, on the other hand, had no thought at all of continuing Democritus’s labor of research. “He sets no store by an exact explanation of natural processes and makes it a principle to set several side by side for choice.” “He was not the first among the successors of the great natural philosopher to treat and develop the atomistic hypothesis not as a principle of research but as a component of a worldview.” “He follows only the trend of his time when he despised and pushed aside the scientific tasks that the last original thinker had set for posterity: the same process can be observed also among the heirs of Platonic and Aristotelian science.... “Hellenic philosophy was, from Plato, indeed from Socrates, science and worldview at once. Even if modern science has had to wrestle hard with the dogmatism with which the Church, as heir of Greek philosophy, had permeated humanity, it owes one thing to the spiritual dominion of the revelation guarded by the [p. 16] Christian churches: it could devote itself exclusively to research and had no need to concern itself with the salvation of its adepts’ souls, like its Hellenic predecessor, which could point to no Decalogue and no Sermon on the Mount.”

<sup>1</sup> E. Schwartz, Charakterköpfe aus der antiken Literatur. Zweite Reihe. Leipzig 1910. p. 34ff.

I believe that, as regards these sentences, which characterize in essence the inner movement of the Hellenic stream of the spirit, we are today again in a similar condition, if on another level; but I also believe that we are entering a period in which, even as natural scientists, we shall not only have to concern ourselves very much with the “salvation of souls” of our adepts, but shall quite of our own accord see in this the only meaning of our research and perhaps of all research. There are objective and emotional symptoms of this.

One can attempt the proof from two sides: from oneself, whereby the empathy with and anticipation of the spirit of the age is essential; and then from the Others, who meet us as persons or in word and writing. As for what is one’s own, let this book here be a proof. As for the Others, one can observe for years, and since the war to an intensified degree, how students and the lay public on the whole approach the scientific posing of questions far more inwardly than we still could in our day; how their seeking and questioning, even with objects seemingly so concretely realistic as those of the history of the earth and of life, seems to be heading toward inwardness. It is no longer that negative spirit which in the second half of the nineteenth century rose from the epistemologically so shallow mudstream of the natural-scientific Enlightenment and attached itself chiefly to the German form of popular-scientific literature; at that time, too, people made out of natural science, from chemistry to biology, a philosophy which with its naive realism wanted to be the only possible one. This is not what is rising today out of and beside natural research. Rather, the present need for knowledge no longer wants to see matter put in the place of the soul, no longer wants to have the depth of the question of life brought near to it through formulas, but through the beholding of the living essence of all things, through a true Theoria.

That is the one kind of seekers. The other consists of the teachers and researchers themselves. Matter as such is no longer [p. 17] worshipped for its own sake. It is deepened into an inner vision. I am not speaking of the artisans but of the contemplative. And beyond that we approach, each for himself and we in the totality, that higher maturity of knowledge in which, to speak with Lange, we do not despise yesterday’s theory and swear by today’s, but see in all theories only a means of surveying the facts and of mastering them for use. And more. It becomes clear to the researcher again that our work leads neither to truth pure and simple nor to individual truths; and that therefore a quarrel over them might in the last analysis be senseless. I agree with the insight of a work<sup>2</sup> that leads out of the narrowness of the Wagnerian scholar’s study to the Easter walk, and which hits the meaning of Faustian natural research in the words: “The belief in presuppositionless knowledge only marks the monstrous naïveté of rationalistic ages. A natural-scientific theory is nothing but a historically preceding dogma in another form.... What decides the value of a working hypothesis is not its ‘correctness’ but its usefulness.... Insights of another kind, truths in the optimistic sense, can in general not be the result of purely scientific understanding, which always already presupposes a view on which it can operate critically, analytically.... True is only the timeless.”

<sup>2</sup> O. Spengler, Der Untergang des Abendlandes. Vol. II. Welthistorische Perspektiven. Munich 1922. p. 330.

That kind of usefulness alone has been the essential mark of every great theory—that is, one harboring possibilities of development, carrying research further and deepening it. All of them, like myths, legends and religious truths, have sprung from ingenious conception, from inner vision, from quiet certainty, and they have been “true” as long as and insofar as they were heuristically fruitful. To the same degree as science exhausts the circle of its possibility within a cultural course, it returns to a conscious faith, grown from deepened contemplation. And perhaps it has been the meaning and worth of all Western research to have brought into discipline, in its long, patient work, often heightened to personal asceticism, the spirit which, now grown riper, gradually raises the outward empiricism through a simultaneous conscious inner vision; not by an arbitrary one, but by an intuitive working fertilized by [p. 18] the soil of rational science, always conditioned by it, consciously keeping it in view, which, like Antaeus, draws strength and vitality only from this its sure native ground.

We may take up once more the parallel with later Greek philosophy given above and say, partly in the words of Schwartz, of the present philosophy too: already we also repeat great natural-scientific theorems, like Epicurus, still with inner conviction and—where we survey them—also in lapidary language. The riper, the more closed and simple. We also place them together with others, older and newer, that are like them or opposed to them. In science itself we have acquired a comparative historical sense and no longer merely despise the old, which we now already begin to place on an equal footing with the new. We have become more clarified and sense or already know that it is not a matter of truth pure and simple but of perspectives on the unchanged existence. And we ask ourselves where, beyond all reflecting science, the resting is in the flight of appearances. It is the way that concerns us; for we know that the goal is not yet attainable.

To the one, however, this theory, to the other that theory, that method, that way is the better, the more surely guiding, according to the basic trait of his being, of his seeing, which he brings with him innate or which is awakened in him during his labor of research. This seeing is his scientific religiosity. Knowing and seeing, knowing and faith, however, exist only together or not at all. The rest is silence or—henceforth artisanal processing of material. From that scientific religiosity springs, in the right pupil, the seeking and questioning; from it too, in the right teacher, the answer, when both want and have inwardness.

Thus it may be the high, if unconscious and surely mostly still unavowed goal of our science, toward which it drives by virtue of the inner life of our spirit: that we shall no longer think so much of continuing the outward accumulation of material, of presenting the outward relation and succession of the phenomena, as of setting up, symbolically, the great edifice of thought [p. 19] of Western research like a measured colonnade in which all, but really all the columns together bear the roof beneath which our search for God walks about. If, according to the beautiful word of Schwartz given above, Western research has so far not needed to concern itself with the salvation of its disciples’ souls, if it could live for the working out of the material alone, then this has not been, as it may well seem to this lofty mind, a final state and perhaps a kind of liberation, but it was the preparatory service for a priesthood gradually growing, after all, from this very science, which with its Faustian inwardness will hardly recall an Epicurean one, but will open the wells of the deep and perhaps find its fulfilment once again in a Sermon on the Mount.

[p. 20]

### Wirklichkeitswert der Sagen und Mythen — Reality-Value of Legends and Myths

We can scarcely yet divine how long the great tragedy with the great promise lasts that we call the history of humanity. Pious sense, supported by an often misunderstood tradition, speaks of a few thousand years. In Babylonian tradition, however, it is reported how, after the creation of the world, primordial fathers ruled the land whose reigns extended over several hundred thousand years; the tradition of the Japanese knows a primordial population of the age of several million years. Natural science, all too stuck fast in the doctrine of descent, formalistic and by now become a banal philosopheme, speaks of a few hundred thousand years; it fills this time with the image of Stone Age man and seeks convulsively among a few Early Tertiary<sup>3</sup> apes the ancestor of man. It displays to us a few Ice Age skeletons and finds in them more “primitive” features than in the European of our day. But the more it contemplates the skeletons, the more traits in common they acquire with present-day men, and the more racial difference they acquire among themselves, the more difference of stock from the pithecoid animal brothers. It then sees the trace of man vanish in the earliest phase of the Ice Age, where only primitive, finally uncertain flint splinters seem to betray a tool culture scarcely in its beginnings; and then, on this path no longer to be followed so far, the human being, the human stock, vanishes into the darkness of a prehistoric age, through which we can follow, palaeontologically and to some extent, only the chains of the Tertiary mammals, back into the still older, Mesozoic era of the earth, where the reptile dominated the scene of the earth in just as varied a multiplicity as later the mammal and, apparently last, man.

<sup>3</sup> Here, as in the whole book generally, a knowledge of the sequence of the individual earth-historical epochs is necessary, and without it everything is unintelligible. For this reason a synoptic table, giving the appearance and the duration of the most important animal and plant types in earth history, has been added on p. 42 for occasional reference. For the understanding of the table, the following should be noted: The lines drawn between the individual earth periods signify no sharp boundaries in the temporal course of the earth-historical conditions and events, whose product the formations, that is, the contemporaneous rock series of the earth with their plant or animal fossil inclusions, lie there, concealed or open, from earlier ages. The individual periods and world-ages, moreover, are not of equal length in time among themselves, as it might seem from the regular columns on the table. If one sets the Cenozoic = 1, then, according to the average thickness of rock in its deposits, the Mesozoic may be taken as = 3, the Palaeozoic as = 12. An absolute duration in time, about which the layman so likes to ask, has not yet been calculable. Yet one may reckon, by estimate, roughly 500,000 years for the Quaternary, 5—8 million years for the whole Cenozoic (including the Quaternary); accordingly 15—24 million for the Mesozoic and 60—100 million for the Palaeozoic. These may possibly be minimal values, yet they show to how small a measure the widely current larger figures must be reduced. The great pre-Palaeozoic ages, back to the primordial time of the earth with the hypothetical first formation of a crust around the glowing globe, exceed those three named world-ages many times over in duration. Of them, however, little that is certain is known, and above all no clearly definable animal and plant remains such as we have from the first three world-ages, which for that reason are also set, as geological-historical time, against the earlier eras of the Eozoic and Azoic. The names of the individual epochs and periods, constantly used in the text of the book as well, are to be understood strictly as designations of time alone. Expressions such as Cretaceous or Carboniferous are indeed originally derived from the strata of the earth’s crust corresponding to these ages, which deserve the designation coal or chalk, and which were formerly mistakenly held to be alone characteristic of those age-levels. But just as today here a calcareous mud, there a clay mud, here a gravel, there a sand is deposited to become a future marble or marl or sandstone, so it was also in earlier epochs; and in the Cretaceous not only the European white chalk but also hard sandstone and gray Alpine limestone were formed, to name only what happens to be singled out. The other names are taken partly from regions (Devonshire in England, Perm in Russia), where the deposits of the earth-age in question were first studied and the presence of that earth epoch among others was first demonstrated; partly they are Latin-Greek word-formations, such as Tertiary, that is, the “third” great world-age, which was formerly preceded by a Secondary and a Primary age. Since it was recognized that there were still other, almost unfathomable world-ages preceding those epochs, and since the ages are no longer divided according to the rocks but solely according to the strictly law-governed succession of the fossil plant and animal races, one has set a Meso- and a Palaeozoic against that Tertiary + Quaternary (= Cenozoic). These three words mean: age of the young (kainòs), of the middle (mésos), and of the old (palaiós) life. Diluvium and Alluvium are old designations, according to which in the present age and immediately before it, accumulation and washing-away were supposed to make themselves noticeable as the most prominent geological phenomena. Diluvium, incidentally, is also a translation for the Flood according to the older conception of time, but is now used only as a designation of time for the Ice Age immediately preceding the present-day Alluvial epoch.

Beside the two visions of our half-divining, half-knowing about the age of our race—religion and natural science [p. 21]—there stood out from time immemorial, leading an existence of its own, in myths, legends and fairy tales, strikingly lively and warmly toned, but remote from reality and not understood, the image of a humanity with fabulous bodily and spiritual properties, linked with strange transformations and enigmatic natural phenomena, such as neither science nor late-age philosophy offers us. Uns ist in alten maeren wunders vil geseit. We hear of giants and dwarfs in human shape; of heroes on whom the most fearful wounds had no effect; of figures with fingers that cannot be spread and a forehead eye. We see them set out to fight with dragons and lindworms; we learn of daemonic powers that animated them. We are told of times when other lands existed; when floods destroyed everything; when stars moved in the sky that otherwise stood still or were not there; when sun and moon changed their course or lost their shine and came back.

Is all this objectless fantasy? Or are they merely personifications of forces of nature and of the soul? Are they “faded gods”? Is it the mere offspring of “world-anxiety”? The enlightened science of our day would still have us believe so in many respects, because all this does not fit the “primitive” Diluvial man, not the Tertiary ape ancestor, not the palaeogeography of the Ice Age and post-glacial age, not the stable astronomical world-picture of our day.

There is a still far gloomier tradition than that of giants and dwarf-men, of dragons and lindworms: man himself is said to have become the occasion of the wretchedness of living nature, his unredeemedness is said to be the cause also of its unredeemed existence. Whence comes this deeply grave idea? It is insoluble for our everyday understanding, is metaphysical through and through, and still echoes alive in the bright ray of the history of religion: in the Epistle to the Romans of the Apostle Paul, where the allusion is found that with man the creature too longs for redemption. What are we to make of this, what do such and other primordial notions of humanity mean?

Always, when cosmogonies or books of legends or elucidations of them came into my hands, I had to marvel at the remoteness from reality of the literary explanations, where [p. 22] often, as if on purpose, simple connections possible in natural history are not recognized in mythical descriptions and symbols, and what stands immediately there is not seen. For another thing, as a connoisseur of the former world and of its evolutionary history I am astonished precisely at the often so unambiguous narrative of facts in earth history and the history of life that confronts us in the old cosmogonies, myths and legends, occasionally also on pictorial works, and that, in spite of all symbolism or even confusion of the features, simply and clearly betrays primeval history, if one only has the courage for once to admit to oneself that in the old traditions more is offered—or should one say: much, much less—than products of a wild, unfruitful fantasy and an unreal symbolism, or even of a flat intellectualized ethics, essentially akin to our irreligious late-age thinking. With seeing eyes we do not see and with hearing ears we do not hear what is reported to us therein about the vault of heaven, about the earth, about the animal and plant world and about the becoming of our own race.

It is not to be denied that myths and legends, often taken over even thousands of years ago at second, third, fourth hand, estranged from their proper sense, reworked, were also repeatedly tied in the process to the astronomical, geographical, social and religious world of ideas of those who happened to be living and composing. Though a local and late-age interpretation can, therefore, mostly be given without difficulty and with an apparent claim to correctness, one may nevertheless not regard such resolutions unconditionally, even if they are conclusive, as an unveiling of the natural- or human-historical content lodged in every myth, in every genuine legend. For it is with the contents of legends as with the truths of religion. What treasures it holds, and how untiringly one distributes them daily in the traditional forms! And yet the intellectualist spirit of the age, of the pious as of the impious, of the priest as of the layman, hardly any longer knows the monstrous mythical experience from which they were born, no longer the simple depth and glory that lie veiled in the words and doctrines, legends and symbols, but in spite of the explanation of word and sentence remain hidden if they do not live as much in the receiver as in the giver [p. 23] lives. We confuse only too much the form with the essence, and we believe we are grasping the essence when we fulfil the form. Nevertheless, for him who can give himself over in faith, even out of the form so dead as it is handed to him, the secret original life still works, even without the intellect perceiving it. Thus the mystery of God can be with the “foolish.”

So too in the contemplation of legends and myths. The intellect is no longer conscious of the depths, of the whole powerful reality of the core of life lying within; it confuses form and life. To the researcher who builds his explanations of myths and legends upon ethnological and philological connections it must seem an aberrant, senseless destroying and throwing into confusion of what is securely ordered, when one wants to press forward, over this certainty of form, directly, though not unprepared, to the life of the primordial. What goes to pieces and is thrown away in the process are, however, shells that veil the essential, and structures of a later poetry that itself no longer understands the nature-bound original life of myth and legend. Though these husks may be of decisive value, in terms of the history of the soul and ethnically, for the study of the spirit of the age of such late poets and their people, and show clearly how they understood the myths and legends, they remain nevertheless of only indirect value for the natural- and human-historical elucidation of the first crystallization point, of the primal legend, of the primal myth; of value insofar as they are, after all, still always bearers and mediators of the original, glow-born and truly beheld secret life that speaks out of them to the simply believing. For always the myths and legends contain, in all distortion, indestructibly their living natural-historical or metaphysical secret, like the doctrines of religion, whether as full core or as fragments of a real, though mythically experienced and therefore also only with mythical feeling somewhat disclosable primeval history of heaven, earth and humanity; a history that one may presumably hope most readily to peel out and interpret when, as in the religious, one throws off the great arrogance of our just-vanished age and no longer wants to believe that only we have a right science and a right historiography, that [p. 24] science and historiography could exist only in the form coined by us, and that earlier tens and hundreds of thousands of years were naive, indeed foolish and superstitious in their knowing and reporting, seeing and believing, measured by our light!

“The beginning of all development,” says Bachofen, “lies in myth. Every deeper investigation of antiquity is therefore unavoidably led back to it. It is myth that carries the origins within itself, it alone that is able to unveil them.... That separation of myth and history, well founded insofar as it is meant to designate the difference in the manner of expressing what happened in tradition, has therefore no significance and no justification in face of the continuity of human development.... Myth has been reproached for resembling shifting sand and permitting nowhere a firm footing. But this censure does not hit the thing, but the manner of treatment. Many-formed and changing in its outward appearance, myth nevertheless follows definite laws and is no less rich in firm and secure results than any other source of historical knowledge. Product of a cultural period in which the life of peoples has not yet departed from the harmony of nature, it shares with the latter that unconscious lawfulness which the works of free reflection always lack. Everywhere system, everywhere coherence, in all particulars the expression of a great fundamental law, which in the wealth of its manifestations possesses the highest guarantee of inner truth and natural necessity.” “... In myths is deposited the memory of real events that have befallen the human race. We have before us not fictions but lived fates. They are experiences of the mortal race, expression of truly lived history. History has brought to light greater things than even the most creative imagination could invent.” But Bachofen’s words<sup>4</sup> have remained unheeded in the deeper sense, and yet we ought to go far beyond them. Let us at last read the old traditions, be they legends or myths, in a spirit that has reverence also for ancient knowledge, not only for modern research, and let us also compare our naturalist’s knowledge with that oldest knowledge, thinking, [p. 25] and in this way seek to grasp that primordial in its depth and to turn it to account for the history of the earth and of humanity. Admittedly one will have to free oneself for that from the scholarly schools, at the risk of committing gross errors in their direction, in order, however, to exchange for it, in faith, the life of the legends. For there is nothing more remote from the world and from life than the image that the customary interpretations of myths and legends furnish us; a true, earth-historically remote concept of time they in any case do not yet have.

<sup>4</sup> J. J. Bachofen, Das Mutterrecht. Eine Untersuchung über die Gynaikokratie der alten Welt nach ihrer religiösen und rechtlichen Natur. 2nd ed. Basel 1897. pp. VII/VIII and 24.

It is so strange, when one has the standard works before one and looks in them for the life of myths and legends without finding it tangible: how all the great workers and thinkers in the field of mythology keep brushing up against the true nature of the belief in daemons and of the nature-sighted vision of earlier myth-makers, and yet never take the truly last, decisive step, the step that penetrates to the living whole, and so never quite arrive at the full truthfulness and at the essential wellspring of the primordial myths. In his “Germanische Mythologie” Golther gives an overview of all the doctrines that have succeeded one another; again and again it appears that there is no real progress there, because scholarship was unable to press through to the primordial life itself, to a genuine co-divining and co-feeling of the most ancient states of the human being, states that perhaps lie closer to the naturalist. Golther himself knows this, when he rejects, for instance, Mannhardt’s explanation of myth as far too artificial and abstract, Mannhardt having set up the concept of the plant-soul as the origin of mythological ideas: from observing growth in the plant world, man had inferred an identity of essence and devised a plant-spirit; the plant-soul then went on to develop the belief in daemonic beings in general; or when Golther rejects the claim that a “comparative school” made the artful myths of the gods the starting point of its whole explanation and traced their origin to wind, clouds, and the light of heaven. El. H. Meyer assumed as the first stage of popular belief the idea of soul-spirits, of ghosts; these were supposed to arise from events in life, in dream, nightmare, and death; next comes the stage of the nature-daemons, where nature is ensouled, where life and movement [p. 26] prevail: in thunderstorm, tempest, and the passing of clouds the daemons weave. From the lower belief in daemons, according to Meyer, the higher is distinguished, which the socially and spiritually higher classes created out of popular belief by weaving in spiritual and moral motives; these are then poetic creations produced more individually and arbitrarily; with that comes the third stage: belief in gods and heroes. In J. Lippert the belief in souls and the cult of souls is the properly myth-generating element; he derives all religions and myths from it. E. Rohde comes closer again to the vital source of myth-formation: he proves the presence of the belief in souls already in the oldest times of Hellenism; even behind these spiritually and artistically highly developed forms of life there lay the dark belief in ghosts, which kept returning and could be pushed back only temporarily in man under the influence of higher education. Laistner, finally, is praised by Golther for being able, with the finest poetic feeling and thorough scholarship, to interpret the lower mythology: the intuition of the mist as a wolf, of the storm as a steed. Precisely the “capacity to form legends out of appearances of mist persisted for a long time”; the origin of the belief in spirits is again tied to the nightmare. In the end there remains only a sapless and forceless, allegorizing reinterpretation of the oldest core legends, a warning extreme of which is a study of the legend of the Flood that I happen to know as a striking example<sup>5</sup>. There Noah is the moon, the three stories of the ark are the three phases of the waxing moon, the ark itself is the boat-shaped crescent of the moon; it sails high in the heavens, when the water of the Flood had risen so high.

<sup>5</sup> E. Böklen, Die Sintflutsage. Versuch einer neuen Erklärung. Archiv f. Religionswissensch. Vol. VI. Tübingen and Leipzig 1903. pp. 1—61; pp. 97—150. H. Zimmern has also made this his own when, in a treatise on “Biblische und babylonische Urgeschichte” (Der alte Orient. Gemeinverst. Darstellungen, ed. by the Vorderasiat. Gesellsch. 2nd year. No. 3. Leipzig 1903. p. 37) he writes: The origin of the flood legend, like that of Paradise, is to be sought not on earth but in heaven. The flood hero, who travels in the ship or in the ark, is originally a star-god, perhaps the moon-god, who travels over the heavenly ocean. “That this originally celestial voyage of a celestial god was later understood in terms of a human hero and earthly waters is entirely in keeping with the usual habits of legend-development.”

But what are all these and those interpretations supposed to advance? Do they give us any intuition, to be lived along with in the depth of soul and spirit, of the original creation of deep myths and legends, weaving wholly and entirely in the essence of nature? Do they show us even a trace of the mighty fact that there were times and human beings who did not “draw conclusions,” “connect,” “assume” with the nature-alien, parchment-like thinking of the scholar, but for whom there stood before the soul, in immediate reality and truthfulness, what later others could only laboriously cast into cosmogonies, myths, and legends, [p. 27] after it had long since ceased to be immediate experience and had to be poetically recast and fancifully reworked out of old tradition, in order to be made at all somewhat intelligible and enjoyable for times that themselves had no immediate vision into the past but still had an inkling of what had originally been seen and experienced by nature-bound and nature-sighted humanity with terrible or glorious and overwhelming immediacy and truthfulness?

But extremes can meet. In a treatise on the symbol of the serpent among the Hellenes<sup>6</sup>, where mythology is finally supposed to be nothing more than “the wrapping of a concept, an experience, an invention in the colorful garment of a fanciful imagery,” the author comes quite close to the warm-blooded original truth when he says: myth-formation was an often luxuriantly proliferating symbolism of what language was still unable to grasp as a simple, naked abstraction, or also of what it disdained to enter simply as a concrete fact into its great repertory. Had he said instead only that language is simply insufficient to carry over into waking consciousness the tremendous impulses and visions that thrust into the deepest depths of nature and draw from these supra-individual depths, he would have hit upon the essence.

<sup>6</sup> E. Mähly, Die Schlange im Mythus und Kultus der klassischen Völker. Festschrift d. Naturforsch. Ges. Basel. Basel 1867, p. 5.

That is precisely the inner origin of the myth: that it is the language of the supra-individual generic being, which breathes and lives in the transcendent, and that this life, experiencing, and acting proceeds beyond the narrowly bounded causal and individual sphere. To be sure, human beings and ages such as ours can at best only still talk about it and try to divine a faint glimmer of that bond with nature — but in order to come closer to it we must firmly refuse to bring to that elemental primordial world a measure of concepts, a manner of thinking, that clings so hopelessly like a schoolmaster to the inwardly unreal, as do the thoughts reproduced above, which are indeed characteristic of the spirit of the century that has just passed, so unflaggingly self-praised as “scientific.”

The sense of the myths has undoubtedly changed, and the justified endeavor of research must certainly also be directed toward [p. 28] disentangling the overlayerings and distortions of myths and legends. How far these go, and how much individual features mix with one another to the point of confusion or can replace one another in the manner of pseudomorphoses, is taught very clearly by a representation on the chest of Kypselos, a casket of jewels belonging to King Kypselos of Corinth described by Pausanias, which shows the tree of the Hesperides as an apple tree with the serpent wound around it — the picture in the Old Testament. Or another example: in China and India the tiger has become the symbol of evil, the reptile on the contrary that of the good, also of water. But originally the reptile, the serpent, the dragon was quite generally the symbol of evil; the serpent then lost, in classical antiquity, in the full world of light, its daemonic hostility living in the ancient myths, and was used symbolically even as the guardian of heroes. It was guardian in the sanctuary of Athena; it had also become guardian of the grave and was much used on funerary urns. Around the head of the slain hero Kleomenes the serpent coiled, to protect the corpse from the attack of the vultures (Roscher). Here we may truly speak of symbolization and see in this symbol allegorical interpretations of an almost purely artistic kind. For here the serpent is long since no longer what it had been in the myth of the primordial age of the human race, neither in nature itself as a living creature, nor in the metaphysical as the real embodiment of deeply daemonic soul-states of man and of the living nature that then surrounded him. But precisely this shows all the more clearly how perverse it is to believe that, with the always easily recognizable allegorical sense of a late-age version of a legend, one has gained anything essential about the animals, plants, and figures of heroes and gods appearing in it, if one does not succeed in beholding, behind this outward sense, the inner glowing life of the most ancient time.

How the living metaphysical sense has been lost to us, the immediate inner intuition of mythical objects and figures, of mythical cults, conjurations, and revelations! In that treatise on the serpent in the myth of the ancients it is said that natural man [p. 29] was a particularly fine observer of those sides and manifestations of organic life that could offer friendly or hostile relations to his own bodily or spiritual existence; the law of utility, it says, had already then sharpened his eye, governed his sensation, and even determined his imagination. And from this cults and conjurations were chiefly to be explained. But is that not the most genuine spirit of the age of the mechanization of life and of soulless scientificity? One can quite see Wagner lecturing in the lecture hall of Faust, where citizens and state officials are to be raised, who must not, through elementary thoughts and a conception of life, become dangerous to the utilitarian uniformity in economy and science. To apply bare utility, or, if it sounds better, the law of utility, as a yardstick to the myths of the ancients — that amounts to no more than what was drawn, in the same age in Germany, out of the English book of Darwin: a possibility of placing the creative unfolding of organic nature solely under the viewpoint of useful natural selection. How far from all real inner life, from all the glow of a nature-bound creative existence, daemonically bright or dark, is such a spirit! Let it by all means be granted that from the life of all sorts of older cultures or present-day savage peoples or tribes proofs can be adduced that the living sense, and with it largely or perhaps entirely the mythical content of their conjurations and cults, has been lost to them<sup>7</sup>, that they were often exploited utilitarily, as in later times the oracle at Delphi: still, that proves nothing against the original living effect and significance, welling up out of the interior of nature and of the soul, of this knowledge and these manipulations inherited from their forefathers, with which they released daemonic forces or, if one prefers to hear it so, forces of nature, and intensified them into unsuspected visions and effects; just as little as it proves anything against the metaphysical in alchemy if today its intentions and capacities and effects have become incomprehensible, and so, despite all the descriptions and expositions handed down, unattainable for us.

<sup>7</sup> Instructive in this respect are, in fact, most of the books and treatises that deal, besides interpretations of legends, also with astrology and its presentation in antiquity. Here we find hardly even a hint that the astrological insights and the star-cult must have rested on more than superstitious number-play or aestheticizing complacency; rather we recognize from them also that already very early “historical” times themselves no longer rightly grasped the sense, and hence the efficacy, of astrological knowledge and ability; that we shall therefore have to descend into cultural circles far older than the historically known, in order some day to get to the bottom of the true sense of those practical rules of life that strike us as senseless or superstitious or unintelligible. I refer to the small, easily accessible little work by F. Boll: Sternglaube und Sterndeutung (with the collaboration of C. Bezold) 2nd ed. Leipzig-Berlin 1919 (Aus Natur- und Geisteswelt No. 638). The utmost that is conceded there to the old star-interpretations is that real human beings of flesh and blood speak to us also from the old astrological cuneiform inscriptions, whose “limited wisdom” was borne by a strong religiosity, like all the research and knowledge of those old scholars, who earned the lasting renown of having made arable the field on which one of the noblest sciences of all times, astronomy, was to grow. — If only we are not one day as anxious about our god-likeness as we have already become about our civilization! Likewise the work of W. Gundel, “Sterne und Sternbilder im Glauben des Altertums und der Neuzeit” (Bonn and Leipzig 1922), which otherwise supplies such rich material, exhausts itself in formal consideration and presentation of external relations, without one getting even a trace of the inner life and the reality that lie veiled here. Precisely because science is so unwilling to press on from form to life, the field of astrology has lately become just as much a playground for uncritical minds as that of suggestion, telepathy, and clairvoyance was, before more serious scientific attention was turned to these things. Also in the work of A. Dietrich: “Mutter Erde, Ein Versuch über Volksreligion” (2nd ed. Leipzig-Berlin 1913) one is led, as a seeking reader, no further than thoughts such as this: that the deep faith in a divine Mother Earth moved ancient humanity again and again in its innermost depths. But one does ask precisely about the essence of this innermost mover, which cannot possibly have exhausted itself in a merely externally symbolizing superstition or in a mere craving for images. So one misses again and again just what alone would be worth knowing. And yet precisely in the mythical there is no ground for the resigned Faustian word that we can know nothing; we need only have the courage to relive it from our own soul. Much more profoundly, doing justice to the inner connection of the astrological, alchemical, magical, sacrificial and soothsaying practices, Th. W. Danzel presents the mythical-magical worldview, to whose work “Magie und Geheimwissenschaft in ihrer Bedeutung für Kultur und Kulturgeschichte” (Stuttgart 1924) I was drawn only through a communication from the author shortly before the publication of the third edition. Apart from the fact that this book does not go beyond a factual-scientific presentation and treats the material in a specialist manner, rejects metaphysics as such, and proceeds in a purely individual-psychological way, it nevertheless arrives at statements that often coincide almost word for word with ours, although neither knew of the other when the two works were written. For example this: “Astronomical and psychological sense are interwoven in the myths. If we wish to interpret a myth exhaustively, we must try to specify simultaneously the psychological and the natural sense... Only the union of both modes of consideration opens up for us the whole content of the figures of the world of legend, otherwise so enigmatic in appearance, and of their deeds.” Compare with this our statements on pages 225—28. Or another passage: “... by the Homo divinans subjective (psychological) and objective (say, astronomical) content of meaning are in fact experienced as an identity, which splits apart only in our interpretation, in our exegesis and analytical mode of expression.” Cf. here our page 228. The inner seeing-together of the natural and the soul side of events, and the symbol that results from it for consciousness, Danzel calls “image-sightedness” (p. 83). Also that, beside rational science, an artistic-divinatory art of interpretation is now unfolding, which first lends sense and form to intelligible knowledge of reality, is stated there with a clarity and certainty that yields nothing to our conception of the coming symbolic world-picture in place of the mechanistic one (cf. p. 13 of our text). Ethnology and the psychology of peoples already seem today to tower high above our desiccated natural science!

In a book, “Mythus, Sage und Märchen,” in which the essence of these genres is elucidated<sup>8</sup>, we read on the contrary: “A stone [p. 30] falls from the mountain and kills several people: those who escaped with their lives revere it, since they have experienced its power. For the Vedda in Ceylon his arrow, his only possession, procures sustenance and protects him from enemies; whether it hits or misses, on that depends the archer’s weal and woe, and often enough it flies otherwise than the archer willed: therefore he seeks to put this being, on which he depends, into a friendly mood; he sticks it into the ground and dances around it.... A myth can hardly form out of fetishism, but the idea on which it rests, that stone and arrow are living beings, one may call a mythical one.... Of the natural phenomena, finally, from which people were so fond of explaining the mass of legends, it is surely the abnormal ones first of all that became the occasion of religious veneration, such as thunderstorm and lightning stroke, eclipses of sun and moon, earthquakes and fire-spewing. The lightning as such has found religious cult, and in more than one mythical image has been made comprehensible to primitive intuition as a missile, a hammer, a leaping steed; the storm cloud is surely the aegis; sun and moon are threatened by evil monsters, the summer fights against the winter. Such mythical ideas are primitive attempts at explanation, the first beginnings of science. They have often become the occasion of cultic acts.... Through such visualization the natural event is explained and the cultic act elucidated; what need is there then of any further connection and grounding?”

<sup>8</sup> E. Bethe, Mythus, Sage, Märchen. Leipzig (no date), p. 109.

Again and again this too little, this far too little! What would have moved the human heart, even in primeval times, to revere the falling stone fearfully and to conjure it; what, to offer a religious cult to the lightning; what, to credit the stars with power over human destiny — if not the living-daemonic in nature and in its soul? For primordial man this daemonism was just such a reality; he saw and understood the stone and the lightning, the rays of the stars as moved and hurled by it in the same way as we see our streetcar moved by “electricity,” the avalanche by “gravity.” When to coming generations, with another spirit, with other insights into the connections of existence, our “forces” have once become an object of pitying looking-down or at least empty concepts [p. 31], they will, as far as the outward effect is concerned, no longer make any distinction between the cult of mythical man and our mastery of the forces of nature. Both manipulations of the mind have in essence the same great and the same slight practical effect. Both worlds of ideas have the same reality and unreality. I do not doubt that the savage or his forefathers, with their conjurations, achieved within the narrow circle of their existence the same thing that we achieve with our scientific conjuring of the forces of nature in a wider circle. That conjuring, too, and its cult was more than a dancing around the arrow stuck into the ground, more than a mechanical or fantastically overwrought thrashing of his fetish. It was the application of natural forces for bringing about a desired state or averting an undesired one — exactly the same as when, by winding wire around an iron bar and sending an electric current through it, we turn the iron into a magnet. Both, the doing of primordial man and ours, rest on insight into nature, even if along different paths and with different ideas; and both had and have real effect, are a binding of nature-spirits, which we now — still symbolic like the savage — call forces of nature; both are “sorcery.” Only what we see, only our mental images are different, not the thing itself. It takes all the arrogance of an enlightened and just now dying age to fail to understand this difference in seeing and to confuse it with a difference in the thing; to imagine that the doing of the savage was subjective and ours objective; both, to be sure, honest and convinced, but the former nevertheless an empty superstition or even occasioned by priestly humbug.

Does one really believe that “concept-formation” and “empty fantasy,” or also “fear and horror,” would in the long run prevail in a healthy, natural human being and alone suffice to find cults and conjurations and to hold fast to them tenaciously through millennia, perhaps hundreds of thousands of years, if here an immediate certainty and a sure experience and application — empiricism we call it — had not promptly yielded living effects?
