If conjuration [p. 32] and cult had not really influenced and deflected the stone, had not really determined the arrow in its course, the “savages” would have gone on conjuring just as little as we would go on calculating the flight of a projectile and rifling the barrel of a gun if we did not know and had not experienced what a mighty reality this doing brings about, immediately or in its consequences. A human being of other millennia, to whom our mathematical world of ideas would be without object and our physical-ballistic knowledge indifferent or unintelligible, would be able to see in our gunnery formulas only dead superstition and in the rifled gun barrels at most only ornaments. No one, as far as I can see, has done fuller justice to those old daemonic realities and brought them closer to us than Spengler, when he<sup>9</sup> says, for example, of the belief in witches and devils in the Middle Ages: “The halo-ringed angels of Fra Angelico and of the early Rhenish masters and the grotesque faces on the portals of the great cathedrals really filled the air. One saw them, one felt their presence everywhere. We no longer know at all today what a myth is, namely not an aesthetically comfortable imagining, but a piece of most bodily reality, churning through the whole waking state and shaking existence to its innermost depths.... What we today call myth, our literature-sated enthusiasm for Gothic coloring, is nothing but Alexandrianism. Then one did not ‘enjoy’ it; death stood behind it. It was quite the same in antiquity. The Homeric figures were for the Hellenistically educated nothing but literature, idea, artistic motif, and already for the time of Plato not much more. But around 1100 a man broke down before the terrible reality of Demeter and Dionysus.”

<sup>9</sup> O. Spengler, loc. cit. vol. II, p. 356 (see note 2).

Even to modern Hellenistic man it is, after all, finally understandable that devils and witches or a Dionysus produced such overwhelming effects in the human heart. He can imagine that the strength of the idea, the greatness of the fear, as individual or mass suggestion, as ecstasy or hysteria, produced such effects of collapse. It becomes harder for the late-age interpreter already to understand the effect of such ecstasies on the body of the individual. He acknowledges it, about that there is [p. 33] no doubt; he also admits, after all, that faith in the physician, indeed faith at Lourdes too, can without doubt improve or cure “nervous” ailments. But he no longer admits that “organic” ailments, indeed bodily diseases, were cured or also produced in this way, immediately or by after-effect, and were cured at certain times by people of corresponding constitution. He will not admit at all that spiritual absorption, say, in the sufferings of Christ could stigmatize the body of the worshipper<sup>10</sup>. For all this he has, besides head-shaking and the disparagement springing from intellectual perplexity, a few rationalistic or pseudo-scientific explanations at hand, in which the words chance, superstition, autosuggestion, and sensory illusion play the chief role, and he withdraws in the last resort to the many unmaskings of demonstrable swindlers and hysterics at spiritist séances. But it is always a sign of flattened science when one simply denies as problems those problems that have always pressed upon the human spirit, however old it is, and have often stood terribly before its eyes, contents oneself with sham explanations and thereby evades them, instead of looking them in the face. People and ages that evade in this way, to speak with Spengler, indeed no longer know what a myth is, or even only a daemonic conjuration.

<sup>10</sup> W. Jacobi, Die Stigmatisierten. Beiträge zur Psychologie der Mystik. (In: Grenzfragen d. Nerven- u. Seelenlebens. No. 114.) Munich 1923. Also pertinent here is a new work, offering fundamental insights, by G. R. Heyer in the same series (No. 121, 1925): “Das körperlich-seelische Zusammenwirken in den Lebensvorgängen.” I have not yet had occasion to study the works of K. du Prel. As far as I can judge, a methodological foundation has already been laid there for what occupies us here and in the metaphysical part.

But we do not want to speak only with the clever, the all-too-clever judges of everything, whose view of world and life is always finished and the more finished the shallower it is; but with those who have the will to look things earnestly in the face, even if they must expect or fear to discover more still between heaven and earth than their scientific worldview can bear. If, then, the significance and effect of the contents of myths and legends or of formulas of conjuration was lost to the consciousness of the late-born, if conception and application degenerated, if they became spurious, untruthful, or senseless superstition, in that they no longer flowed from the life and the nature-bound soul of man but found only a traditional, if here and there still feebly effective, understanding and application, this can never become a proof against the mighty central [p. 34] significance and truth that they originally had, when they were precisely the form and the only possible expression of a nature-mighty, clear-sighted soul-life with inner and outer experience of truth and immediate reaction upon the surrounding world. Thus most of what we know of myths or ceremonies and contents of conjuration is evidently already intellectualized and degenerated in some late sense, even if not absolutely undaemonic.

Recollections from Greek history, such as that of the Spartan singer Tyrtaeus, who led an army to victory by his song, may originally have rested on daemonically ecstatic and therefore myth-like effective forces and events, or have been woven with such later in legendary fashion and lent them their younger name. But how greatly, already in the classical Greek art and literature that has remained accessible to us, the living-mythical with its deep spiritual-psychic surgings had been dragged down into the banal and bourgeois is shown, to name but one thing, by the fact that Linus, the shepherd youth who entered into a contest with Apollo and was killed for it, could finally be represented as an old schoolmaster giving Heracles instruction, because the overwhelming death-daemon of a life mythically heightened, living in the legend, was alien to the intellectualized Greek and at most still good enough for an allegory. As in our enlightened age, so too in Greekdom, over which our Romanticism and Classicism became enthusiastic, the truly mythical sense had been lost. So too, for instance, in the cult of Hecate “with its specters on moonlit streets and crossroads, whose goddess finally became wholly the favorite figure of superstition and of every hole-and-corner practice reckoned upon the superstition of the female sex, of the common people, or also of the sickly and over-educated”<sup>11</sup>.

<sup>11</sup> Preller-Robert, Griechische Mythologie, pp. 325-27.

What does the decay into epigonism prove, one must ask again and again, against the original significance and reality of mythical tradition, indeed against the origin of the myths themselves, or of such conjurations and spirit-vision in ages and in souls that still experienced and therefore knew what a myth is? Whoever in later times should wish to read off, from the psychic [p. 35] powerlessness of the epigonic last German principalities and imperial dignity, the sense and spirit of the heroic, vital, and therefore effective world of rulers in the age of an Otto the Great, would arrive at the same perverse judgment about the essence of those ancient symbolic powers of existence as the researcher who wants to read off, from the mythical images that have become empty superstition in the eastern, western, or northern late-age versions known to us so far exclusively, the sense and meaning of the life that once underlay the myths and the arts of conjuration. To divine this life itself, and divining to behold it, one must try, in one’s own psychic depths, so far as that is still possible for us at all, to feel the spirit of a most ancient soul-life, one not merely of diluvial man, of a time when myths were still lived experiences, and hence genuine, grown into nature, and alive, endowed with elemental power, not yet intellectualized, utilitarianized, or dried up into dead superstition and into the literary and allegorical image.

But it is not enough that one concedes natural reality to the myths. I go further still in seeking the reality-significance of the most ancient legend-lore. Not only traditional reports of great natural events and states of humanity truly beheld inwardly are handed down to us in the mythology of all peoples. Wise men with deep insight into the connection of things may have conveyed to us, partly from their own seer’s power, partly in deep knowledge of what had already been handed down to them as ancient from still earlier times, a knowledge of which we so far do not yet know how to make proper use. The times of the past are to us a book with seven seals — not only in that we know little and for the most part nothing coherent of what happened outwardly on the earth and in humanity; but rather also in that, with the very one-sided thinking of the understanding that distinguishes our cultural age, we cannot easily comprehend and cannot relive that inner deep beholding which did not yet know the temporal and spatial barriers of our understanding to the same degree, but on the contrary was still more intimately bound up with nature and pressed through to the essence and inner connection of outer things, thus saw from within what we see from without. In this way it was in a position to understand and derive the [p. 36] individual, even if long since past, concrete-physical from the whole, the essential-metaphysical, and to carry this over into the normal consciousness of time and space; thus also to arrive, about what is outwardly past, at a knowledge that flowed from wholly other, deeper sources than our present-day rationalistic research, which wants to call itself empirical alone, i.e. resting on experience.

In this I am thinking of very early ages, far before the historical and far before the Quaternary epoch of man. But we need not go back so far to notice what it means to face a time whose thinking, seeking, and beholding is already a book with seven seals to us, although it lies so very near to us. The Christian Middle Ages are in many respects insoluble for our modern understanding. Already, for example, the “Credo quia absurdum” is a saying that is a folly to the scientifically enlightened “Greek.” Whoever immerses himself in the spiritual world, in the thinking and beholding of a Master Eckhardt, and sees it alive, will surely also divine something of the immediate clarity and depth of knowledge of this unscientific saying with its unbreakable certainty. For such men and their age other connections were really and immediately given, hence empirically certain, for which our school-wisdom has no organ; and so that world remains a sealed book to it. And yet this time belongs historically and ethnically still wholly to us, to our cultural circle, is young like us, compared with times that went far before. If, however, on average we no longer understand it today, its truth, its aspect of the world is nonetheless no less in conformity with reality than, say, the metaphysical symbolism of the atomic theory of our present physico-chemistry. It recognized other sides and connections of reality, other spheres of the world in immediate beholding, had other symbols and words for them, which we understand and apply otherwise. So too ancient sages may have known and beheld much, very much that is no longer adequate to our structure of thought. But perhaps they too had already taken over traditions whose sense they themselves no longer understood throughout as it had been for those still earlier, and over which they therefore drew a garment that corresponded precisely to their own way of thinking.

[p. 37] It is for precisely this latter reason that, for the primordial-historical evaluation of myths and legends such as we have in view, it matters less whether and how such inherited traditions or legends mirror the local and temporal circumstances of their various reworkers and narrators, or whether, stemming from a great spatial or temporal distance, they have been amalgamated into a younger circle of culture and outlook — the old essential core is what is to be sought, and in this search we must not let ourselves be hindered by late reworkings, even when they confront us as the oldest. The Nibelungenlied, the Odyssey, the Gilgamesh legend, the Gospels with their heterogeneous building-blocks may in many of their parts, or perhaps entirely, be so understood and so evaluated. Thus Homer, to choose only this one example, welded fragments of the oldest, uncomprehended myths together with younger material, uniting heroes of the Trojan War with ancient mythical figures, quite as happened with the older and still more with the younger Nibelungenlied. Even if “Homer may have used the Cyclops to give the character-picture of a wild and desolate life before all culture,” that proves nothing at all for the original significance of such figures, which he took over and which in late times were evaluated in a strongly intellectualist sense. And even if the Viennese palaeontologist Abel has succeeded in demonstrating the localization of Polyphemus and his kin in Sicilian caves by pointing to the fossil occurrence of the skeletons of the dwarf elephant with the huge nasal opening on its broad forehead (Fig. 10), which at once conjured up before the frightened ancients, always ready to fabulate, the image of one-eyed giants in the flesh, this still by no means exhausts the sense, origin and human-historical content of the legendary figure of the Cyclops, who after all arises also in quite other traditions and, in spite of all metamorphoses, contains as its core something far deeper, more general and older, as I shall yet show.

The decisive question is therefore in each case what first reality-content the individual myths and legends and cosmogonies have, and to which earth-historical times they point; whereupon we can then read out of [p. 38] them historical, natural-historical events and primordial conditions of humanity and of the landscapes and of the heavens, by comparing them with our knowledge of the history of life and of the earth. We shall gain grounds for thinking that the Neolithic and Palaeolithic man is not primordial man. We shall see that he is also not the man who experienced and traditionally handed on the high-mythical phenomena and conditions common in some form to almost all peoples, but that he himself, as we shall find reason to believe, was only an uninvolved side-complex of older cultures that perished at that time, that is, a perioecus or scattered remnant of old centers of full humanity, like the Australian Negro, the New Caledonian and African primitive tribes, who at their discovery by us were still living in the Stone Age. Then it will be shown that we may expect not merely cultural beings in the Tertiary epoch, but we should be as if stepped out of a narrow dark gate onto a wide plain, in whose middle ground we would presently descry human beings of another kind among the dragons and lindworms of the Mesozoic age of earth history<sup>3</sup>, and in which, on the horizon, amid old one-eyed reptiles and amphibians of a still earlier time, the race of Polyphemus appears in not wholly indefinite outlines. But then the old myths and legends will become primordially old and in the end will no longer be fantasies and allegories or unreal symbols, but will bring something real; they will be “legends” in the literal sense of the word.

<sup>3</sup> Here, as in the whole book generally, a knowledge of the sequence of the individual earth-historical epochs is necessary, and without it everything is unintelligible. For this reason a synoptic table, giving the appearance and the duration of the most important animal and plant types in earth history, has been added on p. 42 for occasional reference. For the understanding of the table, the following should be noted: The lines drawn between the individual earth periods signify no sharp boundaries in the temporal course of the earth-historical conditions and events, whose product the formations, that is, the contemporaneous rock series of the earth with their plant or animal fossil inclusions, lie there, concealed or open, from earlier ages. The individual periods and world-ages, moreover, are not of equal length in time among themselves, as it might seem from the regular columns on the table. If one sets the Cenozoic = 1, then, according to the average thickness of rock in its deposits, the Mesozoic may be taken as = 3, the Palaeozoic as = 12. An absolute duration in time, about which the layman so likes to ask, has not yet been calculable. Yet one may reckon, by estimate, roughly 500,000 years for the Quaternary, 5—8 million years for the whole Cenozoic (including the Quaternary); accordingly 15—24 million for the Mesozoic and 60—100 million for the Palaeozoic. These may possibly be minimal values, yet they show to how small a measure the widely current larger figures must be reduced. The great pre-Palaeozoic ages, back to the primordial time of the earth with the hypothetical first formation of a crust around the glowing globe, exceed those three named world-ages many times over in duration. Of them, however, little that is certain is known, and above all no clearly definable animal and plant remains such as we have from the first three world-ages, which for that reason are also set, as geological-historical time, against the earlier eras of the Eozoic and Azoic. The names of the individual epochs and periods, constantly used in the text of the book as well, are to be understood strictly as designations of time alone. Expressions such as Cretaceous or Carboniferous are indeed originally derived from the strata of the earth’s crust corresponding to these ages, which deserve the designation coal or chalk, and which were formerly mistakenly held to be alone characteristic of those age-levels. But just as today here a calcareous mud, there a clay mud, here a gravel, there a sand is deposited to become a future marble or marl or sandstone, so it was also in earlier epochs; and in the Cretaceous not only the European white chalk but also hard sandstone and gray Alpine limestone were formed, to name only what happens to be singled out. The other names are taken partly from regions (Devonshire in England, Perm in Russia), where the deposits of the earth-age in question were first studied and the presence of that earth epoch among others was first demonstrated; partly they are Latin-Greek word-formations, such as Tertiary, that is, the “third” great world-age, which was formerly preceded by a Secondary and a Primary age. Since it was recognized that there were still other, almost unfathomable world-ages preceding those epochs, and since the ages are no longer divided according to the rocks but solely according to the strictly law-governed succession of the fossil plant and animal races, one has set a Meso- and a Palaeozoic against that Tertiary + Quaternary (= Cenozoic). These three words mean: age of the young (kainòs), of the middle (mésos), and of the old (palaiós) life. Diluvium and Alluvium are old designations, according to which in the present age and immediately before it, accumulation and washing-away were supposed to make themselves noticeable as the most prominent geological phenomena. Diluvium, incidentally, is also a translation for the Flood according to the older conception of time, but is now used only as a designation of time for the Ice Age immediately preceding the present-day Alluvial epoch.

I will try to penetrate this path into the darkness of prehistory — to many still monstrous, to the natural scientist seeming merely erroneous — first with natural-historical, then with metaphysical linkages of thought, even though for the time being only a few bushes and trees can be hewn out at the edge of this primeval forest toward a future larger vista.

[p. 39]

## Naturhistorie — Natural History

“Wonders of history, riddles of antiquity that ignorance rejected, nature will unlock for us.” Schelling.

[p. 40]

[p. 41]

### Typenkreise und biologischer Zeitcharakter — Type-Circles and Biological Temporal Character

Little of the knowledge about the ages of the former world has become part of general education. Not many of those who otherwise know their way about in the arts and sciences as in life have a firmly outlined idea of the history of life and of the earth, of the transformations and upheavals which the surface of our home star and its living beings had to undergo in the course of millions of years. They are almost astonished when they hear how clearly, in many features, the picture of the epochs of the former world, of earth and life, already stands out against a still far older primal era of the earth, into which we are not yet able to cast any light. They are more astonished still when they hear that the historical and prehistoric age of man, so far as it has been opened up to us at all, down to the roughly hewn stone splinters of Ice Age man, is by comparison only a last moment in the transformation of the earth’s surface and of the carpet of life spread over it. They have perhaps also learned from all sorts of popular books that man “evolved” from lower animals, and that the races of animals and plants in prehuman times already populated the earth in rich number and variety and are also found fossil in the loose and solid layers of rock of the earth’s crust. All this, however, has in their minds more or less the character of a curiosity that does not further intrude upon their striving for education, and in general they remain far from thinking it through seriously, from creating for themselves a plastic concept of time on the basis of the primordial past, and from asking what such a perspective might mean for our whole conception of existence.

Three great world-ages<sup>3</sup> stand clearly before the eyes of the earth-historian today. Of them he can tell of a never-resting alternation of lands and seas, of mountain-building and mountain-erosion, of epochs of heightened or diminished

<sup>3</sup> Here, as in the whole book generally, a knowledge of the sequence of the individual earth-historical epochs is necessary, and without it everything is unintelligible. For this reason a synoptic table, giving the appearance and the duration of the most important animal and plant types in earth history, has been added on p. 42 for occasional reference. For the understanding of the table, the following should be noted: The lines drawn between the individual earth periods signify no sharp boundaries in the temporal course of the earth-historical conditions and events, whose product the formations, that is, the contemporaneous rock series of the earth with their plant or animal fossil inclusions, lie there, concealed or open, from earlier ages. The individual periods and world-ages, moreover, are not of equal length in time among themselves, as it might seem from the regular columns on the table. If one sets the Cenozoic = 1, then, according to the average thickness of rock in its deposits, the Mesozoic may be taken as = 3, the Palaeozoic as = 12. An absolute duration in time, about which the layman so likes to ask, has not yet been calculable. These may possibly be minimal values, yet they show to how small a measure the widely current larger figures must be reduced. The great pre-Palaeozoic ages, back to the primordial time of the earth with the hypothetical first formation of a crust around the glowing globe, exceed those three named world-ages many times over in duration. But just as today here a calcareous mud, there a clay mud, here a gravel, there a sand is deposited to become a future marble or marl or sandstone, so it was also in earlier epochs; and in the Cretaceous not only the European white chalk but also hard sandstone and gray Alpine limestone were formed, to name only what happens to be singled out. Since it was recognized that there were still other, almost unfathomable world-ages preceding those epochs, and since the ages are no longer divided according to the rocks but solely according to the strictly law-governed succession of the fossil plant and animal races, one has set a Meso- and a Palaeozoic against that Tertiary + Quaternary (= Cenozoic). These three words mean: age of the young (kainòs), of the middle (mésos), and of the old (palaiós) life. Diluvium and Alluvium are old designations, according to which in the present age and immediately before it, accumulation and washing-away were supposed to make themselves noticeable as the most prominent geological phenomena. Diluvium, incidentally, is also a translation for the Flood according to the older conception of time, but is now used only as a designation of time for the Ice Age immediately preceding the present-day Alluvial epoch.

[p. 42] Earth-Historical Time Table.

[p. 43] volcanic activity, of periodic climatic swings, under which it was, even up to the poles, now mild and warm, now cooler through the onset of ice and snow ages, and masses of glacier also pushed over lands that we see lying today in tropical warmth. The great world-ages have in turn their individual periods, always marked by incessantly changing conditions of the earth and by definite plant and animal races, now long-lived, now short-lived.

Countless are the living beings that in this way passed in the millions of years of the ages of the former world over the earth ever changing her garment. Ever again new forms crowded forth, now slowly, now hastily welling out of the womb of the earth. Marine and land animals, mollusks and corals, worms and amphibians, birds and mammals have been handed down to us in fossil remains from all times — only man not; merely in meager remains of body and tools at the very last, from the latest strata, as if his existence were only the last fading pulse-beat of life-creating nature. One appeals to the doctrine of descent and gives a hypothetical genealogical tree of living beings, whose final member man is supposed to be — a late-born.

The classification of living forms into species, genera, families and so on, created by Linnaeus in the eighteenth century and later not fundamentally altered, was subsequently transferred also to the forms of the former world, the fossil forms, and has outwardly brought order for the time being into this confusion. The abstract systematic groups thereby lifted out of life were in the course of the nineteenth century ever more richly multiplied by fossil animal and plant material. The formal delimitedness and rigidity of the Linnaean systematic groups, however, seemed to vanish when the doctrine of descent sought to conceive all living forms as members of a connected chain, as branches and twigs of a natural genealogical tree. In spite of the apparent blurring of firm boundaries between the species, genera and families thereby brought about, the doctrine of descent nevertheless did not bring back, say, the earlier lack of a clear overview, but now supplied systems of lines on which the many organic forms were strung together historically, that is, in accordance with their appearance in the ages of the earth [p. 44]. What was earlier in earth-historical terms could not be the descendant of what was later in earth-historical terms, and vice versa. Thus an exact foundation of the genealogical tree of life seemed to be given, and one should think that by this method clear, unambiguous lines of descent would soon have resulted.

But a new confusion set in. One had thought too simply. For one held the greater or lesser similarity of form of species and genera also to be the immediate expression of their closer or more distant blood-relationship. If, however, one strung together the present-world and the former-world species, instead of by temporal sequence, by their greater or lesser identity of form, this formal order no longer agreed, or agreed only in very rare cases and along very short lines, with the true geological sequence in time. The same species, moreover, could be arranged into quite different series of forms, according to the bodily characters by which one happened to try to judge them genetically. Thus in the comparative study of the transformation of the foot in the ungulate line one had to regard a fossil species as the preliminary stage of another; but if one compared the dentition instead of the foot, the species that follows in the development of the foot now appeared in its turn as a more primitive stage of development. This phenomenon, which always produces a confusion in the history of the stock, is called crossing of specializations, and it has now been clearly recognized that there lies enclosed in it the fundamental impossibility of arriving, from the external stringing together of formations of form, at the genuine genealogical tree<sup>12</sup> of the organic types required by theory but also untenable epistemologically. True, by the joining of stages of form one obtains ideal series, with which a transformation of form can be abstractly and clearly illustrated, but they do not disclose to us the really natural-historical derivation of one species or genus from the other, which runs solely in the pre-embryonic “germ-line.” The visible forms are only the symbols for this. So one learned to separate the concepts of the same, the similar and the formally related from the concept of the inwardly related, which, as one sees, need by no means always, and certainly not always immediately, find their expression in bodily shape.

<sup>12</sup> On the concept of the “genealogical tree,” conceived in its essence falsely by the descent theory hitherto, see: Kurt Lewin: “Die Verwandtschaftsbegriffe in Biologie und Physik und die Darstellung vollständiger Stammbäume. (In: Abhandlungen z. theoret. Biologie v. J. Schaxel, No. 5. Berlin 1920.) Ad. Naef, Idealistische Morphologie u. Phylogenetik. Jena 1919. A methodical presentation of the theory of descent on a historical-critical basis is given by S. Tschulok, Deszendenzlehre (Entwicklungslehre). Jena 1922.

[p. 45] We have long known that the origin of the main branches and of many side branches of the realm of life reaches far down into ever darker epochs of the former world; we have also long known that much that was new emerged abruptly, not linked to what was earlier in the manner of a genealogical tree. But one still does not free oneself for the saving view that can release us from the fruitlessness of all constructions of genealogical trees: to regard the abundance of races given in earth history as the living working-out of firmly given basic types, which, though during the epochs of the former world they stood freely side by side in constantly changing shape yet always preserving their essence, were presumably genetically linked only in an earth-historical primal era not yet illumined by our research, before those three great world-ages.

If one places oneself decisively on the standpoint of such a type-theory, as palaeontology in my opinion forces it upon us — no matter whether one wishes to see something rigid or something limitedly fluid in the types — it might seem as if a step backward had thereby been taken relative to the hitherto prevailing doctrine thinking in genealogical trees. Perhaps a step back from the hitherto prevailing view is indeed taken, with the gain that from this regained, more original, more unprejudiced vantage-point lying behind one gains a prospect which hitherto remained barred by the all too great proximity of the wall of descent. If one holds fast that types have existed side by side from time immemorial, at least for the times reliably explored in earth-historical terms; if one further holds fast that the types, after they had once taken on flesh and blood as organic forms, expressed themselves in ever new shapes without from then on being genetically connected with other types; and finally that under the image of a transformation they appeared ever again, from age to age, adapted to other conditions of life until they died out, and that the theory of evolution holds only to this extent — then it is at once also clear that under definite conditions of time and life the type-circles existing side by side acquired in convergent fashion a like appearance of their genera, like external bodily shape and often like individual organs. Whether [p. 46] such convergences of form depend on the external conditions of life or on an inner constitutive formative force of like kind, on like stages of evolution, is for the establishment of the fact here at first immaterial. But if at a definite point of time the same dress, the same habitual bearing and often the same more or less conspicuous single organ has fallen to them, then many or all genera within such type-circles appear as if they belonged to a genetically unitary group of stock, as, say, apes and man, whereas they are only biologically-habitually alike, or can even cross in their descendants,

Fig. 1. Diagram of the genealogical tree (A) and of the intersecting type-circles (B) with apparent lines of descent (arrows). I—IV geological periods (original figure).

notwithstanding their nevertheless continuing evolutionist capacity for transformation, in which they ever again manifest their basic constitution, their type, their entelechy, no matter whether they thereby remain formally similar or later move apart again in their shape.

To convey a clear idea of the difference between the older conception of the history of the stock, which is certainly not yet overcome today, on the one hand, and the doctrine of the converging and again diverging type-circles on the other, two figures may be given here. The one (A) furnishes the picture of the genealogical tree through the ages I—IV, and indeed as if the tree of life had unfolded from some concrete primal form. The lowest parts would be earlier lower [p. 47] animals, above them would follow, issuing from them, ever higher ones in ever greater variety; perhaps at the last, from a branch of the primates, man. Were this picture of the genealogical tree applicable, as a whole or in plurality, to the emergence of the organic forms in the course of earth history, we should have to find ever fewer numerous forms on descending deeper into the ages of the earth. But the opposite is the case: we come again and again upon new type-circles, which by no means line up in the manner of a tree, but which at the same time often meet one another in their representatives in such a way, in point of form, that they can be linked formally. The other half of the figure (B) serves to represent this phenomenon: it illustrates the typical independence of the circles of development a—d underlying the natural stocks, and shows in what way transitional forms, which according to the older conception were regarded as points of branching in the history of the stock, can come about. They are formally intersecting evolutions within each type, not necessarily appearing regularly and to the same extent, but irregularly, perhaps determined by external conditions and in a more varied quantity. Such intersections of form can also occur at several places and at different geological heights of time, perhaps even repeatedly; there then arises a particularly confusing abundance of like genera, belonging however to quite different type-circles (a—d), at the same or at different geological times. If one imagines this transferred spherically and in its whole plasticity to the formation of shapes in nature and to the fossil forms from the ages of the earth, one obtains a clear picture of how, in spite of firmly bound circles of life, types of life, extraordinarily similar forms can nevertheless appear at the same time without being immediately blood-related and without forming an immediately connected genealogical tree. It is these formal intersections that again and again give occasion for the fundamentally mistaken “genealogical trees,” as they are indicated by means of the arrows and which necessarily mislead, because they designate no real paths of development but merely sum up formal similarities and do not see the [p. 48] delimitation of the type-circles, closed in themselves as living wholes. Examples of such intersections of form may be given, or, what is the same, of the similar formation of organs or shaping of form that recurs again and again at the same or at different times in heterogeneous circles of stock.

In the Palaeozoic age, the oldest known epoch of the evolution of life in the former world, a parietal eye (parietal organ) appears in the cranial capsule in various groups not immediately connected genetically. First an opening of the forehead appears in some ancient Palaeozoic fishes; in others, which in this respect are probably more primitive (Fig. 2), at the same time, behind the united normal eyes, also a parietal opening besides; later in the amphibians only a parietal foramen, which thereby enter the scene in perfected form. They retain it down into the Triassic, where it becomes more and more rudimentary. In the Permian the reptiles are added, and these always possess it in full development (Fig. 3), likewise down into the Triassic, where it also already frequently regresses. All amphibian and reptilian forms, now, that one meets with in the Mesozoic age and that maintain themselves in possession of that fully developed organ are probably members of older type-circles, namely ones already stemming from the Permian. Thus the late Palaeozoic would be the “time” creating the parietal eye in many higher animals. But there lives to this day in New Zealand a small, grotesque reptile of an appearance such as we must in many cases attribute to the old earth-historical reptiles with their skin armor and the sharp contours of their body and head. This lizard still possesses the parietal eye quite distinctly, if already in a rudimentary state compared with the Permian-Triassic forms. But it too is no reptile of recent times. It belongs to a general type [p. 49] that can be traced back into the Palaeozoic age, was there even abundantly form-creating, and also persists through the Mesozoic in a few species. Did we know nothing of its stock from earlier ages and were we to find this lizard living only today, we could, from the presence alone of that so conspicuous organ, hitherto still never interpreted in natural-historical terms, state at once the age and origin of its type. In faintly rudimentary state the lizards, too, still have it, and these also, as palaeontology has reason to assume, arose from a common root with that type of the New Zealand lizard in Palaeozoic times. One sees what significant prospects such a procedure, taking the temporal character into comparative account, offers upon proper future elaboration. But once a form-creating epoch is over, a stock emerging anew later hardly ever again acquires such an organ or such a bodily shape in the same way as corresponded to an older epoch.

Fig. 2. Armor of the forepart of the body of a Palaeozoic fish with two fused normal eyes; in front of and behind them one frontal and one parietal opening each. Reduced. (After W. Patten. Mém. Acad. St. Pétersbg. 1903.)

Fig. 3. Fossil skull of a Permian reptile (Casea) from Texas, with a particularly well-developed parietal foramen. 1/2 nat. size. (After S. W. Williston, Americ. Perm. Vertebrat. Chicago 1911.)

Let us point further to the newt form, which is outwardly distinguished by broadly projecting limbs after the manner of the salamander. The typical age of the newts, however, is the last half of the Palaeozoic era. There we find not only “newts” or, better said, primitive amphibians with the habitual characteristics of such, but at the same time, partly in company with them, true reptiles which, by their broad head, their posture, in short by their whole garb, imitate the newt without belonging to its stock. Yet another epoch of time brings forth the tortoise form. As the late Palaeozoic era created a newt form, so the Triassic created the [p. 50] tortoise. For not only did the true tortoises, still so called today, first appear at that time in order to develop further from then on, but in an entirely different group too there come to light tortoise forms which are precisely not tortoises but borrow their dress. Or we see, from the Early Tertiary onward, in very different groups of mammals, which for that reason were comprised under the name of ungulates, the five- and four-toed foot pass, with regression of its outer toes, into the two-hoofed foot of cattle and deer on the one hand and into the one-hoofed foot of horses on the other. No one- or two-toed odd-toed ungulate is older than the middle of the Tertiary; and although that regression ran its course independently and in parallel on the most various lines of descent, the stages resembling one another remained bound to the same periods of time and therefore permit, in the case of isolated finds of skeletons, a very exact determination of the geological age of their lifetime. The Mesozoic epoch, for its part, is that age of the unfolding of life in which, among the land animals, a more or less upright gait (Fig. 4) on the two hind feet is striven for, in contrast to the originally uniform gait on all fours, such as the “newt type” of the late Palaeozoic era had in its most primitive manner. In the land animals of the Mesozoic era many groups have long, powerful hind legs and shorter forelegs, whereby, supported by their powerful tail, they were able to walk upright. Indeed some of them have hollow bones like birds, whereupon finally, in the Jurassic, bird-like creatures themselves also appear, perhaps feathered reptiles with long hind and shorter fore limbs. This whole company of forms thus betrays, during the Mesozoic era, the tendency toward that raising of the body onto the hind legs by which the more and more upright-walking animal, lightened by hollow bones and finally bird-like, is brought forth, with increasing liberation of the forelimb from the ground.

There is a phenomenon, recurring in the course of earth history not infrequently, which is in essence probably to be put on a par with this law of the formations of temporal character: that at a certain time a similar special form develops in different groups and

[p. 51] Fig. 4. Fossil reptile skeleton of predatory character (Tyrannosaurus), with greatly lengthened hind legs and very shortened forelegs and a half-upright gait. Cretaceous, North America. (After H. F. Osborn, Bull. Am. Mus. Nat. Hist. New York 1917.) For comparison of size a fully grown present-day human skeleton has been placed beside it.

[p. 52] stocks. It is just as if nature had need, in many places, of a certain animal form and stamped it out of whatever other forms happen to be at its disposal in those places. The best-known and also most vivid example is the imitation of many higher mammalian types in the lowly marsupial fauna of Australia. There we find a marsupial wolf, a marsupial lion, marsupial bears, badgers, rats, mice, bats, which represent, not to say imitate, on the foundation of the marsupial body everything that those animal forms of the wolf, the lion, the bear, etc. are in the higher mammalian world familiar to us. “The marsupial as wolf,” “the marsupial as rat”—that would be the correct designation for these peculiar animal forms. Since such forms were already there in the Tertiary, but today are almost confined to Australia and have almost disappeared in our regions and in America, and entirely in Asia, there arose from this the much-quoted but also much-misunderstood saying: Australia, with its animal world, still stands in the Tertiary age. Naturally this is erroneous, because the marsupials, dying out elsewhere, remained in Australia as relics, but did not first originate there in the Quaternary epoch; for only the origin of such forms is characteristic of the Tertiary, and that took place at the time in Australia as well; the persistence may last for different lengths of time in different regions, according to the inner and outer circumstances of life of the animals.

The lemurs, a tribe of half-apes confined at present essentially to Madagascar, appear, like many other types of mammals, already in the earliest part of the Tertiary. Here too all sorts of things are imitated. There arises the lemuroid rodent, the lemuroid beast of prey, the lemuroid bat—all with corresponding dentition and corresponding skeletal characteristics, yet never denying the primal type. Since the signature of the Tertiary in the primates is, ever more, also the development of the anthropomorphic ape, and since this evolution too proceeds convergently in groups of different descent, and is now more, now less fully achieved, the lemur type too is seized by such a tendency to form-building and moreover brings forth its own “ape species,” which now look like phylogenetic transitional forms to typically true apes. [p. 53] As with the lemurs, so in other stock types too, during the Tertiary, all manner of habitual characters come to light of that which the anthropoid apes ultimately represent and which man too, in the form of Stone Age man, still partly bears in himself. Sooner or later there appears among the mammalian world of the Tertiary, in the stock types, the ape-like, and indeed all the more the later it happens. All those stocks and little stocks of mammals which take part in this evolution wholly or only in individual branches nevertheless remain side by side as such, and develop, according to their basic constitution, ape characteristics of their own, as the human stock itself too at that time probably displayed the most pithecoid characteristics and probably contributed its share to the origin of certain anthropoid apes which from then on remained animal. Thus arose those sought-for and partly found formal transitional links which were taken for phylogenetic connecting links between man and ape, whereas they were convergent formations, in the sense of figure 1 B.

With the law of the formation of temporal forms, then, which realizes itself in firmly given basic types under the image of an evolution, we have a new comparative anatomy also for man, which will tell us when he arose. If the previous method of considering organic forms taught us more and more to separate what is inwardly typical from what is outwardly convergent, then through the novel comparison of forms according to their temporal habit a kind of biological temporal signature is established for the individual geological epochs, which in each case permits certain conclusions as to the age of origin of a type, of a stock of primordial forms, indeed of a single organ, even without our being able, through fossil finds themselves, to furnish the objective proof of the earth-historical moment of the first appearance of a type. We are thus also in a position, from persisting or rudimentary ancient formations of form and organ in present-day living beings, hence also in man, to determine the age of origin of the stock itself. The temporal character is thus something thoroughly real and given only once; whoever still bears it later is recognizable by it as to his temporal origin.

[p. 54] A further problem contained in the theory of evolution is the concept of the higher and the lower, of the developed and the undeveloped, expressions which are used in the theory of descent as though self-evident, yet without adequate epistemological penetration. These two concepts too are used formalistically and yet at the same time in an absolute sense. The amoeboid slime-animal was declared lower than the molluscan animal, this lower than the crab, this lower than the fish, this lower than the amphibian or the reptile, this lower than the mammal and man. That was an unscientific form of consideration, behind which, under a mask, work was done with the yardstick of the psychic and the humanly spiritual, or at most only of the aesthetic. For seen from bodily organization as such it is quite impossible to arrive at such an absolute evaluation of the higher and the lower, and the merely natural-scientific mechanistic standpoint furnishes no yardstick for it at all. That even in a doctrine of descent striving after mechanistic aims man was called the most highly developed creature shows only how much metaphysical interpretation lies in precisely this doctrine, which has never got free of the concept of “evolution,” a concept metaphysical through and through, in spite of its formal denial. Without a metaphysically oriented value judgment no criterion is to be found by which one could declare an animal, an organism that is after all never a machine, higher or lower. The thoroughly metaphysics-less characters among the biological researchers of the past age always felt this, and for that reason there was always, even in the high time of descent-theoretical research, a great number of scholars who even declined the description of pedigrees and devoted themselves exclusively to the anatomical-comparative description of the species, whereby they created the broad and secure foundation from which the formalistic, but not biological, theory of descent itself could gradually be overcome.

Closely connected with this is also the clarification of the concept of progress, which likewise has a natural-historical and a metaphysical sense, both of which have not been kept methodologically clearly apart precisely in the theory of descent. [p. 55] Aslong as the organic world was regarded as a closed chain of species proceeding from one another, the Linnaean system was at the same time the ideal image of the family tree. When this ideal image had fallen apart among the fossil finds into lines of descent, the transformation within these was held to be progress and the concept was equated with that of evolution. At last it was seen that evolution and progress were only idealized expressions for one-sided specialization of successive stages of form, and only so does the expression progress still have a tangible value in biology at present. Progress in the strictly natural-historical, phylogenetic sense is therefore nothing general but always a formation in a definite direction, hence downright a one-sidedness; for it always proceeds at the expense of, and with the elimination of, other possibilities which would often be biologically no less important. In given types nature works, so to speak, toward definite forms of life and adaptations, whereby others remain excluded. It is no different, indeed, in the life of peoples and cultures: definite basic dispositions are developed, that is: specialized. Latent possibilities unfold, others remain undeveloped or remain behind half way or wither, according to the external conditions. So it is in the organic world, and in this there is no difference between the genera of living beings and the life-formations of cultures.

In this sense there is also no backward evolution. What springs from the latent bond in the type and takes shape as form may maintain itself or perish again or develop further one-sidedly or wither; but a retrograde development of what has once been formed, a return to the starting point and from there the entering upon of a new path of development, that never occurs in the world of organic formations; they are all guided by the inescapable law of irreversibility. Only in the spiritual, in the moral world is there the complete reversal and the leaving of the long-trodden path amid great catastrophes or inner illuminations; but in the world of the naturally organic we know no such freedom.

But how, under such circumstances, must the primordial forms have looked? Were they neutral beings which were as yet specialized in no direction [p. 56] whatever, and thus—since every conceivable bodily form is in itself already a specialization—had no body at all? Here for the first time the conceptual uncertainty of the externally proceeding old theory of descent shows itself. For to it primordial form means a neutral creature from which, through continued procreation and transformation from generation to generation, specialized forms finally arose; the dispute was only whether this transformation took place for external, hence accidental, or for inner constitutional, that is, in the deeper sense evolutionist reasons. We, however, understand by primordial form not such a phylogenetically neutral bodily starting point, but the typically constitutional boundness and determinateness, already completely present in all the species and genera belonging to a type, even the most incipient, the potency which is there, amid all external evolutionist change of forms, as the living-enduring—an entelechy, as Goethe too presumably conceived the concept of the primordial form. With this the German word Entwicklung (development) also first regains its deeper sense, already unconsciously disclosed by language, as a manifestation of what is inwardly already present.

From the first moment on, when a primordial form presents itself in living nature in the shape of one or several species, it is no phantom but a being of flesh and blood, full of adaptation to the circumstances of life appropriate to it. The mark of living beings is precisely this, that their natural corporeality consists through and through of biologically purposeful adaptations and specializations, even if these have a slight degree of perfection compared with other, better adapted forms, or have even occasionally failed in respect of certain environmental conditions. A type is usually represented by several species living under different conditions of life; but even if it were represented by only a single species and a few individuals, these too would have to be adapted and specialized in their form in some direction or other, even were they the very oldest and most primitive of the type; for otherwise they could not and would not have lived in the real world. One must therefore not expect to find anywhere in earth history primordial forms which would have been neutral, meaningless, phantom-like in [p. 57] their form. Life has always lived itself out in real characterful figures, which also always varied their own basic form and realized their own type, however much it may have been veiled by biological temporal characters. With this the phylogenetic evolution is not denied, but only its unbiological, all too formalistic conception.

This is to be put foremost as the chief point of view also in the search for the oldest human forms. These too, even in their most primitive conceivable condition, cannot have been anything less than phantoms, but must necessarily have given expression to the human type, even if under the most manifold temporal formations. With this all connections of primordial man with the late animals drop out of themselves, whereby one always again connects with man what does not genetically belong immediately with him, from which one wishes to derive him, and with which one is led to such grotesque results as that his type might have arisen, at some point in very late earth-historical time, from specialized mammals.

To the earlier theory of descent there opened up, instead of real family trees, always only formally strung-together habitual chains of forms, while the persistent stock types as such remained invisible to it, above all the human type itself, which the more or less similar ape types at the end of the Tertiary, and last of all probably also the Ice Age man degenerated toward the ape side, surround like a foil, forming as it were the accompaniment to the melody which had meanwhile already sounded independently of it. The human stock confronts us, visible as fossil, only late, in the Diluvial period, probably because before that it lived in regions which have remained inaccessible to geological research. If anywhere, then precisely here the much-invoked chance of finding and the notorious incompleteness of the fossil material have played a trick on us. But once the mind is accustomed to the distinction between firmly given basic types on the one hand and the biological garment on the other, which is thrown over them at times and in changing fashion, then the way is also free for a more correct judgment of the true origin of man himself and for the determination of how old his stock probably is.

[p. 58]

### Das erdgeschichtliche Alter des Menschenstammes — The Earth-Historical Age of the Human Stock

Fossil human remains, be they skeletal parts or mere artifacts, are, as already mentioned, so far demonstrated with certainty only in the deposits of the Quaternary, the last short section of earth history. The last phase of this Quaternary period is the time of historical human history, back to the later Stone Age. This itself is a prehistoric epoch, from which one has ground and polished, that is, well-worked stone tools. Preceding this period comes the Diluvium with an Ice Age as the main section of the Quaternary epoch (p. 42). At that time larger areas of the northern hemisphere and most of the high mountains, out into their forelands, were glaciated; the average annual temperature was presumably only 5° C cooler than now. This Diluvial period with its warmer interglacials is now the period of the Old Stone Age cultures and of fossil races of man. Shortly before, according to the current doctrine, man was supposed to have developed from late Tertiary ape-like mammals; Stone Age man is supposed to stand still nearer to these primordial forms.

The question has already been raised from another quarter<sup>13</sup> whether the duration of the Stone Age period is not significantly overestimated, and whether the origin of culture generally set in only with the Stone Age. Is it possible and conceivable, says Frobenius, that budding culture began in a connection with stone, that is, with the dead part of the body of the earth? Does it not lie far nearer to assume that the “primal,” the beginning, started with a reciprocal relation of human ability to the living environment, to plant and animal? And should there be no possibility of reading off the converse of the prevailing conception from the phenomenon of culture?

<sup>13</sup> L. Frobenius, Das unbekannte Afrika. Aufhellung der Schicksale eines Erdteils. Munich 1923, pp. 23, 26. On the hypothetical, unhewn stone tools from the Belgian Tertiary formation, the eoliths, Rutot had already built up a corresponding thought earlier. It is to be assumed, he says, that man originally used not self-made stone tools, even if only roughly hewn ones, but that he simply reached for an object, say a suitable stone shaped by nature. This condition of his primitive culture must certainly have lasted longer than the later Stone Age one, and Rutot, on the evidence of the eolith finds, places it in the late Tertiary (Miocene). (A. Rutot, L’état actuel de la question de l’antiquité de l’homme. Bulletin Soc. belge de Géologie, Paléontologie et Hydrologie. Brussels 1903. T. XVII. pp. 425—438). To all such notions and postulates, however, clings the old error that the theory of descent of animals and plants too makes again and again, namely that one seeks a formal primitiveness that existed neither in nature nor in human life as a primal condition in time, and which, if anywhere, can just as well appear only later, and need not be at all the historical or natural-historical point of departure for something that appears complicated to our eye or to our schematizing power of abstraction.

Admittedly Frobenius means it differently than we do; for he does not yet have the wide earth-historical temporal perspective striven for here. He indicates only in general that the oldest tools might have been worked not out of [p. 59] stone, but rather out of animal and vegetable material, and that therefore the Stone Age, even the Old Stone Age, would have been a relatively late stage of development of “primordial man”; for first and immediately man lives, he says, in the fine veil of water, earth, plant and animal which, washed by the sea of air and thin, surrounds the hard stone body of the earth. Over against this hard body that veil, to which man himself belongs, stands as a unity, exposed to the influence of the stars and moved by their forces. We men are, regarded as bodies, a form of expression of the transformations of this veil; culture, however, is the expression of the life working itself out symbolically in this veil in its relation to a “beyond.” Accordingly, so Frobenius presumably means, the oldest, the first stirring of culture reached involuntarily, for its formation, for the elements and parts of the veil itself, not for the material of the naked stone sphere stripped of its veil. So he still expects the uncovering of a much older pre-Stone-Age culture, and this would have to lie before the Quaternary epoch, that is, in the Tertiary.

So far only a few naturalists have dared to date the age of man as a being capable of culture back into Tertiary, indeed pre-Tertiary times; so above all Hermann Klaatsch. His thorough and freely outlooking anatomical analysis of the human body has finally made him demand a far higher earth-historical age of man than one, until then, entangled in the schematism of the older theory of descent, had the courage to assume. In a last work<sup>14</sup> on this question the late anatomist Schwalbe, Klaatsch’s adversary, says that, according to the facts known to him, man of the character of the Javanese Pithecanthropus and of Homo Heidelbergensis (Fig. 5 g) can have arisen only in the very last phase of the Tertiary. But if one now investigates in Schwalbe’s own presentations which members demonstrably connect man, in comparative anatomy, with lower forms, that is, in particular with the apes, one obtains in summary the answer that, while the general animal descent of man is not to be doubted, the special descent from definite primate apes is not yet secured. Most probable is the descent of

<sup>14</sup> G. Schwalbe, Die Abstammung des Menschen und die ältesten Menschenformen. In: Die Kultur der Gegenwart. Part III, Anthropologie. Leipzig and Berlin 1923, pp. 316 and 336 (cf. note 16).

[p. 60] Fig. 5. Lower jaws of apes and men of the Cenozoic era. a) Propliopithecus. Oldest ape form from the Early Tertiary (Oligocene). Egypt. Tip of the canine broken off. Alleged ancestral form of all species of apes and men. 1/2 nat. size. b) Pliopithecus. Gibbon-like form. Later Tertiary (Miocene). Germany. 1/2 nat. size. c) Sivapithecus. Gorilla-like form. End of the Tertiary (Pliocene). India. Greatly reduced. d) Dryopithecus. Chimpanzee-like form. End of the Tertiary (Pliocene). Southern Germany. 1/2 nat. size. e) Eoanthropus. Presumed human jaw with ape characteristics. Latest Tertiary (Pliocene). England. The canine was found in isolation at the same time and has been combined with it. 1/2 nat. size. f) Orang-utan. Living. Present day. Indian Archipelago. 1/2 nat. size. g) Homo Heidelbergensis. Diluvial period. Ice Age man. Mauer near Heidelberg. Jaw form ape-like, teeth human-like. 1/2 nat. size. h) Homo sapiens. Living. Note the protruding chin, in which the Diluvial man is more ape-like. 1/2 nat. size. (Compiled from W. K. Gregory, Journ. Dental. Research. 1920/21.)

[p. 61] man from forms with a mixture of the characters of lower Old World monkeys and anthropoid apes. These forms are now hypothetical. From them the path is said to lead to an extinct—again hypothetical—anthropoid form, from which the “line” ascending to the chimpanzee, that is, not yet the chimpanzee itself but its hypothetical precursors, developed. “From this line the stock of the Hominidae leading to man then presumably branched off.”

How can anyone, in the face of so airy a genealogical tree, composed only of imaginary forms, and thus the whole result of many years of research strictly bound to comparative anatomy, still maintain that we are clear about the origin of man from primates, and that other theories, which likewise work with very weighty pieces of evidence, concerning a substantially higher age of the human stock and its original independence from all the mammalian forms of the former world and of the present world known so far, should have no justification?

According to Klaatsch<sup>15</sup> we cannot in any way fix the time of the becoming of man, but we can hold fast to the fact that this process is by no means of more recent geological date. Formerly people wanted to place the whole evolution of man, or rather of the anthropoid apes, out of lower mammals in the Diluvial period or in the last phase of the Tertiary immediately preceding it. The demonstration that the hypostatized line of development ape–man probably ran in part the other way round<sup>16</sup>; furthermore the realization that even Stone Age man was already divided into several clearly separated older races; thirdly the evolutionary-historical fact that all types reach far back into earth history; finally the realization that genealogical trees cannot be drawn up in the external sense that the age of the theory of descent in the second half of the nineteenth century believed — all this has prepared the ground for the expectation of a very ancient and still thoroughly unelucidated origin of the human race. With Klaatsch we see this problem today as follows: Tertiary man no longer needs to be proved, no matter whether we possess artifacts of him and skeletal finds or not; it can now only be a question of in what phase of the Tertiary the [p. 62] separation of the anthropoid apes from man set in, and how old the common stock of both is, which according to Klaatsch’s well-founded doctrine reaches back unconditionally into the Mesozoic era of the earth.

<sup>15</sup> H. Klaatsch, Werdegang der Menschheit. p. 17; pp. 45ff. (see the citation under “General Works”). Also: Die Stellung des Menschen im Naturganzen. Twelfth lecture in the collective work: “Die Abstammungslehre”. Jena 1911. pp. 332ff.

<sup>16</sup> Here and in the text it is not possible to furnish proof as to which theory appears most adequate to the fossil finds and other facts. Only a few arguments chosen by Klaatsch shall be briefly mentioned, from which his judgment on the position of man in relation to the primates results. For the rest, reference must be made to the cited works and to the opposing position, especially of Schwalbe, whom we name only as the chief representative of the whole counter-tendency. By way of anticipation, regarding the argument of the hand, it may be remarked that Schwalbe probably does not hit upon what is decisive in Klaatsch’s argumentation when he says (loc. cit. p. 307) that Klaatsch, in his conception of the human hand and its use as evidence for a high earth-historical age of the human stock, had primarily in view its formation as a grasping and climbing organ, as it is found in the oldest Tertiary section (Eocene) in lemurs and earlier still in marsupials; he forgets, he says, that in those oldest mammalian forms the hand is always an organ of locomotion, hence in a manner of speaking a foot. Klaatsch did not claim that the human hand really descends from these old forms, and that these forms are the ancestors of man, but he says, and in this I agree with him unconditionally, that the primate stock, in particular that with the human-hand character, must already have existed at least in the oldest Tertiary age alongside those five-fingered bearers of extremities, precisely because its hand differs in principle from that extremity and those forms were in any case already thoroughly specialized types in other directions. Since the primate stock — such is the connection of sense in Klaatsch’s chains of proof — did not release man, but is itself rather the descendant of him or an older side-branch running alongside the human path, man must on this ground too be at least early-Tertiary, if not already Mesozoic. On what grounds of probability the latter appears possible is set out at length in the text, partly with Klaatsch’s, partly with my own arguments. Klaatsch’s proofs for the independence of man in relation to the primate stock of later time, as also in relation to the other Tertiary mammals, are among others the following: 1. The primordiality of the human hand consists in its formal approximation to the five-fingered land extremity of Palaeozoic amphibians. In its embryonic development, however, it shows a roundish, oar-blade-like form, comparable to that of still older Palaeozoic fishes. Its fin-likeness is emphasized still more by the arm segments, which are short and stretch only with the development into the actual hand. The division into upper and lower arm is the expression of the former transition from aquatic to land life. In the mechanism of its rotary movements the human hand still lets the old rowing movements be recognized, namely in the rolling outward and inward, to which the basic arrangement of the muscles in the forearm still corresponds. 2. The apes have already regressed or wholly lost the distinguishing mark of the human hand, the opposability of the thumb. In this man has remained more primitive, and so cannot be derived from them. The lemurs alone have fully preserved the manlike hand. But the lemurs are not the immediate ancestors of the higher apes, nor of man, because, in spite of preserving some very primordial characters, they are nevertheless one-sidedly differentiated. If they therefore stood in a rooted connection with the primates, man included, the old suture could lie only in the Mesozoic age, because already in the early Tertiary they appear strongly specialized, that is, strongly different from the human type. Thus the lemurs are a group which on its own line of development evolved away from a very old form-stage perhaps common to the primates and man, and may have acquired the opposable thumb independently, as man did. 3. The human hand has two elements in it: that of the grasping foot and that of the walking foot. On the little-finger side it has a ball of muscle which does not serve finger movement but is a cushion for the protection of nerves and vessels against pressure. This cushion and the muscular ball of the thumb therefore offer a sole-surface for a kind of running-support of the feet by the hands, though in secondary development. But precisely that this is secondarily developed and not primary proves that the preceding, more primordial stage of the human hand was not the simple running foot as in all Tertiary mammals. 4. That the character of a grasping hand, hence not of a simple running extremity as in the other mammals, was originally present in the human foot results from embryonic development, which shows the foot at first still built in the manner of the hand, the big toe standing off like a thumb. The present human foot is thus regressed from a climbing foot, not from a mammalian walking foot. The anthropoid-ape type, however, has already reached with its foot so one-sidedly advanced a condition that it appears therein as a side-branch of the primordial human type, but not as the ancestor of man. The arrangement of the blood vessels and nerves in the human foot, too, is such that the space between the first and second toe is particularly emphasized, although outwardly it no longer comes to expression in the finished human foot. The consideration of the dentition leads to corresponding results: 1. Ape and human dentition are distinguished by their original gaplessness, which no late-Tertiary mammal any longer possesses. But even the lemurs included in the human genealogical tree already have, in the early Tertiary, the gap between canine and molar teeth. Only in one form (Necrolemur) from the early Tertiary does the full primate number of teeth appear. But to place it for that reason in the ancestral line of the primates and of man is forbidden by the strong canine, which is indeed a pithecoid but not a human character, and besides, instead of the gap it has a dentition smaller in number of teeth, and is thus already one-sidedly developed in this respect. 2. The present anthropoid apes have, in great contrast also to Diluvial man, strongly developed canines (Fig. 4; p. 61). If man descended from bearers of such a strong canine, the course of development would have to consist in a reduction of canine strength, hence the oldest common ancestral forms of man and primates would have had all the more strongly developed canines; the Diluvial man too, as the presumable forebear of present-world man, would have to have possessed still stronger canines than the latter, which is not the case, although the skull was more ape-like through its somewhat lower vaulting and its brow-ridges. But since the lemurs too, because of their other differentiation in skull and dentition, cannot be ancestors of man, the path for the human stock is free, outside the primates and the lemurs, for the time being down to the early Tertiary level. The formation of the skull also supplies some points of reference, which may be briefly mentioned: 1. In the human embryo the eyes shift from the side toward the front. The finished man is, in the forward-directed position of his eyes, the most perfect mammal. This perfection, however, is not equal to the most extreme development in this direction. For the anthropoid apes are exaggeratedly specialized in this, beyond the human condition. With that forward shifting of the eyes the original mammal-like nasal region is narrowed. In the apes it has become still narrower than in man, whose eye sockets lie still more laterally, even if they too, in contrast to the other mammals, are moved very far forward. The apes thus cannot be the preliminary stage to man in this respect either. 2. The vaulting of the skull is also connected with the shifting of the eyes. Through the displacement of the olfactory organ in the forward shifting of the eyes, through the resulting broadening of visual impressions, the cerebral hemisphere in man could expand so far that he surpassed all other animals in it. As long as the roof of the skull was still flat or rose toward the back, the front eye region was marked off from the rear skull region. When the cerebrum, and with it the roof of the skull, rose above the front region through its vaulting, those brow-ridges remained as an emphasized remnant which still distinguish Diluvial man and the Australian. In the gorilla the particularly strong jaw musculature attaches to the brow-ridges and strengthens them still further. At the moment in evolutionary history when the vaulting of the skull set in, in the connection described, the point of divergence had been reached, on the one hand toward present-day man, on the other toward the anthropoid apes. From there on there had to branch off, theoretically, on the one hand man with the high-vaulted forehead, on the other the ever more ape-like gorilla stock, whose extreme finally became the gorilla skull with the bony crest over the roof of the skull. The young gorilla still has a very man-like skull in contrast to the adult animal. After the gorilla stock had branched off, the still unfinished human stock went on its way to the finished cerebrum with the vaulted skull; at a higher stage of development reached in the process a split set in once more, which created the remaining complex of anthropoid apes. And with that the finished Quaternary complete man was there. Fr. Weidenreich, Der Menschenfuß (Zeitschr. für Morphologie und Anthropologie. Vol. 22. Stuttgart 1921, pp. 51—282), in his anatomical and static working-through of the human foot, arrives at deriving man theoretically from forms with a five-fingered climbing foot, in which he proves to be the most primitive and most mobile form in comparison with all mammals except the climbing primates and marsupials. In contrast to these, the human foot is quite one-sidedly specialized (p. 262). The foot of present-world man, despite noteworthy primitive characters of individual races, shows no direct relations to any particular group of primates (p. 267). The hominid ancestor must from the start have possessed long lower extremities when he thus took up the terrestrial mode of life. The anthropomorphic apes drop out. There must once have been a primate form which, like the marsupials, possessed a special skin-connection between the second and third toe (syndactyly) beside a general web-formation. This was partly lost, but was preserved in the hylobatid and hominid stock, and also proves the phylogenetic independence of the offshoot that led to man. Where it branched off is hard to say. Weidenreich assumes with Schwalbe that the offshoot “reaches down very far in its independence” (pp. 275/76).

The genera of apes known to us from the Tertiary (Fig. 5) are so specialized that, even by the research maxims of the doctrine of descent to date, they cannot possibly come into consideration as ancestors of a Diluvial man. The inadequate skull remains advocated<sup>17</sup> as ancestors of all Simiidae and Hominidae are partly unusable because of their incompleteness and the absence of all the remaining parts of the skeleton, and partly they are convergent formations of type-circles of their own. The canine tooth, receding in size, distinguishes man in any case from all genuine apes that we know, or at most permits their derivation from him himself. Yet the question of the canine tooth has entered a new stage, since Adloff made it probable that the canine of man and that of the ape need not be homologous formations at all, that perhaps shifts in the dentition set in already at the earliest divergence of the two parent forms, and that the human canine has remained so primitive compared with that of the anthropoid apes that a derivation of man from such apes therefore cannot be assumed. Even if earlier ancestors of man should once have possessed larger canines, these would have been wholly primordial ones, but certainly not ape canines<sup>17a</sup>.

<sup>17</sup> M. Schlosser (Beiträge zur Kenntnis der oligozänen Landsäugetiere aus dem Fayûm, Ägypten. In: Beiträge z. Paläontologie und Geologie Alluvium: Homo sapiens, Gorilla, Gibbon Diluvium: Homo Neanderthalensis, Homo Heidelbergensis, Eoanthropus (southern England) Pliocene (Late Tertiary): Palaeopithecus (India) Miocene (Late Tertiary): Dryopithecus (France, central Europe, India); Pliopithecus (France, central Europe) Oligocene (Early Tertiary): Propliopithecus (Egypt) Eocene (Early Tertiary): ancestral form (hypothetical) Österr.-Ungarns und des Orients. vol. 24. Wien-Leipzig 1911, p. 55ff.) traces the “family tree” of the simiids back to this Propliopithecus, and accordingly the lineage of ancestors shown overleaf would result; the individual genera in it are listed, in brief definitions and partly with illustrations of their remains, in the textbook by K. A. v. Zittel (Grundzüge der Paläontologie. vol. II. Wirbeltiere. 4th ed. München-Berlin 1923. Säugetiere, bearb. von M. Schlosser).
