The genera of this family tree occur distributed over widely separated countries and are founded in part only on jaw remains (p. 61, Fig. 4) or on mere single teeth, of which it is in part altogether doubtful whether they belong together (Eoanthropus). With this family tree Schlosser declares Tertiary man and his supposed primitive tools (eoliths) to have been refuted, especially as the diluvial primitive jaw of Homo Heidelbergensis is also supposed to have rendered the hypothesis of a Tertiary man superfluous. W. K. Gregory, who with Schlosser holds fast to the significance of the early-Tertiary Egyptian Propliopithecus as the direct ancestor of man and the anthropoid apes, gives the following family tree, here only insignificantly abridged (The origin and evolution of the human dentition. Journ. of Dental Research. Vol. II. p. 688. New Haven 1920. Also: Studies on the evolution of the Primates. Bullet. Americ. Mus. Nat. Hist. New York 1916, p. 313ff.): Present: Orang, Homo sapiens, Gorilla, Chimpanzee, Gibbon Diluvium: Homo Heidelbergensis, H. Neanderthalensis Pliocene: Pithecanthropus, Palaeopithecus, Dryopithecus Miocene: Palaeosimia, Dryopithecus, Pliopithecus ⁎) Oligocene: Propliopithecus, Parapithecus Upper Eocene: — ⁎) Placed in the Pliocene by Gregory. What at once strikes one in this table, too, and prejudices one against the arrangement is that precisely again at the decisive point of the alleged becoming-man, where man and ape were supposed to part, so that one might speak of a demonstrably late origin of man, an imaginary ancestral form has to be indicated, a knot of dashes in place of a real progenitor. Such, however, is always the outcome of the formal theory of descent, whatever animals, high or low, one may have to do with: always where we ought to have a real “primal form,” a real ancestor, we must content ourselves with hyphens; the real primal form, however — in this case Propliopithecus — is a lower jaw that is not even complete. Were the whole skeleton found with it, it would still have to be shown whether the genus was not after all so one-sidedly developed that it would in turn have to be eliminated from this family tree as a side branch. Consider, too, that on the abridged table above the circumstance still speaks very much in favor of this family tree that some time-stages in the Miocene and Oligocene have been taken together here, so that in reality Propliopithecus is removed from man still further, without certain intermediate links. Let us therefore draft the family tree with the preceding means, without petitio principii; then it probably looks like this, the dashing representing the hypothetical: Present: Orang, Homo sapiens, Gorilla, Chimp., Gibbon Diluvium (Quaternary): Homo Heidelbergensis, Homo neanderthalensis Pliocene (Late Tertiary): Pithecanthropus, Palaeopithecus, Dryopithecus Miocene (Late Tertiary): Palaeosimia, Dryopithecus, Pliopithecus Oligocene (Early Tertiary): Propliopithecus, Parapithecus Eocene (Early Tertiary): — That kind of reconstruction of family trees, as is customary, rests precisely on the formalistic procedure disputed in principle in the text. Under its influence was written the essay by G. Schwalbe, readable even for the non-specialist, which may be regarded as the summarizing result of anatomical and palaeontological research of the last half-century: “Die Abstammung des Menschen und die ältesten Menschenformen. (Kultur der Gegenwart. Part III. Anthropologie. Leipzig-Berlin 1923, p. 223 to 338). Schwalbe is an opponent of Klaatsch’s doctrine and recognizes neither the derivation of the apes from the human stock nor the existence of a Tertiary-age man. When he points out that man contains many characteristics which he shares with the lower mammals (palatal folds, Jacobson’s organ in the nose, agreement of the embryonic forms, e.g. in the gill slits, etc.) and thereby connects him formally with the lower mammals, then the proper way of expressing these facts would not be that man “therefore” “descends” from lower mammals, but that he has lower mammalian stages in himself; whereby he may just as well be their ancestor as both may have a common starting point. For even the lower mammals, as far back as one goes, are so one-sidedly developed that it is not apparent where man, with his peculiarities that are often not only very primitive but also differently constituted, could be attached later, toward the end of the Tertiary. For the series of apes given by Schlosser — so far as we do not merely compare single teeth and jaw remains with one another — is already, according to Klaatsch’s expositions, included in the original primitiveness especially of the hand, so that one may not let man as such arise out of that only shortly before the Diluvium. For this let reference be made to the works of Klaatsch himself already cited, the critique of which is then to be found in Schwalbe’s essay named here (loc. cit. p. 307ff.). The scientific dispute takes on a different face if one gives up the biologically untenable old idea of the family tree as a fiction and an abstraction not corresponding to nature, makes clear to oneself the essence of the theory of types and the law of temporal character, and then considers the naturally given forms without forcing them into a scheme.

<sup>17a</sup> A. Adloff, Einige besondere Bildungen an den Zähnen des Menschen und ihre Bedeutung für seine Vorgeschichte. Anatom. Anzeiger v. Eggeling. vol. 58. Jena 1924, p. 497ff.

The lemurs or half-apes, above all, stand closer, through their quite different and primitive characters, to a formal hypothetical primal form of the mammals than do all the other Tertiary mammals. The Quaternary man too has several such quite primitive peculiarities, which likewise connect him with hypothetical quadrupeds that must be dated very far back into earth history. Thus his gapless, complete dentition, which does not, like that of all Tertiary mammals, tend toward the formation of a gap between the front teeth and the molars, but is at most somewhat reduced, in us late men alone, by the loss of the last molar. In its completeness it resembles the oldest Tertiary mammalian forms; but the basic principles of the theory of descent to date themselves forbid connecting man with such forms, according to which living beings already distinguished from one another by one-sided [p. 63] differentiation cannot be directly connected phylogenetically. In the five-fingered extremity too there lies a very old character, not an earth-historically recent one, which by no means permits the derivation of the human type from any Tertiary mammal. True, there are Early Tertiary mammals with the primitive five-fingered extremity; but they, like later genera with the same character, are so one-sidedly developed<sup>18</sup> that the origin of the five-fingered primate extremity, especially the front one, can be dated back into much older times. In the simplicity present in man it is already of Palaeozoic age. True, it is an error to regard the fully five-fingered extremity as the primal condition of the land animal, as people have done hitherto. The most recent finds and investigations of fossil primitive amphibians from the Permian have shown that even those early amphibious land animals had a reduced, that is a four-toed, fore extremity, while their hind extremity was still five-toed. If they were in this respect already one-sidedly specialized forms, then they also stand to that extent further from the primitive hypothetical land animal, which was probably at first seven-toed. Not only the embryonic investigation of certain living amphibians yields this, but it is also evident from fossil finds of ancient Permian amphibian forms, whose still five-toed hind extremity contains the remains of a supernumerary little finger, not pathological or atavistic, however, but still present as a normal remnant<sup>19</sup>. Accordingly the five-fingered land extremity was already, toward the end of the Palaeozoic era, itself a stage of regression from a still older form. We can therefore hold fast that at least the five-fingered extremity was a basic character of all beings turned for the first time wholly to life on dry land, even if still amphibious in habit; that hence the five-fingered land extremity is at least an inheritance from the Carboniferous. And so man, together with all the likewise five-fingered mammals, can reach back into that ancient epoch, because all quadrupeds known fossil up to that point are in their extremities or in the rest of the body already far too one-sidedly developed for the derivation of the human body from them to succeed.

<sup>18</sup> In this sense even Schwalbe says (loc. cit. p. 329) that it is by no means admissible to trace the grasping hand of man back to one in early-Tertiary mammals. Marsupials, he says, would not come into consideration because of their otherwise high degree of differentiation, unless one wished to connect with the older common starting forms, to be assumed, of the placentals and marsupials. One sees how, through the factual consideration, even this investigator, who so thoroughly rejects a higher age of man, is led, without noticing it, to an extraordinarily high age.

<sup>19</sup> W. K. Gregory, R. W. Miner, G. K. Noble, The Carpus of Eryops and the Structure of the primitive Chiropterygium. Bullet. Americ. Mus. Nat. Hist. New York 1923, Vol. 48, p. 279. W. K. Gregory, Present status of the problem of the origin of the Tetrapoda etc. Annals New York Acad. Science. Vol. 26, 1915, p. 317—383.

[p. 64] The question whether and how the human hand could have emerged from a primal-extremity stage, to be assumed at most, can be answered neither by comparative anatomy nor by fossil finds. Klaatsch too contented himself with this limit of our comparative-anatomical knowledge and says only that for the consideration of the origin of man it suffices for the present to establish that he shares the hand with the five-fingered form of the ancient land vertebrates, and thus not first with that of the Tertiary mammals. It should also now be clear, says Klaatsch, that the estimation, formerly widespread, of man as the last end-member of an allegedly laborious evolution does not hold. He is not to be conceived as the final result of a very complicated transformation. Such conjectures were not lacking formerly, when people believed that man could descend from four-footed animals. But quadrupedalism means that the limbs of the being concerned are formed into feet, that is, can perform no other functions besides supporting and running. Man, however, has retained the old, many-sided and therefore infinitely valuable tool. “That man remained indifferent, preserved his many-sidedness — therein lies a great part of the secret of his extraordinary success. This is a wholly different interpretation of the course of development from that in the old Darwinian sense. Man is not a triumph of the struggle for existence; no, on the contrary: his victory rests in this, that he was spared from the victims of natural selection, that he kept his hand. If one wants to look for miracles, one need not flee into the realm of the supernatural. Nature herself, our own being, offers miracles enough. By the rules of the order of probability the exceptional position of man in relation to the whole rest of the animal kingdom is in any case a very strange one. It is not the possession of the hand as such — it was once proper to all animals — but the circumstance that this structure was retained in its primordiality and that it could place itself in the service of a mighty unfolding of the brain — that is the remarkable thing.”

Man can also be of at least Mesozoic, indeed Palaeozoic age for this reason, that he is a plantigrade. The [p. 65] oldest, hypothetical mammalian types, and the Tertiary ones formally derivable from them at most, are perfect plantigrades or at least point unambiguously, as descendants, to forms with a plantigrade foot. Man himself, again, cannot descend from such genera, but at best had, in a very early time, quite close to the Palaeozoic point of origin of the mammals, an ancestor to be sought already there in common with them. Thus he has, at least since the Early Mesozoic, preserved the plantigrade in himself on his own line of development, which does not prevent his great toe on the foot from having been at first opposable, so that the foot also possessed the character of a grasping hand. Even in Mesozoic times, then, he was already a mammalian stock of his own, separated from the later Tertiary stocks, and hence present as the human type, although in a form other than in the Stone Age.

Fig. 6. Tracks of an amphibian or reptile (Chirotherium) from the Early Mesozoic Thuringian Bunter sandstone. The hind extremity larger, the fore extremity smaller. 1/4 natural size. (After R. Owen from Zittel-Broili, Grundzüge der Paläontologie II. 1923.)

Klaatsch in his day also drew upon certain land-animal tracks in the central German sandstone of Early Mesozoic age as an argument from analogy for the great age of the human type, and I originally followed him in this. They belong to very ancient land animals, known only from these traces and not from skeletal remains (Fig. 6). They are striking for their great similarity to human hands, even though they certainly did not belong to such. The older ones, from [p. 66] the Permian, are distinguished by the fact that the mutual proportions in size of the fore and hind extremities approach each other still more than those of the somewhat younger ones from the Triassic, where the hind extremities substantially exceed the fore ones in size. From the form of the impressions one can draw the conclusion that the “hand animal” (Chirotherium) to which they belonged, if not quite upright, nevertheless ran on the hind extremity at first less, later more decidedly. The older tracks in particular, it is said, are significant because they have great similarity to children’s hands. One cannot, says Klaatsch, in fact describe them better than by a comparison with such hands or also with embryonic states of the human hand (Fig. 7), in which the plumpness and coarseness of the child’s hand appears still more striking. Such an embryonic character of the human hand, as it also belongs to the Thuringian tracks, is the little extended surface between the ball of the thumb and the remaining fingers, or the extraordinarily broad metacarpus, or the short, stocky fingers. The thumb in the Thuringian track is provided with a thick pad and thereby attests its opposability. The underside of the fingers often shows the joint depressions corresponding to the finger phalanges. The fourth finger was mostly the longest.

Fig. 7. Embryonic hand of man. (From Klaatsch, Abstammungslehre. 1911.)

This view is no longer tenable, since we know through recent fundamental investigations by Soergel<sup>19a</sup> — what had also in part already been conjectured earlier — that the “thumb” on these extremities is not a thumb at all, but lies on the opposite side of the “hand,” hence represents a secondary acquisition, a “false” toe, and so may perhaps be interpreted as an adaptive formation to upright walking, for supporting the sole, or as a broadening of the base of the foot for better prevention of sinking in. On the other hand these forms are a renewed proof of the reign of a formation which coincides also in other Mesozoic types with the tendency to half-upright walking and thereby produces a certain spreadability of one toe-member.

<sup>19a</sup> W. Soergel. Die Fährten der Chirotheria. Eine paläobiologische Studie. Jena 1925.

[p. 67] In general we find in the land animals of the Mesozoic age, as already mentioned earlier, the disposition to the upright gait on the hind legs, and this often in so perfect a manner that it may be spoken of directly as a temporal mark. Linked with it, strangely enough, is occasionally the opposable thumb, which is said to have served also for defense. If the sandstone tracks showed us this already in the finest way, we also have whole skeletons of the most various reptile groups of the Mesozoic epoch, especially of the Cretaceous and of the Upper Jurassic, which have developed that mark quite perfectly. So, for instance, the upright Iguanodons of the Belgian Lower Cretaceous formation. These were land-dwelling herbivores up to 5 m high, in habit very similar to others, for example North American and African land saurians of carnivorous as well as herbivorous character, of very large and small dimensions. As already mentioned, they show the upright gait, but have only a three-toed or at most four-toed extremity, on which the fifth member hangs at most as a rudiment. On the other hand, it was precisely not the younger but the earth-historically oldest land animals of the Palaeozoic age that had a decided disposition to an opposable first toe, corresponding to our human thumb. And this is all the more a proof of the primitiveness of the human type in that the earth-historically younger, Early Tertiary mammals, to be sure, often still possessed fully five-fingered extremities, but had no opposable thumb with it, and moreover belonged to groups whose specific development certainly lets them become, for no investigator, a phylogenetic point of attachment for man precisely or for man-like primates. Thus the human hand goes back in its foundation directly to the oldest land-animal forms, even if we should not wish to derive man himself directly from those ancient forms phylogenetically. For man is, in comparative-anatomical terms, first of all a mammal, not a reptile, as those Palaeozoic land-animal forms were, and so cannot “descend” from the latter either. Yet they show us at any rate in what epoch of ancient formation his extremities are rooted.

Likewise the land reptiles of the Mesozoic described in the preceding section, with their upright gait and their simply stretched hind extremity, whose length is not, as in late-period mammals, brought about only secondarily by the raising of the foot<sup>20</sup>, point to a Mesozoic [p. 68] temporal signature, and we find man too equipped with this character. This also points, by comparison, to his great earth-historical age.

<sup>20</sup> To be distinguished from the uniform lengthening of the whole extremity is its secondary elongation in jumping animals such as birds and certain mammals (jumping hares and jumping mice) of the Cenozoic age. There it is not the hind legs as a whole that are lengthened, and the foot retains its peculiarity, but above all the tarsal bones that are greatly elongated and otherwise modified besides. Only in a certain portion of the dinosaurs from the Jurassic and Cretaceous does the same lengthening of the tarsal bones occur; these are those genera which have something thoroughly bird-like, which walked upright like an ostrich and, on account of their hollow bones as well as some other skeletal peculiarities, may stand in phylogenetic relation to birds. Their in part gigantic dimensions suggest the legendary dragons in them. To be distinguished from this once more is stilt-leggedness, which is attained uniformly not only in the two hind feet but in all four feet through lengthening and fusion of individual tarsal bones, as in deer and sheep. Especially important for establishing long-leggedness as a temporal signature is, furthermore, that the marsupials too, which at any rate exclusively make up the type of the Mesozoic mammal, so far as they walk and stand upright, always have the foot, already somewhat lengthened at its root, yet lying wholly on the ground so that outwardly the habitus of the ordinary Mesozoic land reptile with its simply lengthened hind legs is thoroughly preserved.

In the Late Palaeozoic, intimated in the Permian but emerging clearly especially in the Triassic, we come upon a phenomenon that reveals to us when the mammalian type awakened and was completed as a temporal signature. There exists on a great southern continent, later submerged, as well as partly in North America, appearing somewhat later also in Russia, a reptile group, the Theromorphs, to which, strikingly, in many of their genera, organs and a general habit of mammalian character are allotted. Among them there appear even forms with a mammal-like — carnivorous or herbivorous dentition, such as genuine Tertiary and present-day mammals have (Fig. 8); but in their inner structure and being these apparent mammalian characters were nevertheless reptilian. The skeleton too remains in decisive features the unmistakable reptile skeleton, even if the outward configuration and formation often recalls the mammal most strongly. Under the influence of the formalistic theory of descent people have tried again and again to address those figures as the bodily ancestors of the later mammals; but this has failed every time. We have here once more the formally intersecting circles of forms according to the scheme given on p. 46 (Fig. 1 B). In the Permian the moment has come when the mammalian lies, as it were, in the air, when it begins to become a temporal signature, so that a large part of the reptiles of that age too assume the mammalian habit without ever having been mammals or [p. 69] entering at all, phylogenetically, upon that path. If one should some day find among this contemporary company, as is to be expected, genuine, unobjectionable mammalian ancestors and thus mammals themselves, they will be as similar to some theromorph reptiles as, say, the Tertiary apes are to man: one then again has to do with formal intersections of the fixed type-circles, whose overlapping surfaces feign a natural family and furnish species that will look like parent species or primal forms of the remaining, formally adjoining genera, of reptiles as of mammals, and yet are not.

Fig. 8. Skull of a Triassic reptile, with mammalian characters and mammalian dentition. South Africa. (From W. K. Gregory. Journ. Dental Research. 1920.) Natural size.

With this the point of time is determined at which at least the earliest becoming-mammal took place. There the human stock, with its mammalian nature and its five-fingered, plantigrade extremity, must be rooted at the least — as a mammal-like human stock, that is, distinguishable from other types appearing simultaneously with it. Never, however, can reptiles have become mammals. Their skeletal structure and other characters, such as the absence of skin glands, do not let this appear possible. If we want to attempt genealogical trees at all, we must attribute amphibian characters to the Permian hypothetical mammalian type, not because it had emerged from genuine primitive amphibians, but because at that time the amphibian form and mode of life was a temporal signature, and accordingly the primal mammal wore that garb. Since now the oldest mammals, which we know from teeth found in the Triassic, are already one-sidedly specialized as marsupials, so that they must have separated early from the rest of the mammalian and human stock; since, further, the reptile cannot have belonged to the line of descent, the mammal-like human stock must have had amphibian-like outward characters in the Permian, but must already then have appeared as such independently.

Quite consistent, then, is the conclusion which Klaatsch had already reached in appraising human anatomy, without however having become aware of the whole immense reach of his discovery, when he says: “The oldest stages of human prehistory will therefore be identical with those of the primal history of the land vertebrates.” But this primal history lies already in the Palaeozoic age.

[p. 70] The only palaeontologist who has drawn the most obvious conclusion from the more recent phylogenetic and comparative-anatomical knowledge also for the human stock, although in a still little concrete outline, has been Steinmann<sup>21</sup>. Given the slowness of the development of all animal stocks, he says, one may expect that the becoming of man too in the stock of the Anthropotheria was accomplished extraordinarily slowly. Because skeletal remains and tools of man have become known only from the Diluvial period, man as such is held to be a product only of this last earth-historical period. It is more probable, he holds, that man, just like the animals accompanying him in the Diluvial period: horse, elephant, rhinoceros, hippopotamus, deer, etc., already existed in the Tertiary, although in a somewhat different garb, ancient in phylogenetic terms. Just as at the beginning of the lines of descent the generalized basic forms expected by the older theory of descent, from which subsequent specialized types could be derived, have never been found, so one knows from the oldest section of the Tertiary no fewer than 25 genera of the primate type, of which none fulfills the requirements that would make it, in anatomical respect, a phylogenetic link between man and lower primates. Evidently, then, both types already at the beginning of the Tertiary have a long independent history behind them, so that one can no longer evade the conclusion that the whole kin extends back in several separate lines into the Mesozoic age.

<sup>21</sup> G. Steinmann, Der Ursprung des Menschen. Die Westmark. Köln-Mülheim 1921, p. 457ff.

Klaatsch and Steinmann, with their ideas and conclusions, held something in their hands whose whole reach they did not yet grasp. Perhaps Klaatsch, much attacked and decried as a visionary, sensed it more strongly, yet with him too it certainly did not break through to full consciousness. Neither of the two made it clear to himself, or regarded it as the inexorable consequence of his demonstration, that methodically and in result he had completely overthrown the whole human and animal doctrine of descent, as it has been maintained stereotypically for sixty years without any essentially new, deepening point of view, and had put in its place the type theory and thus also the knowledge of the independence of the human [p. 71] stock back to the oldest times of the becoming of the land animal.

Research has not stood still at this. Since the first publication of these pages, further significant indications of a very high age of the human race and of certain stages of forebears have emerged through the investigation of inner and outer organs<sup>21a</sup>. The formation of the chin, or rather of the jaw, represents a condition that follows immediately upon the primeval inward inclination of the teeth in sharks and certain reptiles, and thus recalls the earliest beginning of the mammalian era and in part even a pre-mammalian stage. One could, says Westenhöfer, make the experiment of handing an unprejudiced person a series of various lower jaws, so that he might put the human one in the place where it belongs comparatively according to its whole development; he would in any case place it before every mammal. But that means nothing other than that in the position of the teeth and the formation of the jaw too all mammals are so specialized that man here too is their ancestral stage, not their descendant stage.

<sup>21a</sup> M. Westenhöfer, Über die Erhaltung von Vorfahrenmerkmalen beim Menschen usw. Medizinische Klinik. Jahrg. 19. Berlin 1923, Nr. 37. —, Das menschliche Kinn, seine Entstehung und anthropologische Bedeutung. Archiv f. Frauenkunde u. Konstitutionsforschung. vol. X. Berlin 1924, p. 239—262.

To the same investigator we owe the knowledge that in the state of childhood, as well as a not inconsiderable percentage of adults, with the funnel-shaped vermiform appendix of the cecum, with a lobed instead of a closed kidney and a spleen notched several times, we have remained standing at a phylogenetically lower stage of development. This threefold “progonism” points, strangely enough, to aquatic-animal stages, in that those formations of the kidney and spleen are reached or surpassed only by water-adapted mammals. And so the possibility cannot be dismissed that an aquatile form was present in the specifically human line of forebears. Westenhöfer holds that this mixture of progonic and non-progonic men came about perhaps because they were mixed from two stocks, of which the one was already somewhat further advanced, the other somewhat left behind; the latter would have stood nearer to the anthropoid-ape forebear, the former would already have been more present-day-human; but the roots of both lay far back in earth-historical antiquity.

As one sees, the indications of the ancientness and primitiveness of man multiply. Only in the development of the brain [p. 72] and thereby in the perfected structure of the facial skull is man ahead of all, while the apes had already deviated laterally before this perfected stage and specialized themselves degeneratively. And Westenhöfer says aptly that all these facts can probably mean nothing other than that the mammalian stock as a whole had taken the path to the cerebrum, but that this development was inhibited to a so much stronger degree the more the dentition inclined, that is, the stronger and mightier it became at the same time. Translated into our manner of expression, however, this means: the mammals have remained behind on the path of development of the human stock and from the point of branching have then become one-sidedly specialized offshoots of the common and continuous primal stock, which at all times was precisely the “human stock” itself.

But however that may be, the ancient stock of the human type — on this there can scarcely be, palaeontologically, a difference of opinion in principle — must have had a different appearance in past periods of the earth, whether it emerged genetically from some animal forms or whether it followed its own course independently from the oldest land-animal age; it undoubtedly had its evolution and produced many mutations. But which? We now recall once more the law set out above, according to which in definite epochs definite formations and organs come to light in the most various types. If, however, as we said, an animal group or a single genus in a later time still has fully developed or rudimentary in itself a temporally characteristic formal condition established for an earlier time, one recognizes by this its geological minimum age, even without possessing from earlier times of its existence fossil documents of it. If, therefore, the human stock — the word used, for my part, in any phylogenetic sense whatever — still goes back into the Mesozoic age, then it must have had Mesozoic formal peculiarities at that time and still bear them fully developed or rudimentary in itself now; thus, for example, the opposable thumb or the upright gait on primarily, not secondarily lengthened or raised hind legs. And this it has. But it has also preserved in its extremity the primitive formal condition [p. 73] of the land animal, though somewhat modified, and that is of at least Late Palaeozoic origin.

With this insight we may now, according to the law of temporal characters, trace the human stock back as far as the Palaeozoic era and assume the following stages for its evolution. At first it must have possessed amphibian and seemingly reptilian features. It perhaps had, like the amphibians, the dragging gait and fingers and toes joined by webbing, and probably also no decidedly opposable thumb as yet. With the oldest amphibians and reptiles it perhaps shared a partly horny, armored body, a feature that may be regarded as a characteristic of the period in the closing age of the Palaeozoic epoch, in so far as the amphibians too, with their skin-mantle that is in itself slimy and studded with glands, pass over to such armor-formation, often on the throat and breast and on the back as well, increasing to the strength of dermal bone plates. With both groups, however, the hypothetical primordial man probably had a fully developed parietal organ, that is, an eye-like opening on the roof of the skull, fully developed, which those oldest land-dwellers had in common without exception and which may be regarded as a definite sense organ, so far undecipherable, though certainly formed like an eye. Primordial man was probably a mammal from time immemorial. This mammalian nature was, however, habitually veiled by the features just enumerated. In view of the fact mentioned earlier, that in the late Palaeozoic distinct mammalian characters make themselves noticeable even among the reptiles, although a reptile can never have been a mammal, we must assume that at that time the mammalian stock as such came into being, but under the outer garb of reptilian character; and likewise man as a land-animal-like being.

In the Mesozoic age primordial man — he will have lived in many stocks and individual forms of different kinds — will in general have slowly lost his parietal organ through atrophy and now revealed his mammalian nature more clearly. In the Triassic we find the first remains recognizable as such and in this sense genuine mammalian remains; they belong to the stock of the marsupials, and only such are found, in rare remains, throughout the whole Mesozoic age. This was the stage of mammalian becoming at that time, precisely as temporal signature [p. 74], and therefore the man who already looked more mammalian externally will also have shared the anatomical properties of the marsupial, just as in the Palaeozoic age he bore those of the reptile and amphibian. In the Mesozoic age we also recognized the epoch in which the upright gait of the land animals was striven for; we transfer this accordingly to the human stock of that time. It was also the time when the more fused, more primitive extremity gave way to the more perfected one with the spreadable fingers and the opposable thumb; so in this respect too man will have become more similar to us. The mammal no longer had a parietal organ; the Mesozoic man had therefore atrophied it more and more and in its place attained the fuller development of the cranial capsule, the fuller development of the cerebrum; he must have acquired a more vaulted skull with a set-off, less flat forehead.

With the Cretaceous, at least its last half, in which we must already assume the final emergence of the typical five-fingered mammalian world, standing complete with the early Tertiary epoch, beyond the marsupial condition, mammalian man too will then have approached closely that condition which confronts us, complete, in Ice Age man. Yet Ice Age man, as a degenerate descendant of late Tertiary man, is formed in a strongly pithecoid way, because at that time, as already shown, the ape-creating temporal character prevailed. Before that, then, man must in his various special stocks have possessed all manner of external features such as hold for the individual stages of the Tertiary; thus in some of his stocks features of carnivores and herbivores, developed to the extreme, will in part not have been lacking. Finally the human stock ended, with the expulsion of all animal characters not belonging to the late type, in our present human stage, which certainly will not have been reached uniformly but along many lines of descent, and which appears so uniform anatomically only because the temporal signature of our human stage is dominant just now. This stage populates the earth today, as in the Palaeozoic age probably the parietal-eyed, amphibian-like, and in the Mesozoic age the marsupial-like human being did.

[p. 75] Man and the vertebrate generally, including even the oldest fishes, has scarcely anything in his organism that would permit us to link him, along with the whole stock of the higher animals, to lower forms, such as crustaceans or worms. A small fish now living, Branchiostoma, outwardly worm-shaped, but perhaps not an original genus, rather one that has become apparently primitive through atrophy, from which one wished to derive the picture of the ancestor of the vertebrates, differs just as fundamentally from all crustaceans or worms by the radically different axial position of the whole body: it has a dorsal cord, a spinal cord, whereas all those lower animals have a ventral cord as their central nerve strand. The gulf seems thereby unbridgeable. And yet there is at least one point of support for the view that in the human organism, as in that of the later vertebrates, a peculiarity may have been left over which in fact points to a stage still older than that of the primordial amphibian or of the fish, and refers directly back to the world of those lower animals. The remarkable organ of the parietal eye, which can be quite literally a symbol of the becoming of man, points us here too, as it were, down to an oldest trace of his origin. In man, as already stressed, the primeval organ shows itself in twofold reduction. First, the pineal gland itself is already an atrophied eye; but it has a lateral companion, which gives the impression of a still further advanced atrophy and suggests to us the assumption that the fully developed parietal organ must formerly have been double. Since, however, [p. 76] its less atrophied part proves, by comparative anatomy, to be an old eye, the formerly fully developed organ would have to have represented two eyes standing directly beside each other. Now among the Lower and Middle Palaeozoic fishes, and also in crustacean-like animals, we do find such a fully developed double pair of eyes, on top of the roof of the skull, although beside it, or farther to the side or forward, two proper normal eyes still lie (Fig. 9). So here perhaps a faint ray of a temporal signature, not of a descent, can still be caught, which may once also have been present at the earliest bodily beginnings of the human being.

Fig. 9. Crustacean-like animal of the Lower Palaeozoic. North America. With two small eye-like ocelli lying in the middle of the head and large normal eyes at the margin, set forward. ¹/₃ natural size. (From J. M. Clarke and R. Ruedemann, Eurypterida of New York. 1912.)

But there the finding of comparative anatomy loses itself in uncertainty. Yet within the rest of the vertebrate stock itself the formations of man lead us back, in the whole series, to the stage of the primordial-amphibian form-existence. And with that we have won the ground for a new determination of the age of the human type, of the human stock, and can now draw conclusions which until now have been touched on only by analogy. We may expect to find the human stock as such already in the early Mesozoic, indeed in the late Palaeozoic, that is, a being that distinguished itself from the rest of the animal world entelechially through its humanness, and hence also through certain possessions of soul and spirit. But if this holds in any sense, then it is also possible that primeval human conditions and experiences from a quite differently constituted natural and psychic world still resound in later myths and legends. To draw them forth shall now be attempted, along a path that leads us to new pictures of existence.

[p. 77]

### Körpermerkmale des sagenhaften Urmenschen — Bodily Features of the Legendary Primordial Man

So our gaze can roam far into the distances of primordial human existence. It has become probable to us that man in many changing forms can nevertheless be so ancient that legendary lore about him, however disconnected, might have been handed down from times which, according to the prevailing doctrine, we should regard as earth-historical and life-historical, indeed, but not as belonging to the history of humanity. The manner and path of the “tradition” shall here be left aside. If, according to our doctrine, man as man is as old as we tried to establish and now wish to assume, and if myths and legends are in many cases, or perhaps for the most part, knowledge of the former world, even if long since no longer original and in many cases distorted, then we may also proceed to the attempt which the preceding sections were meant to introduce: to build up from the legends and myths a world-picture, such as the man of the former world might have had around him and within him, to discover his environment and his own form and soul, by feeling our way into the myths and legends and cosmogonies, striving to win their core, and taking them naively as natural-historical narratives. Thus they conversely acquire documentary value, in that we extend their contents back toward those primeval times.

That the older man has not yet been found fossil is probably due, as was once stressed before, to the fact that he lived in regions which today have for the most part vanished, such as the great Gondwana continent or archipelago, extending from South Africa and Madagascar across India and Australia into the Polynesian island world (Fig. 22); or that other parts of the earth which might still have formed the scene of his existence are geologically all but unexplored. In this respect a great deal is to be expected from the future thorough investigation of certain African-Indo-Australian or Polynesian strata systems of the Mesozoic and late Palaeozoic eras. Whoever, then, [p. 78] shares our standpoint on the great age of the human stock will not find it surprising if one day, in such southern land formations belonging to old Gondwanaland, impressions of footprints, skeletal remains, objects, graves or ruins of buildings of a human being of the former world are found. That human skeletons and also objects appear extremely rarely, even where man once lived in large numbers and in a high state of culture, is shown not only by the general difficulty of getting hold of such remains even in well-preserved caves inhabited by late man, but also by the fact that of our own most immediate forefathers from the brightest light of recent history, say the Franks, indeed even of the people of past centuries, we find hardly any remains worth mentioning in the ground, compared with their number and cultural height. For in order that something become fossil, such extraordinarily favorable circumstances are needed that one may in general expect them only in shallow sea with rapid sedimentation, and only in raised ground left by the shallow sea does one have them from the former world to a greater extent. When on land sedimentations with richer fossil embedding occur, such deposits almost always owe their origin to catastrophic events, for example volcanic eruptions, in which enormous masses of dust and ash come down and in the shortest time cover everything, or in which mud-rains and mud-streams arising in the process swiftly drown and bury everything; or to rapid shifts of rivers with great transports of sand and mud; or to a rapid sinking of animals in swamps. An example of the first, from historical times, is the burial of Pompeii, where we actually find a human settlement as if fossil, and the body impressions of the people besides. In the North American Bridger beds we have the remains of a late Tertiary swamp and lake landscape with rich animal and plant life, which were covered by volcanic tuff masses, probably accompanied by suffocating gases and vapors, which killed the world living there at one stroke and soon, under the covering, let it become fossil; and that not once only, but several times. In such strata systems human traces of the Tertiary might well be discovered some day, [p. 79] if only in the form of utensils. The Pithecanthropus strata on Java, in which the once much-discussed remnant of the ape-man was found, are such volcanic ashes of the Diluvial period, later redeposited by rivers. From very ancient earth-historical times too, especially in the south, as already mentioned, there are such and similar deposits, and it is therefore not excluded that precisely in terrestrial strata systems of the age of the former world we shall some day make a lucky find of the oldest human forms or of their cultural remains, which will then probably go back to a catastrophic embedding. Meanwhile we must content ourselves with other indications and take from the legends what has been handed down to us of the bodily features of the most ancient human races, and examine it anatomically as well as in terms of evolutionary history and clarify it as far as possible.

Already in the preceding section attention was called to the fact, treated by Klaatsch, that those old reptile tracks or, what is more probable, amphibian tracks from the Central German sandstone of the Permian-Triassic period are strongly reminiscent of embryonically formed human hands. This hand form stands in connection with the upright gait, to a certain degree, of such animals and with the opposable thumb. All this is typically developed in Mesozoic times as temporal character, as was shown earlier. Of men with a character of the hand differing from that of the later ones the legends speak now and then. Thus it is said in a tradition of the Jews that the hands of all the children of men before Noah “were still unformed and as if closed, and the fingers were not separated from one another. But Noah was born, and behold, on his hands the fingers were single and each for itself”<sup>22</sup>. To this the Babylonian Gilgamesh epic furnishes a striking parallel<sup>23</sup>. There Gilgamesh, the god-man, journeys into the realm of the dead to his ancestor Utnapishtim, from whom he wishes to obtain counsel about life and death. And as he comes across the sea in the ship, Utnapishtim stands over there on the shore and wonders at the newcomer:

<sup>22</sup> M. J. bin Gorion, Die Sagen der Juden. I. von der Urzeit. 2nd ed. Frankfurt a. M. 1919, p. 177.

<sup>23</sup> Das Gilgamesch-Epos, Neu übersetzt v. A. Ungnad, erklärt v. H. Greßmann. Forschungen zur Religion und Literatur des Alten und Neuen Testaments. Heft 14. Göttingen 1911, p. 49/50.

Ut-napištim — looks toward the distance [his face], / He speaks to himself and [says] the word ... / [p. 80] “Why ... does one sail [in the ship] who does not belong to me (?)? / “He who comes is surely no man at all, / “The right hand of a ma[n(?) he surely has not]. / “I look toward him, but [I do] not [understand it].”

Here, then, the ancestor wonders at the right — that is quite obviously the hand and not the right side — of the descendant. Moreover, they seem, in connection with this difference of hand, to have conversed about their not quite identical bodily form. For apart from the fact that Utnapishtim already notices the difference in the hand as the stranger draws near, they must also have spoken of their difference, with the result:

Gilgamesh says to him, to Ut-napištim, the distant one: / “I look at you, Ut-napištim, / Your measures are not different, just like me you are too ...”

If it were not already clear from another context that the figure of Utnapishtim, who conveys the Flood report to Gilgamesh, is only the ancestor simply, in whose mouth the narrative is placed, and that conversely Gilgamesh too in myth is another than the later Babylonian historical king to whose name the epic was reverently attached, then the remark about the hand and the bodily form would itself show that the Utnapishtim of the realm of the dead is not the biblical Noah for the reason that he tells of the Flood, but that here, in contrast to the younger human form, an older one in general reports on the Flood experienced by it. The heterogeneity of the Gilgamesh epic has indeed already been demonstrated by Gressmann; in it, like the Iliad and Odyssey, the mythological and the recently historical, the externally historical and the essentially metaphysical are combined, perhaps even thrown together, quite uncomprehendingly, by the late author and exploiter. Here it is now clear that Utnapishtim, who according to another legend is also regarded as covered with fur and lost this hair after his expulsion from Paradise<sup>24</sup>, possessed an older form of hand; which one — that remains undecided; and Gilgamesh, as the later one, possesses a differently shaped one. Yet the difference seems not to have been so great that the figures would not have recognized each other as of the same stock. The realm of the dead, where they meet and recognize each other, is a transcendent state, [p. 81] in which the past is not perceived with the outer senses. What significance that has for the knowledge of prehistory will be shown by a later chapter.

<sup>24</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 226/27.

In Utnapishtim, then, we have to do with a primeval human form; he will therefore not have been late man with the spreadable hand, but the older type with embryonically fused fingers. Whether Gilgamesh himself, as the younger human type, already has the perfectly spreadable fingers, or whether there are still other possible intermediate stages involved, cannot be determined on the basis of the legend; but this much may be held fast, that we find ourselves thereby in a primeval time-circle, and that the externally fused hand belongs, according to temporal character, in the formative circle of the Mesozoic, where such fusions of a completely five-fingered primitive extremity occur, indeed, in aquatic animals, but also in human embryonic form in those sandstone tracks. Later, when only with the beginning of the Tertiary the unfolding of the mammals becomes clearly visible to the palaeontologist, the unreduced five-fingered land extremity is in any case fully spreadable. Where it is externally fused, as in some water-dwelling mammals, it is either at the same time reduced and no longer, as in Mesozoic aquatic animals, completely five-fingered; or it belongs to forms that must be derived from land mammals, whose land-dwelling forerunners point to a Mesozoic formation, because with the beginning of the Tertiary they already stand there one-sidedly specialized.

From these, if slight, points of support — better ones I do not yet see at present — I put forward the thesis that the human type surviving the Flood, with the spreadable hand of our kind, is Mesozoic and was complete at the very latest already at the beginning of the Tertiary. Following the Jewish tradition we shall call it the “Noachian human type.” Its cerebral development was probably not yet so highly specialized as ours and that of Diluvial man.

To another remarkable prospect we are led by the report of another human form, of which it is said that it bore an eye on top of the skull, or a “frontal eye”<sup>30a</sup>.

<sup>30a</sup> From the side of specialists it was occasionally objected that a “frontal eye” lies on the forehead, a “parietal eye” on top of the roof of the skull; one must therefore not equate the two.— In the animals bearing a parietal organ, however, forehead and skull roof lie in one line; hence “frontal eye” and “parietal eye” probably mean the same thing in sense. I hardly believe that the transmitters of the legends, in the designation “frontal eye,” attached importance to anatomical-nomenclatural correctness or even thought of the possibility of distinguishing parietal bone from frontal bone; otherwise they would surely have expressed themselves in guild fashion! I therefore let the word “frontal eye” stand alternately with “parietal eye” for the form of the primordial man and renounce such a fruitless hairsplitting, which misses the sense of the legend, especially as the oldest vertebrates too, in the strictly anatomical sense, had a true frontal opening, namely between the frontal, not between the parietal bones. I hope to bring out in the near future a work on the theory of descent in which these questions too will be set out in the most detailed way. Seen from the other side, however, it is not excluded that from the oldest fish-like primordial states mentioned on pp. 48 and 74 a line of development with a true frontal eye branched off on one side and, on the other, the known saurians with the parietal or crown eye. The hypothetical stock of the primordial man might have belonged to the former type, so that one would have to speak in his case of a true frontal eye in the strictest sense. It is important in this connection that the frogs (Anura) have a true frontal spot, which sits in the frontal, not parietal bones, and thereby points to that supposed primeval and true condition of the frontal eye. This peripheral frontal organ corresponds, as Soette showed, to the pineal gland as well, and that shows that its emergence to the outside is not bound to particular cranial bones. The question cannot be decided for the time being, and I therefore still use the expression frontal and parietal eye alternately, in the noncommittal form. (Cf. on this: J. V. Rohon, Über Parietalorgane und Paraphysen. Sitzungsber. k. böhm. Ges. Wiss. [Math. Natw. Kl.]. Prag 1899, p. 1—15).

Nowhere can one see more clearly how the folk elaboration of such a legend can attach itself to fossil finds which were [p. 82] made in historical times and could then become the occasion and the basis for a fresh elaboration of the primeval, prehistoric core. Abel has in this way been able to trace the Homeric elaboration and localization of the Polyphemus legend back to remains of the dwarf elephant in Sicilian caves<sup>25</sup>. Polyphemus is the one-eyed giant with the great Cyclopean eye on his forehead, who slays the shipwrecked companions of the wandering Odysseus, who had penetrated into his cave, and is then blinded by the cunning Odysseus. “According to the notion of the Homeric Greeks,” writes Abel, “there dwelt in Sicily gigantic men with a single great eye in the middle of the forehead. Why precisely Sicily was held to be the land of the Cyclopes? In the caves lying not far from the sea in the region around Messina and in many other places, as near Palermo and Trapani, skeletal remains of the Ice Age dwarf elephant are found even today. They were found in earlier times too. If one looks at the skull of such a dwarf elephant with the eyes of the layman, the huge frontal hole immediately strikes one (Fig. 10). It is the nasal opening; the eyes stand at the side of the skull. The Homeric wanderers did not know the elephant skull as such; the vaulted form permitted a comparison with a human skull most readily, and from this arose the notion of gigantic frontal-eyed beings. Seafarers of the Homeric or pre-Homeric age were probably the first to bring news of these giants to their homeland. They may have sought shelter from bad weather in a shore cave of Sicily and, on lighting the camp fire, caught sight of an elephant skull protruding from the cave loam. Everything else is later addition. An age inclined to see gods and sons of gods everywhere and to encounter supernatural appearances everywhere formed out of this find first the living giant and last the whole legend of the combating and outwitting of the monster.”

<sup>25</sup> Oth. Abel, Paläontologie und Paläozoologie. In: Kultur der Gegenwart. Part III. Organ. Naturwissenschaft. IV. Abt. vol. 4: Abstammungslehre usw. Leipzig und Berlin 1914, p. 303ff. —, Die vorweltlichen Tiere in Märchen, Sage und Aberglauben. (“Wissen und Wirken”, vol. 8.) Karlsruhe 1923.

Fig. 10. Elephant skull with the nasal opening, simulating a frontal eye. Greatly reduced. (From O. Abel, Kultur der Gegenwart, loc. cit. 1914.)

[p. 83] I do not wish to deny that the Homeric elaboration of the Polyphemus legend is directly connected with this body of fact, and I regard the question, so far as it concerns that literary-historical problem, as happily solved by Abel. But I do not believe that he has thereby come very near the core. It must give us pause that the report of the frontal-eyed giant or human being has reached us also from quite other cultural circles, to which Abel’s explanation does not apply. For example we read in the “Thousand and One Nights” of a high mountain<sup>26</sup>, on which a great pillar stood; on it sat a statue of black stone, representing a man with two great wings, two hands like the paws of a lion, a tuft of hair in the middle of the head, two eyes split lengthwise, and from the forehead a third, ugly, dark-red eye stood out, like that of a lynx. Another place, which has no direct literary and ethnic connection at all with the Homeric and the Arabic world, likewise knows the frontal-eyed human form: the Nordic folk fairy tales. “A mother was of the ancient race of men who had only one eye in the middle of the forehead and one breast under the chin”<sup>27</sup>. Also in the fairy tale of Melusine, pregnant with the primordial world, the son of man and daemon appears with the frontal eye<sup>28</sup>. Further, the Chinese vase ornaments show the motif in all conceivable variations again and again (Fig. 11). These are surely testimonies lying far too widely apart, and the picture that confronts us in them is — no matter how it is allegorically or symbolically exploited and distorted here or there — so universally alike precisely with regard to this one organ that [p. 84] Abel’s explanation no longer suffices in the face of it. And all the less so since, with a common source of the legend, the Greeks would sooner have received it from the eastern circle than have brought it up themselves from Sicily and passed it on across to the East. And nowhere has one also made fossil finds like the Sicilian dwarf elephants or the Palaeozoic–early Mesozoic amphibian and reptile skulls which bore the parietal or frontal eye, which, rudimentary as epiphysis or pineal gland, has remained an important brain organ not only in later reptiles but also in man.

<sup>26</sup> G. Weil, Tausend und Eine Nacht. vol. II, p. 272. 5. Abdruck. Berlin. (Ohne Jahreszahl.)

<sup>27</sup> Märchen der Weltliteratur, herausgeg. v. A. v. der Leyen. Nordische Volksmärchen I. Teil. Transl. by Kl. Stroebe. Jena 1915, p. 137.

<sup>28</sup> Deutsche Volksbücher. (Ed. P. Jerusalem, Ebenhausen-München 1912.) “Die Historie von einer Frau, genannt Melusine” usw. p. 385/86.

Fig. 11. The frontal-eye motif in various variations as an ornament on Chinese vases. (From the Chinese picture-work Pokutulu.)

Reference may be made to the preceding section of this main part, where there was talk of the organ formations characteristic of particular geological ages and evidently possible in them alone. Among these the parietal eye was also named, which in lower animals, such as crustaceans, but also in higher ones, such as fishes, amphibians and reptiles, was fully developed in the Palaeozoic age and in the Mesozoic was borne almost only by higher animals, amphibians and reptiles, that had come over from the Palaeozoic age. All younger types among them show it in a strongly atrophied state or no longer at all. The mammals perhaps had it only in the very oldest time, but later certainly no more. The forms that have it go back, then, with their type to the last period of the Palaeozoic epoch. In man we now have that rudimentary organ enclosed by the cerebral hemisphere, the pineal gland, which in its continuation corresponds to the former parietal eye, if one imagines the unfolding of the cerebrum held back. One can imagine that through the unfolding of the cerebrum that organ was suppressed and displaced inward, and that it presumably functioned formerly in part in place of the cerebrum, though with a differently constituted activity. The strong brain development is, however, for the mammal, especially for the mammal of the Tertiary age, hitherto almost alone known, the essential organ formation as against the older amphibian and reptilian types of the higher animal world.

With this cerebral development, however, perhaps the smallness of the younger human form as compared with the older, expressly mentioned more than once in the legendary tradition, is connected. For [p. 85] in recent medicine and anatomy the significance of the human pineal body has been brought into a light which in its turn shines back upon this prehistoric connection. According to this<sup>29</sup> it is a kind of sense organ, which at least in the mammals no longer has anything of a visual function. In malformations, to be sure, it occasionally still comes to light as an epicerebral eye, which is regarded as atavism, that is, as a throwback to the ancestral form. Its present significance in man extends, however, to secretions for the genital sphere, and it also changes during pregnancy in size and form. It is also said to be connected with the secondary sexual characters and with intellectual maturity, which sets in only with the beginning atrophy of the pineal gland. Its destruction in an early period of life leads to bodily and mental precocity and occasionally also to gigantism. In animals not yet fully grown a complete standstill of growth can be observed after operative removal of the organ, just as conversely the removal of the sexual germ gland brings with it an enlargement of the pineal organ. We have, however, like the other mammals, yet another outgrowth on the roof of the brain, which is laid down together with the pineal body, the paraphysis. Both organs are atrophied and formerly had functions that are still unknown to us. “Ancestral household stuff” we drag about with us in them, as Gaupp called it, to whom we owe an exposition of the formation of these strange organs. The hypertrophy of this paraphysis leads in present-day man to functional disturbances or to atrophy of the germ cells and this, allegedly, again to gigantism.<sup>29a</sup>

<sup>29</sup> Literature on the pineal gland (epiphysis): M. Flesch, Über die Deutung der Zirbel bei den Säugetieren. Anatom. Anzeiger. vol. III. Jena 1888, p. 173. R. Wiedersheim, Vergleichende Anatomie der Wirbeltiere. 7th ed. Jena 1909, p. 276; 320. O. Hertwig, Lehrbuch der Entwicklungsgeschichte des Menschen und der Wirbeltiere. 9th ed. Jena 1910, p. 562ff. A. Biedl, Die innere Sekretion. 3rd ed. Berlin und Wien 1919.

<sup>29a</sup> E. Gaupp, Zirbel, Parietalorgan und Paraphysis. Ergebnisse der Anatomie u. Entwicklungsgeschichte von Merkel u. Bonnet. vol. VII 1897. Wiesbaden 1898, p. 208—85.

In the New Zealand tuatara already mentioned, that archaic little reptile which we already meet in the Jurassic epoch and whose root reaches back into the Palaeozoic age, that parietal organ is still a veritable eye-like structure, with a retina-like inner lining of a cavity closed off toward the front by a lens, and showing besides some other details that agree with an eye (Fig. 12). This is also still the case in the slow-worm, the chameleon and the lizard. In mammals, as in man, on the other hand, the organ is greatly reduced and [p. 86] moves, during embryonic development, ever further from the outside inward. Originally the two connected organs (paraphysis and pineal body) were paired, and appear so in their earliest earth-historical forms of development, in Old Palaeozoic armored fishes (Fig. 2, p. 48) and in some merostomatous crustaceans (Fig. 9). But already in the amphibians and reptiles of the Coal Measures and Permian periods only the unpaired parietal organ appears externally, and as such it is characteristic of the higher animals living in that period. That it then took over other, especially sexual functions later, after its regression, is a common occurrence with rudimentary organs. It is interesting and important that, as stated, the longitudinal growth of the bones also depends on irritations of the pineal gland, and that its secretions influence the growth in size, just as they do the development of the intellect; and this is all the more striking since the ancient “forehead-eyed” men of legend are described to us as beings of exceptional bodily size and slight intellect<sup>30</sup>.

<sup>30</sup> The individual age, too, of the early Noachian men is always given as very high, as is known from the Old Testament. Noah lived 950 years until he died (Gorion, Sagen der Juden. “Urzeit,” p. 236; “Erzväter,” p. 146). Without going into the question of what is to be understood by “years” in those traditions, as also by the “days” of the Mosaic history of creation, let it only be pointed out that a lawful relation between the body size of animal forms and the individual lifespan of their individuals appears to exist; let the great individual age of the elephant be recalled. The giant saurians of the Mesozoic age too, with their often uncanny body size, may individually have grown very old and, as single animals, perhaps have outlived human generations, from which individual features of the legends could then again be explained. The primordial man too, if he was very large, might have reached a very great individual age, and it would then not be necessary to quibble overmuch at the word “year.”

Fig. 12. Parietal eye of the New Zealand tuatara, beneath a thin covering of skin. (After B. Spencer, from O. Hertwig, Entwicklungsgeschichte, 10th ed. 1915.) Magnified.

Have we, then, here too good reason to grant a content of truth for the history of humanity to a legend so ancient and so variously transmitted, and, on a correspondingly comparative contemplation of nature, furnishing so definite and viable a picture, as that of the “forehead-eyed”? Then we owe this prospect to the fundamental contrast with a mode of interpretation which from the outset intends to deny the real natural-historical content of truth, whereby it invariably arrives at results which indeed seemingly give a nature-like exegesis, but nevertheless exhaust themselves in an altogether nature-alien allegorizing. Thus it is said of the forehead-eyed in a recent mythology: “The later notions of the Cyclopes were determined, in a peculiar way, at once by the poetry of the Odyssey and by the old image of the Hesiodic fire-daemons, except that these are now [p. 87] transferred to volcanic regions of the earth, where they henceforth work as the smiths of Hephaestus. So especially in the region of Etna in Sicily, which had to show the most striking marks of Poseidonian as well as of volcanic natural revolutions... Polyphemus, on the other hand, continued for the sake of the Odyssey to play his special role in folk legend and poetry...”

Faced with such interpretations, we have ever again occasion to follow our previous manner of consideration with confidence and to ascribe natural-historical value to the old tradition of the forehead-eyed human being, and we may say conditionally: beings of a higher order with a slight development of the cerebrum and a fully developed “forehead eye” can only be of Young Palaeozoic origin and must still have lived in the Mesozoic. The parietal or forehead eye probably had a function by which it replaced later intellectual faculties in part or wholly in another manner, no longer immediately intelligible to us owing to the regression of this organ, and therefore presumably corresponded to a sense unknown to us or to another connection of the senses. With the Mesozoic-Tertiary development of the brain in the human stock, this organ has vanished, and with it also the older human form, bodily presumably larger and therefore perhaps also attaining a greater individual age, and has made room for the Noachian brain-man with spreading fingers and a vaulted, wholly closed skull. We call that older human type the “post-Adamitic” or “pre-Noachian,” because we intend to distinguish it from the younger Noachian, but also from a still older Adamitic and a primordially Adamitic one<sup>31</sup>.

<sup>31</sup> One week before the going to press, after this section had been fixed in content for over half a year, I still got hold of the treatise by A. Sichler: “Die Theosophie (Anthroposophie) in psychologischer Beurteilung” (Heft 112 der Grenzfragen des Nerven- und Seelenlebens. München und Wiesbaden 1921), and find there, as an object of criticism among other things, a summary of the main ideas of a primordial history of humanity according to H. P. Blavatsky, which in some points and in part identical statements about the primordial man touches my conclusions. Cf. also note 56 on Scott-Elliot’s “Lemuria,” where similar things are to be found, but without sensible indications of sources.

After these findings a figurative representation perhaps comes into a brighter light, one found in the Central American Maya manuscript preserved in Dresden<sup>32</sup>, from which a characteristic panel is reproduced below, in a row, in the illustration (Fig. 13 b). The history of the Mayas, as also the meaning of that pictographic script, comprising many leaves, and of its hieroglyphs, still lies very much in obscurity. In the panel in question two human-like beings of quite different form travel over the water. The rear, more daemonic figure rows; the front, human-like one, represented as female, makes a gesture of [p. 88] astonished or surprised or observing gazing. The movement and character of the rower have decidedly something more active, also more brutal, in contrast to the bearing and form of the human being, who looks spiritualized; the rear figure has something grotesquely daemonic, the front one something cultivated and human. What is especially striking besides is the suggestion of a forehead eye in the rower-daemon, and the fact that his hand is clumsy, has a very large opposable thumb, like a Mesozoic Iguanodon, and again the fused, embryonic-seeming surface. As soon as we see the picture in this way and recall what we found about primordial man, there is perhaps a certain possibility of interpretation for this panel. Either it belongs to a symbolic narrative about the succession of the stocks of man, in which the forehead-eyed, more daemonically disposed type, with the larger, more brutal body and the fused fingers, plays a role over against the Noachian type with the perfected hand and the present-human countenance; or it is even a narrative similar to that of the Babylonian Gilgamesh, who, with the boatman and tribesman of his ancestor, travels over the sea or into the realm of the dead and has visions there, such as the picture immediately to the left (Fig. 13 a) seems to indicate; thus perhaps a primeval report, set down at a late time in pictographic and hieroglyphic script, that — they travel from the east — once [p. 89] a human being with the “strange right hand” came traveling over the sea or into the realm of the dead; thus perhaps at bottom the same thing that is handed down to us in the Gilgamesh epic behind a confused veil and symbolically, yet unmistakably resting once more on primeval history, only seen here from the western shore of the Atlantic Ocean instead of from the eastern, and transmitted once again? Attention may also be drawn to the similarity of the hand of another daemon (Fig. 14) to an embryonic hand, and besides to the Palaeozoic-Mesozoic sandstone footprints.

<sup>32</sup> The Dresden Mayan manuscript (Codex Dresdenensis) is published in: “Antiquities of Mexico” by Lord Kingsborough. vol. 9. London 1831—48 (vol. 3, p. 74ff.). Also reproduced by: E. Förstemann, Die Mayahandschrift usw. Dresden 1892. (Reprint; first edition: Leipzig 1880. The illustrations in F. Helmolts “Weltgeschichte,” 1st ed. vol. I. Leipzig und Wien 1899, p. 230/31 are equivalent reproductions. The illustrations reproduced in the text in each case are taken from the edition of 1880. Explanations of the figures of gods or daemons are given by: P. Schellhas, Die Göttergestalten der Mayahandschrift. Dresden 1897.

Fig. 13. Tripartite panel from the Dresden Maya manuscript. (The wavy hatchings are water; the black dots and lines probably point to the realm of the dead.)

Now, to return to the type of the post-Adamitic forehead-eyed primordial man: if he, like the higher animal world around him, possessed that remarkable sense organ, there follows from this also an inference as to the formation of his head: it must have lacked a high-vaulted cranial capsule to receive a cerebrum, and instead have had a pointed form, or one running rapidly backward, no set-off, that is, a flat, receding forehead, or only a highly raised back of the head, where the still substantially smaller cerebrum lay more toward the back and top and on which the parietal eye predominated. In many places these men are also described to us thus in legend; and perhaps the Indian custom, too, of giving the head that pointed form from youth on by constricting it between little boards, with pressing back of the cerebral capsule, points to a traditional knowledge of that primeval organ, or has as its unconscious aim the re-exposure of the rudiment, so as to give an impetus to its renewed unfolding and finally to put oneself once more in possession of that old efficacy, of which the next part of this book will treat in detail. A legend comes to the aid of this conception, one that stands in the Bible book of those Central American Quiché Indians<sup>33</sup>. There the creation of beautiful and perfected men after the Flood is related. But because they were so perfect, the gods feared that they might wish to become like them. Therefore they weakened [p. 90] the bodily power of sight of the newly created. Thus their knowledge and their faculty of cognition sank; they could now see only what was nearby, whereas earlier their gaze had roamed into immeasurable distances.

<sup>33</sup> F. Bumiller, Die Bibel der Quiche-Indianer, Beilage zur Augsburger Abendzeitung, Nr. 56, 1912, p. 6.

Fig. 14. Daemon from the Dresden Maya manuscript with fused embryonic hand. (Cf. Figs. 6 and 7.)

That the later Noachian man, who represents the Mesozoic-Tertiary mammal in man, like all genera, did not take his origin from a single local and phylogenetic point, but at any rate sprang from special branches of the total human stock already typically different beforehand, is very probable from general experiences of evolutionary history concerning the becoming of forms. Moreover, a many-stocked origin is assumed also for Diluvial man. For this too the legends, if we are willing to follow them, offer all sorts of points of support. Thus it is not impossible that among the early Tertiary human beings, whose most perfected type was presumably the Noachian, there were still found also some with very animal characteristics of bodily structure. Reference may be made here only to a legend of the Fiji islanders, according to which the survivors of the Flood amounted to only eight tribes; two perished, and of those one consisted only of women, the other of men with a kind of dog’s tail<sup>34</sup>. The dog’s or ape’s tail indeed recurs in many places in tradition. And even if it was afterwards often used for mockery or for allegorical fables, still the primeval meaning shines through, which seems all the more important since present-day man has at the end of the spinal column the distinct rudiment of a tail.

<sup>34</sup> Andree, Flutsagen, loc. cit., p. 59.

Noachian man lived through the great Flood. That still lower figures, appearing animal-like to the already more highly developed Noachian men, propagated themselves after it and gradually became more human shines through just in an Indian legend, in which it is said that after the Flood the earth was repopulated by the transformation of animals into men<sup>35</sup>.

<sup>35</sup> Andree, ibid., p. 84.

Who knows what human types and human kinds and sub-kinds have gone through the world in the earth-historical millions of years. I believe we cannot imagine the peoples diverse enough. Just as the mammals of the Tertiary lived in many fundamentally different orders, families, [p. 91] genera and species and came and went comparatively quickly, emerging in the process from lines of descent and types which we are up to now unable to connect genetically with one another, so it may also have been with the diversity of the human stocks and types. And so there is also room to let the many legends and imagined pictures of men with bird faces or dog heads, of centaur-like or faun-like ones, or of shapely bodies of sylvan delicacy and beauty speak, and to wring natural-historical sense from them. This is not to be misunderstood, however, to mean that fauns and centaurs or giants and dwarfs themselves are real human beings in their legendary image; rather, such figures are entities recognized by man, which were for primordial man, bound to nature, precisely those immediately beheld realities that we call natural forces, but which appeared, and had to appear, living and essential to those men who were endowed with a corresponding, nature-sighted soul — a concept that will later stand at the center of our consideration.

Apart from the generally known biblical tradition, not to be repeated here, that after the catastrophe of the Flood the Noachian sons of men spread over the earth as various new basic races, the history of legend furnishes us with two further striking narratives, which, though not in wording, yet in sense should be fairly alike, especially as the one valuably supplements and illuminates the other. They are the Greek tradition of the Noachian Deucalion and the Babylonian one of the wild mountain man Engidu. They are new races. Little pure, and evidently taken over at third and fourth hand, an echo meets us in the Greek and Ovidian tradition of the Flood legend, to the effect that a post-Flood human stock came from the rough mountains as its original home. It has often been pondered what it means that Deucalion with his wife Pyrrha throws stones behind him and thereby begets new men. Let us remember that here a symbolic language sounds, no longer understood by the late period which transmitted it, and that in older pre-Ovidian tradition it does not say: they threw stones behind [p. 92] them, but: they threw the rock of the mountains behind them<sup>36</sup>, that is, in other words: they left the cliffs of the mountains behind them, whence they had come. Let us now compare with this the legend, reported much more clearly and naturally in the Gilgamesh epic, of the irruption of a young, wild, only just created race out of the mountains, where it still romps about with the cattle, living without culture, and then penetrates into the old cultural circle of Gilgamesh. In the further course, a kind of expulsion from the Paradise of nature-like innocence is again bound up with this unmistakable parallel, such as is ascribed in the Bible to the Adamite, so that here, as said, the materials seem to be woven through one another. The content<sup>37</sup> of the important lines is, with some omissions, as follows:

<sup>36</sup> Preller-Robert, Griech. Mythologie, p. 85.

<sup>37</sup> Gilgameschepos, p. 8/9 (see note 23).

When Aruru heard this, / She created in her heart an image (?) of Anu;.... / Clay she pinched off, spat (?) upon it... / Created a hero, an exalted offspring... / [Covered] (?) with hair was his whole body... / He knew nothing of land and people; / With clothing he was clothed.. / With the gazelles he eats herbs, / With the cattle he provides himself at the watering place, / With the swarming of the water his heart is merry. / A hunter.. he confronted... / [When the hunter s]aw him, his countenance was troubled .. he cried: / “[My] father, [a] man who has come [from the mountains], / [In the land] strong is [his] power... / He goes about on the mountains c[onstantly (?)] ...”
