We have now been brought close to two principal human stocks, from the meager points of support which can be won from the legends compared with natural-historical facts and possibilities, and which a connoisseur of legends could perhaps document or multiply still more strikingly and put in a better light than we, poorly, can — and of these we call the Noachian that of the age of mammals, thus essentially of the late Mesozoic and Tertiary periods, because it lacks the old property of the parietal eye and its hand is unfused. It would have existed, in a pushed-back position and number, already since the Permian period and in the early Mesozoic age; perhaps, like presumably all the earliest mammals, still endowed with a [p. 93] small forehead eye, and probably, like the Mesozoic higher animal world in general, at first still without a fully upright gait, but having acquired this only during the Mesozoic from the four-footed creeping or walking condition. The other human stock was the pre-Noachian, with parietal eye and fused hand, which, lacking a fitting personal name, we wished to call the post-Adamitic human type or the pre-Noachian, and whose lifetime will essentially coincide with the Permian-Mesozoic period, especially with the very early and middle Mesozoic, when, as shown, those prominent bodily characteristics still existed in the animal kingdom, perfected, as a temporal signature. In accordance with the development of its parietal eye it must go back at least into the Upper Permian, and there we may hope to find points of support for the “Adamitic,” that is, the first, earliest, most strangely alien human type.

What would this have to look like if, without information from the legends, we tried to derive it for ourselves from the anatomy of late man on the one hand and from the temporal signature of that epoch in the animals on the other? He will still have possessed strongly amphibian characteristics; his hand will have been fused, five- to seven-fingered, without an opposable thumb, perhaps even suited for swim-rowing in the water, his forehead eye small or double, his body skin scaly, partly armored; for precisely that is the temporal character of the oldest land dwellers.

We find little in the legends that points to that primal condition of the human being; but quite sporadically something does resonate. Thus it is said in a well-known, often reproduced American Indian pictographic manuscript, in which the Flood is also described, of the grandfather of men and animals, that he was born creeping and can move about on the tortoise island rising from the sea<sup>38</sup>. Further, it is said in a legend transmitted by the Babylonian Oannes: in the first year after the Creation a rational being appeared from the Erythraean Sea with a complete fish body. Under the fish head, however, a human head had grown out, and human feet from its hind end or tail; it also had a human voice, and its image is preserved to this day. This being associated [p. 94] by day with men, without taking food, gave them the knowledge of letters and sciences, taught them to build cities and temples, to measure land, to grow fruits. Since that time nothing else going beyond this has been invented. At sunset this being plunged back into the sea, spent the nights in the deep, for it was amphibian-like. Later other similar beings appeared. One such, with a fish body, yet with the arms and feet of man, taught astronomy<sup>39</sup>.

<sup>38</sup> Andree, Flutsagen p. 74. This Indian pictorial narrative is probably to be found in every good encyclopedia and is often reproduced also in other popular works; furthermore in R. Andree, Flutsagen.

<sup>39</sup> A. Jeremias, Das Alte Testament im Lichte des Alten Orients. Leipzig 1904, p. 5.

If one wishes to see a contradiction in the fact that this oldest amphibian human being is said to have come to men, that there were therefore already men, and that it could thus not have been a phylogenetic initial form, then, to meet this, it is first conceivable that only the emblematic manner of expression of the narrative brings the contradiction with it. For that the amphibian human being comes to men need mean nothing other than that it itself became man. Such a being, only just becoming human, had to become aware of its surroundings, with consciousness or instinctively clairvoyant, as soon as its humanness set in; and since the intangible sparkling night sky belongs among the oldest and most overwhelming impressions upon the human soul, there began soon to awaken in its soul, in its consciousness, that which in the oldest mythic times of human existence was bound up with the beholding and the nature-sighted feeling-through of the world of stars and of its connection with earthly nature; for the sense of the word star-lore, or indeed astronomy, is here late-period. On the other hand, as accords far more with my own conception, one can stay with the more literal content of the legend and must then conclude, as already indicated above, that beside an amphibian primal type of the human being a terrestrial one already existed, which had developed on another line of descent, and that therefore both, in their different manner of development, were not immediately connected genetically. For it is probable, and would correspond to the development of the rest of the animal world, that even types standing very close to one another are of many-stocked origin, so that individual type-circles [p. 95] within the total human stock also arose and were organized in different ways and adapted to different conditions of life. Of this too there are some points of support in tradition. Thus we read in Moses that the sons of Adam went into another land and there wooed the daughters of men; we learn there and elsewhere in the legends that there were ordinary men and angels come from heaven and children of God, who took pleasure in the daughters of men and joined with them.

According to the discoveries of Westenhöfer, already mentioned on page 71, on some inner organs of man, a type of the human stock adapted to aquatic life perhaps existed also in pre-Tertiary time. This investigator, who knew nothing of our line of thought, writes: “Such aquatic periods for man could quite well have existed in the Cretaceous and still earlier. Human tradition reaches back extraordinarily far, and it is certain that man cannot invent anything that does not really exist... Thus, for example, for me the legend of Beowulf’s fight with the dragon under the water is an indication that man lived and fought in the water with such dragons.” Now one of the most prominent temporal signatures of the Mesozoic epoch is the adaptation, then setting in and completing itself, of many land-animal stocks to aquatic life. They are mostly reptiles; but the aquatic mammals too, which appear only in the Tertiary age, all point already to a Mesozoic shaping of their form. The human stock itself, then, might also have developed alongside, at that time, a form adapted to the water. This interpretation too can be applied to the Babylonian legend that that fish-being came to already finished land-men.

Besides that Babylonian legend of primordial man we still have traditions that depict a genuinely Adamitic human type; they treat of the appearance of Adam and Eve at their expulsion from Paradise. According to one version they were hairy like the wild man Engidu in the Gilgamesh epic; the hair fell off and they became naked. According to the other reading, however, they had a horny armor like crab and scorpion. “The skin was like our nails,” it says in the Mohammedan tradition. It was a hornlike soft shining [p. 96] red armor, which now gradually fell off; only the toenails and fingernails are still remnants of it<sup>40</sup>.

<sup>40</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen, vol. I, p. 226/27.

Whether the fish-man handed down by Berossus as the oldest type of the human stock, and whether the scorpion-man of the old legend, who also plays his role in the Gilgamesh epic, where he appears as a figure of terror but yet as a being humanly approachable, connects with that scaly and armored primordial human body of the Adamite, whether he is only a distortion of it or a parallel figure to it and somehow has to do with the earth-historically oldest Adamite, is not quite evident. At any rate one thing is suited to throw light on the matter. If one asks what scorpion-man can mean in a bodily sense, it is precisely that being with armored and probably spiny or knotty skin. For such armored and spiny beings, however, the younger phase of the Palaeozoic up to the end of the Permian period is the span of time in which such forms appear: scaly amphibians and reptiles, in part even with thorn-processes on the body, in particular on the back and on the skull; so that here there might at any rate be intimations of a most ancient temporal signature, in which the oldest part of the human stock too might have had a share. We should then have before us, in those armored types of legend, the “primordially Adamitic” human type, preceding the more refined, though still horn-skinned, Adamite.

That Siegfried at bottom is perhaps such an Adamite can be surmised from the following quite obvious parallel: Siegfried has a horny skin, from the dragon. According to the German legend it falls off him or becomes worthless when he practices betrayal and can therefore be betrayed in turn. It is the motif of guilt, as in the Fall of Adam. And in the Jewish tradition, which has passed into the folk legend of the Little Russians, it says: “Long before the first man had sinned, he was covered over his whole body with such horn as we have on our nails. And he desired neither clothes nor shoes, as we do now. But when he sinned, the horn fell off him<sup>41</sup>.” Whether in the armored Achilles, too, the uncomprehended echo of the horn-skinned Adamite still lurks?

<sup>41</sup> Dähnhardt, ibid., p. 226.

It is an old, deeply truthful intuition, which meets us in a last modernized and symbolizing echo still in Herder’s “Ideen zu einer Philosophie der Geschichte der Menschheit” [p. 97], that in the human being everything is contained, bodily and psychically — we should say entelechially — that living nature forms, as also this, that the living nature of man mirrors his bodily and psychic becoming. Here too we have a myth full of reality How true, how deep, how inescapably determining it is, a glance shows us into the science of descent in natural science, seemingly exclusively opposed to it. We find in it the thought that man in the course of earth history ran through all stages, from the lower single-celled aquatic animal by way of the worm, the fish, the amphibian and the mammal, up to his Quaternary human existence. Then the biogenetic fundamental law was added, according to which the embryonic formal states of the human individual should repeat in sequence, though in much displaced and concealed fashion, the general formal states of this ancestral series. Lastly this whole doctrine was transferred from the organic-physical sphere also to the development of the senses and of the mind, as of the cultural souls.

If one grasps what this means, it was nothing other than this: that the human stock was once an amoeba, a fish, an amphibian, and so on, that the amoeba, the fish, the amphibian were therefore also states of form of man. This in turn is nothing other than the notion we ourselves hold, that man, in natural-historical terms, is an ancient stock which also embraces the other organic states of form. For even the hitherto prevailing form of the doctrine of descent, as it is still almost universally valid, or at least was until a short time ago, never held that any present-day amoeba or amphibian was the ancestor of man, but rather that there were other, geologically older ones, which became man, either along a single line or along several, through many other animal intermediate stages, and from which side branches occasionally split off and strayed into evolutionary-historical blind alleys and produced what was not human in kind. On this thought, one may well say, the biological students of nature are essentially agreed, if they admit an evolution at all<sup>42</sup>.

<sup>42</sup> But it is precisely from this point that it can once more be shown that even in the age of the most extreme theory of descent one could think and imagine nothing other than the following three cases: 1. Man is a stock of his own, persistent, if with all possible transformations, back to the oldest earth-historical time. That includes two different possibilities of development: either this independent stock was a primitive mammal in the Mesozoic and, at the beginning of the Mesozoic or in the youngest Palaeozoic, a reptile or amphibian, and developed through these stages into an anthropoid ape and finally into man; or, since that oldest time, it was already outwardly a distinct form-type of its own, even if appearing in all manner of transformations of form, and thus nonetheless precisely entelechically man. In the former case we should have nothing essentially different from the old grotesque family tree of Haeckel, in which sharks and reptiles were our ancestors; precisely that. But it is exactly this that the investigator who commands the palaeontological material no longer believes today. So only the second remains: late man descends from his own line of descent, not from sharks and newts. 2. Man and many higher animals go back phylogenetically to a primal form, very old, common to them; man is merely the most far-advanced tip, and that common primal form is precisely also his primal form. The remaining, less highly developed genera, branched off from this primal form sooner or later, are then precisely one-sidedly differentiated or arrested stages of his original becoming itself. 3. All animal life, so far as we can trace it back, descends from one and the same primal form, which is thus also the ancestor of the later human animal. That would be the old picture of the family tree, founded on the Linnaean system, of the earlier theory of descent, which had man at its apex. Thus his essence too would belong to the whole stock back to the beginning — it would always have been his primal form from which everything proceeded. He would potentia have brought along and carried with him the whole animal world in his stock, and would always have emerged in just the degree more purely as the animal element split off from this his line of descent in special forms. For surely it will no longer do to assert as a last way out — in order to save a doctrine of evolution without entelechy — that man has come to be purely by chance out of some animal forms, without the inner potency toward a man? Where would that which constitutes him have come from? From itself? What would this self be? Or from nothing? Or from an act of creation? Or from external hazard? One may turn the theory of descent as one will: one will not get out of the above alternatives and has not got out of them even in the most Darwinist age — unless one wishes to put the senseless, empty, external chance, as a most mystical occurrence, in the place of a directed evolution. However one turns and formulates it, then: man remains, as the true primal form, the stock of all higher beings.

[p. 98] Thus, in the consequence of a purely natural-scientific thinking-through to the end, we also have the proof that no other idea of the coming and becoming of man exists, and probably none is possible at all, than the one that confronts us as the oldest and most firmly closed doctrine in all myths and religions: that man is a being of his own, a stock of his own, was from the very beginning what he was to be and become, though with all manner of fundamental changes in his form; and that, related in stock to the animal world in body and soul, he must nevertheless, as the higher potency from the beginning, have released the others from his stock, and not the reverse. The full unfolding of the pure, present-world human form then occurred when at last the ape form latent in him too had been released from him, just as through the release of earlier formal potencies he had already become ever more present-worldly human—from faun to Apollo. And Apollo slew for Zeus his Cyclopes and their sons; so the knowing legend reports<sup>43</sup>.

<sup>43</sup> C. Robert, Die griechische Heldensage. vol. I. Berlin 1920, p. 30; p. 565, note 1.

The last phase of the becoming of man, which alone we see recognized as such in natural science up to now, took place at the time when, in the Tertiary, ape characteristics and human characteristics were developed in all the stocks as a temporal signature, as was already shown in the preceding section. It was then that those half-animal, half-human figures must have appeared of which many legends tell, and which seem to contradict one another in that they let now ape-like animals issue from man, now men from ape-like animals. If the Tibetans know how to report the latter, while the Malayan fairy tales on the other hand tell the story of a wicked son of man who was cursed and became an ape, then both are simply possible, and there is no contradiction between them or with our theory. For in the circles of forms that intersect in evolution, there had to appear, in the age of anthropoid and pithecoid formation, both something man-like in the primate stock and something ape-like in the human stock, as biological habitus. And such convergent forms, if they were ever found fossil, would once more be apt to cause confusion and to make people believe that man descends from Tertiary animals. That such approximations of habitus were possible on several lines and in several circles of [p. 99] forms, and actually took place, is a self-evident possibility for the palaeontologist, and it is also attested in Mongolian-Tibetan tradition. There it is told: A king of the apes was sent by a Chutuktu into the rocky chasm of the Realm of Snow, to take penances upon himself. Then a female Manggus, a hostile, destructive spirit-being, came to him, of hideous appearance, but with the gift of appearing beautiful and charming, and wished to wed him. The ape rejected her, because his penitent’s state forbade him marriage. But the Manggus put it to him that otherwise she would join with the male Manggus who were left over, and that her race would then multiply anew to the ruin of the inhabitants of the Realm of Snow. In his doubt he heard a voice from heaven, that he should take the Manggus to wife. With her he begot six young, each with a different disposition of mind peculiar to itself alone. After their weaning their father brought them into a forest of fruit trees and left them to themselves. But when after some years he went to look in on them, they had already multiplied to five hundred and had already eaten up all the fruit in the forest; driven by hunger, they ran toward him with a piteous howling. The ape complained to the Chutuktu how, by not keeping his vow, he was now to blame for the existence of so many wretched beings, and begged him to have mercy on his children. The god threw down to him from the height of a mountain five kinds of grain in abundance, which sufficed not only for the immediate satiation of the starving apes, but also grew and offered them sustenance for the future. But the eating of the grain had remarkable consequences: the tails of the apes and the hair of their bodies visibly shortened and finally vanished altogether. They began to speak and became men; they clothed themselves with tree leaves as soon as they noticed their humanity<sup>44</sup>.

<sup>44</sup> J. J. Schmidt, Forschungen im Gebiete der Bildungsgeschichte der Völker Mittelasiens. St. Petersburg 1824, p. 210/13.

So the father of these apes who were later to become men was himself already a very “human ape,” expressed in natural-historical terms therefore a man with the pithecoid temporal characteristics, such as we still see echoing so strongly in diluvial man. In this qualified sense, then, man here descends from the ape [p. 100] and, with the onset of soil cultivation and systematic grain-growing, becomes a full man.

If we now summarize what we were able to gather from the traditions, it is in its main features the same thing that emerged in the preceding section from purely palaeontological considerations as a heuristic thesis on the age and the changing basic form of the human being, but which we can now fill with more vivid life, whereas there it confronted us only skeletally, as it were only fossilized.

[p. 101]

### Urmensch und Sagentiere — Primordial Man and Legendary Animals

In all literatures, dragon and lindworm legends play a great part. These too have been explained as offspring of uncultivated fantasy or as products of fear before natural phenomena, or have been interpreted merely allegorically. Thus we read in Golther’s “Germanische Mythologie”:

“The mighty poison-worm, whose winged shape and whose name, dragon, are modeled on the fabulous beast of antiquity, appears in the folk legend under the impression of natural events. Fantasy lets these dragons issue from water, mist, meteor-fire. The world ocean was called a serpent; Grendel is perhaps originally a water-serpent. The dragons lie upon gold and, when they sally forth, harm land and people. The foremost task of the heroes is the combating of these monsters, deliverance from the plague upon the land, the raising of the hoard. In the giant worms the folk legend thinks of devastation by the sea, and in the interior of a sudden plunge of water from swelling rivers or brooks. If strength and insight at last protect the land against such dangers, then a great treasure, the prosperity of the whole region, is thereby won. Next to the sea-dragons those of the high mountains stand out especially, with which Dietrich of Bern, like all the other giants of the mountain world, has much to do. Where the brook breaks down from the high rock, there the grim dragon, foam before its jaws, leaps ever and again at its opponent and seeks to devour him; at the flow of a fountain before the mountains that rise high into the air, great worms dart to and fro and strive to burn the heroes; at the approach of such a one, who threatens to devour horse and man, a sound is heard, just like a thunderclap, from which the whole mountain resounds. Easily recognizable, these monsters are identical with the seething, thundering waterfalls themselves”<sup>45</sup>.

<sup>45</sup> Golther, German. Mythologie, p. 178/79.

With all due respect for the work on which the philologically comparative literature of myths and legends is founded and [p. 102] with which it recognizes and uncovers connections of a historical-etymological kind, there can nevertheless be no doubt that with such explanations the same error is made which we find again and again in all explanations of legends of this kind: that one contents oneself with an uncovering of allegories and philological connections which at most demonstrate only a late exploitation of a myth or natural event whose living, original content had long been lost.

I must here still set aside the deeply soul-like metaphysical significance of the serpent and dragon myth; but it must be pointed out that Abel’s explanations of the dragon legends are already much more nature-like at this point and come in principle closer to reality<sup>46</sup> than such allegorical interpretations. His explanation of the one-eyed giant Polyphemus has already been referred to (p. 82), as indeed, according to his expositions, finds of fossil bones, which were made in the Middle Ages and in modern times and in part are still preserved today, have constantly given occasion for the revival of old giant and dragon legends—not for their origin, as Abel says. He gives various instances: “In the time of Louis XIII, in the Dauphiné, in the Chaumont field, which is still today called ‘le champ des géans,’ enormous bones came to light, from which the scholars of that time let King Teutobochus arise; on January 11, 1613, a skeleton had been discovered in a sand pit near the castle of Chaumont, which a physician from Beaurepaire named Mazurier took possession of and exhibited for money, first in Paris, later also in other places in France and Germany. According to Mazurier it was supposed to be the bones of the Cimbrian king Teutobochus, who fell in battle against Marius. He related that he had discovered the bones in a walled-in tomb thirty feet long, on which the name of the Cimbrian duke had been written. This swindle kept the scientific world of the time in suspense for five years; numerous polemical writings appeared on this question. The remains are still preserved today in the Jardin des Plantes in Paris; they belong to a Dinotherium. If such adventurous ideas were still seriously discussed by the academies in the first half of the seventeenth century, one need hardly make fun of [p. 103] Pausanias, who took a skeleton ten cubits long, found near Miletus, for the bones of the Telamonian Ajax.”

<sup>46</sup> O. Abel (see note 25).

“In Klagenfurt there stands on the town square a mighty lindworm monument; it is tied to the legend of the slaying of a lindworm. This Klagenfurt lindworm, as Cäsar von Leonhard reported, was found in the Zollfeld at a place which is still today called the ‘Dragon Pit.’ The skull of the lindworm was brought to Klagenfurt and hung up there in the town hall on chains; it demonstrably served as model for the artist who in the year 1590 made the lindworm monument on the Klagenfurt town square; but this ‘lindworm’ was nothing other than an Ice Age rhinoceros.”

“The dragon legend, like the lindworm legend, has repeatedly received new nourishment through finds of fossil skulls. From the find of a cave-bear’s skull in a limestone cave, a fight with the living monster could easily arise through the boastfulness and exaggeration of the finder; the retelling heightened the terrors of the dragon and the dangers of combating it in its cave; the numerous bones of the bear cave gave sufficient testimony, after all, of the monster’s rapacity. One should think that the skull of the bear would have had to be recognized as such even by our forefathers; yet I point only to the fact that in the catalogue of the collection of the Benedictine abbey of Kremsmünster a ‘dragon’ appears listed, which was found in the eighteenth century, and this dragon’s skull belonged to a cave bear. If this was still possible in the eighteenth century at a place of lively intellectual activity and humanistic education, the interpretation of Ice Age animals as dragons in the Middle Ages can hardly surprise us.”

“The German dragon legend has quite unmistakably a South German stamp. This is certainly connected with the fact that in South Germany there lie rich deposits of fossil saurians, which quite certainly had to be found as early as the early Middle Ages in the quarrying of building stones for castles, churches, and palaces, exactly as they are still found today. ‘At the foot of the Hohenstaufen,’ writes O. Fraas in 1866, ‘dozens of saurians are found every year in the Lias there on the occasion [p. 104] of the breaking out of stone slabs. This slab industry is of great antiquity; ruins on the Hohenstaufen castle show that slabs were already being quarried there at the founding of the cradle of the old imperial dynasty. The saurians could no more escape the attention of the workmen then than today; the thought of subterranean animals lay near. Thus Quenstedt calls attention to the similarity between the dragon image on the old town church at Tübingen and the remains of the Swabian lindworm, which is found frequently on the banks of the Neckar in the uppermost Keuper. We may therefore not hesitate for a moment, at least locally, to trace the origin of individual dragon legends to the chance find of fossil saurians.”

“Since ancient times the Chinese have designated the bones and teeth of fossil mammals as dragon bones and dragon teeth. The dragon bones (Lung-ku) and dragon teeth (Lung-tschih) occur in China in enormous quantities; they come for the most part from the interior of China, where they are found either in caves or in stratified deposits in extraordinary numbers. The Chinese carefully collect these dragon remains, since they form a much sought-after article of trade; the bones and teeth of the dragon still play a very great role today in Chinese medicine. According to the Chinese idea, the Lung-ku and Lung-tschih are the remains of dragons which, owing to a lack of clouds and rain, were not able to soar up into the sky.”

I have given these examples from Abel’s treatise a broader space because they show us how completely the old meaning of the giant and dragon legends had been lost to late man, yet how undiminished the legendary cores themselves lived on, and again and again stirred the imagination to clothe them in directly given natural images. But with this nothing is proved about the sense and content of the primal legend itself, and we pursue further the path taken in the preceding section of this chapter.

Above all, the same objection must be raised against Abel’s so plausible explanations that was already to be raised in the preceding section against Polyphemus: the local and late attachment of a legend, of a tradition, to finds of bones and skeletons [p. 105] proves nothing against the high and highest age of such a legend, and nothing against the originally quite differently constituted experience of the history contained in it. If, according to Jak. Grimm, the Alpine folk has still preserved many legends of dragons and worms which in olden times dwelt on the mountains and often came down devastatingly into the valleys, and if even now, in torrent catastrophes, the saying goes round: “A dragon has come out,” this proves quite obviously at first only that the old idea of the dragon is by the people and its fathers now thought, experienced, felt only in this form; the Tatzelwurm too in the Upper Bavarian Alps, whom Scheffel celebrates in song, is tied to a waterfall that roars down in a hard-to-reach rocky gorge and creates caves. But all this touches the origin and essence of the legend-idea itself, and of the natural-historical reality unconsciously handed down in it, as little as do the natural-scientific, but not natural-historical—the two to be sharply held apart—explanations of the dragon legends, as they were cited above.

Once again a fact at once strikes us which in itself does not let the explanation as late phantasms derived only from fossil finds appear sufficient. The old descriptions of dragons and lindworms, and the pictorial notions handed down after such descriptions or also traditionally as such, by no means correspond to the fossil skeletons themselves, which alone one found and finds, since soft parts of the body do not occur petrified at all; but these legendary serpents and lindworms are not seldom depicted in full corporeality just as palaeontology, only now after decades of strictly comparative-anatomical research, ventures gradually to reconstruct them in flesh and blood, and with which it may well already have come close in many respects to the former form of this Mesozoic animal world. How then should there have formed in mere fantasy, from often very meaningless bone fragments or even skeletons, precisely such fully corresponding images of the exterior of the dragons as we now obtain scientifically? In a later section such reconstructions after the works of American investigators are given (p. 262), with whom we in Europe, in abundance of material and purposeful industry,

[p. 106] Fig. 15. Reconstruction of a stockier form of the sea serpent (Mosasaurus), Cretaceous. Belgium. Greatest body length 12 m. (After L. Dollo, from O. Abel, Paläobiologie der Wirbeltiere, 1912.)

[p. 107] cannot measure up. Are these not the unmistakable lindworms of the legends, as we divine them? Where, in face of such forms, remain “the spurting mountain brook” and “the devastation by the sea” and whatever else in the way of allegories we still have to hear? Or did our forefathers in the forests of Germania reconstruct these dread saurians by comparative anatomy from finds of skeletons, like the scientifically trained artist in the American Museum of Natural History, and then poetically recast them into “legends”? Ah no: here primeval real life speaks to us, through the confused and recast legends, still from a far, far distance, and shows us the man of the Mesozoic age in this highly daemonic environment, fleeing in terror or fighting, and often conquering or succumbing. When we consider that among these dread saurians there were forms of the height of a house of two or three stories; that from the tip of the snout to the tip of the tail 32 m have been measured in one American specimen; that skeletons excavated in the former German East Africa point to still more gigantic genera—what need is there then to doubt what the man of the former world saw alive, which was inherited onward in legends because it consisted of overpowering impressions of nature, perhaps often decisive for the life of peoples, upon the still childlike receptive mind of those precursors of our days?

Not only of land animals, but also of water and air animals from the reptilian stock, there is talk in the legends. The sea serpent, descended from land lizards and discovered in the deposits of the Dutch and North American Cretaceous, in skeletons over 12 m long, of which Fig. 15 mirrors only a stockier form: again and again it lives on in fairy tales and travelers’ reports of older times, and has been interpreted just as superficially and emptily as the rest. What does it prove against the kernel of truth of the ancient legends of the sea serpent, if this has been tied by later and latest seafarers, with heated imagination, to things and natural phenomena that certainly had nothing more to do with the Mesozoic sea serpent so well known to the palaeontologist? If seafarers versed in legend—and these know and have always known [p. 108] many fairy tales and have always been very receptive to daemonic “superstition”—if these people in distant seas took dolphin backs swimming one behind the other for sea serpents, or if a Curtius Rufus embellishes the siege of Sidon by Alexander the Great with such an old legend, why should the ancient legend, which here was rejuvenated once more with what is false, not have its kernel of truth corresponding to the literal sense, when we know indeed that such beings really once existed, perhaps even still in the Tertiary, but certainly in the last phase of the Mesozoic age? After all the foregoing expositions we have no reason any longer to content ourselves with the artificial pseudo-explanations, when with full right we may believe in the Mesozoic man and this animal world seen by him, and have it fossil, truly before our eyes.

Fig. 16. Assyrian relief depiction of a dragon from the fight with the god Marduk. Compare the horns with those of the Mesozoic giant lizard, Fig. 19, p. 116. (From R. G. Seeley, Dragons of the Air, London 1901.)

Yet the image of the dragon proper, as Fig. 16 for instance reproduces it and as it recurs, alike or similar, in descriptions and sculptures too, has remained forever a riddle, for this reason, that it seems, and also is, an anatomical impossibility for four-footed animals to have had wings of their own in addition, that is, as it were two extremities too many at the side or even on the back. For the wing, especially also in the reptilian flying dragons of the Mesozoic age, is by no means a supernumerary extremity, but hangs on the transformed fore-extremity, as in the bird. Nevertheless I believe that in that tradition of feathered dragon-animals too, no fantasy but immediate truth of nature speaks to us. For we have fossil a group of dinosaurs which has an [p. 109] altogether bird-like body structure, namely long hind legs, short fore legs, upright gait, and hollow bird bones. But the great dread saurians too, of the habitus of those depicted in Fig. 4 (p. 51), of which there were also more lightly built forms only man-high, which in part hopped springing on their hind legs, are for the most part hollow-boned, thus had a light skeleton and also laid eggs. According to the law of correlation, whereby in the animal types the anatomical details of the skeleton and of the organs mostly correspond in such a way that from the presence of the one property one may infer the other, it is now quite probable that many upright-hopping, bodily light, hollow-boned, if very large saurians had, instead of the reptilian armor of horny scales, a feather dress; for the bird-like feather is a direct heightening of the reptilian scale, which is also demonstrable embryologically. Possibly even the only genuine bird type of the Jurassic is merely a feathered reptile with convergent bird peculiarities. It is therefore quite conceivable that among the half-erect dread saurians there were not a few which possessed a kind of bird-dress and could spread it, in a posture of defense, attack, or wrath, [p. 110] across the back and out at the sides, rather as a peacock spreads his wheel. Then, despite the four-leggedness, the animal had to appear as if endowed with bird’s wings, all the more since the feather dress from the outset caused the beholder’s imagination to expect the wing. Yes, under certain circumstances they may even have made use of this feather wheel, by beating movements, to support and heighten their leaping, or as a means of terror in the sudden bristling up, which certainly did not take place noiselessly. And finally this train of thought would make intelligible one more characteristic which is ascribed to such winged dragons: their cheeping cry. For this too is a peculiarity resting on an anatomical basis of the birds, which doubtless issued somehow from the reptiles; and with the other bird-like habitus and the feather dress of many dragons this kind of voice would then also have been a quite comprehensible property. Great dread saurians with bird-like beaks are already known in several kinds. Some had strong bony cones like living rhinoceroses on nose and forehead; the roof of the skull too was extended backward into a shield protecting the neck against the bites of still mightier enemies (Fig. 20, p. 119); or it had a perforated bridge, indeed finally a cassowary-like horn helmet (Fig. 17), which in lesser stages, together with the horny beak, which could also occasionally be broad like a duck’s, nevertheless recalls the head of the dragon on a Babylonian clay cylinder (Fig. 18) [p. 111] so much that such forms too may have existed, and that this coincidence is therefore probably more than a chance play of fantasy. That in such or similar fossil saurians the feather dress has not yet been demonstrable is no weighty ground against our conclusions, because such types have either not yet been recognized or not yet found, or because the feathers are in themselves perishable and therefore preservable as fossils only under quite rarely favorable circumstances; they perhaps also fell off quickly after death, more quickly than in true birds; and all this may be the reason why in corresponding skeletons no trace of plumage could ever be discovered. It is therefore to be assumed that some reconstructions of the outer body of some Mesozoic dread saurians, as the American investigators now give them to us, may later be supplemented by finds with feather dress.

Fig. 17. Skull of a great dread saurian with bird-like horny beak and cassowary-like horn helmet. Marsh or water dweller after the manner of the ducks. End of the Cretaceous. Canada. Whole skeleton about 10 m long. (From W. D. Matthew, Dinosaurs, New York 1915.)

Fig. 18. Fragment of a cylinder seal from Babylon with a dragon of bird-like appearance. (From Jeremias, Das Alte Testament, 1904.)

Fig. 19. Blurred field from the Dresden Maya manuscript, with an archaic flying-animal figure.

The Dresden Maya manuscript, mentioned once before, shows an unmistakable admixture of Mesozoic animal notions. Reptile and serpent figures keep appearing there that have more of a primordial than a present-day look. Selected specimens are already reproduced on page 88, of which the right-hand one (c) shows an encounter between a bird and a serpent-like lindworm, the bird mating with the reptile. But the bird has something archaic in its habitus, in the sense of earth history. So too another animal figure, which presents itself in the picture field here beside it and which could rather symbolize a Mesozoic flying animal, especially as regards the large eye that was peculiar to such animals, and of which some skin-winged forms hung themselves, at rest, by a short tail-end curled up in just the same way as the Maya picture also shows. This too confirms the view already expressed above (p. 88), that the Maya manuscript describes legendary phases in the evolution of primordial humanity, and that here we are dealing in part with an [p. 112] archaic animal world that refers to the time from which the traditions are reported; without prejudice to the symbolic or religious contents that may possibly be contained in it and expressed at the same time, which may be tied to the natural-historical element therein and symbolized by it.

To sum up, then, in the old dragon and lindworm legends we have before us, unmistakably, a genuinely Mesozoic animal world with its biological formal character, also determinable palaeontologically, which we now wish to accept as something lived through by man, since, as has been shown, we no longer have any insurmountable ground for resisting the existence of man, or of a human being, at that time. Thus here too the legends would find a natural, unforced explanation, and in sense I can only underline what another researcher<sup>47</sup>, arriving by a different path at the same goal, has already once said:

<sup>47</sup> P. Fauth, Hörbigers Glazialkosmogonie. Eine neue Entwicklungsgeschichte des Weltalls und des Sonnensystems. Kaiserslautern 1913, p. 513.

“Should that dragon of the Chinese, which darkens or devours the orb of day at eclipses of the sun, really be only the offspring of a pre-Christian mandarin fancy, or do the ancient Chinese archives perhaps preserve records of primeval dragon traditions of the origin we suppose, which could have fertilized such a fancy? Should it really have been only the backs of dolphins swimming in rows that gave the impetus to the sea-serpent stories of old seafarers, or did the latter perhaps already have, from earlier times, a traditional predisposition for the possibility of such plesiosaurs and mosasaurs of the present? Should the singers of incredible dragon-slayer adventures (Heracles, Siegfried, Siegmund; Beowulf) really have written down only pure invention, or were they inspired by obscure traditions? Whatever of such dawning traditions, in sparse remains, finally reached our early historical, or even only the immediately prehistoric, age of the first literature, poetry and priesthood was then caught up by its representatives, collected, preserved, constructed into our Quaternary-alluvial conditions of life, and worked up by the descendants into the dragon-slayer stories and dragon symbols that we find today among all the older civilized peoples. [p. 113] It is surely quite out of the question that palaeontological knowledge could have inspired the old singers of dragon combats, since all these dragon legends are older than our first knowledge of fossil saurians. Equally unthinkable it seems to us that the so widely diffused notions of dragon-like monsters should be purely invented.”

What is suited to fill up the measure of this striking coincidence of fabulous animals and reality, a coincidence hardly any longer to be regarded as chance, is the not infrequent recurrence of the myth that the serpent originally went on four legs, or more, upright on two hind legs, but later had to go on its belly because it lost its feet. Numerous Jewish legends, passed into later texts, hand down this picture. Dähnhardt has compiled several traditions in his “Natursagen”<sup>48</sup>. The serpent, however, that crawls on its belly really appears as one of the last reptilian types. It is, as can be demonstrated anatomically, a later degeneration of a formerly four-legged reptile that has lost its extremities and still occasionally shows inconspicuous remnants of them, by way of suggestion, inside the body. It probably comes to development as such at the end of the Mesozoic era, and is first reliably found as a fossil in the Early Tertiary.

<sup>48</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen, vol. I, p. 216/18.

If the legends already know this, then here too fossil finds cannot have been the occasion for this strangely correct knowledge, and probably not comparative anatomical investigations either. To be sure, the natural scientist or philologist may want to maintain that the anatomical examination of snake skins had occasionally betrayed the rudimentary extremities even to the ancient physicians, which then, by means of the sufficiently familiar “fantastic embellishment,” became the legend of the serpent that once ran on legs and was then made by God to crawl. We should thus have once more the same mode of explanation that we got correspondingly for dragons and lindworms generally, or for Polyphemus—and just that I hold to be thoroughly false.

It is further to be considered, in transferences of names and notions from legendary ancient monsters or animals to younger, [p. 114] present-day types, that many primordial types in the Tertiary as well as the Mesozoic age already represented, habitually, forms that we find in the Quaternary period of earth history or in the present world<sup>49</sup>. Thus, for example, the rhinoceros, a well-known present-day and already Tertiary animal form, has its imitating, or rather anticipating, forerunner in habitus in a terrible lizard from the kin of the giant reptiles already partly described (Fig. 20), and it is to be concluded with the greatest probability that other late-world mammalian types too, such as the hippopotamus, with which the Old Testament Bohemoth or the Leviathan have occasionally been identified, will be discovered, similar in habitus, among the Mesozoic giant reptiles. Have we not, as already mentioned in passing, among those also forms that possessed large duck bills and, after the manner of marsh birds, sought their food with the head submerged in the mud of the water, indeed in related forms wore high horny helmets after the manner of the cassowary? By this it is not meant, however, that the Old Testament Bohemoth and Leviathan were not originally cosmic-mythical powers; it is only to be shown how little the recognition of a present-world animal as the bearer of a legendary animal notion need necessarily exclude the natural-historical truth of a still older notion and tradition of a nearly identically described animal.

<sup>49</sup> H. Gunkel, Schöpfung und Chaos in Urzeit und Endzeit. (Mit Beiträgen von H. Zimmern. Göttingen 1895. There the interpretation of Behemoth and Leviathan is set out and it is shown how such monsters were later stripped entirely of their character (pp. 61/62).

No less frequent is the legend of gigantic birds, not dragons, with which primordial men had to do at the time of the Flood, but evidently at a somewhat later period of earth history than with the dragons. The old Persian bird Rock, which also occurs in the Thousand and One Nights, is such a type<sup>50</sup>. In this figure too one has probably not hit upon the right thing if one traced the legend too simply to extinct giant flightless birds of Madagascar and New Zealand “which man still experienced.” These birds were, in habitus, ostrich-like types, that is, running birds with degenerate wings; on Madagascar late Diluvial fossil remains of Aepyornis have been found, whose eggs are three times as large in diameter as ostrich eggs; the Dinornis of New Zealand stood 3.5 m high when upright. Both types probably became extinct only in historical times, [p. 115] exterminated by man. Bölsche traces the legends of Siberian tribes about gigantic birds to finds of fossil rhinoceros claws<sup>51</sup>. But we already know from the Early Tertiary gigantic birds of a similar kind, with a large, compressed beak, short, thick neck, stocky trunk, degenerate wings and tall, four-toed running legs. They have been found in the Tertiary formation of North America and are anatomically close to South American types of the later Tertiary, which had an enormous skull more than half a meter long, and whose size can be gauged if one transfers this in corresponding proportion to the rest of the body. Even those Early Tertiary giant birds of North America mentioned were already degenerate flying birds. They must therefore have had a longer evolutionary history behind them and descended from flying birds that were already large in themselves—and only of such is there talk in the legend, not of wingless running birds—and these must therefore be geologically somewhat older, that is, very early Early Tertiary or late Cretaceous, like the sea serpent.

<sup>50</sup> H. Welten, Das Märchen vom Vogel Rock, “Kosmos”. Stuttgart 1920, p. 39.

<sup>51</sup> W. Bölsche, Entwicklungsgeschichte der Natur. Neudamm 1896, vol. 2. p. 753.

The legends of giant birds, too, had to serve again and again to enlarge still further the adventures of seafarers. Like the strange natural phenomena or reports that met them in distant lands, so too the Madagascan or New Zealand giant birds of recent times may still have been substituted, as living models, for an old legendary material already brought along or found elsewhere. Just so Homer did with Polyphemus, so the Nibelungenlied with horny Siegfried, so with animal and flood legends did above all many Roman and Greek writers, such as Diodorus, Curtius Rufus, Pliny, or the Babylonian Oannes, or the Old Testament transmitter of the Flood and of the Psalms, or the author of the Gilgamesh epic, or the Indians of North America—all of them, a thousandfold, again and again. This is precisely the eternally characteristic thing, a constant fate of all legends, wherever we may reach, that puts the garment in the place of the core. And so let it be repeated again and again: for this very reason every interpretation of legends that exhausts itself in such present-world, often merely allegorical-formal relations or interpretations is no real interpretation of legends; it teaches us indeed to understand the construction of the shell, [p. 116] but it withholds from us the life-giving power of the interior. And this interior discloses itself when we search for natural-historically certain pictures of existence of the deeper past that correspond to the clear sense and report of the myths and legends, and that, at their touch, resound from within and let the doors of their enchanted castle spring open. This never happens, as the literature shows, when we always want to bind everything to our little bit of surrounding world, or weave philological and aesthetic nets in which the primeval life, so audibly calling out of the legends and myths, is strangled ever anew. If we therefore take into account, besides, the experiences we had with the lindworm and primordial-man legends regarding their possibility of natural-historical truth, the origin of the legend of the giant birds and of the flying bird Rock would also have to be placed at least at the beginning of the Tertiary, where descendants of such stocks are actually already to be found palaeontologically, or themselves still to be expected.

But something else, too, becomes explicable if we accept this date in earth history, something that would otherwise have to appear as senseless exaggeration: the lifting up of elephants by those giant birds, to which, with all our inclination to see something real in the legends, we should otherwise hardly be able to assent. If one compares a bird of the height of the historical moas or emus with a camel or elephant of the same geological age, it would be, even if we imagined those running birds in full possession of a strong power of flight, a grotesque absurdity to consider them capable of carrying up such large quadrupeds. But if we have to imagine the process in the earlier part of the Tertiary age, then quite different proportions of size obtained there between the animals in question. For it is a general law, emerging especially in Tertiary mammalian evolution, that the older forms of a special stock are smaller than those that come afterward; this is demonstrated, among others, for the horses, the elephants, the camels and the rhinoceroses, which at that time were in part only the size of a mastiff or a calf, as fossil skeletons show. At that time, then, the genera of these stocks, even in the adult state, were of a substantially smaller bodily habitus than [p. 117] those of today; and flying birds of the size of even a moa alone, or of a presumed Early Tertiary giant bird, if we think of it as flying, would have been capable of carrying those elephants and camels up into the air, several of them even; and this probably more easily than a large eagle nowadays a young lamb.

The Early Tertiary epoch is the time when the later mammalian types, still living today, of the elephantids, camelids, horses and ruminants mentioned above were particularly small, only to grow larger and larger with increasing approach to the present. Smallness seems at that time to have been the temporal character of such initial genera, though only of mammals, and so the later man, too, might at that time, in certain lines of his stock, have possessed this temporal property and been of corresponding size in relation to this mammalian environment. Over against all these, those gigantic Tertiary running birds and their presumable flying parallel or ancestral forms would then have been giants to a still much greater degree than the historical New Zealand and Madagascan running birds are in relation to present-world man.

The giant birds recur especially in Chinese traditions as well, where in part there is talk also of small men who could be carried off by gigantic cranes and who were therefore greatly feared by them; or whose eyes they pecked out, or whom they carried off while they were tilling the fields. In connection with such giant birds, the Chinese tradition mentions a kind of pygmy-like men who were exposed to their attacks. It says there, among other things: In the region of the western sea lies the land of the cranes, where men and women are only seven inches tall. They live according to the natural moral law and shrink from injuring one another. They seem to fly when they walk, and cover seven miles in a day. The only creatures they fear are the cranes that come from the sea. These cranes, which make a thousand miles in one flight, can swallow them; only people of three (?) years and upward do not succumb to their attack<sup>52</sup>.

<sup>52</sup> B. Laufer, Ethnographische Sagen der Chinesen. “Festschr. f. Ernst Kuhn.” München 1916, p. 198.

If these people are described here as especially small, there are two possibilities for interpreting them: either they were really pygmies compared with our bodily size [p. 118] or they were of our normal measure and therefore just as readily snatched by real giant birds as the smaller Tertiary elephants mentioned above.

And in this case the original report may first have been communicated by men of substantially larger bodily dimensions than ours, to whom these giant birds could do nothing, or at most could occasionally peck out their eyes in battle. A legend conveyed by the Arab Qazwini also points to this: A man, on a voyage across the sea, was driven onto an island whose inhabitants (by his measure) were a cubit tall and mostly one-eyed, because the great birds had put out one of their eyes. They shut him in a cage. Soon afterward, however, they were again beset by a flock of storks. The man set himself against the birds with a great stick and put them to flight, whereupon the dwarfs held him in high honor.

The tale, incidentally, served as the basis of “Gulliver’s Travels to Lilliput”; this would thus also transmit the primeval legendary core correctly to later researchers, and permit the same natural-historical inferences as the old Arabic and Chinese tales themselves. So unchanged does the essence of legendary materials preserve itself, even in conscious reworking. But if a legend scholar of later ten-thousands of years were quite rightly to discover in “Gulliver’s Travels” a novel with a tendency from the Quaternary age, and were to show that Gulliver is the figure of a man of post-glacial age tormented by the pettiness of his contemporaries, he would have made a certainly correct finding and yet he would not have found the whole truth, not the essence of the legend, but only the external relation between its late-period version and a late-period meaning laid beneath it.

There is, in particular, yet one legendary animal figure of which one has so far been unable to make anything: the unicorn. Not the unicorn that in Germanic mythology bores into the world-ash; that is something quite different; but the unicorn that appears as a later fabulous animal, ranked equally with other legendary animals. Even the purely allegorizing interpreters of legends do not know what to do with it. And however good Abel’s explanations otherwise

[p. 119] Fig. 20. Giant saurian (Triceratops) from the end of the Cretaceous. North America. 8 m long. Anticipation of the rhinoceros-like form. Bird-like beak. (Semi-schematic reconstruction using sketches by Ch. W. Gilmore, Smithson. Rep. 1920, Washington 1922, and O. Abel, Paläobiologie 1912.)

are for elucidating the local formation of legends, even for the unicorn he can give no unambiguous find to which he alone would tie it, as for instance Polyphemus. He merely enumerates a multitude of the foolish late-medieval and modern unicorn notions that rest on fossil finds of quite different animal remains of recent times. He says of it: “Closely linked with the giant legends is the unicorn legend. Today hardly any serious doubt can exist that it was tusks of Ice Age elephants that were interpreted as unicorn remains. The peasant of the Lower Austrian loess country still today designates the isolated finds of mammoth tusks as the ‘Hurn von an Danghürn’ (horn of the unicorn); the notion of the great ‘unicorn’ thus still lives on among the people. The first ‘reconstruction’ of a unicorn comes from Otto von Guericke, the famous burgomaster of Magdeburg. He had dug up at the Zeunicken near Quedlinburg a heap of bones and teeth, which he boldly combined into the ‘Unicornu fossile’; Leibniz depicted this monstrosity for the first time in his Protogaea, and from there it passed into almost all textbooks of that time.” There are indeed also among

[p. 120] Fig. 20a. Present-world rhinoceros for comparison with the reptile Triceratops. (Sketch after illustrations in Brehms Tierleben, 4th ed. 1915.)

the fossil animals several whose skull bears a large horn, and they are even to be found among the terrible lizards of the Mesozoic era; but nowhere is the horn as large as the unicorn legend represents it, and I do not know which of these animal forms could be related to it. In the final chapter we shall return to this legendary being, which so far has not been identified even through fossil finds.

Without “fantasy” grounded in real inner intuition no science can be pursued at all, no chemical synthesis made, no combination, of the most commonplace kind, penetrating into the conditions of the former world. In the end, in science too, the seer comes into his right; for he knows the source of all knowledge: seeing. This knowledge, and hence this right of the poet and seer, an original German writer<sup>53</sup> once brought out, precisely with regard to seeing into the former world of earth history: “Since Arnold Böcklin painted tritons and nereids, fauns and centaurs, the fabulous beings of fantasy have again become as familiar and credible as ever before. What no learning and philological explanation could achieve, the enlivening power of artistic work accomplished. [p. 121] One would like to wish for a Böcklin to whom it were given, instead of the knowledge of half-forgotten myths, that of bygone days of the earth. How infinitely much there would be to discover and to shape! The understanding has made the essential accessible to us again. But the understanding is a poor fellow, and all philological learning, with its scientific reconstructions, is nonetheless powerless as long as an artist’s nature does not let us again see and believe what we for the time being only “know.” Know, say, as a sober and spiritually poor jurist knows of a murder when he holds merely to a few items of circumstantial evidence and incriminating objects; for our geological finds and scientific labors, so meager in proportion, are no more than that either, by which we estimate and pass judgment on the past like a poor defendant. Into no epoch of the earthly past can one sink oneself without feeling this opposition of knowledge and intuition. What we might gain if the scientifically recovered treasures were also discovered artistically, Böcklin likewise lets us divine. We know his picture “In caves dwells the old brood of dragons.” Out of a rock hole in which it had been hiding, slumbering, the giant neck of a monster pushes itself. Passing travelers have awakened it. The animal is in no hurry. Half asleep still, it blinks into the day and stretches its neck as slowly and lazily as it is itself unwieldy and clumsy. But this neck is of primordial-world size, it can fathom ravines and bend over rocks—it will cost it no effort to reach out leisurely and take in the riders speeding madly away. One thinks one sees a nightmare, unthinkable in reality. But as a scholar was able to prove to Böcklin that a flower “only thought” by him really blooms in India, so too the monster of the rocky gorge, only thought, was once reality: in the most monstrous fabulous beings of the Jurassic, the lindworms and terrible lizards.” And—let us add—man experienced them.

<sup>53</sup> W. Pastor, Lebensgeschichte der Erde. (Leben und Wissen, vol. I). Leipzig 1903, p. 197/98.

If the dragon and lindworm legends were for me one of the very strongest proofs of probability for ascribing a Mesozoic age to man, I should like to cite yet [p. 122] another passage from the literature of myth, in which, in the last analysis, an allusion to the same period might also be contained.

We read in the Gilgamesh epic that after the mammal-like mountain man Engidu enters the cultural sphere of Gilgamesh, a struggle breaks out. Engidu is defeated; they conclude a friendship and undertake a campaign against a foreign ruler, the owner of the sacred cedar, who has an entire park of cedars. This protector and cultivator of the sacred cedar, which in later times has a quite definite symbolic significance, thus guards, in terms of legendary history, this species of tree, which accordingly in the age of Gilgamesh and Engidu was not yet the common property of the earth, but a rarity that had arisen in one particular country and was found only there, so that a struggle for it was worthwhile. But if at that time the cedar, presumably as a representative of the younger fir-like conifers, was still so great a rarity, this agrees with the dating that we had in any case already ascribed on other grounds to the mammal-like Noachian human type, a Mesozoic age. For those conifers, to which the cedar also belongs, occur at the earliest in the middle phase of the Mesozoic era, in the Jurassic; the cedar itself, if one wishes to take the name of the tree literally, probably occurs somewhat later still, in the Lower Cretaceous, that is, toward the end of the Mesozoic era. The protector of the sacred cedar would thus be the ruler of a country and of a human stock among whom this genus of plant had perhaps first arisen and spread. This naturalistic trait of the legend, detached from the later, transferred significance, could therefore be referred to that epoch. And that agrees with everything that we believed we could otherwise already determine about those ancient fabulous human beings and their age. It is no refutation of the conjecture expressed here if in historical times the objects of poetry demonstrably took on an allegorical or religious-symbolic meaning, if, then, the cedar in particular, as a majestically great tree resting in itself, had become the symbol of life or of the deity that governs life. It is as with the serpent, which later was often (China, Greece) the symbol of the good and the pure [p. 123] and which nevertheless embodied evil and the daemonically dark in the oldest mythological consciousness.

An essential question may be touched upon here for the time being. Against our interpretation of legendary natural forms one can object that imagination is surely creative enough of itself to produce lindworms and dragons without real models; so that it need by no means be natural, earth-historical memories that speak to us out of those ancient fabulous creatures.

Undoubtedly ordinary imagination can form its figures after external experiences and give birth to caricatures of another kind; but whether without tradition it could form such true-to-life animals as appear in the legends and as actually lie before us in fossil form is nevertheless doubtful. Imagination will hardly create anything out of the void; external points of attachment must be offered to it. That these points of attachment were only present-world animals or finds of bones seemed to us improbable already, precisely because of the lifelikeness of the legendary animals. But if one nevertheless wants to ascribe the origin of the dragon and lindworm legends to a work of imagination in late times, then one must not overlook another possibility as well. Despite all evolution, man does not stand rootless above the days of the former world. He is a descendant and as such is endowed from primordial times with a living inheritance in body and spirit. Precisely for that reason he also participates, as an individual, in unconscious instincts of the species and in an unconscious memory of the species, which, now intuitively touched and in places awakened again, could cast reflexes into the individual consciousness of the seer and in such a way call forth images of forms which, for all the subsequent attachment to the present or for all fantastic dress and distortion, can nevertheless harbor in their innermost essence a primordial natural truth, because they stem unconsciously from temporal depths that were once traversed alive by the primordial ancestors.

This, perhaps, is the hidden source from which late ages drew a legendary knowledge of the long-vanished, which in individual deep-penetrating, strong seers reached consciousness and so, without language and writing, without immediate tradition, was nevertheless “handed down” from primordial times.

[p. 124]

### Die Atlantissage — The Atlantis Legend

Three conceptions, as far as I can see, are essentially noteworthy, in legend and in research, concerning the sunken western continent of Atlantis.

The oldest and best known is that from Greek literature, handed down by Plato, retold or expanded also by many other writers, such as Herodotus, Hellanicus, Diodorus, Strabo, Pliny, and which, according to Plato<sup>54</sup>, has the following essential features: In ancient times a mighty military power broke in from the west into the Mediterranean cultural sphere and met its end here. At that time the Atlantic Sea could still be navigated; for before the Gate of Heracles there lay an island larger than Asia and Libya together. From it one could at that time still cross over to other islands and so reach the mainland lying on the far side, which bounds that sea. On this island of Atlantis there existed a great kingdom, which ruled also over many other islands and parts of the mainland and held sway too over Libya as far as Egypt and Tyrrhenia. Later there came mighty earthquakes and floods, and in the course of one terrible day and one terrible night the island of Atlantis disappeared into the sea. For that reason the sea there can no longer be navigated now, because there are high-heaped masses of mud, arisen at the sinking of the island.

<sup>54</sup> Platons Timäos, Kritias, Gesetze X. Ins Deutsche übertragen von O. Kiefer. Jena 1909, p. 9—16; p. 141ff.

Such, roughly, is Plato’s account in the Timaeus. It was brought by Solon from Egypt, where he had heard it from old secret records of priests, who loaded him with honors. “You are all young in spirit,” they had said to him, “for in your heads there is no intuition from ancient tradition and no knowledge grown gray with time” — one feels that the priests would have to call it out even to us of today, who have learned to look so far already into the earth-historical past without yet having seen in it what is most essential. And then they told him of floods of fire and of water that had already come and are yet to come, of altered [p. 125] motions of the celestial bodies circling the earth, and of all kinds of causes by which men had earlier perished and will yet perish. That sounds like a fable; so they themselves told him; but it is true tradition. “For what happens among you or among us or elsewhere, so far as it is in any way significant, is all recorded from the most ancient time in our temples and remains preserved in memory.”

Now follows a description of Atlantis, which rose high and steep out of the sea; only the region near the capital was throughout a plain, enclosed all around by mountains that ran down to the sea. At the edge of this plain, about 30,000 feet from the sea, stood a mountain flattened on all sides. “Poseidon” also set several smaller and larger rings, two of earth, three of water, around the hill, each uniformly distant in every direction from the others — the typical picture of a crater island with several phases of eruption and therefore also secondary craters, and with it a main cone, probably dating from the oldest period of eruption. Poseidon also caused two springs, one cold and one warm, to rise from the earth and abundant fruits of every kind to spring up from it; thereupon he peopled it with men. Of animals, elephants are especially named. The description unmistakably fits a volcanic island risen and piled up from the sea, though one of special size.

Geological research knows two elements of Atlantic land. The one, evidently the youngest, is of volcanic nature; the other, older and very old, was purely continental in nature. On plant- and animal-geographical grounds palaeontologists already many decades ago assumed a north- and south-Atlantic land bridge from the old world across into the new, which can also be corroborated by geotectonic facts, such as the sudden break-off of ancient mountain stumps running transversely toward the Atlantic and of corresponding fossil materials from older epochs on this side and on that. When the continent had vanished, a process evidently distributed over several earth-historical periods, a strong [p. 126] volcanism asserted itself in the region, now ever more purely oceanic, to which not only perhaps the whole mid-Atlantic submarine ridge, but also whatever of it still projects above the water as island complexes, owes its origin; by contrast, the Cape Verde Islands and the Antilles are remnants of land from the late Tertiary that have remained standing. The ancient Greek descriptions of Atlantis, so far as they have the land as such for their subject, point unmistakably to the last phase of Atlantic soil development, namely the volcanic, and it is to be assumed that in the last analysis they stem from the time when the last continental remnants vanished amid earthquake phenomena and at the same time with volcanic eruptions breaking through, amid continual alterations of the ground and incursions of the sea or subsidences of land, whereby the geologically relatively short-lived volcanic accumulations, even above sea level, may have taken up very large areas and nevertheless have lasted for thousands of years, so that settlements of animals, plants and men could develop and live out their lives there. But since on such volcanic islands the sea too is continually at work destructively with great success, and since a volcanic region, especially after a certain weakening or after the extinction of paroxysmal activity, also emits gases and warm springs or acidulous waters, the alterations and arrangements ascribed to Poseidon in Plato’s account fit excellently with the geological facts; so too the later silting-over after the sinking, which could form for a long time through last eruptions, by the ejection of great quantities of porous ash material, and through the at first perhaps only slightly deep subsidence of the volcanic land surface.

A second significant conception of Atlantis stems from the North American scholar Hosea. He advanced the theory<sup>55</sup> that the Greek account of Atlantis must have a very real historical foundation and needed only the correct, scientific interpretation to reveal this old history. The interpretation he finds in the fact that the American continental culture was of common origin with the Egyptian, or that at least a connection existed between the aboriginal inhabitants of America and the Egyptians or other peoples of the East. [p. 127] Perhaps the old Atlantis itself was actually a larger island in the ocean or a group of islands, which was likewise settled. To be sure, Hosea sees in the particular size of the land of Atlantis asserted by tradition, which is said to have surpassed Libya and Asia Minor together in extent, an exaggeration resting on the Greeks’ ignorance of the oceanic regions of that time, which is quite understandable in the Oriental, inclined as he is to fancy. Precisely in this Hosea also wants to see grounded the assertion of a rapid catastrophic sinking and the allegedly vast spatial extent of the event. On the other hand he assumes that in the Atlantic there existed a band of volcanic islands, which offered a possibility of getting across the ocean for the navigation of very ancient times, still clumsy. For early navigation did not yet have the means to make extended voyages on the open sea, except when a chain of islands enabled it, so to speak, to feel its way over short distances from one fixed point to another. That this too was the manner of the supposed traffic between Egypt and the Atlantic continent, he says, emerges from the legend itself. Thus we could well understand how the news of such a sinking, bound up with the destruction of life, brought into the Mediterranean cultural sphere by frightened seafarers, magnified the event into a terrible cataclysm and how its memory could in fact deter seafarers for many generations from any further exploration of that region. The reason given by tradition for this, however, is namely that through the catastrophe the very place where the island lands had formerly lain had become impassable through enormous masses of mud. In short, these more natural-historically grounded explanations and the correspondence advanced by Hosea between the ancient cultures on this side and on that side of the ocean, which manifests itself besides in word roots also in buildings (pyramids, obelisks) and figures (sphinxes) and religious astronomical notions (transmigration of souls, calendar feast days), led Hosea to the following conclusion: Even if the theory of Atlantis transmitted by tradition is not yet so well founded that it could be accepted with scientific certainty, yet it [p. 128] promises important results concerning the cultural origin of the American continent; for one notices in it traces of foreign influence in a far-distant time, and these phenomena agreed in the last analysis best with the tradition conveyed by the Atlantis legend.

<sup>55</sup> L. M. Hosea, Atlantis: A Statement of the Atlantic theory, respecting aboriginal civilization. Cincinn. Quart. Journ. of Science. Vol. II. Cincinnati 1875, p. 193ff. Also: M. Clarke, Examination of the legend of Atlantis in reference to protohistoric communication with America. London 1886.

A third indication, scientifically conceived and uniting the historical with the natural-historical, of the existence of Atlantis as lived through by man and of its sinking, is given by the French geologist and Alpine researcher Termier. From various geological factors<sup>56</sup> there emerges, he says, a late-Atlantic island land. The Azores form the highest part of that mid-Atlantic submarine ridge which runs through the ocean in an S-shaped curve, parallel to the coasts, right through the middle, and whose chief elevations, the Azores, lie exactly west of the Strait of Gibraltar. The deep region of subsidence of the ocean floor, extending from there southward past West Africa, is strongly volcanic; here rise the pedestals of Madeira, the Canary and the Cape Verde Islands. Many of them are built of volcanic rock and it is a region of the earth’s crust of little stability. In the summer of 1898 a French cable-layer discovered 900 km north of the Azores, at a depth of 3000 m, a sea floor of mountainous character with high peaks, steep slopes and deep valleys. From the rocky peaks glassy lava was brought up with grapnels, which in Termier’s view could not have solidified under water, but only in the open air, that is, on the surface of the land. Since this glassy lava still possessed its fine points entire, it cannot have been exposed long to weathering, but must very soon after its formation have been sunk, relatively quickly, to that depth of the sea. Noteworthy for the geotectonic unrest of that Atlantic zone is also that in the Atlas region mountain-building folding movements were still taking place in the Quaternary, just as the mid-Atlantic region generally, as the point of intersection of the Atlantic depression running north–south and the Mediterranean depression meeting it east–west, had to be exposed to particularly strong movements of the earth’s crust. The volcanic movements described were therefore certainly very young in earth-historical terms, and nothing stands in the way of the assumption that Quaternary man lived through them. [p. 129] Termier closes with a vivid outlook: From the points of reference that geology and Plato’s account give us, it is not well possible to assume anything other than that a Flood was the disappearance of Atlantis. A Flood or at the end the Flood, the Flood of religious doctrine, which has occupied our imagination from childhood? Could Noah have been an Atlantean? The aboriginal inhabitants of Mexico, tradition reports, came from the east, when the land there went down. Why did the Mexicans have names for their cities that were formed with “atlan,” when the Spaniard set foot on the land? Why the same word-formation on this side and on that side of the great water, before the connection from Europe to America demonstrably existed? Did they somewhere and sometime in gray prehistory hear the voice of those on this side?
