If the Atlanteans left their land eastward and westward before the irruption of the Flood, then we also know why the legend of the Flood clung in the memory of the peoples here and across the water. The Chinese did not have it; the circum-Atlantic peoples have it.

<sup>56</sup> P. Termier, L’Atlantide. Bullet. Instit. Océanographique de Monaco. 1913, Nr. 256. The geological literature is summarized in: O. Wilckens. Atlantis. Geolog. Rundschau. vol. IV. Leipzig und Berlin 1913, p. 441. A summarizing survey of the scientific, legend-historical and occultist literature and views on the Atlantis problem is given by: J. Peter, “Atlantis. Die versunkene Welt.” Pfullingen 1922. 34 pp. Likewise F. Wencker-Wildberg, Atlantis. Der Roman einer untergegangenen Welt. Leipzig (no year). The book, however, is no “novel,” but a very factual compilation of all the traditions. The theosophical-occultist literature, which also reports on Atlantis, as well as on the continent of Gondwana, to be discussed more closely in the treatment of the Flood, has been disregarded here, as in the whole book. Although it is certainly not to be denied that facts of the history of humanity are in part transmitted in it according to old oral or written tradition or from a brilliant grasp of the past, it nonetheless wraps itself, so far as I know it and therefore esteem it little, in a mantle of the primitively mysterious and gladly leaves the reader in the dark about its sources. This holds quite especially also for the two publications that otherwise come into question here: W. Scott-Elliot, Atlantis nach okkulten Quellen. Transl. by “F. P.” Leipzig (no year, before 1905) and —, Das untergegangene Lemuria. Transl. by A. von Ulrich. Leipzig 1905, whose author, instead of an honest indication of sources, prefers to appeal to all sorts of secret records and “libraries” accessible only to the especially initiated. No matter whether this is meant figuratively or literally, in any case it is a deception of the serious reader, who has a claim to learn unambiguously the source from which a writer draws, be it ever so personally intuitive in kind. So it is customary among honest investigators, who wish that their opinion should be of value also for honest people, not only for those in need of sensation; or else one should refrain from public book-writing if one holds one’s knowledge — as I should find comprehensible — too good for publication. In the small work on Lemuria, which evidently rests on all sorts of legends, perhaps only orally transmitted, or on a novelistic spinning-out of such, there are found statements about primordial men and primordial animals, in part quite confusedly thrown together with non-corresponding and geologically wrongly dated fossil animal forms. Nevertheless I must concede to a part of these ideas priority over my own thoughts, since they at the time stimulated me to reflect on these questions, so far as they concern man himself. The two-volume work by H. P. Blavatsky, “The Story of Atlantis,” I know only from the bookseller’s window, have never had in my hand, and am also not informed of its contents through excerpts or reviews, so far as it goes beyond what is said in note 31).

Termier’s conclusions, as will yet be shown in what follows, are not quite our own. The Noachian Flood is for us connected with the sinking of the older Lemuria and perhaps of still more easterly parts of the Gondwana continent, a land formerly situated in the Indian Ocean, and the emigration from the circum-Indian region explains far better why the actual legend of the Flood was widespread, not as Termier thinks in the East Atlantic, but in the Malayo-Polynesian region. The sinking of Atlantis, on the other hand, was for us too a late-time event, in any case a Quaternary one, and, according to the reports from antiquity that seem positively historical, in any case dragged developed cultures with it into the abyss, while at the same time on European soil the Stone Age man perhaps led his solitary existence. In any case it is a fact that in a very recent geological time we can demonstrate Atlantic land complexes, even without the legendary and semi-historical reports from Greek antiquity. Apart from other proofs of probability from earth history, the fact already suffices that the very young Atlas range, in earth-historical terms, runs toward [p. 130] the Atlantic and breaks off abruptly there, so that the break-off can have happened only after the folding-up of the mountains.

A legend now which lets us recognize the mountain connection between Atlantis and the Atlas range is that of the daughters of Atlas, the Pleiades. This name, as emerges from the legend itself, was given to the constellation only later and referred at first to beings that were on the earth. As a constellation they stood, in the historical legendary age of Greekdom, in connection with seasonal periods; but before it was otherwise. According to Preller-Robert<sup>57</sup> their linkage with other figures is as follows: The relation of the Pleiades to the season is evident from the fable of the seven daughters of Atlas, an old poem probably of Peloponnesian origin. Already Hesiod calls the Pleiades Atlageneis. They were daughters of Atlas, the sea-giant in the west who bears the heavens, and of the Oceanid Pleione; the latter is also a mythical abstraction of the whole group. They were born on Mount Cyllene in Arcadia, whence Pindar and Simonides call them mountain goddesses. Yet they are also called Ouraniai, daughters of heaven, since their original meaning seems to be that of fertilizing heavenly nymphs. They became daughters of Atlas because he bears the heavens and because the clouds rise from the western ocean, where Atlas has his station; for the constellation of the Pleiades too is at home in this region. As the cause of their transformation into a constellation Aeschylus invented the infinite grief of the Pleiades over the sufferings of their father Atlas.

<sup>57</sup> Preller-Robert, Griech. Mythologie, p. 464ff. p. 564ff.

So far the philological expositions, which do not touch the essence of the legend. For what became of the Pleiades in later poetry is of no importance. It is not to be disputed that the constellation of the Pleiades, once it had received this name, played the great role in the calendar of the ancients. But all that is advanced here in the way of such external connections does not penetrate to the heart of the legend, in which a post-Atlantic sense is surely to be distinguished from an Atlantic one, that is, an older, objectively natural tradition from a younger, misunderstood or transferred reshaping. If, then, we also detach from this complex of [p. 131] legend the earth-historical element, we can gain from it an insight into the structure and the geography of Atlantis.

What we can directly take from the geology of the northwest African continental margin (Fig. 21) — the former continuation of the Atlas range, with breaks and subsidences, into the Atlantic Ocean — the legend confirms for us. Indeed we learn from it still more, which can also be justified geologically: that this continuation of the mountains was not so high and not so uniformly closed as the continental body of the Atlas, but was presumably dissolved into single ranges and strands of lesser height, which formed ridges and mountains standing together like a Pleiades-cluster. The oceanic part of the Tertiary Atlas folding, given the subsidence setting in already soon afterward, probably never rose at all to the considerable heights of the African Atlas, but was rather an appendage, perhaps a dying-away portion of the fold, and therefore lower, more scattered, more dissolved. Those were the daughters of the giant Atlas, the Pleiades. As this mountain part later sank relatively soon and at the margin has sunk even within the present African mainland, as the map [p. 132] sketch shows, so too it presumably did not at its folding-up grow into a closed structure of Alpine massiveness, similarly as the Eastern Alps with their smaller strands did not attain the massiveness of the Western Alps, and indeed for this reason, that the floor of the Eastern Alps was already more labile in pre-Tertiary time and the last Alp-building force setting in in the Tertiary era was already dissipated beforehand in partial movements, whereas the Western Alpine zone had been calmer beforehand and then, with full force in the Tertiary, could be rolled up into a nappe mountain range. Analogously the geotectonic relation between the structure of the Atlantic and of the African body of the Atlas may have been, and so the Pleiades mountains may not have attained the high measure of the Atlas. The Cape Verde Islands are remnants of them.

Fig. 21. Schematic map sketch of the Atlas region and of the northwest African continental margin and island region. (After P. Lemoine in Handb. d. Region. Geologie VII. 1913.)

Thus the Pleiades are quite naturally originally the Atlageneis and for that very reason also daughters of the Oceanid’s daughter Pleione. Later, when the Pleiadic Atlantis had sunk and only the legendary name was saved across to primordial Hellas, they were “set in the heavens by Zeus” as a symbol of what had sunk; but the memory of their mountain nature still trembles on, for we hear indeed that Pindar and Simonides called them mountain goddesses. They are now, presumably because one no longer knew how to localize them otherwise, transferred to the Peloponnese, for the old tradition about Atlantis had long since vanished from the consciousness of the Greeks of that time, as Solon indeed already learned from the Egyptian priests.

Up to this point we can trace the legend clearly back to its natural foundations, and we see the changes which the content and word-meaning of ancient traditions must have undergone in the men of late times. But even in this late transformation the core lies clearly recognizable. Yet we are able to recognize a further trait. As the cause of the transformation Aeschylus gives the infinite grief of the Pleiades over the sufferings of their father Atlas, for whose sake Zeus took pity on them and set them in the heavens. The philological explanation holds that the grief broke out because the Atlas towers up to heaven and the dark storm clouds are wont to rise from the western ocean and to wrap his head.

[p. 133] Let us again interpret simply and without constraint, naturally-historically, in accordance with the geological findings. How beautifully expressed in this allegory is the end of the Atlantic land as the continuation of the Atlas body. The latter is shattered and in part sunken; it is the Ur-Pleiades themselves, the mountain parts, that feel in their innermost marrow the pain of the shattering paternal body, that now go under, in order “to live immortal in song” and to be transferred to the western oceanic constellation, which, like them, represents the cluster of sisters. Not wholly has their mighty father Atlas gone under, only his body is painfully distorted and torn. All around, against all, the Mediterranean coasts too, he is smashed, broken; and from the north the Hispano-Alpine fold-arc has pressed itself in upon him (cf. the map). But we learn also from geological research that even in the Quaternary epoch, that is, almost within historical human times, his body was subjected to renewed foldings, which were a posthumous after-pain of the mountain-building of Tertiary times. The whole Atlas region must then have been exposed to considerable earthquakes and displacements, and so the paternal body lay there in painful convulsions, in connection with the Atlantic breakings-off and subsidences. I believe that natural history itself is here the full content of the legend of the downfall of the Atlas land, at a late earth-historical time, still quite close to our own cultural development. Then came the volcanic eruptions and with them the creation of that island complex whose topography Plato’s account given above indeed describes, and of which the Canary Islands may be a younger, smaller image. That was then the very last time of the Atlantic land, which had probably been preceded by an older continental time, and it is from the catastrophic ending of that older time that the Pleiades legends probably derive. Of the older continental time of the Atlantic region, evidently not preserved in Greece, a brief glimpse will yet be gained in the discussion of the Noachian Flood and of the migration of peoples connected with it.

Another legend becomes clear as well. Heracles comes across to Atlas, who induces him to take the celestial sphere from him. Might the penetration of the Spanish ranges be linked to this? [p. 134] And why is it precisely their interruption that is called the Gate of Heracles? Atlas towered up into the heavens; and the posthumous folding movements, as is entirely self-evident geotectonically, not only raised the summits but certainly also lowered them, temporarily or permanently. Then Atlas rolled the heavy burden of the vault of heaven from himself onto the opposite mountain ranges, equally high, at the Gate of Heracles, perhaps only for a short time, in order soon to take it upon himself again by newly thrusting up his shoulders.

Around the late-Atlantic island mountains there dwelt, according to the Greek legend, Amphitrite. What the name means seems not yet to have been established with agreement. Amphitrite is, in late mythological times, the sea goddess and probably also the consort of Poseidon. According to some legends she fled to Atlas from Poseidon, who was pressing her; another legend, on the other hand, has her indeed reach Atlas in her flight, where, however, the dolphin finally finds her. The etymology of Amphitrite in any case contains the word amphi, that is, on both sides or all around; Trite, the same stem as in the name of the sea god Triton, is, according to Preller-Robert, the roaring flood. If one accepts this, then Amphitrite is the personification of “the one surrounded on all sides by the roaring flood,” in our legend-complex therefore the oceanic island continent itself and, in a transferred sense, its inhabitants. The latter could be the more probable; for it was not the land that fled to Atlas from the Poseidon pressing in, but its inhabitants, while the land sinks. It will not have sunk all at once, but gradually in several stages; hence the dolphin, figuratively therefore the pressing sea, reaches Amphitrite, who has fled to Atlas. But it may also be that Amphitrite was the land itself, which sank more and more gradually from west to east. In this way there would arise the picture of a land shrinking ever more toward the Atlas, which thus as it were fled to him, in order there, at the edge of the present-day Atlas, to be overtaken by the dolphin, that is, by the sea; for there the movement of subsidence, and with it the pressing on of the sea, came to a standstill.

Still a word is to be said about the inhabitants of Atlantis itself. Their culture is indeed described in Plato and by others. [p. 135] Here only the general legend-allegorical aspect shall yet be pointed out.

The Atlantis people meet us in legend under yet another name: Hesperides, that is, inhabitants of the West-land, of the land in the evening. We learn also of a fruit which they cultivated and which they had perhaps themselves bred from a wild tree. It is their golden apples that Heracles had gone to fetch: orange or citron. From Atlantis, then, these fruits seem to have been introduced early into the Greek-Mediterranean cultural sphere. For not only do they echo in the very old Heracles legend, but they also play a role at various weddings of Greek mythology, so that they evidently were, in older, prehistoric times, a coveted object but not yet native in the Mediterranean region, and were instead brought in from the Atlantic region or, occasionally, as by Heracles, fetched amid sacrifices and dangers.

In the Heracles legend the West-land as such emerges clearly. The mythical tradition has derived from the existence of that land and of its inhabitants, and when the definite knowledge of it had faded, men took pains to place the Hesperides somewhere on known land. Preller-Robert says: “At first they served, like Atlas, to mark the boundary of navigation, that is, of the known sea, usually in the region of the Pillars of Heracles, but sometimes also in that of the Hyperboreans. Or men sought them on certain islands of the Atlantic Ocean, especially in the regions of the Atlantic mountains, until finally from these wholly mythical nymphs and trees the so-called Hesperidean fruits of later times came to be, which were again interpreted as various kinds of southern fruits, usually as the golden orange.”

Here again the philological interpretation is correct, so far as it concerns the transformations and interpretation of the legend in quite late times, namely in Greece itself<sup>57a</sup>. But one remains on the outer side of the problem and halts before the decisive step toward illumination has been taken, if one does not go still further back and take the Hesperides as the prehistoric inhabitants of Atlantis, which was later simply no longer known; likewise their golden apples, of which it is in the end [p. 136] a matter of indifference whether they were oranges or citrons. At any rate these Hesperides must have been an older human race with a riper culture than that from which “Heracles” came to them. They also possessed things which were presumably the result of great skill and knowledge, thus perhaps refinements of grain and fruit varieties, which was a coveted novelty for the poorer, more primitive Mediterranean folk, probably more inexperienced in such matters. Perhaps it was the time of a very remote primal Hellenism, a Ur-Pelasgian time, in which men were concerned with the cultivation of higher kinds of fruit? For it is reported that Deucalion’s son Orestheus, that is, the man from the mountain, created the vine from a buried piece of wood. In the Old Testament too, God first gives the vine as a gift to the post-diluvian man. Should one not come to the thought that “Noah,” who survived the Flood, and his race settled the continental Ur-Atlantis, since we find these three parallels: Noah’s vine, Orestheus’ vine, and the skill of the Hesperides, in essentially the same sense? In any case the Atlanteans seem to have distinguished themselves in this, and what the primal Hellenes did not succeed in cultivating, or for which they lacked the basic plants, that was fetched from afar, and that is why the old emissary went out for the golden apples of the people who lived in the evening. All this may fall in relatively late stages of the Tertiary, but the final volcanic catastrophe of Atlantis probably only in the Diluvial period.

<sup>57a</sup> In a comprehensive work, “Von rätselhaften Ländern” (München 1925), R. Hennig gives an account of the mythical geography of antiquity and also of Atlantis, which he interprets as Tartessos and the mouth of the Guadalquivir. Here too I cannot agree, because present-day geography proves nothing for the interpretation of ancient traditions but merely misleads in it. For every geologist knows that precisely in the Mediterranean regions and on the West European coasts great changes have taken place even in the last millennia. Thus philology and ancient history will always limp when they do not take along geologists on the spot, who are helpful in the interpretation. From friendly and learned quarters I was made aware that one can demonstrate exactly how Plato came to his mythical poetic works and how in doing so he was concerned with quite other things than, say, transmitting real or supposed primeval history. Thus his Atlantis too is an invented symbol. But to this also it must be replied that, just as with the Homeric Polyphemus, the poetic or philosophical use and shaping of a legend proves nothing against the core and the primordial tradition hidden in it, let that be “invented” as it will.

If one attributes to the legends and myths, though certainly not to all of them uncritically, a symbolic sense, that is, one signifying reality, and therefore not merely an allegorizing one, then it is always and inescapably a matter of the answering of the question what they symbolize — and that must be real events, conditions, things, natural objects, living beings, thus natural-historical and human-historical matters in the whole objectivity of their existence or of their being lived through. One must therefore give them an interpretation corresponding to the real, earnest but not poetically objectless, that is, playful; thus in every case a nature-historical or human-historical one in the most essential sense. But if one succeeds in finding in them such a [p. 137] sense as a closed whole, then one has indeed also the essence and the fitting interpretation, however fairy-tale-like and fantastic and contradictory and patched together and flatly allegorizing the garment in which the core is wrapped may be. It is the working out of such legendary cores that is at issue here. The method consists in bringing natural-historical connections to bear on the old legends, which themselves mostly point, by hints, directly toward definite possibilities, if one follows them without prejudice. Admittedly, at present not much can be done here with exact methods, and an entirely different kind of empiricism must be woven right through.

We have now behind us an epoch of scientific research into and interpretation of old narrative materials in which it was apparently taken as self-evident to see in every myth, in every fairy tale the poetic presentation and elaboration of a natural process or natural state of ordinary kind, or even merely a symbolization, indeed only a shallow personification of spiritual or psychic states and transformations. The most positive natural-historical and prehistoric utterances and reports were then forcibly recast into poetic occurrences, and a frame of mind was attributed to the ancient transmitters of the legends, the creators of the myths, which does not exhaust their essence, notwithstanding the artistic value in itself. An example of this is the legend of Atlantis with what belongs to it. The image-rich but genuinely symbolic language of the oldest time had later, in the mythology of Hellenism, which in the deeper sense was then none any more, to make way for the conception of unreality. If, therefore, e.g. the traditions of the continent inhabited by men and later sunken already existed in the time of the primal Hellenes only in the form of the personifying legend-image of the fleeing Amphitrite, then it was not, as the philological scholar believes, a poetic recasting by Plato into the geographical, but it was positively a cognition in the history of legend when by him and others the geological and human-historical core was at last peeled out and the events were now again reported simply and naturally from which the allegory, left uncomprehended, had once before already [p. 138] taken its departure. It is therefore undoubtedly an interpretation of the ancient material that directly inverts the state of affairs when the philological scholar, holding to the word-images instead of to nature and history, writes:

“In the course of time these legends changed by being given a geographical turn. For a long time the Pillars of Heracles had been for the Greeks the farthest goal of navigation, when the Samians and Phocaeans first pressed beyond them and an immense distance opened up, where fancy again found the richest nourishment. In this sense Solon composed his fable of Atlantis, handed down by Plato in the Timaeus and reworked in the Critias, the great mainland beyond the Pillars of Heracles, where the name Atlas first appears in an extended meaning. To this came the image of the heaven-bearing mountain Atlas, which the Greeks, according to Herodotus, appropriated from the natives of those regions and could appropriate all the more easily since the image of mountains that support the heavens like pillars was also familiar to them. Native fairy tales and the poetry of the adventures of Perseus and Heracles in these regions contributed to making this mountain appear ever more in the light of the wonderful, while under the influence of other factors the notion arose among the Greeks of a mythical king Atlas who had ruled in these regions and was very experienced in heavenly things, that is, in astronomy and philosophy, until he was transformed into that mountain.”

The consideration would have to go, as said, the opposite way; it should not proceed from the idea of a primary allegory of the gods and its later attachment to real regions or situations, but should see in ancient, truly natural-historical events and experiences of humanity the impulse and primal ground of the later allegory, which then, in historical times, was stripped of its allegorical garment by a scientifically clearer mind and thereby led back to the picture of reality which it veiled and which originally alone was there, not first invented: an earth-historical and human-historical event.

[p. 139]

### Die geologische Erklärung der noachitischen Sintflut — The Geological Explanation of the Noachian Flood

More familiar than all the other legends is to us from childhood the “Flood.” How it has worked upon our heart and our imagination, supported by the story of Noah’s ark with its living contents.

The legend of the Flood has undergone many a change in the estimation of its truth. At first, in older times, it was held undoubtedly to be an event of world history. The simply believing mind of the Christian has always regarded it so as well, and has thereby been nearer to the truth, if blindly, than the incipient geognosy of the eighteenth century and the earth-historical research, then at its height, of our past century. The former, represented most drastically in respect of the doctrine of the Flood by the Swiss naturalist Scheuchzer, had, as soon as petrifactions or fossils were recognized as remains of real former animals — not merely as sports of nature, as the Christian Middle Ages had believed — found the natural-scientific explanation for them precisely in the biblical Flood, during which the water had stood above the highest mountains. As it ran off (Diluvium), the remains of the perished living world had remained behind petrified in the hardened mud.

This diluvial theory was soon shaken when, in the first half of the nineteenth century, men learned to read off from the strata of the earth the multiplicity of such Diluvia, and to interpret these strata themselves, as well as the fossil remains, ever more surely. Now the Flood was left aside as an objectless phantasm, until in the eighties Eduard Sueß took up the meanwhile-discovered Babylonian Flood narrative as the archetype of the Chaldean Flood legend in general and interpreted it, as reproduced in what follows, as the introduction to his monumental “Face of the Earth,” which long dominated geology<sup>58</sup>. His conception of the Flood has since remained largely authoritative also in the wider scientific world, so far as it [p. 140] concerned itself with this problem at all, thus also among theologians and philologists. With a view to great earthquake catastrophes and to the sea flood-waves often generated thereby through the shocks and undulations of the suboceanic crust of the earth, which race across a whole ocean and flood coasts, indeed whole tracts of country, devastatingly, and have swept away cities, Sueß discusses in detail the Chaldean Flood account, which is for him essentially embodied in the Babylonian Gilgamesh epic, and shows, holding to the details of the poem, that, down to the shipbuilding, everything in it can be explained as follows by the assumption of a seismic sea flood combined with a cyclone.

<sup>58</sup> Ed. Sueß, Das Antlitz der Erde. 3rd ed. Wien und Leipzig 1908. vol. I, p. 25—98.

It is a lowland, now of course enlarged by the land-depositing activity of the two great rivers Euphrates and Tigris. At that time the shore-fringe of the Persian Gulf reached 400 km farther northwest into the land, and that much has since been gained for the then lowland, essentially by delta formation. The Flood event described in the Gilgamesh epic therefore took place essentially farther inland than the confluence of Euphrates and Tigris lies today. The use of asphalt in the building of the vessel points directly to the asphalt-rich strata in the surroundings of the Euphrates and Tigris lowland. The warnings of the sea god consisted of smaller quake-floods preceding the main event; perhaps also of a rumbling of the earthquake occurring with them. The heavy rains were mixed with one of the dust-spouts of a whirlwind, which occur in those regions also otherwise, and which darkened the sun. The opening of the wells of the deep is the ejection of groundwater, as has been observed at Lake Baikal and in India already during earthquakes. The main inundation, however, is said to have been a sea flood, whipped by storm through a cyclone. “Sudden and terrible,” says Sueß, in explanation of his view, “are the inundations that are brought about by cyclones. They occur only near the sea, either on islands or in the lowlands of the lower course of great rivers. Over a breadth of hundreds of nautical miles the cyclone wave approaches the mainland, and if it is dammed up by the narrowing outline of the sea [p. 141] it rises higher and finally plunges devastatingly over the flat country. Positively horrifying are the consequences that have been experienced on the West Indian islands and at the East Indian river mouths.” “There are examples from our own days where the loss of human life that occurred in a single night is estimated at one to two hundred thousand souls.” “As a rule extremely violent masses of rain, often described by present-day observers quite literally as ‘flood-like,’ fall from the sky, especially on the front side of the advancing cyclone; frequently strong thunderstorms occur at the same time.”

Now follows an exact geological analysis of the Euphrates land, whereby Sueß does justice to the details of the epic, which he — they are the external circumstances in which the event is clothed in later times — then lets resolve into the following sober picture: “We have to regard as the scene of these events the lower river basin of Mesopotamia, from the city of Surippak, lying on the Euphrates near the sea, to the slopes of the mountains of Nizzir beyond the Tigris.” And later, in the summary, it says: “The natural event known under the name of the Flood occurred on the lower Euphrates and was bound up with an extensive and devastating inundation of the Mesopotamian lowland. The most essential cause was a considerable earthquake in the region of the Persian Gulf or south of it. It is very probable that during the period of the most violent shocks a cyclone entered from the south out of the Persian Gulf. The traditions of other peoples in no way justify the assertion that the flood reached beyond the lower course of the Euphrates and Tigris or even over the whole earth.”

To the proof of this last sentence many pages are still devoted in Sueß. He shows that in the lowlands and delta regions of the Indian rivers too, through enormous shifts of river courses, with and without earthquakes, whole cities and landscapes were catastrophically destroyed, flooded and swept away. “In the lowland of the Indus great and populous cities have become victims of natural events. With thousands of inhabitants they were probably often wrecked within a few moments, and the destruction of the irrigation works [p. 142] or the diversion of the river’s course altogether prevented rebuilding by the survivors. After centuries the traveler then comes upon extensive ruins and upon the figure-rich sculptures of an abandoned capital on the dry channel of the diverted river, and the discovery of even its name may become the goal of the ambition of our antiquarians.” Another example, at the mouth of the Indus, is the wide lowland basin of the Rann of Kutch, which in the eighteenth and nineteenth centuries was created and recreated by an earthquake and an associated irruption of the oceanic flood, and whose lake-level owes its existence to an upwelling of groundwater and the simultaneous settling of the ground. The whole region is about the size of Bavaria. There is said to be complete agreement with such events at New Madrid on the Mississippi and in the Buryat steppe at Lake Baikal in Asia. In the Bay of Bengal, where the Ganges and Brahmaputra debouch, quite enormous changes of ground, landscape and settlement have likewise constantly occurred in the last centuries. Here too it is a matter of immense areas. In 1762 a large part of the lowland was shaken by a violent earthquake. “The waters rushed like a roaring sea from their channels over the land; far and wide fissures opened, masses of water were thrown many feet high out of the ground, and the surrounding land sank thereby.” In 1810, 1829 and 1842 “the earth tremors repeated themselves in Calcutta. A few months before the last earthquake a cyclone had passed over Calcutta. In 1869, during a quake in Cachar east of the Brahmaputra, cracks formed in the ground, out of which first dry dust, then viscous mud surged up with the violence of a cannon shot.” Still far more terribly than the earthquakes there rage, according to Sueß, in the flat country of these river lowlands, from time to time, the whirlwind storms coming from the sea. “Many of them originate near the Andamans. From there they move, bringing ruin, toward the north, northwest or west. Now they enter, carrying along enormous masses of water and accompanied by immeasurable rain, the mouths of the Megna or of the Ganges, now they plunge upon the east coast of the mainland as far down as [p. 143] Pondicherry, or they strike the island of Ceylon.” Countless are the examples that Sueß gives, and which ought to convince, especially since the number of people perishing in such natural events is often so great that they seem like the account of the Flood. Thus in 1831 a storm flood struck the extreme west of the flat country of the Ganges south of Calcutta toward Cuttack; 300 villages were swept away and at least 11,000 people drowned; famine followed, and the total loss from this event with its immediate consequences is estimated at 50,000 souls. Thus Sueß holds that in many places on earth the legend of the Flood could arise from very limited individual catastrophes and from there be passed on.

Sueß distinguishes several kinds of Flood legends, according to the coloring that such an account had to take on among a people near the sea or an inland people. The Flood account of Genesis, he says, is of inland character and is derived from the Babylonian Gilgamesh account. The latter in turn belonged to a people that knew seafaring. This emerges from the difference in the description of the ship, from the absence of the helmsman in the Mosaic narrative, and also from the designation chest (ark), which had become out of the Mesopotamian ship; hence the Mosaic account is borrowed. Borrowed, according to Sueß, is also what may at most be designated as a tradition of the Flood legend from Egypt; perhaps Chaldean accounts are mixed with it. In any case such a catastrophe never occurred in Egypt itself; for this is indicated also by the fact that in the Egyptian narrative the destruction of mankind is brought about not by a flood but by the blood-shedding Hathor. Then there is yet a group: the Syrian accounts. In comparing them with the others one must not overlook that the coasts of the eastern Mediterranean, the Hellenic shores too, were frequently washed over by seismic floods in antiquity, as in more recent times. An example of seismic movement of the sea, recalling the destruction of Pharaoh in the Red Sea at the exodus of the people of Israel from Egypt, occurred at the siege of Potidaea by Artabazus in 479 B.C. Herodotus tells how the besiegers one day noticed a considerable ebbing of the sea, whereby the bay toward [p. 144] Pallene became passable, which they now crossed, and were thereby overtaken by the returning flood. Many other such floods are known. “Under such conditions it is understandable,” says Sueß, “that in Hellas there were traditions of repeated floods, such as those of Ogyges, of Deucalion, of Dardanos; beside these there existed isolated traditions on the islands, as on Samothrace. To these, and especially to the accounts of the flood of Deucalion, individual parts of the Chaldean tradition were attached, such as the rescue in a floating chest, the taking along of animals and the sending out of birds, particularly a dove.” But from none of these accounts can an extension of the event from Surippak into the basin of the Mediterranean be demonstrated. Given the great age of Egyptian culture and the strangeness of the myth there, one can, on the contrary, assume with no small certainty that the Mediterranean basin was not reached.

Finally Sueß, remaining so wholly on the outer side of the problem, goes on also to the flood accounts contained in the ancient Indian books of the Rig Veda and others. There are great echoes there of the Chaldean tradition, even in the person of the hero; but, he says, “all these echoes of the Chaldean tradition, recognizable under manifold transformations, indicate probably that the tradition of the great event was carried hither, but not that the flood itself reached hither. The very fact that in the oldest of these accounts, in the Rig Veda, the rescued man Manu Vaivasvata fastens his ship to one of the high peaks of the Himalayas shows that the legend was imported from a foreign land and localized in a wholly unnatural manner.” The Chinese accounts go back, according to Sueß, probably only to inundations of the land by the great rivers shifting their courses, and also claim nothing other than to be simple historical records. “Free from all miracles, without the claim to a higher revelation, they relate the events as a rule in sober and definite language.” Individual missionaries have mistakenly wanted to find echoes of the biblical Flood in a great and devastating [p. 145] inundation, which may well be ascribed to the Ho, that broke out under the reign of the emperor Yao around 2357 B.C. For the rest, the Flood legends apparently fail completely in North and Central Asia, if one except Tibet; likewise the East Asian island world does not in general know them. Only in the Malayan world, thus also in Polynesia, are they there again. In the Buddhist writings of China they are said not to recur. “What is called Flood in China,” says Andree, “refers to quite natural, local and historically known events, to the inundations of the Hoangho”<sup>59</sup>.

<sup>59</sup> Andree, Flutsagen. p. 35/36.

It is remarkable how briefly and without justification Sueß brushes aside with a wave of the hand, or does not mention at all, the New World and Polynesian flood accounts, which are so striking because in essence and in many details they are so similar to the Chaldean ones, not to say identical with them; whereas in the Mesopotamian-Mediterranean region he becomes so detailed, even divides what partly agrees into four groups, and there also takes in the earthquakes mentioned in the prophet Amos, although there is no talk at all of floods in that passage, not even of sea floods. As his reason, however, for passing over the flood legends of the indigenous peoples of North America, he states that they are accompanied by such definite details drawn from the biblical account that the influence of the missionaries upon them is unmistakable<sup>60</sup>. In so far as the South American region of legend and the oceanic islands possess the flood account, such traditions have, as already remarked, in his view arisen independently from seaquake floods of the ocean.

<sup>60</sup> Th. Waitz, Anthropologie der Naturvölker. vol. III. Leipzig 1862, p. 187. The Oceanic flood legends are collected in: A. Réville. Les réligions des peuples non civilisés. vol. II. Paris 1887. (After E. Sueß.)

Is this, then, an exhaustive explanation of the flood legend? And has its essence thereby become in any way manifest?

[p. 146]

### Der Wesenskern des Sintflutereignisses — The Essential Core of the Flood Event

From this great explication of the Gilgamesh epic, undertaken by E. Sueß and seemingly bringing a solution to the ancient problem, we can at bottom only gather once more how, in the most various countries, as a result of later local catastrophes, an ancient legendary material handed down by tradition, or a world event extending over wide areas of continental dimensions, is variously elaborated. We found the like already in the old animal legends, the basilisks and dragons; it met us in the myth of the Cyclopes, and now we meet it again in the Flood.

Sueß has not brought a solution of the problem of the Flood. It seems to me quite incidental, however much importance Sueß attaches to it, whether two or still more traditions or basic accounts have been worked into one another in the Mosaic text or in the Babylonian; for in a certain core they are unified, and this core we want and must extract — also in similar legends of other regions and tribes of peoples — and we must disregard the garment in which they appear. But that is the reverse of the road Sueß took: he labors precisely to dissolve what is unified in the tradition in favor of the elaborations that clothe the core.

The problem of the Flood culminates in the fact that among so many peoples, demonstrably independent of any mutual influence or influence passing through third parties, an ancient and far-reaching memory has been preserved and handed down, whose core, often recurring and, wherever it recurs, always stepping into the foreground of the picture, was not in the first place incursions of the sea, but an immense atmospheric catastrophe, downpours of rain, thunderstorms and subterranean outpourings of water, which produced the flood. Not salt water, but rainwater! This is the criterion by which every explanation must be measured, by which the gold of knowledge must be separated from the fool’s silver of the all-too-manifold and assessed. For we truly have no [p. 147] right, merely out of scientific prejudice, to represent precisely what is particularly emphasized in the tradition as not present, and to impress on the core of the legend, arbitrarily, another essence, when it displays it as plainly as the Flood legend displays its weather catastrophe.

Again and again the flood accounts corresponding in sense to the Noachian one — in fundamental contrast to the sinking of Atlantis — bear unmistakably this mark of the meteoric flood of rain, joined with thunderstorm, in the first place. All these traditions evidently refer to the same event of very great extent, which has come down to us in the biblical retelling and in the Babylonian epic. Among the accounts that mean the same event as the Bible, that is, those that refer to that quite particular flood of rain, occurring only once, but over a great extent, in the age of primordial man — no matter whether they were influenced by the Chaldean account or were handed down quite independently — I therefore also reckon, contrary to Andree, a legend found among the blacks of Western Australia to the following effect<sup>61</sup>, that many years ago two tribes lived on the banks of a great river, on the north side the whites, on the south side the blacks. They intermarried, held festivals together and fought peacefully with one another. The whites were the stronger and had better weapons, so that they were superior to the blacks. That made them proud and they broke off intercourse with the blacks. This state of affairs lasted long; then one day it began to rain, and it poured and poured for months; the river overflowed its banks and the blacks had to withdraw inland. It took as long for the waters to run off as it had taken them to rise. But when the blacks wanted to return to their old hunting grounds, they found there, instead of the river, the sea, which had swallowed up their proud neighbors.

<sup>61</sup> Andree, Flutsagen. p. 55/56.

What induces me not to identify this legend with the Chaldean one as having flowed from the same source, but to regard it as an independent account, yet one concerning the same earth-historical event, and therefore to equate it in its core and essence with the Babylonian-biblical account, while separating it from that of similar legends of a cosmogonic character, for instance from [p. 148] Germanic mythology, is, first, again the decided emphasis on rain as the fundamental cause of the inundation, whereas the incursion of the sea appears only secondary and local; second, the clear allusion to the morally reprehensible in the life of those who perished, while those who remained good are saved — absolute identity of sense and content with the Chaldean legendary material, for all difference of garment and of naming, and undoubted origin from another continental region, where precisely the same world event could have run its course differently.

Among the Fiji islanders too, moreover, it is an offense against God because of which dark clouds gathered in the sky, out of which rain streamed down ceaselessly upon the earth. Settlements, hills and mountains, everything was gradually inundated. The rebels as well, entrenched on the highest heights, were finally seized by the waters and in utmost distress called upon a god, who built them a vessel in which the survivors saved themselves until the waters ran off. The legend is certainly, as even Andree thinks, original. And yet it quite unmistakably touches the biblical event again, and by its whole essence thinks of no flood of the sea in the first place. This is all the more striking as it must accordingly be of continental and not of insular origin, which will find its explanation later.

So everywhere, and indeed in widespread accounts that we can trace from the land of the Chaldeans to the Indian Ocean and from there to Polynesia, in order to find them again, identical in sense, still farther east, in America — everywhere it is, again and again, the mighty flood from heaven, then the groundwater, and only occasionally and secondarily the sea, from which the Flood sprang. But what did Sueß make of it? He simply denies the natural-scientific justification of precisely this so essential content of the ancient tradition and, after this original sin against the spirit of the legend, loses himself in explications of its outward garment. “It must,” he says in the first lines of his exposition, “be maintained from the very beginning that in such great floods atmospheric precipitation can have only a subordinate share. By the whole manner of its origin it cannot exceed [p. 149] a certain measure; in its most violent forms it remains spatially limited, and it runs off by following the gradient of the valleys. Incomparably more powerful are the floods caused by cyclones, and the most extensive are those caused by earthquakes.” So much respect has the superior natural scientist of the nineteenth century for the ancient knowledge of humanity! In so far, then, as the flood legends of Chaldean character — the reader now understands how the word is meant — refer to the same world event, this was quite unmistakably an immense and unique flood of rain and in the first place not an incursion of the sea. Only with the catastrophic process hurling the immense masses of water from the ocean of air upon the earth did there then occur also tremors of the ground, violent earthquakes and probably also inner-continental as well as marginal subsidences and breakings-off, in which fissures of particular depth tore open, such as arise, to a lesser degree, even in present-day earthquakes when these have a certain violence, at which the groundwater can escape.

If one proceeds, then, to an explanation which, as one sees, must be fundamentally different from that of the sinking of Atlantis, then, for all the elaboration of the Flood legend in detail, light must be thrown above all upon the entirely unambiguous account it contains of an unusual, unique flood of rain, joined with some other natural processes, partly ordinary, partly unusual, such as earthquakes, the bursting open of the groundwaters, the sinking of land into the sea. We have before us a unique natural event, which occurred, if not over the whole earth, yet in a great living space that therefore certainly did not behave quite uniformly in the catastrophe, and which therefore adhered firmly in the memory of humanity, an event that for that very reason cannot be explained from local accidents, however catastrophic. For local catastrophes do not cling in the memory of a whole world, but at most in that of the affected tribe, and there often not even for long. Sueß himself tells us of many, many such local catastrophes of the most terrible kind, and yet the news of them hardly reaches the next neighboring people, and certainly is not passed on over half the globe, and still less is told through millennia [p. 150] with ever the same freshness, held fast with ever the same tenacity. But they promptly serve to revive old, firmly rooted legends reaching up out of the deepest past, to imprint on them a new living color and with it the coloring of a local event, especially when, under the impression and on the occasion of what has newly been lived through that is terrible, they are poetically felt through and elaborated anew.

In proof of this enveloping of the ancient Flood legend in recent local events I set two American versions of the legend side by side, of which the one is evidently the more original, the other, however, the later one, revived by catastrophes of sea floods and cast in a more recent garment of intuition.

The Sac and Fox Indians, a tribe of the Canadian Algonquins, relate: At the creation of men, subterranean gods fought against the god of the upper world. When they could accomplish nothing against him, they turned to the mighty Thunder God and begged him to let a great flood of water come upon the earth. All the clouds of the world came together, so that the sky was black and the rain plunged down in drops as big as wigwams, covering the earth up to the highest mountains, to which the god of light had fled. To save himself he built a roomy canoe in which he and his animals found comfortable room. When he had drifted about on the water for some days, he untied one of his largest fishes, bade it fetch earth from the deep, and made from it the dry land, which his red children inhabit to this day<sup>62</sup>. This is the genuine Flood legend.

<sup>62</sup> Andree, ibid., p. 80.

The other legend, preserved by the Makah Indians (Washington Terr.) and to be set against the former, runs, according to the same source: Long ago, though not too remotely, the waters of the Pacific Ocean poured over the land which is now occupied by the swamps and prairies between the village of Waatch and Neah Bay, so that Cape Flattery formed an island. The water suddenly withdrew, so that the bay stood dry. After four days the water had reached its lowest level; then it rose again without waves or surf, until the cape was under water, and the whole land too, except for a few mountain peaks. The water, as it rose, was very [p. 151] warm. Those who had canoes saved themselves in them with their belongings and drifted northward with a current. When the waters had resumed their usual level, a part of the tribe was found beyond Nootka, where its descendants still live now.

Here the old Flood legend appears wholly saturated and wrapped up in the late, self-experienced event of the tribe, which Swan, who recorded this tradition, brings into connection with volcanic forces. It can hardly have been volcanic upheavals and subsidences of the land itself, however; for these could not have taken place with such rapidity without earthquakes of the most violent kind, of which the legend reports nothing; nor would the sea water have grown warm thereby; rather, a volcanic seat will have opened outside the coast on the sea floor, with a mighty initial sinking and formation of fissures, which sucked in the water, which made itself directly noticeable on the coast before the leveling-out was restored by the ocean surface flowing in after it. Hand in hand with this, mighty magmatic masses issued submarinely, heating the water locally and later solidifying on the floor of the sea, without making themselves, or their vapor, immediately condensed again in the water, noticeable above on the land.

One could imagine that the people on the Ganges and Brahmaputra, if they possessed an authentic Flood legend, would have enveloped it in their experiences not just once but ten times in the course of the centuries and so handed it on; or that the Egyptians on the Red Sea, if they had heard or possessed the genuine Chaldean Flood legend, would have woven the event of the Exodus of Israel into it and promptly localized the legend on the Red Sea. Into the explication of such purely external moments, partly very late in time, partly wholly incidental, and of images and additions of fiction, the explanation of the Flood by E. Sueß has strayed, and has therefore remained remote from the essence of the legend.

It must be especially emphasized that in the case of a world event, to which alone we should restrict the name Flood in the Chaldean sense, the most various original versions, with and without incursion of the sea, with and without [p. 152] lasting sinking of continental land, with and without annihilation of “all” men, could arise, indeed had to arise, as soon as this event possessed an extent not merely local but continental, and therefore struck simultaneously regions with the most various coastal and land configuration, with the most various river systems and weather tracks. Then there had to come into being a legend, in its innermost core unified, meaning the same event, widespread, clinging in everyone’s memory because it assumed precisely a continental extent, but for that very reason also supported and filled from the start with the most various outward processes, such as volcanic eruptions, earthquakes, shifting of rivers, incursions of the sea, which were not everywhere the same; especially as the peoples too who experienced it directly did not all have one and the same spiritual and psychic constitution with which they beheld, survived and worked up such an event.

Fig. 22. Schematic sketch of the position of Gondwanaland in the Permian period. In the north the Asiatic Angaraland. The present boundaries of land did not yet exist. (Original.)

We have now, in the region of the Indian Ocean and in its continental marginal areas, immense fracture systems of the earth’s crust, [p. 153] along which both vertical and horizontal continental displacements have taken place in a time not too remote in earth-historical terms, in particular in the late Mesozoic and in the Tertiary period, and are even today not wholly extinct. Constant earthquakes and local incursions of the sea have been in this the very least of the accompanying phenomena. The fracture systems are fissures, which the dissolution of a great continent, taking up the area of the Indian Ocean in Palaeozoic times, the Gondwana continent (Fig. 22), followed, and which finally broke up in the late Mesozoic (Fig. 23), whether it disappeared by subsidences to oceanic depth, as Sueß thought, or by the drifting apart of the Indian, Australian and Madagascan blocks, or by a combination of both processes.

Fig. 23. Schematic sketch of the island areas after the break-up of Gondwanaland at the end of the Cretaceous period. The present boundaries of land did not yet exist. (Original.)

Most grandly the remainder appears in the East African fracture field, where only after the disappearance of the more easterly Gondwana continent an immense geotectonic line of movement led to the subsidence of the East African marginal region from the Zambezi in the south to the Red Sea in the north. To this subsidence [p. 154] the lake basins of Victoria Nyanza, Lake Nyasa and Lake Rudolf also owe their origin. The marginal line of the East African escarpment and of the Abyssinian highland is identical with this fault fissure of the earth’s crust. By chance the eastern wing of the system has not yet sunk deep enough to grant the sea permanent entry; on the contrary, the coast there is even rising again of late. But farther north, in the Red Sea, we have the trench subsidence before us so penetratingly chiseled out that here the ocean water has penetrated between Arabia and northeastern Africa. The fault system continues and has created in Palestine the Jordan valley and the Dead Sea. The Dead Sea lies several hundred meters below the ideal level of the sea surface; it is strongly salty, but not through inflow of oceanic water, but merely through the evaporation of the inflowing land water, which is probably connected in part with the naphtha and asphalt springs there. Sodom and Gomorrah, Lot’s wife as a pillar of salt, may by the way recall the nature of that field, so significant geotectonically. There too, as in East Africa, it is only a chance that the sea, when the Dead Sea basin broke down, did not penetrate as into the long-drawn fracture basin of the Red Sea, where at its southern end, between Africa and Arabia, the free ocean was crossed by the fracture system. Had the Palestinian trench subsidence by chance forked out a little farther toward the Mediterranean, or had the line between the Red and the Dead Sea turned out somewhat deeper, then in Palestine too, instead of the Jordan valley and an inland lake, an arm of the sea of the character of the Red Sea would have arisen as its direct continuation. And how easily it might have been that today the Indian Ocean would reach up to the East African escarpment, now set with volcanoes, and that Kilimanjaro and Kenya would have appeared as volcanic islands in this marginal sea, if precisely in the late Tertiary, when the breaking-off from the Zambezi to the Jordan was accomplished, the subsidence in East Africa had gone a few hundred meters deeper, as in the Dead Sea region.

Let us now imagine that such a thing took place swiftly; to right and to left, more or less distributed at the time of such movement, men had dwelt. They would all, even if they belonged to [p. 155] so many heterogeneous peoples with the most various languages, of the most various psychic and cultural height, indeed often without acquaintance with one another, and had perhaps been separated by impassable deserts or volcanic mountains or primeval forests — they would all report in agreement of mighty earthquakes, of fissures that opened toward the depth, of inundations by rivers deflected, dammed up and deprived of their accustomed bed, of waters of the deep that poured forth as warm or cold springs, of volcanic eruptions. According to the region where now the one, now the other appeared as the foremost phenomenon, this or that motif of nature would press into the foreground in the tradition. But Others too would know how to tell that the sea was there when, after the flight, they wanted to return to their old hunting grounds; that this sea had swallowed whole countries together with the inhabitants, animals and plants. Others again would report that they themselves saw how, with the first earthquakes, the floods of the sea already broke in, but afterward withdrew again. All this would meet us in a legend spread from the Zambezi to the border of Syria, extending over two continents, native to the soil and in essence wholly unified, but with deviations in the many details of incursions of the sea, volcanoes, hot springs, outflows of groundwater, formation of fissures, destruction of tribes — that is, in a legend that would quite resemble the flood legends of Chaldean character, if — yes, if with it only the dreadful inundation by the immense masses of rain from heaven, that is, the principal piece of our Noachian Flood legend, were also included. That, however, would be wholly lacking in the earth-historical process described, as it is lacking in Sueß’s explanation<sup>62a</sup>.

<sup>62a</sup> So as not to be exposed to a misunderstanding criticism, let it be stressed that the breaking up of the African continent is not itself to be drawn upon as an explanation of the Flood, but is only to serve as an example of what different results the same continental movement can produce and over how large areas it can extend. Region and name have nothing to do with the matter here.

Riem sought to remedy this deficiency<sup>63</sup> and to give a geological explanation in which not only the immense meteoric precipitation but also the universality of the event would become intelligible. In earlier earth-historical time the earth’s own heat is supposed to have warmed the ground through the then much thinner crust, so that far more water than today evaporated and remained suspended perpetually in the atmosphere as a cloud cover, comparable to the planet Venus. The [p. 156] sunlight penetrated not directly but only in scattered form through the white cloud cover, and the earth had the character of a covered, dimly lit hothouse. Thereby a uniform warming of all zones up to the very poles was ensured, and the finds of luxuriant fossil floras and marine faunas as far into the high Arctic regions prove this. The balance between warmth of the air, warmth of the earth and moisture content did not remain stable. More and more of the earth’s warmth was given off through the veil of cloud to cold space and not replaced. Finally the earth’s crust was so cooled that the evaporation of water declined, that the cloud cover became thinner and a catastrophic event was now initiated. At some place, perhaps within a polar region which was just passing through its half-year night, the cooling reached the lowest permissible limit and passed beyond it; the clouds began to condense, to tear apart, and through the opening the warmth radiated still more strongly outward, so that through this decrease of temperature the raining began. This process must have spread with tearing swiftness over the whole globe, and everywhere the clouds began to pour down water, destroying and laying waste everything. To a great extent the water content of the air renewed itself at once; with the diminishing pressure of the air the atmosphere could take up ever new quantities of water, and the rain received ever new nourishment. It may have rained for months; there followed an inundation over the whole earth, which destroyed river courses, washed the soil from the mountains, altered the shape of the surface of the lands and killed all life that was not strong enough to withstand the enraged element or to save itself on high mountains, in caves. This event was not a flood, it was the Flood of which the oldest traditions of the human race speak.

<sup>63</sup> J. Riem, Die Sündflut. Eine ethnographisch-naturwissenschaftliche Untersuchung. In: Christentum und Zeitgeist, Heft 9. Stuttgart 1906.

What constitutes an advantage in Riem’s explanation over that of E. Sueß is the clear answer to the origin of the rain catastrophe. What makes his explanation impossible, however, is the earth-historical dating that he gives to this constructed rain event. He places it at the close of the Tertiary period, where he rightly already surmises man. [p. 157] The event, as he depicts it, would have been geophysically possible only at a time when the inner heat of the earth really still warmed the ground through the crust. But that cannot already have been the case at the beginning of the Palaeozoic era, because already in that oldest epoch, offering animal life, we know great ice coverings as well, which repeat themselves in the Permian period, where we at the same time have on the northern hemisphere the clearest signs of a very dry and hot climate, interrupted again by downpours of rain to be sure, so that the hypothetical Venus-like veil of cloud and the hothouse certainly did not exist as a permanent condition and over the whole earth in the earth-historical time somewhat surveyable for us. To place the existence of man and the Flood in quite ancient, pre-Palaeozoic times is out of the question. Only one thing would remain that could save the principle of Riem’s explanation as an interpretation of the Flood: to assume temporarily such strong cloud formation in the Tertiary period, where in fact luxuriant plant growth, pointing to a more southerly climate, has been demonstrated on land areas that are today covered with polar ice; the end of this geologically temporary condition, perhaps conditioned by greater solar warmth, could then occasionally have brought about a great rain catastrophe, such as Riem constructs, which gave occasion to a kind of Flood and which Tertiary man would have lived through with it.

Whether this interpretation is admissible, the future will teach, once the question treated here, of a relation of the legends to a human race of the former world, has been better grounded. For the present another explanation seems to me to come closer to the essence of that prehistoric natural event.

[p. 158]

### Die kosmische Erklärung der noachitischen Sintflut — The Cosmic Explanation of the Noachian Flood

A great thought, perhaps one that recasts the well-shaped system of a science or the philosophy and worldview of an age, almost always steps into the light in the mind of an investigator with a single stroke. It may well have stirred here and there, in another connection or concealed and misunderstood, whatever the thought brings: there, where it radiates, gaining life and movement, it is born in a moment. So report all investigators who have overheard their genius at work. Often it is only a small, as it were accidental occasion, though prepared by long labor, that decides the birth of the thought. It seizes the thinker immediately, without his having known, willed, or divined it at that moment. It falls to him “like scales from his eyes.” All his particular knowledge, all the images that had already surfaced in him here and there, been weighed, been judged unripe or as yet unshapable by him, often seemingly lost to him again or deliberately set aside again, suddenly shoot together into a new, unheard-of vision. In this wholly exalted moment he looks into an endless bright expanse, where the dead becomes living to him, the distant near and tangible, what was never understood familiar, and a great certainty fills him. He “knows.” Then he must return to everyday work; he must cast the free thought in solid bronze. There comes the distress of building out and of rational grounding, of self-negation and self-contradiction, faith and doubt, questioning and knowing — the thought becomes a system and now stands in the midst of the strife of life and of science, burdened with all the faults and deficiencies of human handiwork.

In his surroundings the investigator now sees all manner of waves arise: some of the same rhythm as his own, which bear him up, indeed combine with his own to double strength and height; others which swing similarly enough, but have a different fundamental [p. 159] direction and therefore chime fully into his rhythm only partially and occasionally, for moments, then cross it again and form unharmonious wave-crests. Still others call forth only unharmonious images of motion and cross-waves, constantly disturbing and paralyzing his vibrations as he does theirs; the crests and points of crossing spray up and form only foam, which stands in contrast to the deep green of the waves with their equal heights and depths. But all of them together form the wide, wide sea and beat upon the unmoving rocky land, conquering only little of it in all the centuries of their surging and struggling.

Often a new, great thought remains solitary. Perhaps it sprang from a head that lacked the scientific equipment to clothe it in the language of those who have the advantage over him in the possession of larger, more comprehensive specialist knowledge. He has seen what is right; but the grounding, and otherwise the knowledge of facts or the calculations, do not suffice to give his thought the secure foundation of a good scientific theory and to let other minds share in the same vision. Or he steps with his insight between two more or less well-delimited fields of knowledge, without mastering either so well that he could do justice to the specialist in each; then both sides reject him. Or finally, he has brought something so great, or so far turned away from the knowledge of the time, that it becomes assimilable to hardly any of the known materials of knowledge, or able to throw light on them; then the fairly unanimous judgment of the authoritative world of specialists will be, all the more, a rejection. And yet a new idea, while it leads a solitary, misjudged Cinderella existence, can be the king’s daughter sought by all and have had ancestors who dwelt in the light. If one compares her features with the old ancestral portraits, one notices with surprise whom one has before one, even without those at the table having recognized and tolerated her there. They must first let themselves be instructed by the old ancestral portraits that are held up to them as to whom they thrust out. And while the some, the reflective, now fall to thinking, the others, and always the majority, will call into doubt the authenticity of the old ancestral portraits themselves, decry them as mistaken, substituted, [p. 160] painted over, or as worthless, freely invented personifications of natural forces without historical value — until the truth, at some time and in some way, even without further recourse to the ancestral portraits, shows its own vigorous life.

Hörbiger’s glacial cosmogony<sup>64</sup> is such an unheard-of insight, to which everything applies that the foregoing words say. It has also taught us to understand the Flood and resembles ancient accounts of the heavenly waters flowing in. Let it be set forth here as an ingenious idea — regardless of whether it is expertly beyond reproach in all its astronomical details, which are not to be decided here — because it signifies a solution for our own search and rises far above everything that has ever been thought and written about the Flood from the naturalist’s standpoint. To be sure, it too makes the old mistake that is peculiar to most scientific theories won from one great stroke: they will not restrict themselves to the section of world-events for which they were intuitively born, but are all too easily overstretched by the master or his pupils and extended to things for which they received neither measure nor content. That, however, should not and will not keep us from bringing the new cosmogony into the light within the field of phenomena belonging to it here, even if in some direction it proves an exaggeration, occasionally even makes hair-raising digressions, but nevertheless remains grand as a whole, as an idea, and, like every ingenious deed, works redemptively, clarifyingly, liberatingly. Its train of thought, important for us though greatly simplified here, is briefly as follows:

<sup>64</sup> P. Fauth, Hörbigers Glazialkosmogonie (see note 47). For an introduction to the structure of ideas of the very prolix, if in content grand, work, better suited and also used here is: H. Voigt, Eis, ein Weltbaustoff. Einführung in Fauth-Hörbigers Glazialkosmogonie (mit Atlas). Berlin-Wilmersdorf 1920. H. Fischer, Die Wunder des Welteises. Berlin-Wilmersdorf 1922. The doctrine has so far been examined with particular care neither by astronomical nor by geological specialist science, let alone recognized; I myself got to know it only after working through my ideas, and took over from it what stands in the text. I am not in a position to represent the mathematician and astronomer; but from the earth-historical standpoint some objections can indeed be raised against details of the doctrine, not against its overall idea. Thus the “Ice Age” is a concept that is very much misunderstood; the immense ice covering depicted did not exist. If one draws a map of the earth for the Ice Age in an equal-area projection, that is, not in the usual Mercator projection (cf. the map by F. Levy in E. Dacqué, Grundlagen und Methoden der Paläogeographie, Jena 1915), one notices what a weak phenomenon the diluvial ice sheet was, practically not noticeable at all for the more southerly countries. Catastrophic inundations likewise did not occur in southern countries since the end of the Tertiary; we see, for example, in Egypt and the adjoining Sahara, where precisely the stratifications of the geological past since the Early Tertiary are excellently known, no trace of such catastrophic or marine eroding action as the glacial cosmogony with its hypothetical Tertiary-Diluvial capture of the moon demands. Its deductive application is simply not so easy. The geological formations are neither, except in limited places, sharply separated petrographically or temporally, and so are not earth-historical periods universally divided by interruptions; nor have the marine displacements of the former world ever proceeded uniformly on both hemispheres; nor do they throughout have anything catastrophic; nor are the formations of mountains unique paroxysmal processes, but for the most part extend over longer periods of the earth, even though at times they matured to quite special violence. The fossils too have arisen not throughout, but only occasionally and locally, through washing together. It is just here that the glacial cosmogony, in its present formulation, makes the grave mistake of throwing together the ages and animal worlds of earth history instead of keeping them chronologically and neatly separated; yet that would be the immovable, only secure foundation for any research into the former world, be it earth-historical or cosmological or phylogenetic or concerned with the history of man and of legends. Of the catastrophic extinction of animal worlds asserted there, too, nothing is to be noticed in earth history, except in narrowly bounded regions. It is furthermore quite incorrect that the individual geological periods or epochs would be characterized by an animal world repeatedly and completely dying out; on the contrary. Not only the great majority of the stem-types but also many special generic forms pass over from one epoch into the other, and where we can directly observe the closed sequence of strata from one age into the other, there is not throughout any question of a sharp faunistic-floristic dividing line at all. That the Hörbigerian doctrine, however, brings and will bring quite unsuspected astrophysical, as well as cosmological and earth-historical prospects and insights, the near future will surely prove. For research into earth history stands in a crisis, where it feels that with the older catastrophe-free Anglo-Saxon doctrine of transformation (Lyell) not much more is to be got out of the past. A way out of this narrowness will be found above all by a renewed taking in of cosmic influences on the aging planet. The struggle over the new doctrine is in the midst of becoming and every decision is premature. A detailed refutation by specialist scholars is attempted in a collective volume published by the “Bund der Sternfreunde,” “Weltentwicklung und Welteislehre” (Verlag Die Sterne, Potsdam 1925). It is, as with all emerging scientific theories, impossible to arrive at a conclusive judgment. However mistaken many details are presented on both sides, and however justified every critical examination of new ideas is, one must nonetheless, as was already stressed in the 1st and 2nd editions, well distinguish the truth content of every theory from its insufficient elaboration into a doctrine. For in the end it has always been the seers in a science who placed knowledge on a new ground, on which the individual research afterward again had to plow for a long time. Were the glacial cosmogony, after its present exaggerations, to confine itself to developing its central idea of cosmic supplies in the form of fine ice, veil ice, ice bolides and planetoid bodies, and to founding this earth-historically and also astrophysically, then it could in fact offer a full explanation for weighty and unsolved geological problems, such as the question of the permanence of continents and oceans, of the ice ages and of climatic change, indeed perhaps of the formation of fold mountains, of earthquakes and of the periodicity of volcanism, as it has already conveyed to us in principle a general explanation of many legend-images and above all of the event of the Flood.

In cosmic space countless fixed stars move, of a magnitude often so astonishing that the ball of the sun, indeed the whole space occupied by our planetary system, may be small beside it. White-hot, red-hot, faintly luminous and dark giants may be there and are there, partly circling one another closely and forming common systems, partly so far from one another that they no longer influence each other by their gravity. Of many such systems the proper motion through space, independent of the others, has already been established. The sun itself moves at a speed of 20 km a second toward the constellation of the Lyre and of Hercules. If fixed stars rush in [p. 161] a definite path through space toward unknown goals, if our solar system flees toward Lyra and Hercules, this need not be the effect of a gravitation; for where should the giant body stand that exerted gravitational action over such distances, which after all stands in inverse square relation to distance? But it may well be an impulsive force imparted to the solar system an infinitely long time ago, with which it speeds aimlessly toward its unfelt goal.

As the planets their moons, as the sun its planets, so too the bright and extinguished suns united in a system, and their giant planets, attract one another. We know that many luminous fixed-star suns have their dark companions. These may be formerly independent but now extinguished suns, which in their course were caught in the orbit of a still white-hot giant sun and from then on had to follow it. The dark companion may have on its surface ice or water as the precipitate of the once glowing hydrogen, which on the cooling of the body condensed with its oxygen into water; but the water may also be contained, chemically bound, in the rocks of its crust and of its interior.

The larger a world-body is and the denser its material, the more inner solidity it has, which already in our not very dense earth exceeds that of steel threefold. If a larger world-body is captured by a giant sun and plunges rapidly into the central body, it need by no means be drawn asunder and dispersed like a small moon, but, given the great swiftness with which this must happen and its considerable solidity, it can plunge in as a whole.
