If one brings a piece of ice into the glow of a blast furnace, it does not fizzle away instantly, nor is it immediately dissolved entirely into steam, but after a brief hissing it surrounds itself with a crust of slag, which isolates it and substantially holds back the penetration of heat into the body of ice. So it melts only slowly into water; but the water too does not turn at once to steam in the mantle of glow, but enters into delayed boiling. Thereafter, at the slightest disturbance of pressure and equilibrium in the surrounding mass, it explodes with enormous tension and works the greatest destruction.

[p. 162] If now a world-body of a certain solidity has been shot into a still white-hot fixed-star sun, it penetrates, according to its weight, its speed, and the kind of resistance of the solar body, to a certain depth in it. It is not dissolved at once, but arrives at self-isolation, and the water contained or chemically bound in it is set free. Thus there arises in that solar body an explosive bomb of greatly superheated water and steam, and finally it comes to the catastrophic explosion. A funnel-shaped piece of the solar body shoots out, amid all sorts of accompanying phenomena, in a straight line and speeds away, consisting of its own melted-in matter and of the solar matter torn along with it, into cosmic space. Masses hurled out sideways in the process fall back upon the solar body, which must create a new equilibrium within itself, surging up and down in the process. The main mass shot out, however, under the enormous centrifugal force imparted to it continues its way into cosmic space and leaves more and more the sphere of attraction of the mother body. But since the primal solar body was in rotation, the ejection-motion of the new world-mass too was not absolutely rectilinear, but had the figure of a mass of sand which one scatters over the ground with a swung shovel. Soon it turned about its own center of gravity. This mass had thus gained its direction in cosmic space not through gravitation, but through impulsive force and the inertia of flight inhering in it.

All this is a cosmological construction, like the Kant–Laplace theory of the becoming of worlds, so long authoritative and still held by many. But the impulsive force is no less far-reaching than Newtonian gravitation, which after all is taken only from the space of our so small-dimensioned planetary system, but is practically ineffective in the wide intersolar space. Hörbiger’s theory has a physical foundation, hitherto unheeded by the astronomers, taken from blast-furnace technology. But beyond this the world of the fixed stars supplies further grounds for this new doctrine. For there are speeding fixed stars whose direction of motion, prolonged backward through space, leads back to a common point of departure — the opposite of the Newtonian notion of order in the universe.

[p. 163] Does our solar system too come from such a point of departure, and does it owe its earliest origin to such an explosion hurling it away?

On the firing of a gun the gases pushing out have at first a greater speed than the projectile; only afterward does the latter gain the lead. So it may well be also with a solar explosion. Since cosmic space contains only finely distributed matter, in particular very rarefied gaseous hydrogen flowed off from solar bodies, the friction is slight, and the main mass bearing the center of gravity remains far behind the gases speeding ahead. As soon as the new system has withdrawn from the sphere of gravitational force of the sun of departure, it runs on, quite unlike a terrestrial projectile, only according to its own inner relations of gravity and motion. Although it thus scarcely meets with resistance, the friction, delicate in itself, against the finely distributed cosmic matter will, owing to the great speed and endless duration of action, gradually brake the gases speeding ahead, and the main complex following, by virtue of its greater living force, overcoming friction better, will gradually begin to overtake the parts that sped ahead at first. Meanwhile a formation has also been accomplished within the whole system itself, owing to the force of gravitation active in it. The new world-mass had previously been altogether irregular and unequal in weight. Masses and clouds of gas speeding ahead sideways will gradually have taken another direction than the denser, heavier parts, in particular than the superordinate center of gravity of the mass itself. But other, denser complexes and agglomerations too, which themselves mean only subordinate quantities, will at first be drawn toward the center of mass and now begin to revolve. Hereby arises a multitude of revolving bodies that again disturb, weaken, intercept one another, or reinforce one another into common force. So through selection there come to be new, more ordered accumulations of mass with more definite orbits. They enlarge themselves further by capturing the weaker, the less ordered, and finally revolve about the main center of mass. This capturing, now in full swing, set the subcenters in rotation at the same time, [p. 164] just as the main center too got into rotation in this way.

When the originally dark, captured world-body had plunged into the solar giantess and was being melted in, it stood under enormous pressure. It is an experience of metallurgical chemistry that glowing and molten masses of metal under pressure bind much oxygen to themselves; relieved of pressure, they expel it again. As soon as the new world-system had been shot out into space, its matter was therefore relieved of pressure; and the oxygen, freed in great quantity, could at once combine directly into steam with the hydrogen present in abundance and, moreover, torn along from the corona of the solar mother, as soon as the cooling had advanced only a little. Thus the whole projectile mass was at once immersed in a vapor envelope. This envelope, however, in contrast to the denser materials, had to be pressed most easily to the edges of the now rotating system; and so it came out of the heat into contact with cold space. That was the place and the moment where the first fine ice, the first ice-dust, formed in the periphery of the plane of rotation of the new world-system. Agglomerations, ice-agglomerations, could soon arise here too, partly even with firmer cores of matter. This marginal zone naturally at first still took part in the rotation of the whole, but was pushed slowly away more and more by the outflow of new quantities of vapor and their speeding away into cosmic space, and thus ever more withdrawn from the gravity of the rest, especially as the pressure of radiation too on the mass, for the most part still finely distributed, sought to expand it toward cosmic space and push it away, so that it hardly condensed.

The rotary motion of this marginal ice-matter had in the end to flag through friction against the most finely distributed matter pervading cosmic space, and probably also through the resistance of the ether. If, then, the whole original projectile-cone had, through what had meanwhile taken place in it, passed over into lens form, the marginal ring of the lens now gradually came to a stand and was caught up more by the center in the former direction of flight. If our solar system, speeding through space and long since ordered, owes its becoming to such processes, then we have [p. 165] in it, indeed, the completed image of those world-creating events: a planetary system circling about a center of mass, of the following structure: nearest the center of mass the inner planets consisting of solar matter; farther out the outer planets built up of solar matter as well as meteoric material, on whose surface ice has accumulated. Outside the planetary orbits ice-agglomerations (planetoids) circle, and from them come the comets, which are bodies of ice. There follows a gap and then, at a distance of 4—5 Neptune-distances, the no longer revolving cometary Milky Way ring, consisting only of ice-dust and ice-bodies, from which the shooting stars well forth that also enter our atmosphere. It is that faintly luminous broad band of the heavens, brighter in some places than in others, whereas the sidereal Milky Way consists of fixed stars and is not meant here.

The plane of the ice Milky Way moved at the time of its origin in the same direction as its main center, the sun. The sun, owing to its greater and heavier mass, is more enduring against the resistance of the medium than the fine Milky Way, parts of which even lag behind. The latter will therefore strive to set itself perpendicular to the direction of flight, which has already happened by 15°. Consequently the sun has apparently stepped out of the ring and finds itself nearer the inner edge of the ring on the left in the direction of travel than the right, which already lies deep “below” it. Since the sun slowly pushes its sphere of gravity in the direction of orbit of the whole system toward the parts and particles of the Milky Way ring gradually lagging behind, more or fewer of them are drawn ever more into the planetary system. These ice-bodies caught up will then plunge directly into the sun if they come on the shortest path; those sweeping in from the side will first move in sharply curved orbits around the sun and be guided in only gradually. According to the different manner of the entering and drawing-in of these large and small down to smallest bodies, an ice-veil forms about the ball of the sun, which the earth crosses and in which, according to the direction of origin, the size, the speed, the mutually disturbing deflections or attractions and the influence of the planets, a separation and [p. 166] sorting is accomplished. According to the position of the planets, these, when the ice-bodies have come into the spell of the sun, have already drawn larger and smaller ones to themselves, swallowed them at once or occasionally made them into moons. For the earth, for certain reasons, only smaller ones come into consideration, only seldom perhaps a large one. They are the causes of hailstorms and other weather catastrophes. When they pass by above the air-space and escape again from the sphere of attraction of the earth, we see them as shooting stars. But if such a body enters the earth’s atmosphere, it bursts at once owing to the resistance of the air and dissolves, through the effect of heat as well, into water, which now comes down as catastrophic thunder-rain, partly mixed with hail. If the ice-bodies and masses of ice-dust arriving pile up, or if such a body is exceptionally large, they can bring about not only weather catastrophes of greater extent but also “open the floodgates of heaven,” so that it rains endlessly for seven or more days and nights, the sun remains darkened and fearful thunderstorms rage, perhaps mixed with earthquakes, which, after all, can by experience be set off by strong changes of atmospheric pressure in any case. The equatorial zone would be exposed to this most severely, because the larger ice-bodies are in general drawn into the plane of rotation of the equator.

Would that not be an event such as Noachian man experienced? Here we should have an explanation of the enormous meteoric masses of water falling upon the earth, which Eduard Sueß asserted could not be possible — in spite of the clear ancient account of the Flood. But Hörbiger’s theory goes further and probably too far: it explains the Flood through the dissolution of an earlier moon of the earth.

The surface of the present moon is supposed to be a sea of ice through a former uptake of water from cosmic space; the forms of the moon’s surface and the specific gravity of the lunar body do not contradict this. The moon is supposed to be drawn into the earth in the course of time, dissolved thereby into a spiral ring, and so consumed bit by bit by the earth. First, then, enormous floods of water would again come down into the earth’s atmosphere through the dissolution of the ice. The more the [p. 167] ice mantle was consumed and the deeper stone zone of the lunar body dissolved, the more mud-rains, and thereafter cosmic rock-dust, would fall; last, probably, metallic substances, since the moon too, like the earth, would presumably have strong accumulations of metal in its innermost part. Then would be fulfilled what the Revelation of John foresees, and describes in many details, as the downfall of the present surface of the earth and of its human race. As our present-world moon has an ice mantle and is gradually dissolved by the attraction of the earth and later united with the body of the earth, so an earlier moon, it is said, was drawn out into a ring-shaped spiral and incorporated its ice as water, its stone core as mud-pulp, into the earth. Through the then lunar body coming ever nearer beforehand, however, an enormous equatorial ring of oceanic flood had, owing to the force of attraction, gathered, which after the dissolution of the satellite and the vanishing of the lunar attraction previously given by it, flowed off again poleward on both sides and thereby produced the Noachian Flood with weather catastrophes, earthquakes, and inundations by the sea<sup>65</sup>.

<sup>65</sup> This doctrine contradicts to a certain degree, though not throughout, a theory more recognized in science, that of the English astronomer G. H. Darwin (“Ebbe und Flut, sowie verwandte Erscheinungen im Sonnensystem”. Transl. by A. Pockels. 2nd ed. Berlin und Leipzig 1911), according to which the moon once left the earth, has since circled it in an ever-widening spiral orbit, thus recedes from it, but is not drawn into it. The mechanism consists, according to Darwin, in the following: the tidal bulge of the sea produced by the moon on the earth is constantly torn ahead by the rotation of the globe, which proceeds faster than a lunar revolution; it thus does not stand opposite the moon but runs ahead of it, and so seeks to pull the moon retroactively forward. Hereby an acceleration is imparted to the moon, which slowly drives it centrifugally away from the earth, so that its orbit around the earth widens more and more spirally. Despite the acceleration thus imparted to the moon, its period of revolution around the earth becomes ever longer, because its orbital curve widens. On the other hand, however, reciprocally, the tidal bulge of the sea is drawn back by the lagging moon against the rotation of the earth and therefore continually brakes the earth’s rotation. Both things, the acceleration of the moon and the reduction of the earth’s rotation, increase until a lunar revolution = one earth day. The earth day, which has become very much longer, then amounts to = the time of one lunar revolution, or, expressed quite generally: the month lasts one day, the day one month. But then stability has entered into the earth-moon system. For whereas today it is still as if the moon were drawn along behind the earth on an ever more stretching rubber band, according to Darwin both celestial bodies in the final state behave as if they were joined to one another by a rigid, inflexible band and revolved with each other stably like the outer and inner part of a wheel. According to the teaching of Fauth-Hörbiger, however, the common center of gravity of the earth-moon system still lies within the body of the earth, and the earth therefore draws in the moon, which stands unstably outside it. For the two bodies fall, so to speak, toward each other (A. Prey, C. Mainka, E. Tams, Einführung in die Geophysik. Naturw. Monographien u. Lehrbücher. vol. 4, Berlin 1922, p. 86ff.), and that would have to turn the spiral path of the moon toward the earth, especially since the tidal action on the moon is, by comparison, too slight to bring about the opposite, in Darwin’s sense. This argument is decisive for the capture theory. Yet it must be further considered whether, with the gradual approach of the moon, Darwin’s effect does not set in again and become ever more powerful the nearer the moon comes to the earth. For at an approach to 1/3 of the present distance the tide, and with it the rotation-retarding, or moon-driving, force is already 27 times as strong as now, and this increases with a still greater approach (at 1/4 of the distance = 64 times) so much that the Darwinian principle could then exceed the principle of falling and the body of the moon would in fact be driven away again, as was probably the case at its capture, when it came very near the earth and then, for Darwinian reasons, drifted off again centrifugally. To this the Fauth-Hörbiger view now replies that such a great approach of the moon will not come about at all, because long before that the satellite will have been drawn out into a thin spiral ring and gradually absorbed by the earth. This process, it holds, has already been initiated by the egg shape which the body of the moon now already has as a result of the constant attraction of the earth, whereby it turns its more pointed side toward the earth; for the body of the moon itself no longer rotates on its own axis, but, through the constant attraction of the earth, has long since been condemned to a standstill of its rotation and always turns the same side toward the earth.

I cannot share the view that the Noachian Flood had anything to do with returning equatorial flood-waves from a lunar girdle; but rather with the intrusion of cosmic-glacial bodies, which by dissolution in the terrestrial atmosphere produced those enormous but short-lived floods of rain of which it is reported. For if a moon had run into the earth, an enormous girdle high-tide, first east–west, then north–south, and finally east–west again, accelerating the rotation of the earth, would have had to arise. But the geological effects so vividly described in the Hörbigerian doctrine and in the writings of its interpreters, namely inundation and renewed drying-out of whole hemispheres and uniform stratifications, are most conclusively refuted by the findings in all the formations. Just as little can I see in the Noachian Flood perhaps the downfall of Plato’s Atlantis, as already mentioned once before. And even into the last, the diluvial Ice Age, I should by no means place the Noachian Flood, according to the hints of various attendant circumstances, but in a much earlier earth-historical epoch. The glacial-cosmogonic [p. 168] literature explains in more detail how the suction of air which the old moon, approaching the earth toward the end of the Tertiary and allegedly absorbed, effected from the poles toward the equator, through polar rarefaction of the air brought about an intrusion of the cold of cosmic space and with it the diluvial Ice Age; for just as the sea of water, so too the sea of air had been drawn toward the equatorial zone. This too cannot be maintained geologically. For the Ice Age was no time of great cold, but only a time of greatly increased snowy precipitation and of somewhat cooler annual mean temperature. In particular the summers must have been cooler, not the winters colder, and a lowering of the whole annual mean by about 5—6° in this sense would suffice to bring about again today the, by the way, not too great extent of the diluvial ice-cover, provided that the precipitation were more abundant.

At all events, however, we can grant the glacial-cosmogonic theory the glory of having opened the way to the first really decisive, fundamental solution of the question treated here, of earth-history and the history of humanity, indeed of having for the most part already given it<sup>66</sup>. That, with the relatively frequent entry of ice-bodies or ice-planetoids from the Milky Way girdle into the circle of our planetary system, the earth is nevertheless so seldom affected by them, and therefore possesses no glacial secondary moons, comes from the fact that Mars captures such bodies first of all. This, it is said, is — as earlier also the “former planet Luna,” now captured by the earth as a moon — the shield that keeps the blockade-runners from penetrating to us. Conversely, sunward it is the planets Venus and Mercury that essentially take up the zodiacal fine ice-dust radiated to us from the solar corona. All three planets, Mars, Venus, and Mercury, are therefore, it is said, stars standing deep under water, as the moon too is from its earlier planetary time, in comparison with which the earth has remained relatively poor in water, so that only its deepest surface depressions are filled by oceans.

<sup>66</sup> It should be pointed out expressly that the glacial cosmogonists, too, give deeply penetrating explanations of the legends; that the original work of Fauth-Hörbiger also traces man back into the Mesozoic age (loc. cit. p. 510 ff., as well as H. Fischer, Weltwenden. Leipzig 1924), though with different earth-historical datings of the legends and proceeding from quite different presuppositions than our exposition. So here too, at the same time, by quite different paths, the same ideas come to light. In so far as we coincide in substance, priority belongs to the glacial cosmogony outwardly. That is clearly indicated in the text; I have drawn nothing else from it.

It does not matter here to reproduce the glacial-cosmogonic theory as a closed whole; one must study it in the detailed grounding of the original work. It was meant [p. 169] only to show by what path an explanation of the Noachian Flood might perhaps be satisfactorily carried through — for the first time, after it had previously been interpreted in vain as a phantasm, allegory, or local event under false geological and meteorological presuppositions. But if we accept this new explanation of the possible supply of water from cosmic space in the form of ice-planetoids or ice-dust, at least in principle, thus without the dissolution of a moon, we obtain, there can be no doubt, a closed theory for the elucidation also of other primordial and widespread legends, of which the Noachian Flood was a principal event of catastrophic kind, which still always mocks all pettily local interpretations, even if it has been carried to individual peoples and there assimilated to their other narrowly bounded experiences.

In the flood legend of the Chippewayan Indians it is stated quite expressly that the great Flood began with snowfalls: “In the beginning of time there was a great snow flood,” it says there. But the continuation gives the impression of being interwoven once again with cosmogonic legend material: Then the mouse gnawed through the leather bag that contained the heat, which now spread over the earth. In a moment the whole mass of snow melted, so that the tallest spruces were flooded; the water rose higher and higher, until it overtopped even the peaks of the Rocky Mountains<sup>67</sup>. It may be a matter of volcanic eruptions of a still older primeval age, which appear linked with it, although it must always be kept in view that one and the same cosmically conditioned fundamental event could occur in various regions, in manifold variation, according to the structure of the earth’s crust prevailing there and the volcanic or seismic conditions of the ground, as well as the general climatic situation, and yet could have been the same event as elsewhere, where other basic conditions happened to be present for its effects. But one can explain it still more simply. That fire meteors also took part in the plunging down of the floods of water from the aerial space, which again speaks strongly for Hörbiger’s conception, emerges from the following description of the Flood event: When the people saw the water welling up out of the wells of the deep, they took their children, of whom there were so [p. 170] many, laid them upon the openings of the wells and pressed down upon them with their bodies without mercy. But the Lord let a flood fall upon them from above. But strong was their power and great their stature; and when the Lord saw that neither the wells of the deep nor the floods of heaven could do anything against them, he let a rain of fire fall from heaven, as it is also said: the rest was devoured by the fire. It is also said: every drop that the Lord let rain upon them he first made boiling hot in hell, and after that he let it fall<sup>68</sup>.

<sup>67</sup> Andree, Flutsagen. p. 82.

<sup>68</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden I (see n. 22), p. 195/96.

Here a confluence of legends from two different regions of the theater of the Flood now seems to be present. In keeping with the streak-like descent of the hailstorms and meteor falls, such glowing meteorites had probably come down in individual parts of the continent and had made the rain descending with them hot. Where the meteorites themselves no longer fell, a hot downpour could still fall if shortly before it had passed through the meteorite track in the ocean of air or had been crossed by it. Thus three different phenomena can have come about at the same time: hail-snow and a cold downpour at one place, meteor fall and hot torrents of rain at another, and hot rain alone at a third. These are therefore no contradictions, but rather prove the truth to nature of the original legend, precisely on account of its different versions.

That the Flood was a great cosmic event and is connected with the approach of a cosmic body or agglomerate of bodies of some kind to the earth and into its final orbit seems to be taught also by a Jewish legend<sup>69</sup>, according to which changes in the astronomical constellation had already occurred beforehand. For beforehand, so it is said, the Lord had altered the order of creation; he let the sun rise in the west and set in the east. From another legend one can read off how, before the Flood, a cosmic body circled the earth periodically: “The waters, too, used to rise high before Noah, morning and evening, and washed the corpses of the dead out of their graves. But when Noah came, the waters also stood still.” Not only the sea water is moved by a satellite into [p. 171] regular ebb and flood, but the groundwater table as well. If the satellite circled near and fast, the groundwater, rising and falling rapidly, also washed through the soil. The phenomenon had to vanish again with the removal of the satellite, just as the latter, at its last and greatest approach, had to break open “the wells of the deep.”

<sup>69</sup> For this and the following legend: Gorion, ibid., p. 197; p. 176/77.

It is indeed, in sense, a veritable anticipation of Hörbiger’s doctrine when Babylonian science explains the origin of the Flood from the motions of the stars and of the universe. According to this<sup>70</sup> there must have been a time when the vernal equinox, which passes through the whole zodiac in aeons, stood in the water region of the zodiac (the realm of Ea). Then the earthly universe sank into the flood of water, and from it a new world era emerged. Likewise there has been a time when the earthly universe entered the opposite part of the universe, bordering on the fiery heaven; then a flood of fire set in. According to Babylonian doctrine, therefore, the Flood must recur, just as the fire-meteor flood will recur.

<sup>70</sup> Jeremias, loc. cit., p. 134 ff.

We regard it as settled that the ancient Babylonian star-gazing and star-reckoning had an insight into cosmic relations that has remained closed to our school wisdom. If, therefore, we here encounter a doctrine that has been spread so tenaciously and held fast through long ages by men whose wisdom can certainly rival that of a modern scholar not only quantitatively but also in essence, and perhaps surpassed it in many respects, inasmuch as it had a different world-picture, then we shall meet such a doctrine with seriousness as something well-founded, especially when we see that a brilliant thinker like Hörbiger builds up essentially the same doctrine, though certainly with entirely different means of knowledge and from an entirely different side of consideration than those ancients.

[p. 172]

### Datierung und Raumbegrenzung der noachitischen Sintflut — Dating and Spatial Limits of the Noachian Flood

It has already become clear to us, by our comparative method, where we have to place the Noachian man, both in the evolutionary stage of the human stock itself and in the earth-historical time scale. This Noah-type — one must not think of a person, nor merely of individual generations, but must grasp quite freely, from above, the human type in all possible special stocks and developments of its bodily form and capacity for culture, yet within certain limits as the same — this Noachian human type, as we have tried to show, is perhaps already of early Mesozoic age. The Flood did not set in immediately with the emergence of this Noachian human stock. For Noah himself, as he appears as a human figure in the Flood account, had already lived for some time among degenerate men. He was an already more highly developed representative of the Mesozoic, mammal-like intellectual man.

One feature by which one can first try to fit Noah’s Flood experience into earth history is the plant world that surrounds him. The olive tree is named, which he found again first after the Flood. Then the cultivation of the vine, which he succeeded in after the Flood. The olive tree, like the vine, belongs to the flowering plants, which we meet in earth history for the first time in the last half of the Cretaceous, that is, at the end of the Mesozoic era. Since the vine as such does not occur wild but must be regarded as a product of cultivation, it cannot in any case have been cultivated by the man of the former world earlier than in the upper part of the Cretaceous. Since, according to the legend, it was produced only after the Flood in the newly settled regions, this resettlement cannot have taken place much before the end of the Cretaceous. With this an oldest temporal limit is given.

[p. 173] Among the animals that Noah took with him into the ark — the metaphysical significance of the image of the ark we shall characterize later — only mammals and birds are ever named, such as elephant, rhinoceros, mouse, hare, dove, and thus no more reptiles. These types point just as generally to the Tertiary; but they probably arose already in the last phase of the Mesozoic era. Thus we obtain once again a lower temporal limit going backward into earth history: the end of the Cretaceous.

A further natural-historical feature, derived from the myths and determining the same earth-historical age of the Flood, is the mention of the giant bird in connection with the man of the Flood. The Knistino or Cree Indians have a legend<sup>71</sup> according to which such a great bird must have lived at the time of the Flood. “At the time of the great inundation, which .. destroyed all the peoples of the earth .. a young woman seized the foot of a very large bird flying past and was .. carried to the top of a very high cliff. Here she gave birth to twins, whose father was the war eagle, and her children have peopled the earth ever since.” For all the later conscious allegory that is at the same time contained in this version, it is nevertheless based on the archetype of the great bird as such. The presumed flying giant birds we have, however, already determined in a previous section as Early Tertiary or Late Cretaceous (p. 114).

<sup>71</sup> Andree, Flutsagen, p. 88/89.

The assumed dating of the Noachian Flood can be further corroborated in terms of the history of legends. For one of the legends already reproduced, that of the Binnas of the Malay Peninsula, still reports that only later, after the Flood, the mountain Lulumut and the mountain ranges came into being. “The mountains” are of course the particularly noteworthy mountains, thus probably those of the Indian–Further Indian–New Zealand arc, whose origin falls, in earth-historical terms, definitively in the middle or last phase of the Tertiary. We have another legend that aims at just this, namely among the Somalis, according to which the Red Sea did not yet exist before the Noachian Flood. This event is Late Tertiary<sup>72</sup>; the Flood would accordingly have had to occur at the latest already in the Early Tertiary.

<sup>72</sup> Cf. A. Wegener, Entstehung der Kontinente, p. 114 (see n. 74).

The Binnas of the Malay Peninsula know, as we do, that the earth is not a solid mass but has a thin skin. In [p. 174] ancient times God broke through the crust, so that the world was destroyed and flooded with water. Later he caused mountain and mountain ranges to arise, and also the lowland inhabited by the Binnas. On these mountains the firmness of the earth depends. After the mountain had emerged, a prahu built of wood floated about on the water, in which God had enclosed a man and a woman created by him. When it ran aground, they cleared a way out for themselves. At first everything was still dark, it was neither evening nor morning. Later it grew light and they had children, from whom all men descend<sup>73</sup>. Andree says that this legend seems to him, if it has been well recorded, not unsuspicious; it is cosmogonic in nature, but shows a mixture of the biblical story of creation with the account of the Flood, indeed verbal echoes, for example that God looked with pleasure upon the people, whose number he counted. But it is probably wrong to describe the origin of the mountains offhand as “cosmogonic” and therefore as corresponding to the biblical account of creation. The student of legends should not see things that are widely separated in time on one plane. The origin of “great” mountain ranges is a very late earth-historical process, and precisely what we have today in the way of high mountains on earth mostly stems from folding processes of the Late Tertiary and came about so rapidly, relatively speaking, that this can almost be called catastrophic, if one considers by contrast the duration of the geological periods. Thus of a cosmogonic age, that is, an age pointing to primordial states of the earth, one cannot speak at all in the case of mountain formation, and so in the legend cited a very late earth-historical process is presumably touched upon, namely the origin of the Tertiary high mountain ranges, to which the Himalayan chains belong just as much as the New Zealand Alps and the Further Indian mountain chains and our Alps, whereas cosmogonic or, more precisely, geogonic flood legends seem to us to meet us in general rather in Nordic mythology, where the Flood legend in the Noachian sense is not present as something lived through. It therefore seems to me of the utmost importance for the natural-historical clarification of the old legend-images to keep this difference in view, lest [p. 175] heterogeneous things widely separated in time flow together. In the Binna legend, then, we have to do with the same historical core as in the biblical natural event, and therefore date this event here too at least in the Early Tertiary.

<sup>73</sup> Andree, Flutsagen, p. 29.

From this point of view even a Flood tradition that offers something so impossible geophysically at first sight appears rational, such as the legend, already mentioned also by Sueß, that after the Flood those drifting in the ship tied their vessel fast to the summit of the Himalaya. If one accepts our dating, then at that time the whole Himalayan region, like the Alps, can have been only an archipelago of islands, whose later highest parts lay in part still completely below sea level (cf. Fig. 22, 23) or had emerged as islands and ridges of land. If, then, the memory of humanity goes back to that time we have assumed, it is quite correct that Late Mesozoic–Early Tertiary men landed at the highest peaks of the later South Asian high mountains, which lay directly opposite the sunken Gondwanaland. Indeed, if one allows the migration of the survivors of the Flood, driven out by the sinking Gondwana continent, some breadth of time, the legend gains still greater natural-historical weight, because the sinking of Gondwanaland can be directly causally connected with the slow first emergence of Himalayan mountain masses.

Already in the preceding section, following Hörbiger’s Flood theory, it was pointed out that a drawing-in of cosmic ice dust, or the dissolution of a planetoid rich in water or ice, or of some other cosmic body captured in whatever way, must have exerted a Flood-like effect essentially in the equatorial zone. Such a cosmic event, however, could not remain without influence on the isostatic situation of the continental region primarily affected by it. The enormous masses of water had to produce a pressure load, which tended to press downward the belt of land that had until then been in planetary equilibrium, and so soon let the sea enter the lower-lying parts. This all the more because the revolution of the captured [p. 176] foreign body must already have produced beforehand an equatorial flood, if only a short-lived one. Then, in connection with this, earthquakes had to arise, fissures to form, and wells of the deep to break open. If it was a larger cosmic body that was drawn in, then as it approached an influence on the form of the earth’s body had to be produced, so that in the equatorial zone, within the swelling of the waters, the attractive tendency toward an uplift of land was also produced, as now in the ordinary lunar flood and ebb. Now, however, every continental surface is always in certain regional states of tension, which at a given moment proceed to equalization and then bring about upward or downward movements, horizontal and vertical displacements, whether these work themselves out along older, preformed lines of discontinuity and fault fissures in the rock framework of the continents, which had already been used before in the same sense, or whether new fissure lines form on such an occasion. Such tensions are probably released most readily by certain astronomical changes, thus perhaps by fluctuations in the eccentricity of the earth’s orbit or at most by changes in the obliquity of the ecliptic, and could of course be released in a more catastrophic manner on such an occasion as the approach of a cosmic body offers. In such continental movements, and the tearing open of fissures and systems of fractures that sets in with them, springs, hot thermal waters or volcanoes then also come to the surface. These are ordinary geological phenomena, which need not first be justified here and whose invocation is nothing special.

We know now from earth history that at the end of the Mesozoic and at the beginning of the Tertiary era the great Gondwana continent, often mentioned already, between Polynesia, Australia, India, Madagascar and Africa broke apart, whether it collapsed to oceanic depths or its parts, under lesser collapses, drew apart in the sense of Wegener’s doctrine of continental drift<sup>74</sup>. In any case this continental disintegration is an earth-historical fact and first made itself strongly felt in the Jurassic. Even if this process extended over a longer time, it can in places and [p. 177] over wide areas nonetheless have taken place catastrophically. In the Upper Cretaceous and Early Tertiary there finally remained, with a temporary, simultaneous heavy flooding of the marginal regions of the African continent, a large continental island lying in the Indian Ocean, comparable in size to Australia, which extended from eastern Madagascar to India and, since Wallace’s zoogeographical studies, also received the name Lemuria in the geological literature, and which likewise disappeared definitively with the Tertiary (Fig. 23). Since then the extent of the oceanic regions and the receding of large continental surfaces in the equatorial zone and the zone of the globe directly adjoining it to the south have also been particularly striking. If we, then, interpret and date the Noachian Flood, contrary to Ed. Sueß, in the manner pursued so far, much becomes comprehensible that was previously opaque, namely not only the generally legendary side of the Flood event and the remarkable spread of its tidings, but also, in terms of the history of humanity, of animals and of the earth, the fact that presumably those oldest pre-Noachian and Noachian men dwelt on that southern Gondwanaland and later on its Lemurian remnant; that there too the first mammals of reptilian habit arose together with pre-Noachian and Noachian man, and that after or with the Flood and the sinking or inundation of that first continent the emigration of men and mammals took place. According to the distribution of land and sea at that time, however, it may be regarded as improbable that the emigration took place northward, since at that time there stretched there the broad world-Mediterranean extending from Further India over Persia, Asia Minor and the Mediterranean and Alpine region, to which geology gave the name Tethys and which is among the most permanent seas of earth history, so that an emigration to the east and southwest will come into consideration, where tracts of continental land may be sought, or at least archipelagos of islands, which the Noachites, who according to the legend were then hardly yet versed in navigation, could reach. Thus they easily came to the South Atlantic continent in the west, which still existed, and probably reached South America, with which that continent was connected; on the other hand they could reach southern India by land [p. 178] and also Australia. Finally it is to be assumed that at that time, even after the sinking of the greater part of Gondwanaland, a Polynesian land complex still held out in the Pacific, which was likewise reachable by the Noachian man driven out by the Flood, and from which he could likewise reach South or Central America through garlands of islands. And everywhere he took with him the original Flood legend, regionally different from the start though agreeing in its core, which entirely corresponds to the spread of the legend that appears to our legend research to be original.

<sup>74</sup> A. Wegener, Die Entstehung der Kontinente und Ozeane (Sammlg. “Die Wissenschaft”, vol. 66.) Braunschweig 1922. 3rd ed. The theory teaches that the continents, as specifically lighter blocks of rock, are embedded in the specifically heavier, more basaltic material of the earth’s crust, which lies exposed on the floors of the oceans, and can shift within it in a horizontal direction under a constant one-sided pressure. In this way former continental connections between Europe, Africa and America, or between Africa, India and Australia, could have been dissolved by a horizontal sliding apart of the parts, without one having to postulate collapses to oceanic depth, which would contradict the conditions of equilibrium in the earth’s crust. A fierce struggle over the theory has flared up in the specialist sciences. To appraise it requires thorough geophysical and earth-historical knowledge, and one must go far beyond the usual physical notions of the laboratory in order to follow it. According to the present state of the discussions it seems as if wide horizontal displacements of continental parts did take place in earth history, without the sinking to oceanic depths having therefore to be excluded. Probably both movements occur in the stone mantle of the earth as soon as the planetary equilibrium in the body of the earth is disturbed by cosmic or intratelluric processes. The Wegener theory, too, is above all to be valued as a work of ingenious vision, and has already now proved of the greatest fruitfulness for research into the former world. It arose from the contemplation of the striking congruence of outline of the two shores of the Atlantic Ocean, which can be made to coincide almost perfectly, like mirror images, if one imagines the two Americas with Greenland pushed up against Europe and Africa.

Fig. 24. Pictorial panels of the Maya manuscript, perhaps with allusions to the animal world at the time of the Flood.

That the Flood legend in Central America is original, that is, was present there before the Spaniards came into the country, is today probably settled. All the more important, therefore, does a piece seem to me from the already repeatedly cited Aztec Maya manuscript, in which an allusion to the Flood can perhaps be recognized (Fig. 24). The man who traveled over the water with the human demon sits there in the flood, at the bottom of the water, which stands above his head. Opposite him is an animal which, however, is not a snake, for it lacks all the features belonging to one. It is rather comparable to a worm; but the remarkable spiral coiling also recalls an ammonite, the most characteristic lower marine animal of the seas in the Mesozoic era. In the smaller partial picture this ammonite-shaped form appears once again, and so involute that an unspiral extension is scarcely any longer present. Natural-historically it can be said briefly of the type of the ammonite that it became completely extinct at the end of the Cretaceous, that is, precisely at that earth-historical point of time which we should like to fix as the date of the Flood. The snake-like animal, however, which the demon holds in the water, could be an allusion to the fabulous sea serpent which we already came to know in the chapter on the legendary animals [p. 179] and which likewise lived in the late phase of the Cretaceous and at the boundary toward the Tertiary; it is also noteworthy in this connection that there are indications that those sea serpents lived at the surface of the water and probably dived beneath only briefly and transiently to seize prey. It is at any rate not outside the realm of possibility that, as the Flood narrative of the Maya manuscript, something of all that has been said resounds here. Without wishing, given the uncertainty of the object, to make more of it, let this feature at least be commended to attention in the elucidation of the old picture-writing.

Important for judging the level of culture already attained at the time of the Noachian Flood is that the Babylonian texts<sup>75</sup> report expressly that ruling dynasties had existed a long time before the Flood, that even sayings of sages from before the Flood are communicated, and that written tradition is expressly asserted also for the time before the Flood. Ashurbanipal says that he read stones from the time before the Flood. We have no right to declare such a statement a mere figure of speech, brought forward to lend dignity to his words and laws. It is expressly said again, indeed, in other writings from Babylon, that a great multitude of men of different stocks was found there, who had come together without order and lived like the animals. One recalls the biblical tradition of the confusion of tongues at Babel. That culture and written tradition already existed before the Flood is also attested by the tradition that Xisuthros, the last of the ten antediluvian primeval kings, established the connection with the sages across the catastrophe of the Flood by burying all the writings before the Flood; the kinsmen of the Babylonian Noah were then said to have dug them up and spread them among men<sup>76</sup>.

<sup>75</sup> Jeremias, loc. cit., p. 118 and 139.

<sup>76</sup> To exclude misunderstandings it should be remarked here that of course even among the Babylonians themselves disbelief in legends prevailed, and the same flat natural-scientific explanation was attempted as with us. Berossos, the Babylonian priest at the time of Alexander the Great, followed almost the same train of thought as Ed. Sueß. According to him the Flood did not take place at some time in early primeval ages, but is a Babylonian historical event. (Ungnad-Greßmann, Gilgameschepos loc. cit. p. 213.)

In Plato’s account of Atlantis it is said that at the time of the settlement of Atlantis there were as yet no ships. If, then, Atlantis was settled from the east after the sinking of the Gondwana continent, thus after the Flood, then it was the Noachite who settled it. He would therefore have had to get across by traveling over land. If one wishes, one can at most connect his ark, as a first [p. 180] attempt at shipbuilding, with the first invention of shipbuilding at that time. For me it is, as will be set forth in one of the concluding sections, also of another significance. But if the ship was invented only with the post-Flood settlement of Atlantis which we assume, then light will also fall on the “stone chests” with which the boatman of the Babylonian Utnapishtim has to do in the Gilgamesh epic and which Gilgamesh smashes for him before the crossing, whereupon they build a ship in place of the old stone chests. It is precisely over this that the ancestor Utnapishtim marvels, when he saw from afar the two of them crossing the sea in a ship.

Also in the Atlantis fairy tales of Frobenius, to single out only this as well, there is a passage which points to quite primeval nature-somnambulistic states of the human essence and at the same time denies the existence of ships at that time; it is told of the daughter of an Agelith, who slept one month and waked one, until she should marry. And in this connection it is said that at that time there were as yet no ships<sup>77</sup>. The remarkable sleeping until she should become a human being, as it stands in another place, and the unfamiliarity with the ship again point to that quite ancient pre-Noachian and antediluvian age of man.

<sup>77</sup> L. Frobenius, Volksmärchen der Kabylen III. “Atlantis”. vol. III. Jena 1921, p. 267.

That through the onset of the Flood men were driven from their dwelling places and settled other tracts of land is suggested by a Flood tradition of the Jews. When the flood began and Noah with his family was in the chest, men came up and wanted to burst it open, in order to find shelter in it after all. But then God let the cattle and the wild animals that surrounded the chest fall upon them. And the animals overpowered the men and beat them and killed many of them and drove them off. Then each sought his own way and they scattered over the earth<sup>78</sup>.

<sup>78</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden, loc. cit., p. 185.

At the end of the Mesozoic era the present boundaries of land and sea also already took shape. Great sinkings of land and far-reaching marine transgressions no longer took place, apart from the Atlantic one that follows later, which was however probably not very extensive. Noteworthy, however, is the fact that with the end of the [p. 181] Cretaceous a great dying sets in, the mighty reptile world in the seas and on the continents vanishes as with a single blow, and thereafter the mammals, previously hardly noticeable, essentially rule. Among the lower marine creatures too, some types still dominant in the Mesozoic era disappear. It is now very probable that the deep sea too only then fully took shape, in connection with the last great general fold-mountain formation that was then setting in and was completed in the Tertiary.<sup>78a</sup> Mesozoic lower marine animals demonstrably migrated at that time into the deep sea and live there still today. For all these geological and biological phenomena, however, a cosmic influx of water onto the earth would give a good explanation. Thus many marine animals would have died out rapidly from the fresh water penetrating into the surface layer of the seas, and especially the ammonites, hitherto extremely numerous and living at the water surface; others could withdraw into the deep water that remained saltier, and it is significant that the families of mussels, snails and crabs living more in the depths and on the bottom survived this epoch, while the surface dwellers named and some sessile groups of mollusks living at the sea surface, which were still so characteristic of the Cretaceous, have afterward disappeared entirely. At that time the ocean would then also have been filled up by the cosmic water flowing in.

<sup>78a</sup> More fully substantiated in the treatise “Tiefsee und Faltengebirge” by E. Dacqué, in the Berichten der Senckenberg. Naturf. Ges. Frankfurt a. M., no. 9 and 10, 1925.

All this, then, would have to be placed directly at the boundary between the Cretaceous and the Tertiary, and at that time the pre-Noachite too would have perished along with it; thereafter only the Noachite still held sway, and remained surrounded for the most part by a world of mammals. That all these interpretations, however, as has been said more than once already, are only preliminary attempts to arrive some day at a more solid explanation, of this I am well aware.

[p. 182]

### Jüngere Fluten und Landuntergänge — Later Floods and Submergences of Land

Under the collective term “Flood” all sorts of things have often been put together that are of quite different origin, and that stand to one another not merely as legendary core and poetic elaboration, but presumably also go back to the most various external earth-historical events. The mere fact that Greek mythology, long since worked through, knows several floods, which it nevertheless seems itself to throw into confusion, and which have occasionally been identified with the Noachian Flood, shows that the flood legends must be kept carefully apart. It was therefore only sensible of R. Andree to designate his well-known compilation quite generally as “flood legends,” and to point to their wholly different causes of origin, not merely their regions of origin. Golther, in his “Germanische Mythologie,” seems on the other hand not to follow him in this, but unites, for example, the cosmogonic flood welling from the blood of the slain giant Ymir with the biblical narrative on the strength of a far-fetched etymology, although the two, viewed without prejudice, cannot possibly mean the same thing, and must belong to events and paths of tradition lying very far apart in earth-historical terms and very different from one another<sup>79</sup>. The mingling of the cosmogonic Ymir-flood legend, as we might quite generally call it, with the ordinary Noachian flood legend is already shown by a narrative from Borneo, which through the appearing stream of blood is immediately reminiscent of the former, but on the other hand belongs also to the group of Flood legends, in which man as such takes part. It seems to me, in general, that there is a southeastern and a northwestern circle of flood legends, which occasionally overlap somewhat and then yield a mingling of cosmogonic and Noachian flood-images. For the northwestern circle seems originally to have known nothing of the Noachian Flood, but to have produced only cosmogonic legend-images. The tradition from Borneo, which betrays the seam and the area of overlap of the two worlds of legend, [p. 183] runs roughly as follows: Once some Dayak women went out and sat down in the jungle on a fallen tree. All at once the tree began to bleed, and some men who came up saw that it was no tree, but a great snake that lay there stiff. They killed the creature, cut it open, took the flesh home with them and roasted it. Then there arose a clamor, it began to rain heavily, until all the mountains except the highest stood under water. All human beings perished, only one woman was saved, from whom afterwards the fire-drilling human beings descended<sup>80</sup>.

<sup>79</sup> Golther, German. Mythologie, p. 516/17.

<sup>80</sup> Andree, Flutsagen, p. 32.

For the Flood legend of the Noachian kind we have as its center a southeastern earth-complex, to which Greece, for example, no longer originally belongs, but which, rather, borders with Babylonia and Chaldea on the west and northwest. On the other hand it extended over the region on and in the Indian Ocean as far as Polynesia and from there, probably — that is, not from the west and not from northern Asia — to South and Central America. China, Tibet and Central Asia, like the Germanic-Nordic and the Mediterranean-Atlantic circle, belonged to the northwestern complex of regions. Our present orientation, proceeding from Europe, into a western hemisphere, by which one understands the two Americas with the Atlantic, and an eastern one, by which one understands the Orient and Asia with Australia and Polynesia, lays the line of division right through the middle of Europe. The aboriginal inhabitants of both Americas are, however, for our manner of consideration here, eastern peoples.

There are now many reports of floods of older and of more recent date which may go back to all sorts of regional breaking-away of portions of the mainland, incursions of the sea and, in coastal countries, probably also to seismic floods, such as Sueß adduces in such large number in his explanation of the Flood. Many of them will presumably have been brought together later with that Noachian Flood overrunning one great continent, and so it will perhaps be impossible ever to untangle these interwoven threads. For even since the most recent phase of the Tertiary so many changes of sea and land have taken place that there was material and occasion enough for a constant revival of the oldest Flood legend, which thus had always to take on new forms [p. 184] among peoples in whose memory it was not rooted in the true grain, but to whom it had only been carried. Hence it need not surprise us if we come across not only the most diverse flood legends and Flood legends, but also find them, in one and the same people, in one and the same land, often several times layered one upon another and partly matted together.

It lies beyond the possibilities given here to pursue all such flood legends. Only a few, more easily grasped, shall still be discussed; above all those from the Greek circle of legend<sup>81</sup>, probably connected in part with Atlantis.

<sup>81</sup> Preller-Robert, Griech. Mythologie, p. 31.

The Ogygian flood, named after King Ogyges, an aboriginal inhabitant of Greece, is said to be the oldest; the Deucalionian the second; that under the Boeotian king Dardanus the third. It seems that one must not set much store by the Greek accounts of floods, and above all by the datings of the floods, indeed that they have been treated in a downright misleading way in earth-historical terms. Nowhere has so much been spoiled for us in the ancient life of legend by unmythical intellectualism and the purely allegorical or humanistic manner as by Greek poetry and authorship. Already in Homer, with regard to the most primordial legend-values worked up by him, we encounter a chiefly theologically allegorizing rationalism and sheer romance. It may be that in the Orphic circle and in primitive Hellenism this was different and better; but of that we know scarcely anything. Thus the Noachian Flood myth, which in any case was probably not native in Greece, nor among the Atlanteans, nor among the Egyptians, has been distorted in natural-historical terms by mixture with the later autochthonous experiences of flood, which evidently took place in the Mediterranean, especially in Greece and its Aegean world of islands. Even if man in the Mediterranean region had only a Quaternary age — there were certainly Diluvial men there — he must have experienced such regional floods or submergences of land fairly often. For the incursions of the Aegean Sea and the subsidences of the land of Greece, whose bays are for the most part river valleys drowned in the sea, belong to a time lying so near to us in earth-historical terms that it would be downright a wonder if we had no reports of them. If, to be sure, Ogyges, the Ancient, were only [p. 185] another form of the word Okeanos, then the “Ogygian” flood too would be only a mingling of the very latest events, localized in Greece, with the somewhat older Atlantic processes of subsidence of continental regions.

Of special value for us is that in Plato’s account of Atlantis there is mention, besides, of several catastrophes, in part simultaneous, in the Mediterranean region itself. Greece is described, in a geologically unobjectionable manner, as the remnant of a formerly more extensive, continuous land-mass. “The whole land stretches from the mainland far out into the sea and lies there like a promontory; the basin of the sea by which it is enclosed is deep everywhere along its shores. Since now in the 9000 years that have elapsed from that (Atlantic) time until now many mighty inundations have taken place, the earth that in this time and in such events was washed down from the heights has not, as in other regions, been piled up high, but was each time washed away all around and vanished into the depths. So now, as happens with small islands, compared with the former land, only the bones of the sick body, as it were, have been left behind.” After these sentences Plato describes the old, richly wooded land with its herds and its population. It appears here in an analysis that strikes one as downright modern and scientific, geologically quite correct, as a remnant of land whose main body has fallen victim to the sea.

Fr. Frech, one of the geological explorers of Asia Minor, explains these and related Greek flood legends by the numerous post-Tertiary incursions of the Aegean Sea. This, like the Pontus, that is the Black Sea, and the Propontis, broke in during the later section of the Diluvial period. The line of the Dardanelles as far as the Bosporus is an old river valley that has gone down and is now covered by the sea. Fossil finds also prove this. Thus freshwater snails have been found in the bed of the Bosporus, which lived there when the Sea of Marmara and the Black Sea still formed part of that great, ever narrowing freshwater lake which formerly, in the Late Tertiary, also still filled the Hungarian plain and the Vienna basin<sup>82</sup>.

<sup>82</sup> F. Frech, Kleinasien. Zeitschr. der Gesellschaft für Erdkunde. Berlin 1913, p. 424ff.

[p. 186] Such and similar events in the Mediterranean region, which must after all have been numerous, were at once or later linked to age-old myths and legends of humanity, interwoven with them, and so there occur not only earth-historical obscurities but naturally also false datings and transpositions of the one over the other, indeed perhaps crossings of several halves of legends. So, as I found in Roscher’s Lexikon, Welcker is certainly right when he says that the Boeotian, that is Ogygian, flood legend was obscured by the Deucalionian. The Deucalionian was, in fact, equated with the Noachian, or rather taken over altogether as the Noachian from the east and transformed in its nomenclature, because the latter was not native in the Mediterranean region at all. This Noachian-Deucalionian flood legend can, as the most primordial stuff of tradition, even have met with a Greek-autochthonous local legend and been at once interwoven with it; thereafter, who knows how often, have been newly identified with one of the certainly not infrequent Aegean-Mediterranean incursions of the sea occurring later, or recast into such a one; besides, it need not have happened everywhere in the whole of Greece. In any case there is, for the Ogygian flood alone, besides the Boeotian tradition, also an Attic one. And at the sacrificial festivals later linked to the Attic flood legend there was shown — just as, on the other side, in Hierapolis at the festival of remembrance of the end of the Noachian Flood — the cleft in the earth through which the Attic flood was supposed to have drained away — a motif that lives also elsewhere, namely among quite northern Indians<sup>83</sup>, and which, by its striking vividness, points all the more to one and the same extra-Mediterranean source, as it is geologically impossible for gigantic inundations to drain away in a smaller cleft in the earth without coming to light again elsewhere, like the seeping away of a karst river.

<sup>83</sup> Andree, Flutsagen, p. 84.

Here, in the legend of the northern Dindje Indians, where it is said that the waters of the Flood afterwards withdrew into a monstrous abyss of the earth, it is quite evident, even apart from the Attic flood, that so identical and specifically peculiar a trait cannot have been created only through the influence of Christian missionaries, the more so since the [p. 187] biblical account does not spend a word on precisely this draining away, so that the motif must originally derive from the time of the Flood itself, when, with the collapse of the Gondwana continent, so huge a cleft, suited to the reception of great masses of water and to their discharge into the sea, could form. Because this trait recurs originally in the same tradition at two such wholly opposite points of the earth, it must have been brought to the aboriginal inhabitants of North America probably from Polynesia and not by way of the Mediterranean region, which originally did not possess the Noachian Flood legend at all, and hence also not through Western Christianity, and so here too we should have before us the eastern complex, this time in an offshoot pressing to the north of the New World, in contrast to the western-northwestern.

Fig. 25. Early Diluvial course of the Rhine before the separation of Great Britain from the Continent. (After F. W. Harmer, from A. Rothpletz, Monatsschr. f. naturw. Unterricht. vol. 8. 1915.)

Attention may yet be drawn to a tradition which could possibly lead us to recognize even a component of the Nibelung legends as at least Ice Age or perhaps wholly Late Tertiary. This is the voyage to Iceland from the Rhine. I believe one can hardly assume that the Rhine ships of the Nibelungs were so seaworthy that they could venture on voyages to Iceland, across the North Sea, which after all is not exactly small-waved, as something taken for granted and apparently often repeated, [p. 188] and thus known to them. Now we know, however, that before the Ice Age and probably also in the Ice Age itself, as long as the North Sea was not yet frozen over, the mouth of the Rhine (Fig. 25) did not lie in Holland at all, but that a wide continental dry area (Fig. 26) extended, embracing Friesland, Holland and England, up to the northern border of Scotland, while the mouth of the Rhine itself lay beyond the Dogger Bank and evidently [p. 189] opened into a wide bay of the sea. From this land and its bay it was only a short crossing to Iceland. As we thus already believed we had reason to regard the primordial form of Siegfried as an immensely ancient component, belonging to the history of humanity, of the late Nibelung legend, so the voyage across to Iceland too could point to Late Tertiary human traffic between a more southerly Atlantic and a more northerly, at that time demonstrably still more extensive and above all warmer northland. Likewise this could explain the many remarkable voyages of Nordic men, such as, for example, the voyage of Leif the Lucky to Greenland or that of Djarni, who seeks Greenland<sup>84</sup>. It would be worth a future investigation to extract from the Icelandic sagas the elements which, according to the whole climatic conditions that find expression in them, go back to the pre-glacial time, that is into the Tertiary. For it is quite impossible that in the Quaternary such journeys and discoveries could have taken place in the far north. The low annual mean temperature prevailing there and the gloomy polar winter could never have permitted that one should find a land where fields of wheat stood that nobody had sown, where vines grew green and maple trees grew; and the voyages to Greenland itself would have brought just as little of such experiences. Even if one interprets all this as referring to America, where the Vikings were indeed probably present, such an interpretation is still wholly impracticable for the older elements of the cycle of legends. For over there too northern America would at the very least have given no vines. But if one keeps the earth-historical facts in view, if one bears in mind that in the last part of the Tertiary maple and laurel trees and other evergreen plants still grew up there in the high north, that the seas were free and that scarcely at the Pole itself had there been ice, then space and time, in which the Icelandic sagas took place, take on a quite different breadth, notwithstanding all demonstrably later, Quaternary voyages of discovery, into which those primordial materials may well again only have been woven, just as into the late seafarers’ adventures the primordial legends of the sea monsters, the Mesozoic sea serpents or the giant birds.

<sup>84</sup> “Thule”, vol. 13. Grönländer und Färinger Geschichten. Jena 1912. p. 10 and 32.

Fig. 26. Presumed Tertiary land connection between Europe and Greenland (original).

[p. 190]

### Sagen von Mond und Sonne — Legends of Moon and Sun

If we may reckon, after the foregoing expositions, with the great probability that man as a being of his own, though endowed with other powers and, as we shall yet see, with other qualities of mind also, lived through ages of the primordial world which hitherto, according to fairly general scientific doctrine, were to be regarded as empty of men, then it is also no longer a further cause for wonder if we hear in tradition, now more definitely, now less definitely, of celestial constellations which, to our apparently so stable world-picture, are great riddles or empty fancies. Yet it must be borne in mind that all our scientific astronomical ideas and calculations, without the support of a secured, very ancient knowledge, are derived only from quite recent celestial constellations and celestial movements, not reaching back even into the Diluvial period, and that the computational tracing backward of any perceptible discrepancies can after all have validity only for a stretch of time reaching at most to the boundary of the Diluvial period, quite apart from other observations that cannot be interpreted.

Already in ordinary earth history, ascertained only from the material of the earth’s crust and from the fossils, we know all sorts of processes and conditions which compel us to regard as probable greatly altered astronomical positions of the earth, probably also changes of the sun’s heat, shifts of the poles, indeed perhaps even an influx of material and water from outer space. Here astronomy can teach the student of earth history as good as nothing, but the student of earth history will on his part set new scientific problems before astronomy, lay down postulates in a definite direction, and demand of it that it now work them through hypothetically in its own way, with its own methods. But if we also have, from sources other than the earth’s crust itself, a more or less definite knowledge about [p. 191] the earth-historical past and the uranic movements then prevailing, then these traditions too, from earlier epochs, can report of world-pictures and motions of heavenly bodies with which astronomy itself so far knows not what to do, which it cannot derive from the present world-picture, which it cannot grasp by calculation; but which it will one day, if they are further confirmed as facts of natural history, have to draw into the circle of the possibilities considered by it. And this all the more if it should turn out that those primordial reports of fact were handed down further by a mind of so high an astronomical standing as the Chaldean-Egyptian, and had evidently been brought into a thoroughly scientific system.

One knows from biblical history the narrative in the Book of Joshua (chap. 10, verse 12 ff.), where the king speaks with God and cried before the assembled army: Stand still, sun, at Gibeon, and moon in the valley of Ajalon. Then the sun and the moon stood still, the sun in the midst of heaven, and hasted not to go down, for almost a whole day; and there was no day like it, either before or after. This is, however, only one of many passages which we meet in all literatures, where wonders of the heavens are told, over which we enlightened ones must shake our heads. If a primordial event from a much, much earlier time than that of a Chaldean king still shines through here, then it is conceivable that behind it lies not a change in the course of those heavenly bodies, but a displacement or rotation of the body of the earth or of the earth’s orbit, perhaps, connected with it, a certain horizontal shifting of a small part of the earth’s crust, whereby an apparent standstill of the heavenly bodies, thought of as alone in motion, could then arise. Yet here everything possible is to be conjectured with little prospect of real conclusiveness. On the other hand, other astronomical events handed down to us can first be grasped more exactly and then more readily explained.

One of the most positive statements about an essential change in the astronomical constellation, in which the earth found itself in relation to other heavenly bodies or these in relation to it, is the legend, occurring in various forms, of men who lived [p. 192] before the moon shone. Among the Greeks these were the Proselenes, aboriginal inhabitants, allegedly, of the Peloponnese, who, according to a tradition transmitted by Aristotle, were subjugated by the later, that is still pre-Mycenaean, original inhabitants. Mindt has partly compiled the reports<sup>85</sup> and points to a passage in Apollonius Rhodius, who designates Egypt as the oldest inhabited land, originally occupied by the same Arcadians as the Peloponnese, at the time when not yet all the stars circled in the heavens, when the Danaans did not yet exist and not yet the Deucalionian race. These Proselenes dwelt on the mountains, eating acorns.

<sup>85</sup> Fauth-Hörbiger, Glazialkosmogonie, p. 395 (see n. 47).

It must strike us that the moon mythology of the Greek primordial age is also not uniform and points both to disturbances of the course of the moon or of the body of the moon itself, and to two different forms of the moon<sup>86</sup>. Artemis counts as a virgin and had remained untouched; to Semele, however, a hidden love is occasionally ascribed. Yet then again it is said that Artemis could keep herself hidden in the forest as a black she-bear and was thus once embraced by Zeus, bearing him thereafter Arcas, the ancestor of the Arcadians. Are there here interweavings of the oldest legend-knowledge, from different times, about the present satellite and one unknown to us, or a meeting with another, say a transient, body? Or of two moons, one of which was darkened for a long-lasting period or later was no longer there at all, or of which the other came later?

<sup>86</sup> M. Dunker, Geschichte des Altertums. vol. III. Berlin 1856, p. 39.

The explanation most convincing to me for the Proselenes, as for the Flood, has been supplied by the brilliant doctrine of Fauth-Hörbiger. It seeks to furnish the proof that the moon is a smaller neighboring planet captured by the earth, whose bondage to the earth we already mentioned in the section on the cosmic explanation of the Noachian Flood. The moon, it holds, is a captured planet, formerly standing between Mars and the earth, and now moves toward the earth. G. H. Darwin holds the moon to be a body which once left the earth and is now provisionally drifting away from it on an ever more widening spiral orbit.<sup>87</sup> Yet (according to Fauth-Hörbiger) Darwin overlooked in his calculation that all celestial bodies are exposed to a [p. 193] resistance of medium which, if one leaves out of account at most the ether, hypothetical in its nature, results from the hydrogen gas also distributed in planetary space and enriched in the vicinity of the planets, and which compels them, according to their mass, density and velocity, quite apart from attraction itself, to gravitate inward in spiral orbits to the center of force of their orbital curve. As soon as a celestial body has chained a satellite to itself, as the earth the moon, the two together form a system with a common center of gravity. In the earth–moon system this lies about three quarters of the earth’s radius from the earth’s center, hence still within the body of the earth. This common center of gravity draws the moon, which is unstably placed outside, toward the planetary body, letting it, so to speak, fall toward it, although at first no plunge occurs, because the centrifugal force of the satellite counteracts this and so makes the moon’s orbit an infinitely narrowly wound spiral course. The spiral orbit, however, narrows more and more toward the body of the earth by reason of the incessantly acting gravity, and the end will be, amid great changes in the form of the moon, finally a spiral-striped winding-in of the moon’s mass into the equatorial part of the earth’s body.

<sup>87</sup> G. H. Darwin, Ebbe und Flut, p. 260 ff. (see n. 65).

Just as this end awaits a moon-body, so it awaits all the planets that are drawn into the sun. Their orbit around the central center of gravity of the whole system, it is said, grows ever narrower, quite apart from intermittent changes. The planets, however, run in ellipses around the sun, and at different speeds. The sun itself stands in one focus of their ellipses, and thus not in the ideal center of the orbit. Consequently every planet is once during its revolution near the sun (perihelion) and once far from the sun (aphelion). Its period of revolution is its “year”; for the earth it amounts to 365 days, for Mars 687 earth-days, for Mercury, as the nearest to the sun and therefore hastening in a rapid revolution, 88 earth-days. Since the planets do not all enter their aphelion and their perihelion at the same time, it happens that neighboring planets of different sizes approach one another so closely that the larger exerts a faintly deflecting [p. 194] effect on the smaller. The attracted smaller planet will indeed move away again, but from then on its orbit has shifted somewhat toward the larger brother. Moreover the planetary orbits are continually narrowing. The less rapid a planet’s revolution around the sun, and the less dense and the specifically lighter it is, the further the narrowing of its orbit will advance. Thus in the end there come approaches so close that from a given moment the smaller planet no longer passes the orbit of the larger but is gently drawn around by it and begins to move toward it. From this moment on it receives a gravitational acceleration toward the attracting planet, whose satellite it has now become. To the inhabitants of that planet it appears at first in increasing size, but then the repelling effect of the tides sets in, and the conditions discussed above for moon and earth ensue.

So too, it is said, the moon was originally a planet between Mars and the earth, and was captured in ages past in the manner described, without catastrophe. A proof of such captures of moons one may well still see in the fact that other planets also have moons which describe circles around their principal body that lie in part in a wholly opposite direction or not at all in the equatorial plane of rotation; so the moons of Jupiter. How the moon may have been captured is described very vividly by the new cosmogonic doctrine in terms of the possible future chaining of the smaller Mars to the earth. Both experience a continuous shrinking of their orbits toward the sun. But these shrinkings advance in such a way that Mars, which suffers more from the fine consumption of energy, gradually comes so near the earth’s orbit that, given the considerable fluctuations of their respective orbital eccentricities, there takes place at some time a last perihelion approach of Mars to the earth’s then existing aphelion.
