Then the attraction of the earth will prevail so far that Mars, instead of moving away again, is turned out of its orbit and from then on travels on in company with the earth, as the moon does now. In the same way the “planet Luna” of that time was captured and made a satellite of the earth.

[p. 195] Let us now imagine what proselenic men must have experienced before the moon was captured. At any rate they felt no catastrophic event. For many generations they would have noticed among the wandering stars an upper planet which came into opposition every year and a half and made itself known as a conspicuous little red disk, far surpassing Jupiter and Venus in brilliance at such times. When the earth and this planet came into immediate proximity, with the earth at aphelion and the planet at perihelion, the earth exerted an ever more powerful perturbation of orbit on Luna and, in proportion to their mutual masses, naturally the reverse as well. Both orbits therefore became ever more eccentric, the earth’s aphelion each time moved somewhat farther out, Luna’s perihelion farther in by about eighty times as much, so that finally an opposition so close arose that Luna no longer got past the earth’s orbit, was drawn around in front, and so for the first time came as a moon into the position of new moon. This capture of the moon was so uncatastrophic and simple an event that the planetary orbit of Luna was not broken off at all. For strictly speaking the moon still runs in its old orbit, which is merely wound around the earth’s orbit. If, then, the capture of the moon made itself noticeable to the men of that time, it was simply so: that at the penultimate opposition of Luna they already perceived the planet in the size of a small plate in the sky. After three or six years, at the time of closest approach, the little planet visibly and rapidly grew to the size of a dish or a wagon wheel. From then on it shone permanently in the sky as a moon, though not in the position and distance of today.

Proselenes who dwelt at that time in the interior of the land will have noticed nothing of further effects. But the coastal dwellers, and those who lived on flat land close to the seashore, experienced from then on, for the first time, ebb and flood, and the first flood is likely to have had a remarkable effect on them — not as a Flood in the sense of Eduard Sueß, but as a flood of the sea, which repeated itself steadily every day from then on and, as an everyday phenomenon, afterward did not remain legendary in the memory of tradition.

[p. 196] That the moon was for a time larger, hence nearer the earth and brighter, and then was perhaps partly driven off again according to the Darwinian principle that holds for a short time at first entry, is reported by a legend of the Jews which allegorically turns only on the enmity of cat and mouse. When the mouse complained to God of the cat, formerly not hostile to it, the Lord said to her: “Nasty creature, have you drawn no lesson from what happened with sun and moon? Both were equal in size and form; but because the moon slandered the sun, I made its light smaller and increased the light of the sun<sup>88</sup>.” How much can be divined from such remarks, legendary in their ring, about the former aspect of the heavens, and how much we may expect when these sources can first be opened up with sounder methods than we still have here!

<sup>88</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden, loc. cit., p. 211/12. An allusion to the late origin of the moon as such is also brought by an astrological compendium from the library of Ashurbanipal, which goes back to almost 3000 B.C. There it says: When the great gods entrusted the laws of heaven and earth to the shining moon, they let the crescent of the new moon come forth radiant, created the month and fixed the omens of heaven and earth, so that it might shine out radiantly in the sky and come forth radiantly in the midst of heaven. (A. Jeremias, Handbuch der altorientalischen Geisteskultur. Leipzig 1913, p. 131.)

A quite wonderful parallel to celestial motions that are conceivable precisely according to the Hörbigerian cosmological principle lies in a complex of legends which concern the marriage of the sun and “whose archaic appearance strikes the eye at once,” as Dähnhardt, from whom they are taken, remarkably says himself, without of course having thought in the remotest way of our great earth-historical concept of age. In all those legends one sees the picture of a dark world-body approaching the sun, with the danger of a union. Were it to happen, the dissolution of the stranger would lead to tremendous eruptions of the solar body, new masses of world-body would be thrown out, which would hasten into planetary space and beyond and would have to become harmful, even deadly, to the earth together with its inhabitants. The sun is darkened for a few days by the passing of the dark body, which has therefore remained invisible to the dwellers on earth, and does not shine until it emerges again from the ocean and until, evidently with the departure of the stranger from planetary space, the danger is then past; presumably a certain disturbance, though only a passing one, in the position of the earth toward the sun was bound up with the whole episode. Such, roughly, is the real astronomical-historical [p. 197] sense of the legends. Retold from several versions taken together, it runs:

Once the sun stood very low above the earth. It wanted to marry. Then men and animals were frightened and said: Even now the sun shines so hot in summer that stones and trees burst; if it now marries, many suns will be born and we shall all burn alive. When the sun had heard this, it dived unwillingly beneath and there was dark night. The animals fell into fear and terror. But the cock spoke: Be without care, I shall sing my song to her tomorrow early and fetch the sun out of the sea. He sang his song, but the sun did not appear. Thereupon he bathed on the next morning in the sea and beat with his wings. Then the sun saw it, and in answer to her astonished question the cock said to her: My friends wanted to marry me, to bring shame upon me; but there is nothing better than being single. When the sun heard that, she was glad that she need not marry and shone again as before, every morning<sup>89</sup>.

<sup>89</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 132/33.

The rescue of the sun from this danger of marriage is also given by an American legend, taken over from Asia, possibly, according to Dähnhardt: When God created the world, the Evil One did everything to destroy it and especially to harm the human race. From it he wanted to take the sun, since it could not live without light and warmth. Therefore he rose early in the morning, long before the sun had risen, and thought to swallow it. But God knew of his plan and made a crow to thwart him. When the sun now rose, the Evil One came and opened his jaws to swallow it. But the crow, which had lain in wait, flew into his throat and saved the sun<sup>90</sup>.

<sup>90</sup> Dähnhardt, ibid., p. 145/46.

It is hardly necessary to explain the picture further, so simple and natural is it when one gives it the interpretation above. Only one more remark, possibly serving later, more penetrating, more exact researches, may be made. If that foreign world-body circled near the sun, its mass had to reinforce the attraction of the sun upon the planets for the time of its near circuit, and so bring about either a temporary greater drawing-in of the planets, thus also of the earth, or [p. 198] certain disturbances in the position of the planetary bodies in their orbit around the sun. It is further clear that the phenomenon was not to be observed in the same way everywhere on the earth, just as an ordinary solar eclipse does not show the same covering of the disk everywhere and in many places is not visible at all. Thus a temporary change in the earth’s course and a greater approach to the sun and then withdrawal from it could produce in the different quadrants or zones of the earth a positively opposite picture, and in one region a long-lasting total darkness, in another region nothing more than a passing apparent standstill or an apparent slowing of the course of sun and moon. With this the narrative of Joshua would be explained too, and an identity of the cores of this and that legend made probable.

The image, frequently employed in the traditions as in ordinary late-mythological language, of the swallowing of sun and moon during eclipses should therefore not be taken, in the interpretation of the legends, in a flatly allegorical sense. To be sure, in Greek times and also among the later Babylonians men fabled of the threatening of moon and sun by monsters and meant this allegorically each time when eclipses occurred. But the mode of expression and of conception itself surely takes its point of departure from other and probably very ancient natural events, perhaps even transmitted only mythically, astrophysical events or catastrophes, or an occasional and again passed-over threatening of the known heavenly bodies by foreign intruders or stars thrown out of their course, as the legend of the marriage of the sun tells.

On the sun<sup>91</sup> a hail of meteors falls without interruption; they are foreign bodies coming from cosmic space. It was assumed hitherto, for the most part, that the impact of such foreign bodies produces the sunspots by breaking through the cooler outer gas shell and so giving the hotter gas within an exit. As in the Bunsen burner the darker core of the flame is the hotter, but the outer, yellow-glowing edge the less hot part of the flame, so the hotter gases emerging from within [p. 199] too should burn darker and so produce the appearance of the sunspots. The sunspots are often of tremendous extent; thus a large spot of 1894 was as large as Scandinavia. If the spots were merely openings struck by meteors in the shell of glowing gas, this would have to close again soon. But this is not so; rather the spots often remain for a very long time, even for more than a year, and change in the process; pictures arise at times that recall a whirling motion of the edges.

<sup>91</sup> H. Voigt, Eis ein Weltbaustoff, p. 253 ff. (see n. 64).

The surface of the sun consists, then, doubtless of glowing metallic gases and is some 300,000 km deep. Beneath it lies a core of high specific gravity, a metallic core, whose diameter is probably more than half the diameter of the sun, which corresponds to the distance earth–moon. The outer shell is held fast attractively by the core and can therefore not escape as a whole into space. The meteor hail steadily falling keeps it in stormy motion. Through it so much heat is supplied to the sun, in the impact, as converted energy of motion, that the loss by radiation is amply made good, indeed that the sun may possibly become still hotter in the future. “Whoever has once seen a well-developed sunspot through a rather large telescope,” says Voigt, “will have gained the vivid impression that we have here to do with a phenomenon which owes its existence to an external cause. This cannot have limited itself to knocking a hole in the solar shell, but must have carried forces into the photosphere which cause it to expel the foreign body again; these reactions, however, call forth the manifestations of force standing in closest connection with the spots, such as northern lights, earth-magnetic disturbances, the falling of cosmic dust. It is established that when a spot has passed through the meridian turned exactly toward the earth, some fifteen hours later one of the named phenomena makes itself noticeable on the earth. But if this connection cannot be contested, then the spots must be real formations, and it is our task to investigate how they arise and what forces may be present in them that produce these effects.”

[p. 200] Among the meteors falling into the sun there are perhaps for the most part ice-bodies from the Milky Way ring. In contrast to metallic meteors these have a low specific gravity and therefore penetrate only to a slight depth into the photosphere. They are at once decomposed into their elements oxygen and hydrogen, when they are small; but if they are large, then there occurs what has already become known to us (p. 158) in the penetration of an extinct but water-bearing world-body into a fixed-star sun as the cause of a new world: they remain preserved at first up to a certain heating. But since they are nevertheless relatively small, streams of vapor soon break through the slag mantle surrounding them, which will have about the structure of pumice. The mass of ice, however, is preserved in spite of this long enough that it can sink deeper, down to a zone in which the buoyancy of the mass corresponds to its weight. At last the ice has passed into water and now becomes superheated steam. Now this presses outward in the direction of least resistance, thus in most cases in a radial direction. As from the safety valve of a steam boiler, a continuous jet of steam blows out and so creates an opening that for the time being no longer closes: the sunspot.

Certain portions of the steam are decomposed at the glowing walls of the blow-pipe into the chemical elementary substances oxygen and hydrogen. But the remainder hastens out, forms prominences and cosmic dust, which arises from a combination of the oxygen freed by the decomposition with the metallic gases of the outermost photosphere and with the simultaneously blown-apart oxidation and slag products of the old mantle and of the wall of the explosion funnel. If the explosion and the unfolding of prominences is so strong that water vapor and dust are blown out into planetary space and escape the gravitational attraction of the solar body, then we have on the earth all manner of phenomena, from the northern light to the falling of cosmic dust and fine ice.

It is indeed also possible that a very large ice-body or a larger planetoid body was slowly captured by the sun and drifted about for long centuries in planetary space [p. 201] until it came nearer and nearer to the sun, whereupon either its plunge into the solar body followed at last, or it sped away from it once more at raging velocity and, wholly thrown out of its course, had to approach another star of planetary space, thus perhaps the earth. Such a body had to be noticed for a long time also by the dwellers on earth and in its final circuit to bring about a catastrophic process, though only of quite short duration. Earth-magnetic and electrical phenomena were released in the earth’s atmosphere, as also the world-body set aglow gave off matter in flame- or streak-form and, at its last or perhaps only approach to the earth and its near solar circuit shortly before, may have produced a great effect of heat. But if it had already plunged into the sun before, then its explosive expulsion there, later, by analogy with the formation of world-bodies described earlier, may have produced a catastrophic and swiftly passing effect on the planets near the sun or only on the earth.

If one accepts such ideas, at least in principle, the legend of Phaëthon, who wrongfully appropriates the steeds of his father Helios and perishes in the process, causing disaster, acquires a clear, natural sense. To be certain of his descent from the sun-god, the youth seeks him out in the nearby castle of his rising, demands the sun chariot for a day and mounts it in spite of all the entreaties and warnings of his father. Soon the horses bolt, and a dreadful confusion arises. Many mountains and rivers are withered forever, Libya has become a desert, the Ethiopians have become Moors, the Nile has hidden its sources ever since. At last Zeus hurls his lightning, and Phaëthon plunges, shattered and burnt, into the Eridanus, where the nymphs bury him and his sisters, the three Heliades, bewail him until they are changed into trees, from which golden tears still ever drip. The sun changes the tears into the shining electron, which the Eridanus carries through northern lands of peoples into the Oceanus<sup>92</sup>.

<sup>92</sup> Preller-Robert, Griech. Mythologie, p. 438/39.

We can read out of this legendary report that the planetoid or sun-expelled body, as offspring [p. 202] of the sun-god, was formerly a well-known and for a long time harmless-seeming celestial form, until through disturbances of its orbit it came too near the sun, produced convulsions there, fell aglow, then plunged into the sun and, expelled again in altered form, was sent to the earth, or earlier, in any case, had entered the earth’s orbit and here, outshining the warmth of the sun and, through its nearness itself now appearing like the sun but far surpassing its heat, brought about a catastrophe, until somewhere, perhaps over a northern sea, it came down, burst and, as a soon-extinguished fall of meteors, came to earth. But what may be especially valuable in the whole narrative is the connection with the amber trees. Amber was a very striking article of trade in the Mediterranean region and came from the north. We know that it is a fossil resin from sunken Tertiary forests, where it arose by strong exudation from the trees and became fossil in the mud of the bottom. Today it is obtained industrially on the coast of Samland, after it had hitherto been washed out of those older strata on the bottom of the Baltic by the waves and cast ashore. From there, from the land of the Hyperboreans, it came in antiquity as an article of trade to Greece. Thus from the singular connection with the amber trees in the Phaëthon legend there leaps out, possibly, a primeval relation which indeed also lives in the Germanic world of legend. Whether the still quite unique Hyperborean amber deposit, in spite of other, weaker occurrences, had itself anything to do with the heat effect of the planetoid Phaëthon may be left undecided, but is, even if improbable, still worth considering; yet it might well seem that Tertiary man up there shared in experiencing the spectacle when the cosmic body plunged in, and that he himself perhaps created the legendary connection which, after endless time, suddenly shines toward us in the Greek version of the legend.

[p. 203]

### Sternsagen — Star Legends

Once the devil stole the heavenly power from the Lord and with its help created a crystal heaven. The archangel Michael wanted to take it from him again. The devil dived under the sea. Meanwhile God covered the sea with ice, and the devil had trouble getting up again. A Serbian tradition paints it somewhat differently and reports that the devil could not get out and thereupon dived down once more and fetched a stone with which he broke through the ice and chased after the archangel, through whose blowing the sea had covered itself with thick ice. But Michael turned around, tore the wings off Satan so that he fell into the sea; according to another version: so that he burst into pieces, which rained down for four days<sup>93</sup>.

<sup>93</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 136ff and 224.

One sees at first glance in the legend, if one has once taken a natural-historical view of the matter at all, that here, in a not even very opaque disguise, an action from cosmic space upon the earth is presented, as a consequence of which a great formation of ice set in. The mass of ice, covering the sea as well, was broken through by volcanic eruptions which were very strong and went far up into the atmosphere. Later a tremendous fall of meteors lasting several days seems to have given the spectacle its conclusion — and from all this there again emerges the connection of cold or action of water from the universe, formation of ice on the earth, volcanic paroxysms and disintegration of a wandering cosmic body into meteors and their falling to the earth, whereby the ice-sheet then finds its end and warming sets in again. In this very legend, if one reads it in Dähnhardt, one can again follow the later form, differing by region, which finally makes use of biblical persons again, is retold later and recast, but preserves a real primeval cosmological core. Here too, by the way, in one version, the darkening of the sun plays a part [p. 204] which Michael rescued again, so that one finds in it a further moment showing that the narrative has a cosmic foundation in the sense just indicated. The storm-like phenomena unavoidable here, whether in connection with the volcanic eruptions or, what is more probable, with the cosmic evolutions, are suggested by a Bulgarian version of the legend, in which Elias drives with his fiery chariot, which thus reminds us of the connection mentioned in the Greek Phaëthon legend.

The origin of two stars is reported in the Edda, but no one knows so far which constellation is meant thereby, says Jakob Grimm<sup>94</sup>. Thus Thör comes to Gröa with a basket on his back, in which he had carried her husband, the bold Örvandill. Örvandill’s toe, it is said, stuck out of the basket and froze, whereupon he broke it off, threw it up to the sky and made a star of it, which is called Örvandilstá. Another feature of the myth is that Örvandill went out on adventures and in the course had lost the toe set in the sky by the god. The whole legend hangs together with the stone in Thör’s head, which he wants to be rid of, for which Gröa, Örvandill’s wife, is to speak a spell, which she forgets over the story told her by Thör, so that Thör does not get the stone out of his head.

<sup>94</sup> Jac. Grimm, Deutsche Mythologie, 4th ed. by E. H. Meyer, Berlin 1876, p. 603 and 611.

The other is the narrative that the Aesir killed the giant Thiassi, and that Odin took his eyes and threw them up to the sky, where they formed two stars. The legend hangs together with a more detailed, cosmogonically suggestive legend, which, however, perhaps preserves a later memory belonging to the history of humanity. It is that of Thör and Hrungnir<sup>95</sup>.

<sup>95</sup> Golther, Germanische Mythologie, p. 268 ff.

Let one imagine the possible disturbances in cosmic space and within our solar system that have become intelligible through the Hörbigerian doctrine of the universe, and one will easily feel a general cosmic-historical sense in these cycles of legends, without being able, of course, to specify now the particular cosmological event. At any rate, a very far-reaching memory of man seems to be present here itself [p. 205] Let me give the legends after Golther with some abridgments and adaptations in sense: Thör, the terror of the giants, drove, ringed by flames, into the stone mountains to the cave-dweller. Jord’s son drove to the fight, beneath him the moon’s path, the heaven, resounded. The air was filled with fire, hail came down, the earth burst, when the goats drew the god of the chariot to the strife with Hrungnir. The mountains quaked, fell, the sea surged up. The giant lost heart when he saw the battle-bold god; he threw the yellow shield under his soles; not long did the rock-man need to wait for the cast of the hammer. The slayer of giants brought the monster down over his shield, so that he bowed before the sharp hammer. Yet a hard splinter of the giant’s stone weapon flew into Thör’s skull, until the wound-healer loosed it with a spell. Thör had gone east to smite monsters. Odin rode to Jotunheim. He came to the giant Hrungnir, who asked him what manner of man that was who rode with a golden helmet through air and sea. Odin replied: in Giantland there was no equally good horse. Then Hrungnir sprang angrily on his horse to catch Odin. He escaped, but the giant pursued him as far as Asgard. There they invited him to the drinking-feast and he received the cups from which Thör was wont to quench his thirst. Hrungnir became drunk and boasted he would bring Valhalla to Jotunheim, sink Asgard and kill all the gods, except Freya and Sif; those he meant to carry off with him. Thör stepped into the hall and, angry at Hrungnir’s visit, swung the hammer in the air; he should atone for it before he left. Hrungnir answered that it was small glory for Asathör to kill him, a weaponless man; greater would be the feat if he met him on the border of the lands in the district of the stone enclosures; it had also been a great folly to leave his shield and his stone club at home. Thör is accompanied in the duel by Thjalfi, and at Hrungnir’s side stands Mokkurkalfi, a clay giant, whom the giants had made big beforehand; they also put into his body the heart of a mare, which, however, proved little steadfast. Hrungnir, on the other hand, bore a heart of hard stone. As weapon he swung a whetstone. So they awaited Thör. Thjalfi calls [p. 206] to Hrungnir that Thör will fare into the earth and attack him from below, and therefore Hrungnir pushes the shield under his feet and stands upon it. Hammer and whetstone meet, the stone breaks in two; the one half falls to the ground; from it come all whetstone rocks. The other half flies into Thör’s forehead, so that he collapses. Thjalfi had meanwhile brought the cowardly clay giant down. Hrungnir had fallen forward, one of his feet lay on Thör’s neck. Neither Thjalfi nor the other Aesir were able to lift it away. Then came Magni, the son of Thör and Jarnsaxa, who was then only three nights old. He threw Hrungnir’s foot from Thör’s neck and said: What a pity that I came so late, I would have beaten the giant to death with my fist if I had met him earlier. Thör went home to Thrudwang; the broken-off piece of the whetstone still stuck in his head. So much for the report, to which the narrative of the sorceress Groa mentioned above and the rest then attach.

Golther now gives the following interpretation of the whole legend: “The basic idea of this Thor legend is clear, especially in the skaldic poem: a thunderstorm crashing into the rocky mountains. The other details are harder to interpret. Hrungnir means the Noisemaker (rungla, to make a noise), Mokkurkalfi the Fog-Calf (mokkr, fog; kalfi, calf of the leg), an image of the bank of fog. The giant is made of clay, while Hrungnir rules in the rocky stone. Perhaps what is meant by this is the tough, watery clay soil at the misty foot of the rocky mountains. Clay and stone resist cultivation and are subdued by force. Landslide, mudslide, water and fog, all the phenomena of a thunderstorm raging in the mountains, may have worked together in the notion that meanwhile Thor struck the giant. Uhland explains it beautifully: ‘Up the clay hill, on the mountain slope, laborious cultivation stirs; above, the monstrous rock-horn juts out, on which a thunder-cloud flashes and thunders, so that the whole massif suddenly trembles. The field workers look up, and behold! the rock becomes a stone giant; in the cloud stands the fiery charioteer Thor, hurling the crushing hammer. Then Thjalfi feels that he is not working alone; a mighty god is helping him, and while he does the trifling work, that one [p. 207] accomplishes the great and has already done the hardest part in advance.’ The rest fits the interpretation by nature less naturally.”

No, and again and again no, to these harmlessly aestheticizing, one is almost tempted to say charming explanations, which all too much recall the bourgeois Sunday stroller with his harmless friendship for nature. They were mighty catastrophes of a cosmic kind which, with the whole force of apocalyptic events, impressed themselves upon the soul of primordial man and now live on undimmed in myth. Even if the skalds themselves later had no inkling any more of the original natural violence symbolized here: the primeval age had experienced it as myth, as one great, mighty event or as a chain of cosmic entanglements lasting centuries, perhaps millennia. For what could have made the everyday thunderstorm so urgently terrible? What would have moved — to say it once more — the nature-strong primordial man to chisel out in stone, in such elaborate, profound myths, so everyday and essentially harmless a natural phenomenon as the thunderstorm? Where and when does a mountain massif tremble from a thunderstorm? Where and when does an earthly force of nature create “whetstone mountains”? Where and when do limbs of giants, shields, hammers, or riders with golden helmets fly through space? Where and when do giants hurry from one end of the world to the other and wish to bring the one across to the other? In the thunderstorm over the green meadow, or at the foot of the mountain horn? No, again and again no: here mightier events are being told.

Set against this a description such as H. Voigt gives on the basis of the glacial-cosmogonic doctrine of a dissolution of a satellite<sup>64</sup>, which fits almost equally well the capture of a body penetrating from farther space, or of some other planetoid. Here the mighty images from the Revelation of John come alive, and these are what essentially resemble the old Nordic legends of gods and giants of the above kind. One need only read them over and one will soon feel the kinship, as well as the impossibility of comparing such myths with harmless scenes of nature. Such a capture of a satellite made the sea surge up on the earth; it drew out its body, shattered into pieces, into long streaks of fog [p. 208]; the pieces flew into the sea and created new masses of rock on the body of the earth; they struck one another and raced around the earth and through space; they released parts of themselves and struck other bodies with them. These are the hammers, these the shields, these the golden helmets, these the limbs, these the chunks of stone that strike into the body of the god and the giant.

<sup>64</sup> P. Fauth, Hörbigers Glazialkosmogonie (see note 47). For an introduction to the structure of ideas of the very prolix, if in content grand, work, better suited and also used here is: H. Voigt, Eis, ein Weltbaustoff. Einführung in Fauth-Hörbigers Glazialkosmogonie (mit Atlas). Berlin-Wilmersdorf 1920. H. Fischer, Die Wunder des Welteises. Berlin-Wilmersdorf 1922. The doctrine has so far been examined with particular care neither by astronomical nor by geological specialist science, let alone recognized; I myself got to know it only after working through my ideas, and took over from it what stands in the text. I am not in a position to represent the mathematician and astronomer; but from the earth-historical standpoint some objections can indeed be raised against details of the doctrine, not against its overall idea. Thus the “Ice Age” is a concept that is very much misunderstood; the immense ice covering depicted did not exist. If one draws a map of the earth for the Ice Age in an equal-area projection, that is, not in the usual Mercator projection (cf. the map by F. Levy in E. Dacqué, Grundlagen und Methoden der Paläogeographie, Jena 1915), one notices what a weak phenomenon the diluvial ice sheet was, practically not noticeable at all for the more southerly countries. Catastrophic inundations likewise did not occur in southern countries since the end of the Tertiary; we see, for example, in Egypt and the adjoining Sahara, where precisely the stratifications of the geological past since the Early Tertiary are excellently known, no trace of such catastrophic or marine eroding action as the glacial cosmogony with its hypothetical Tertiary-Diluvial capture of the moon demands. Its deductive application is simply not so easy. The geological formations are neither, except in limited places, sharply separated petrographically or temporally, and so are not earth-historical periods universally divided by interruptions; nor have the marine displacements of the former world ever proceeded uniformly on both hemispheres; nor do they throughout have anything catastrophic; nor are the formations of mountains unique paroxysmal processes, but for the most part extend over longer periods of the earth, even though at times they matured to quite special violence. The fossils too have arisen not throughout, but only occasionally and locally, through washing together. It is just here that the glacial cosmogony, in its present formulation, makes the grave mistake of throwing together the ages and animal worlds of earth history instead of keeping them chronologically and neatly separated; yet that would be the immovable, only secure foundation for any research into the former world, be it earth-historical or cosmological or phylogenetic or concerned with the history of man and of legends. Of the catastrophic extinction of animal worlds asserted there, too, nothing is to be noticed in earth history, except in narrowly bounded regions. It is furthermore quite incorrect that the individual geological periods or epochs would be characterized by an animal world repeatedly and completely dying out; on the contrary. Not only the great majority of the stem-types but also many special generic forms pass over from one epoch into the other, and where we can directly observe the closed sequence of strata from one age into the other, there is not throughout any question of a sharp faunistic-floristic dividing line at all. That the Hörbigerian doctrine, however, brings and will bring quite unsuspected astrophysical, as well as cosmological and earth-historical prospects and insights, the near future will surely prove. For research into earth history stands in a crisis, where it feels that with the older catastrophe-free Anglo-Saxon doctrine of transformation (Lyell) not much more is to be got out of the past. A way out of this narrowness will be found above all by a renewed taking in of cosmic influences on the aging planet. The struggle over the new doctrine is in the midst of becoming and every decision is premature. A detailed refutation by specialist scholars is attempted in a collective volume published by the “Bund der Sternfreunde,” “Weltentwicklung und Welteislehre” (Verlag Die Sterne, Potsdam 1925). It is, as with all emerging scientific theories, impossible to arrive at a conclusive judgment. However mistaken many details are presented on both sides, and however justified every critical examination of new ideas is, one must nonetheless, as was already stressed in the 1st and 2nd editions, well distinguish the truth content of every theory from its insufficient elaboration into a doctrine. For in the end it has always been the seers in a science who placed knowledge on a new ground, on which the individual research afterward again had to plow for a long time. Were the glacial cosmogony, after its present exaggerations, to confine itself to developing its central idea of cosmic supplies in the form of fine ice, veil ice, ice bolides and planetoid bodies, and to founding this earth-historically and also astrophysically, then it could in fact offer a full explanation for weighty and unsolved geological problems, such as the question of the permanence of continents and oceans, of the ice ages and of climatic change, indeed perhaps of the formation of fold mountains, of earthquakes and of the periodicity of volcanism, as it has already conveyed to us in principle a general explanation of many legend-images and above all of the event of the Flood.

In all mythologies we find this cosmic-catastrophic picture again and again. How manifold the forms in which it is offered to us, always written in the same primordially mighty tone, which leaves far behind it anything earthly and thunderstorm-like and can be expressed only in images of giants and gods. Let me recall, for instance, the Babylonian Ishtar legend, where likewise a celestial body that had not been there before, namely the heavenly bull created by his father Anu, comes racing along and perishes in combat with the warrior Engidu, who tears off its thigh and strikes it down<sup>96</sup>.

<sup>96</sup> Das Gilgameschepos. In: Die Religion der Babylonier und Assyrer. By A. Ungnad (vol. III of the “Religiösen Stimmen der Völker”). Jena 1921, p. 83. Here, then, Engidu is beyond doubt not the simple primordial man he appears as in the earlier part of the epic, but a star that meets the wandering celestial body; whereby the lack of unity in the materials of the epic and its figures is demonstrated.

The fairly numerous star legends and the traditions according to which stars collided — evidently such things were observed at various times, or one was drawn into the affair — drag themselves finally even into the Christian legend narrative, like the Germanic-pagan feasts into the Christian calendar of feasts, and show once again how little the garment matters once the essential core is grasped. Thus Magyar legends report that Jesus and Peter drove around on the Milky Way and let straw fall from the wagon; Peter had a blind horse and met a drunkard; their two wagons ran into each other and Peter lost the straw. In another tradition this happened to the “Gypsy,” that is, to the star Altair. Any commentary here could only cloud the clear, cosmically self-evident description of the event. The same legend lives in Hungary, according to which Christ and Peter meet a mangy dog, which Peter gave a wide berth. Soon afterwards they met a drunkard, which Christ now gave a wide berth. To Peter’s astonished question the Savior replied: Look, Peter, that mangy dog harms nobody; but this swine attacks everyone; therefore one must keep out of his way. Since then the “highway” in the sky has been split. According to [p. 209] another version Christ steps aside from the drunkard at the constellation Cepheus; the tavern where he got drunk is a few stars of Cassiopeia. Still other traditions call these stars the tavern and the eye of Cepheus the mangy dog; the drunkard is the star Deneb<sup>97</sup>.

<sup>97</sup> Dähnhard, Natursagen II. Berlin 1909, p. 83 and 275.

All sorts of significant astronomical movements and catastrophes in the starry sky still shimmer through such traditions, which certainly were no mere allegories and can easily be stripped of their moral garment, which they received only afterwards. A methodical research will one day be granted to recognize here, as in other legends, the natural-historical, perhaps former-world connections and events, and to describe them as such. In the same substratum the legend of the “devil’s stone,” applied to all possible landscape scenes, may also ultimately be rooted.

That the stars or heavenly bodies again and again acted upon the earth is eloquently expressed by still other legends. The Arabic scholar Qazwini gives in his cosmography of the thirteenth century the following tale, which once more demonstrates the astronomical conditionedness of the Flood: The three stars at the left hand of Aquarius the Arabs call the lucky star of a swallower, because they were compared to a mouth opened to swallow. Others, however, derive the name from the fact that the star rose at the moment of the Flood when it was said: O earth, swallow up thy waters<sup>98</sup>.

<sup>98</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 289.

Again and again the action of stars, that is, of cosmic bodies, perhaps illuminated by the sun before their entry into the earth’s orbit and therefore appearing as moving fixed stars, even if they were exclusively bodies of ice. A part may well have withdrawn again from the earth’s sphere of attraction and then gradually moved away, so that after the Flood the spectacle of moving, fixed-star-like points may long have continued in the sky, which then may have created the tradition of fixed stars bound fast once more. At any rate the helplessness of explanations other than natural-historical ones, such as are put into our hands by Hörbiger’s theory, emerges here again too, when [p. 210] one reads, as an introduction to the tradition just reproduced, in Dähnhardt, from whom we take it: “It would be a strange lack if the Noah legends had not also drawn the stars into their domain.” No: the transmitters of the most ancient happenings drew in nothing arbitrarily, but stood under the impression and the consciousness of most overwhelming reality!

Herewith the difference can now be felt between the symbolism which reinterprets Noah’s ark as the moon (p. 26), and a symbolism like the one aimed at here, which does not import an image seen from outside, but seeks to present, out of the essence of the matter, the earth-historical, the lived experience beheld by man and deeply impressed upon him. For the one, the symbol is the unreal; for the other, the real, that which essentially is.

That for the ancients and their traditions the region of the sky around Sirius, that is, the constellation of Orion, was a kind of cosmic weather corner is evident not only from the comparatively clear Babylonian legend of the star Ishtar, but also from the stuff, still quite confused for us, which Greek mythology transmits about Orion. I draw out of Preller-Robert some things which I believe could at all events be utilized in the manner indicated above<sup>99</sup>. Orion is the giant with the brandished club, the wild hunter of the Greek sky, whom one pictured now and then hunting in the mountains and forests, whose shade Odysseus saw in the underworld, driving before him there too the game in herds that he had once killed in the mountains, swinging the brazen club in his hands. Or one told of monstrous works and depicted him as a sea-giant who churns up the ocean, darkens the sky with dense clouds, or drags great rocks together, building headlands and harbors. Another time he lays hands on the wife of Oenopion, the god of wine-growing of Chios, who blinds the drunken man; Orion gropes his way to Lemnos, seizes there a companion of Hephaestus, sets him on his shoulders, and lets himself be led by him toward the sunrise, where the light of his eye is kindled anew at the rays of Helios. Then he hurries back to avenge himself on Oenopion [p. 211], whom he does not find. When, hurrying to Crete, he boasts that he can exterminate all the game on the whole earth, the angered earth-goddess sends a scorpion that stings the giant to death. Or it is told that he dared to compete with Artemis in discus-throwing and was therefore killed by her. Or she shoots him when Apollo shows her the dark head of the giant swimming in the sea. According to another legend he is to woo there the pomegranate banished by Hera into the underworld, or to have been born of the earth himself.

<sup>99</sup> Preller-Robert. Griechische Mythologie, p. 448 ff.

If one lets all such myths act freely upon oneself, and if one has once grown accustomed to the thought that from stellar space, or perhaps even merely from interplanetary space, planetoids and foreign bodies can penetrate to the earth and in earlier times perhaps penetrated occasionally or periodically, once or several times, then all the phenomena which the Orion myth presents to us are explained without difficulty and naturally, even though the naming of localities no longer has anything to do with the essence of the myth, and a geological dating cannot be ventured for lack of other points of reference. That the constellation of Orion itself did not reach the earth is clear. But if foreign celestial bodies came from this, as we said, “cosmic weather corner,” they had to appear at once larger and more luminous than the radiant Sirius of Orion itself. If such a body did not unite with the earth, and returned after a cometary circuit in the same direction from which it came into space, it could be beheld as a star returning home, whether it was self-luminous or only threw back sunlight. If it was not self-luminous but, like the moon, only sun-illumined, then at its approach to the earth it had to become dark in certain positions. It had become blind, blinded, and when, hurrying past the sun, it left the planetary space again, it either began to glow itself or soon appeared again in reflected sunlight. If parts of it broke off and came to the earth, then it lay stunned on the ground; or it plunged into the sea and one saw its dark head killed. In hurrying past it probably also stirred up the sea attractively by producing a flood; the then captured one was killed by the earth. Or a collapse [p. 212] into the earth burst the crust somewhere: he wooed the pomegranate shut up in the underworld, producing volcanic effects; perhaps he hurried on after having only touched the earth or struck it with a broken-off fragment. Had the whole body collided with the earth, it could have killed all life.

In ever new shape the restless, danger-threatening world of Orion returns in the legends. Thus among the Jews: Since in the time of the Flood the deeds of men were perverted, the Lord too reversed the order of creation and let the constellation of Sirius rise by day at that time, although in the month of Ijar, when the Flood broke out, it is the time when the constellation of Sirius sets by day. Then he tore two stars out of their place and the windows of heaven were opened and the Flood poured over the world. But after the Flood was over, the Lord wished to fill up the openings of heaven again. Then he took two stars from the Bear and covered the holes; but some day he will bring the stars back to their place<sup>100</sup>.

<sup>100</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden. I. p. 205/06.

This mysterious constellation of Orion, which borders on the Milky Way and from which, or at least from whose direction, foreign bodies and other material influences evidently came upon our planetary system, hence upon the earth too in primeval times, and could come again, is also in Germanic mythology always spoken of in a sense hinting at such a thing. Thus Orion was called a troop of wild boars, and Jak. Grimm adds again: why, he did not know<sup>101</sup>.

<sup>101</sup> Jac. Grimm (see n. 94).

From the various world-regions emissaries of quite different kinds may well have penetrated now and then into the planetary space — who knows whether we shall not experience it once again. The intruders were now cold, bodies of ice; now hot, bodies of glow; or dark and cold like bodies of stone and metal. Thus the following legends can be interpreted: “If Orion with his warmth were not there, the world would freeze up from the frost of Sirius; and if Sirius with his cold were not there, the world could not endure for heat”<sup>102</sup>. Or another, which clearly betrays the incoming body of ice, and which is handed down in the Bundehesch, a younger offshoot of the Zendavesta. [p. 213] In the first times of the world the star Tistar in threefold form brought the rain into the world. The earth was then filled with noxious creatures that the evil principle had created: Tistar rained in each of his forms for ten days. First the water rose to the height of a man upon the earth and all noxious creatures had to die. Then a heavenly wind came and swept the waters away; but the mud spread poison of putrefaction. In the form of a white horse Tistar came for the second time. The demon Apaosha stepped against him in the form of a black horse, to hinder his undertaking. Long the battle wavered, until at last Tistar is victorious. He strikes Apaosha with the lightning fire, who now strikes up a thunder-howl. Tistar now rains anew, and the poison of the noxious animals left on the earth mixes with this water and makes it salt. Again a great wind arose, which drove this water in three days to the end of the earth; from it arose three great and twenty-three small seas<sup>103</sup>.

<sup>102</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden. I. p. 205.

<sup>103</sup> Andree, Flutsagen, p. 14.

When a cosmic body approaches the earth, there arises not only a greater or lesser effect of flood and ebb in the sea, running in the orbital plane of this body, but the earth’s envelope of air too is drawn upward more strongly or more weakly in the same direction. As soon as that body bursts, or is dissolved in the sea of air on being captured, or unites in some other way with the body of the earth, not only does the effect of flood and ebb in the ocean cease, but also the tension and accumulation in the sea of air, which now returns to the normal form of the spherical shell. Strong currents of equalization arise, which run in the same direction as the ebbing of the flood-wave of the ocean and apparently, as the legend reports, drive the water to the ends of the world. Tistar was a cosmic, strongly water-laden body, perhaps a pure body of ice, which a dark body met in relation to the earth; perhaps it burst in its circuit, came again, and at the last bursting in the sea of air there arose the howl as of whizzing shells. Strong thunderstorms were the consequence and tremendous, Flood-like downpours. Thereafter not only was the water on the earth increased, the oceans fuller, thus the salt [p. 214] water of the earth’s oceans increased by the cosmically inflowing fresh water; but through the disturbance of the state of equilibrium of the earth’s crust connected with the process, a different distribution of land and sea also had to take place in places, soon or as a geotectonic aftereffect, and for both reasons the number of sea-surfaces could have been increased. Perhaps the whole process falls in a very ancient time of earth history, and if it has a geogonic character, then for its transmission as legend the same may hold as will be said later about the origin of the cosmogonic myths.

One need only bear in mind that the penetration of a cosmic foreign body into the planetary space or into the sphere of attraction of the earth does not take place as an isolated phenomenon, but that under circumstances a whole sheaf of larger and smaller splinters and bodies arrives, which perhaps in hurrying past other celestial bodies had already been more or less divided and now at their approach to the earth called forth all the various kinds of images that we have just let play past our mind. The apparent contradictions in the mythical description and the cosmological interpretation therefore do not exist in essence.

It was already mentioned above in the explanation of the Flood (p. 169) that one and the same cosmic event, if it took place in the neighborhood of the earth or acted directly upon it through an influx of matter, had to offer in the different zones also a quite different spectacle. For slowly approaching and gradually incorporated bodies, or dust and ice or masses of water, had to be drawn little by little into the plane of rotation of the equator. If now the Nordic legends tell mainly of shields and hammers and wagons and stones and mountains that hurried across the sky, whereas the legends arising more toward the equator speak of descending floods and fire, then this indicates just one and the same event, in which the Nordic man remained for the most part only a spectator, but the southern man was directly the one who suffered; but perhaps it also shows that the legends arose where they have been preserved through all times.

That outer space was water-dispensing, and that such influxes of water, perhaps occurring long before the Flood, set in to a considerable degree and could have been transmitted, [p. 215] seems to me to be indicated, on closer consideration under the light of glacial-cosmogonic notions, by the conception of Okeanos in Greek mythology. Commonly Okeanos is regarded partly as the world-ocean encircling the earth, conceived as a disk. Only later does Plato speak further of the western land which bounds that world-ocean. But besides this there seem to lie within it still more far-reaching, namely cosmic notions, which perhaps contain the older components, not yet spoiled and reinterpreted by Greek rationalism, which saw in spatial and temporal nearness, of a knowledge that had penetrated deeper into the cosmic past. Thus it runs in Preller’s Mythologie<sup>104</sup>: If Okeanos was there first, then the earth, even heaven itself, must have sprung from him, yet ordinary mythology gives no more definite hint about this; it knows Okeanos only as the general boundary of the world, as the ancient boundary-stream encompassing earth and sea, which with deep and mighty flood flows back into itself like a serpent and thereby forms the boundary of all visible things, while it itself is unbounded: a realm of wonder and of all the secrets of origin; its coasts and islands the home of the gods and of blessed men and peoples. There Okeanos himself also reigns, as an old-fatherly, but mild and all-friendly old man, who lives in his beyond as if outside the world and remains uninvolved in all the wars of the world. His consort is Tethys, the primeval grandmother. Hera grew up with this pair when the whole world of the gods was aflame in the battle of the Titans. Rivers, brooks and springs descend from Okeanos. In cosmogonic respect, however, among all the sons and daughters of Okeanos by far the most remarkable are Styx and Acheloos, the eldest daughter and the eldest son of the old water of origin.

<sup>104</sup> Preller-Robert, Griechische Mythologie, p. 544ff.

Hereby Okeanos is evidently not only the earthly world-ocean, but perhaps the glacial Milky Way supplying the planet with water. The knowledge of this connection of the feeding “world-ocean” with our earth-star, which is indeed reopened to us also by the glacial cosmogony that now unlocks so many doors to mythology, may have been present already in most ancient times by another path of knowledge.

[p. 216]

### Gondwanaland — Gondwanaland

The mythical tradition of the Japanese traces the age of the population there back to such immeasurably distant periods of time that even the boldest fantasy pales before it. And even if the millions of years,” it runs further in Helmolt’s Weltgeschichte, “which the son of the distant island empire believes he can look down upon with pride, are unable to withstand criticism, yet on the other hand there always lies a grain of truth beneath the chaff of national vanity.”

In the meantime we have indeed found the measure of the fantasy of the old Japanese myth no longer all too great. Even if the myth undoubtedly does not originate with the Japanese themselves, but must already have existed at their immigration, and perhaps was itself brought along in deep primeval time from the Polynesian or Indo-oceanic region and then regarded by the later inhabitants of the Japanese islands as having existed there from the beginning, the “grain of truth” is just perhaps a remnant of the great fact that we must seek the oldest, pre-Noachian men, and then the Noachian man himself, first of all in the region of the Indian and probably still of the more southerly Pacific Ocean, that is, in a continental region sunk or scattered there (p. 178). There alone is the place for that old, geologically proven continent which existed at the end of the Palaeozoic era, at that time even had a strong cover of glaciers and inland ice, and fell a victim to the floods recognizably only in late Mesozoic time. In this territory, extending perhaps with interruptions and in the form of island complexes formerly probably still into the Polynesian-Pacific region, I seek the dwelling place of the oldest humanity. There on that continent the main mass of the peculiar oldest and in part mammal-like reptile world also probably lived, while Polynesia, the Indian land-plate, Western Australia and the eastern half of Madagascar, as well as [p. 217] South Africa, are just remnants of the rim of that old Gondwana continent, for which reason, as already mentioned, finds of primeval men and remains of culture are most likely to be expected there. The sinking of the Gondwana continent, however, whose submergence in the floods extended over several geological ages and was completely accomplished only in the Tertiary, also stands somehow in connection with the catastrophe of the Noachian Flood.

On that continent a great glaciation prevailed in the Permian period; from a few stratigraphic profiles we can gather that it also advanced into the sea and possessed an enormous areal extent. The debris deposits of the ice streams have been demonstrated in India, Australia, southern and central Africa, and probably came down from mountain ranges which perhaps traversed the Gondwana continent or partly bordered it. The Adamitic and post-Adamitic primordial man could have lived through this ice age, and may thereby also have been driven to wander and to settle the wide circuit around his primal home. Later, after the end of that ice age, the great Gondwana continent was more habitable in the Mesozoic epoch, and into this fell, probably, the spread of Noachian man, who multiplied while the old post-Adamitic types still lived alongside him, until the continent gradually fell apart (p. 176) and was flooded, and until the Flood, at the end of the Mesozoic or at the beginning of the early Tertiary, exterminated those old men, who now perished with the inundation and submersion of the greater part of the continent, while the intellectually higher-standing Noachian man, in his representative, already highly developed by then and personified in the figure of the biblical Noah, knew how to save himself by means of his technical ability and timely precautions, inhabited the Lemurian remnant (p. 153) and emigrated.

If we now take as correct the earth-historical dating that we assigned to the appearance of the human being there on the physical earth, a number of legends at once come into their proper light. Thus an Arabic source tells: After the expulsion from Paradise Eve sat mourning on the desolate earth. No flower grew, and the snow fell thickly as a [p. 218] pall for the untimely burial of the earth after man’s fall. But an angel caught a snowflake, made a beautiful flower of it, and said that it should be a sign that summer and sun would return<sup>105</sup>.

<sup>105</sup> Dähnhard, Natursagen I. p. 224.

It was in any case a peculiar time, back then, when the southern Gondwana continent still existed in its whole extent and bore the presumed primordial man with his manifold forms. Terrestrial nature was then affected by changes so significant that the thought suggests itself that unknown cosmic forces may have been at play. Above all, the coincidence of quite different kinds of unusual conditions is striking: after a fold-mountain formation extending over the whole earth in the Carboniferous, that greater ice age sets in; yet only a part of the southern hemisphere is ice-covered, chiefly the region of the Gondwana continent, and also a part of South America, though probably not analogous to present-day Greenland and not under great cold, but after the analogy of New Zealand, where glaciers still reach from the mountains down to the sea, form only isolated streaks, and forests grow on their sluggishly advancing, debris-covered backs. The ice streams of the Gondwana continent may likewise be imagined as correspondingly larger and more extensive in area. The northern hemisphere was then dry, in contrast to this evidently more precipitation-rich Gondwana region, and had, at least over wide stretches, the character of a land that was indeed not everywhere without plants, yet in many parts desert-like, in which mighty downpours often interrupted the dry periods for a few days or for a longer time. Then the ice cover, not quite simultaneous and of varying strength in the different parts of the southern hemisphere, disappears; but the effect of drought remains in the north as in the south, and the great southern continent shows the first traces of its decay in the Triassic. If we assume that a larger cosmic body or — what amounts to the same in essence — a swarm of smaller bolides, perhaps gathered together or drawn out like a tail or arisen from the disintegration of a larger, planetoid-like body, approached the earth, circled it, was captured by it and finally [p. 219] united with it, and if this body or these bodies were essentially water-bearing or even consisted of ice itself, then a great and decisive part of the earth-historical conditions in the younger half of the Palaeozoic era, and their strangely contrasting coincidence, as well as their succession, is to some extent explicable. For if a world-body of unknown origin had come within the earth’s sphere of influence, its gravitational attraction was bound at once to bring great unrest into the world ocean and, according to its periodic approach to perigee and its renewed withdrawal to apogee, to produce alternating ebbs and floods, which in the shallow epicontinental seas and along the continental margins brought with them now longer, now shorter inundations, now dryings-out and swamplike conditions. The alternation may have amounted to millennia and hundreds of millennia. Such a world-body, circling the earth for a certain time on an eccentric orbit and having by chance become satellite-like, or a herd of world-bodies, exerted, however, its flood-producing effect also upon the less yielding earth’s crust and upon the subcrustal interior of the earth, which is always in states of tension and strives after a balancing of forces. Thus the crust fell into movements productive of mountains and simply conditioned by fracture — it is the picture of the Carboniferous, with a rapid alternation of floods, dryings-out and moorland formation, as also with its particular geotectonic unrest in the earth’s crust. But as soon as the body, or the masses of bodies, on their spiral orbit resulting from the effect of gravitation, drew nearer to the earth in the course of tens of thousands or hundreds of thousands of years and the orbit became less eccentric, marine transgressions with a general raising of sea level were bound to set in in the equatorial regions. What of the water-rich world-body, or mixture of world-bodies, drawn out into a spiral ring was finally captured, dissolved in the atmosphere and conveyed to the earth, was bound to lead to short-lived and frequently successive heavy precipitation in the form of downpours, and in higher regions, or regions otherwise especially predestined by their situation, to copious and frequent snowfalls. These were bound, on the warm southern hemisphere nearer the equator, indeed under [p. 220] the equator itself, hence on the Gondwana continent, to produce an ice cover — an ice age; yet on the equally warm but perhaps lower northern hemisphere to leave in existence a desert region periodically rich in precipitation, otherwise dry. But once the cosmic stranger was finally absorbed, the source of the ice cover too was exhausted, and its likewise descending cosmic stone and dust material was incorporated into the mechanism of the terrestrial cycle of deposition. All this need not have happened at once, just as the alien world-body and its drawn-out mass certainly were not consumed all at once. That volcanism, too, was bound to become very active in all the processes described is an inference that finds its confirmation in the geological findings from that epoch. Now with the beginning of the Mesozoic era an epoch set in in which we notice neither ice covers nor strong geotectonic and mountain-building unrest, in which the climatic contrasts vanished and the so thoroughly accomplished balancing of the intracrustal tensions was complete. With such balancings the Gondwana continent now went more and more toward its disintegration.

It is a much-discussed problem of earth-historical research whether the present continental surfaces, notwithstanding their demonstrable periodic flooding from the ocean, have remained permanent as such since ancient earth-historical times, or whether ocean floors themselves could be transformed into dry land and land surfaces into ocean floors. The chief obstacle to assuming so far-reaching an exchange of the two crustal elements consisted, besides considerations of states of equilibrium and conditions of gravity in the outer shell of the earth’s body, above all in the following calculation made by A. Penck: if one theoretically levels out all differences of height in the earth’s crust, the water now present would surround the ideal spherical surface as an envelope 2640 m thick. Now we know with certainty, however, that in all the earth-historical epochs surveyable so far there was always land, or at least shallow sea, in the region of the present-world continental surfaces, and that consequently the oceans themselves must always have been more than 2640 m deep and must have lain in the same place as today. To be sure, if [p. 221] a reason could be found for the assumption that during the known geological epochs the water on the earth had increased substantially or the circumference of the earth had diminished substantially, this calculation would no longer hold, and one might then reckon with a very far-reaching exchange of dry land and ocean floor, in particular also with the extensive sinking of continental surfaces. If, therefore, the new glacial-cosmogonic doctrine is valid in some form, and one could reckon with certainty on an influx of water from space for the epochs surveyable in earth history, then this central problem of palaeogeographical research too would gain a new face, a more far-sighted solution would become possible than has been had so far, where contradiction stood against contradiction; and one would thereby also come again to the recognition of cosmically conditioned catastrophes, as the heroes of earth-historical research at the end of the 18th and the beginning of the 19th century still divined and beheld, and with which the old legends, after the critical sifting undertaken for that purpose — which cannot be carried out here — would once more be no longer a phantasm or the expression of states of soul, but positively guides in penetrating to a more living earth history.

Thus it may perhaps yet one day become certain that we have not remained so cosmically unmolested as Lyellized geology still wants to maintain today, but that captured world-bodies with and without water, splinters and dust, just like ice-matter and ice-bolides, have entered into our sphere of attraction and have brought in here not only outpourings of water and weather catastrophes to flood-like proportions, but also lithic materials in the form of loess, rains of mud or sand, meteorites, indeed perhaps also dissolved larger bodies; that in doing so, when they were larger and approached or circled at varying distance, they produced floods which cyclically and materially influenced the formation of strata, disturbed the earth’s crust, released geotectonic tensions in the earth’s framework and so brought about not only great earthquakes but also vertical displacements of the continental crust, hence “Atlantic” or “Gondwanic” submersions, marine inundations and recessions, and altogether a lasting alternation of land and sea.[p. 222]

In earth-historical research we have been forced, with the old Lyellian system of the endless summation of the smallest processes into great planetary effects, to speak with E. Sueß, much too far into a comfortable conception of the earth-historical course. Just as with Ch. Darwin the accumulation of the smallest useful varieties was supposed to lead to the transformation of the specific character of living beings, so too the Lyellian earth-historical doctrine breathes the sated, catastrophe-hostile spirit of the time of the bourgeois 19th century. This way of thinking in science and in life has pushed the deeper values that lived in Cuvier’s old elemental doctrine of catastrophes quite into the background. Only lately do both earth history and biology come again to a stronger emphasis on the fact that times of quiet evolution alternated with times of revolutionary violence on the body of the earth and in the unfolding of life, as we too see it now again so monstrously in the life of the nations. Universe, earth history, life history and human existence mirror the same primeval rhythmic law.

[p. 223]

## Metaphysik — Metaphysics

“Is not the kernel of nature / in the heart of man?” Goethe.

[p. 224]

[p. 225]

### Das Metaphysische in Natur und Mythus — The Metaphysical in Nature and Myth

If we now compare, after all that has been set forth, our natural-historical interpretation of the core traditions of the human race, so often surprisingly concordant, with the one hitherto probably generally customary, the whole reality-potent essential character of the primeval legends and myths comes into full light. First, then, the naive natural-historical interpretation is directly justified. This, however, becomes possible if one abandons the older doctrine of descent, applies the type-theory and the law of temporal characters consistently to the origin of man, and thereby recognizes the high earth-historical age of the human stock as such. The new, though not yet fully matured, world-picture of glacial cosmogony first completed the natural-historical interpretation of those legendary events to which terrestrial nature, and within it the humanity of the former world, was exposed. Thereby we were able to give to the contents of legends and myths a temporal breadth and depth that they did not yet possess in the notion of researchers. And what has hitherto been crowded into a narrow prehistoric span of time acquires thus, spread out toward the earth-historical primeval age, a truly world-historical face.

This view is not, however, to be misunderstood as if we meant that in the core legends and myths of great antiquity only a natural-historical side were to be unveiled. In their essence they are certainly just as much symbolically determined. Everything outward, everything trivially real is appearance and as such likeness; but likeness not in the sense of an allegory brought in from outside, but likeness of what is immanent in it. As our body is the living expression of the soul immanent in it and the two are experienced by us together as the expression of an inexpressible higher unity, so too the natural-historical body of legend and myth is at the same time [p. 226] the immediately co-posited expression of a metaphysical occurrence, like every natural phenomenon as well. Therein, probably, are grounded also the ideas and actions, which strike us as so legendary, indeed almost mythical, of the old original, not later, astrologers and alchemists; in every star, in every substance they recognized and felt a real-metaphysical, of which the outwardly perceived body and substance is the co-posited necessary, not merely the emblematic, presentation. It is as with a scientific book or the work of a great thinker. One cannot read it if one does not livingly re-experience the connection of thought out of which the investigator created his work; otherwise, despite an outward understanding of the sentences, it remains dead or is received only dogmatically. For not everything, indeed often precisely not the inwardly finest, can be expressed in word and image; and whoever does not know the inner spirit is unable to grasp that unspoken and unspeakable something which stands between the lines and cannot be offered or received in any other way. Therein lies, by the way, the justified core of that often pointlessly stressed distinction which is made between the specialist and the layman, in the religious sphere between priest and people; which does not mean that he alone is a specialist who calls himself so. So too in the reading of nature and of history. It is not the analysis of the script, not the outward course of the sentences, not the classification and outward comparison of objects that discloses the meaning; but the living grasp of the inner essence, of the inner world. Therefore the wise of all times demanded of themselves and of others not only the outward contemplation of things — that was the exercise of the mind, to accustom it to ordered thinking — but they demanded absorption, the dissolving of the I into the All-One, in order to penetrate to the essence of things. For they recognized “that true understanding is faith.”

An explanation of myths, then, which wished to proceed only in a natural-historical way and believed its work done with an outwardly natural-historical resolution, would be, even with correct results, just as one-sided as an interpretation striving to unveil the metaphysical alone — to say nothing, for the moment, of the allegorical as something subordinate. Only both, comprehended and presented as the working-out of an [p. 227] all-embracing One, signify the unveiling of the essence of a myth; for only both together is reality at all.

Every life-and-suffering story of a mighty personality, or every great religious experience and event in humanity, necessarily becomes a myth, not merely a history, because the outward event, the outward gesture and form is always experienced as the naturally necessary symbol of a creative inner being that works itself out with power. Every such story of a life and of experience is at once or later recast into a myth or a gospel, in which it is able to communicate and present itself as a unity to others, to whom that immediately all-embracing, timeless vision has not yet dawned and who are not yet capable of worshipping without image and word.

Thus the origin of the most ancient myths too becomes clear to us in its root: they were never and nevermore a poem, as, say, Goethe’s Iphigenie was a poem, which develops a soulful or moral-spiritual connection into an ideal and mirrors it in its contrasts, in order to let the victory or downfall of the personality be experienced in it; but a myth in the primal sense is precisely what distinguishes it from every poem into which it might later be cast and has always been cast: the great experience of unity in the event beyond the personality, wherein this personality itself, or the historical-natural-historical sphere around it, is not simply reality, nor merely the intellectual emblem, but itself the co-created expression and the inherent counterpart of the metaphysical, both together the revelation of the unspeakable-eternal in two sides of its essence — as in great music and painting, where it is certainly not technical skill or sensuous reproduction, but unspeakable creative experience that is the essence of the sounds and forms, for which reason even the forms most impossible in the naturalistic sense can nevertheless bring the sense of metaphysical reality to expression. So, for instance, the origin and power of the sun myth and of the sun cult derived from it will also have been anchored in a mythical, not poetic, beholding and real experience [p. 228] of the natural sun also as a metaphysical, in the grasping of its Platonic idea in the transcendent-real, the creative. Who can put that into words! Only later followed a re-poeticizing and elaboration into myths and religions, when the mythical beholding and primal experience itself was already no longer original, no longer native. And at last flat allegories were made of them besides, a stage in which our tradition of myths and legends today still remains almost entirely.

If we wish to give a clear definition of the concepts legend and myth, as both present themselves according to our reflections, then legends are all those traditions in which the simply human- and natural-historical is the core; myths, on the other hand, those traditions in which the outward experience and the rendering of images is the counterpart of an inwardly unspeakable, indeed perhaps merely transcendent, occurrence. That we in fact scarcely ever meet a myth that does not also contain a simple legendary core, and that conversely we so often see legends used as a means of clothing mythical events, alters nothing in the fundamental character of this definition. For fairy tales too, as soulfully significant narratives, not seldom attach themselves to legendary elements or work them up, without our being able on that account to dispense with the concept of the fairy tale; and finally legend and myth too can still become legend in the ecclesiastical sense without losing their essential core. It is therefore quite fitting that our preceding natural-historical sections had chiefly to occupy themselves with legends, while in the metaphysical part that follows the actual myths will come to the foreground of our consideration.

Our age, our manner of understanding, is almost wholly unaccustomed to a genuinely mythical experience of unity, even in its weaker degrees, and therefore also to a genuinely religious heroism. And when it experiences something great, like the World War, where the shaping of life of whole races is in question, then it is — we have all experienced and felt it — marked by a morally earnest, tenacious power of suffering and of self-overcoming, or, where it has enthusiasm, it is romantic, as the beginning of the war probably was for our people; but it no longer has any genuinely mythical experience, it hardly any longer feels the mighty character of the event as the [p. 229] necessarily co-posited expression of innermost bonds. Indeed our age is even consciously averse to the genuinely mythical, because it misjudges its reality-value and confuses it with empty fantasy or allegorical play. Hence nature too remains for us an outwardly empirical object and as such unriddled; hence one makes the demand for a world to be explained mechanistically, even an organic world to be explained mechanistically, and creates out of this structure of thought a natural science suited only to this technical aim, while on the other hand one sharply separates metaphysics from it or seeks to separate it methodically, and so wants to understand the world, existence, reality outwardly. Thus we chase after bodies without soul and after the soul-like as though it were a bodily thing, hence a phantom, a specter in the most literal sense of the expression. Those worlds of mind and those seers of other epochs, who as heroes beheld the inner formation of reality and so had a genuinely mythical life and experience, are in their proper essence still as dead to us. What our historical cultural ages made of them, that is now only poems and at last allegories, which as such seem, to be sure, to justify the ordinary, flat, natural and outwardly emblematic interpretations as the summit of wisdom.

When one now sees how the legendary work of the Babylonian Gilgamesh epic is, on the one hand, described as a “meager, arid product of a mind in which we can detect not a spark of genius”<sup>106</sup>, and on the other hand, from another quarter, praised as the quintessence and primal source of all epics and legends, permeating the whole of world literature with its solar myth<sup>107</sup>, one recognizes from this alone what a riddle such an ancient mythic tradition must conceal. Is the one right in his judgment, or the other? But the question must not be put this way at all; for both are right and are one-sided only in what they draw from the work. The solution of the opposing views lies in this: that — as in almost all heroic songs and mythic poems handed down from “antiquity” — what lies before us in the Babylonian Gilgamesh epic too is only an external reworking of the oldest materials, no longer knowing what is deepest and last, whose parts, welded together and scarcely brought any longer [p. 230] into a unified poem, were collected by an age that one is almost tempted to call bibliophile (the library of Ashurbanipal).
