Nevertheless a secret meaning lives in the letter, so dead, which can well be freed from the contradictions that arose through the late and, who knows how many times repeated, reworking of the epic, once a truly grounded knowledge of the ages of humanity, with their events, their succession of races and their states of mind, has been won — a knowledge which we are as yet in a position only to anticipate by feeling. Such ancient materials, as the Gilgamesh epic offers them just as, say, Genesis, the Iliad, the Odyssey, the Edda, the Bhagavad Gita do, are to be taken and read precisely in the twofold sense indicated: as historical-natural-historical materials, but also as mythic-metaphysical visions and lived experiences. As such they are, to be sure, symbolic, yet in such a way that the outer history and natural history are the mirror and the veiling of the religious seership. Thus neither the one view nor the other alone does justice to the whole of such traditions; and so, when one practices either one-sidedly, such opposing judgments can come about as we showed a moment ago. Think of Jesus’s parables of the Kingdom of Heaven, familiar to everyone, or of the myth of his appearing on the water before the boatmen in the storm: in such narratives an outer image, or a quite everyday event, or a mythically transmitted material is always employed to present something otherworldly, something not to be expressed directly in words. Here, in the latter example of the walking on water, the solar myth is unmistakable; and just as unmistakable is the specifically religious lived experience of the Evangelist in it; and just as unmistakable the state of bodily levitation in ecstatic conditions, which was probably not yet unknown to the people of that time but is now occult. All this is still jumbled together in such a late religious myth, partly intended, partly already misunderstood and taken over by the writer or poet. According as later profane or religious poets could no longer master such primal materials and their duality, originally conceived as a unity, or misunderstood them one-sidedly, they detached, consciously or unconsciously, skillfully or clumsily, the one side from the other, presented only the one alone, or threw both together piecemeal. [p. 231] So, I believe, the Gilgamesh epic came into being in its late version, and was moreover dedicated to a ruler who was certainly not the primordial form of Gilgamesh.

<sup>106</sup> L. v. Schroeder, Die Arier und ihre Eigenart. (In: “Vorträge über wissenschaftliche und kulturelle Probleme der Gegenwart.”) Riga 1913. p. 26.

<sup>107</sup> See n. 154.

This is how it can be explained that interpretations of legends and myths, especially those grown on philological-etymological ground, can bring to light correct allegories, however shallow, and that nevertheless another investigator discovers in them, just as correctly, deeply religious truths, and that, besides, a third is able to read off from them prehistoric or natural-historical events. For all this, and perhaps even more, of which we as yet have no inkling because we lack the organ of thought for it, is truly contained in them. It is therefore thoroughly wrong, in the case of such opposed research results, to say that the one explanation must exclude the other. Quite the contrary: to show all these individual features as facets of a self-contained inner structure, and thereby to grasp this as a unity in the spirit of its oldest builder and to open it up to the astonished beholder — that is the true explanation of myths and legends.

The development as the myths and legends, in the preceding sections, have already offered it to us for the history of humanity, of the animal world and of the earth’s surface, thus becomes intelligible in its inner, essential coherence only through a simultaneous metaphysical consideration. Without this, all that has been said would remain only an external stringing together of images and stages of form. Considered inwardly, in terms of the soul, however, we obtain a real history, because only then does a meaning, a real, that is, an inwardly connected occurrence, show itself to us. Even the best fossil skeleton finds of primordial man, should they ever come, would tell us nothing about the essence and content of his existence; from them alone we should not succeed in reviving his psychic and specifically human world. But without this psychic world the man of the former world would be as dead to us as the fossil animal world remains to us when we merely describe it by its skeletons and divide it into genera or species or join it together into lines of descent. In this way one arrives only at a bodily or temporal side-by-side and succession without soul and life. But we have the need to penetrate into the inner coherence, [p. 232] into the essence of things and phenomena. No matter whether nature, as Spinoza and Goethe would have it, is divine, or, as Aristotle holds, daemonic: in no case is it a mechanism or dead, as the dogmatism of late-age science would have it — mocking nature’s own soul, its own inner existence.

Is it not the case, then, that there is no natural occurrence at all that would not at the same time, seen from within, be a metaphysical one? The simultaneous and equal beholding of both sides of the essence of existence, however, is religious certainty. For this reason, for a naturalist who grasps both as a unity, research becomes religion and religious vision becomes the ultimate result of research. Not as if a duality as such existed in nature; it is only our humanly two-sided kind of mind, in seeing or being able to see. The uninwardized, if necessary, method of natural science in the last centuries, with its practical partial successes, has had the singular vocation of bringing to presentation a nature conceived and thought through only physically-mechanically, like an “as if.” It has made possible our outward looking into a great distance of time and space, a decomposition of matter into its basic functions, and has thereby created the frame, the skeleton, into which those to come will have to build the living. That ancient complexes of knowledge, grasped metaphysically, thereby come closer to us again and gain new deep meaning is not surprising. Such visions, where they appear alongside mechanistic science, are still dismissed as mysticism, that is, with a catchword largely misunderstood and unreflected upon by most, which has indeed, through theosophical abuse, rightly fallen into disrepute. That our whole existence, every thought, every stirring, every coming-to-be of a being from its germ, every opening of a blossom, every play of the features, in spite of all the mechanism under which it proceeds, is the expression of an inexpressible inner life and in the superconsciousness is also experienced in no other way — this is forgotten in the study of nature or methodically set aside. When we work scientifically in the ordinary sense, we abstract a priori, deliberately, from that essential inner side, because it does not belong in [p. 233] the mechanistic world-picture we initially aim at. But then to hold the ordering and comprehension of natural events thus made possible somehow for truth, instead of for a mere symbol, is a weakness of memory regarding that from which one had set out.

All existence, even the inorganic, mineral kind, is never a machine — it is always a mode of the all-pervading life and thereby always has relation to the whole and metaphysical meaning. Otherwise, from the little that we know of the All, we could never derive laws, never anything general, and so could not take even the first step toward a science.

In the smallest speck of dust nature lies enclosed; / Everywhere mind tracks down mind.

It is therefore foolish, not only before God but also before men, to want in the long run to let a natural science without metaphysics alone stand; but most foolish to want to create a total worldview with natural science and its method, when one does not even know the metaphysical side of natural existence in it as a problem, let alone as reality. Hence also the present turning away of all seeking men from the philosophemes offered for a century, oriented only to natural science. How forced and unreal they already seem to our sense now, even when we do not yet quite dare to free ourselves entirely from the old method of knowing. “Even the problems of space and time,” says Harnack in one of his last speeches, “lead today, with our scientific methods, to more paradoxical assumptions than any scholastic philosopher ever ventured. Already on the question of the principium individuationis the mechanistic worldview founders. Living nature forces upon us a method of consideration that cannot be contested with the means of mechanistic abstraction, and that leads to a sublime metaphysics. We find in the surrounding nature not only quantities, hence not only mechanical effects, but ‘life’ in independent centers, of which we too feel and know ourselves to be a part. But all the specific marks of life are [p. 234] incommensurable with the abstracting, calculating understanding. Our science is today very one-sided, indeed it threatens us with a certain barbarism, in that it positively draws away from the living and wants to replace its knowledge and empathy by the mechanical. Not as if a misunderstanding were thereby to be shown toward the science of the mechanical and its outward progress; but the delusion must cease that it embraces everything worth knowing, that it is able to offer a perfect explanation of the world. There is a knowledge of life and of reality without which one cannot live at all. To seek this is the highest stage of scientific cognition. Not under the light of individual items of knowledge to be verified quantitatively has humanity found its way to the heights, but led by minds who sensed a central sun and had the courage to press on from physics to metaphysics, from history to metahistory. Their contemplation led them to an Eternal, to problems that lie behind the world of our ideas. They ventured the voyage on the infinite ocean. And even if coasts which they thought they saw proved deceptive, they nevertheless brought back from their voyage heightened life to their companions”<sup>108</sup>.

<sup>108</sup> A. Harnack, Über wissenschaftliche Erkenntnis. (In: “Vorträge über wissenschaftliche und kulturelle Probleme der Gegenwart”.) Riga 1913. p. 9.

Science and life are through and through metaphysical; and in no other way can any phenomenon, any whole or any single thing, be seized and comprehended. For even the outward seeing of a single thing is possible only through the simultaneous metaphysical feeling of a whole of which it is a part or special case. The simplest juxtaposition, distinction and description, and still more the calculation of two bodies acting on one another with mechanical force, indeed every calculation or foreseeing of an effect on the basis of experience, is full metaphysics and symbolism and is possible only as such. Do we not already bring the fundamental mechanical law of the action of forces to nature in a thoroughly metaphysical way? The law of cause and effect, in which we all believe, yes believe, is metaphysics. And above all the insight into the world of the organic! Is any insight possible there at all that did not turn in directly upon one’s own “feeling”? Even every valuation of whether a being is organized higher or lower is already through and through a metaphysical judgment. Let every investigator examine himself [p. 235] in his hours of deepest knowledge, if he finds the strength for self-contemplation and has the courage to look himself relentlessly in the face: Where does he have his most valuable certainty from? Surely not from external “proving”? First the certainty of what has been beheld lives; then new facts well up of themselves and things beckon to us living, which we had perhaps not even seen lying by the wayside before.

We therefore turn now, with a method other than the merely natural-scientific one, to metaphysics or, if one will: to the mysticism of the natural-historical evolution of humanity and that of its environment, in order to be a naturalist there in another sense.

[p. 236]

### Natursichtigkeit als ältester Seelenzustand — Nature-Sightedness as the Oldest State of the Soul

In the preceding sections we have occasionally used the expression “nature-sighted” without having explained it. For the time being it could give food for thought, and the context let it be seen through, one hopes, what was meant by it. It is now to move into the middle ground of the discussion and furnish us the pivot for understanding the myth-forming power of the most ancient men and their soul.

Brought into a formula intelligible to us today, nature-sightedness means that psychic-spiritual state, closely touching daemonism, by virtue of which a living being — not only man — partakes of a knowledge, a beholding, feeling or sensing of the relations that govern and weave in, between and above the things and beings of physical nature. For example, the ability to feel in advance coming natural events, even of the most insignificant kind, or perhaps the arrival of a friend, or to obtain in a countenance, in a movement of the eye of a person, immediate certainty about his character or about the hitherto concealed state of affairs of an event with which he is bound up, as Dostoevsky presents so overwhelmingly in “Raskolnikov” — all this means: to partake of nature-sighted seeing. In its simplest, most attenuated form this at first does not differ much from what we now call clairvoyant. Nature-sighted ability, by contrast, is the capacity, on the basis of such seeing and knowing, to gain influence, consciously or unconsciously, on the things of nature or the souls of living beings, thus to call forth formations, alterations, ideas in the environment and in other living beings; expressed in modern terms: to work telepathically or strongly hypnotically or teleplastically, to call forth radiations and materializations at a distance; expressed in archaic words: to conjure and to bind by spell.

[p. 237] A wonderful picture of this, taken from life, even if it should be partly composed, is unrolled by Frobenius<sup>109</sup>. On his journey in the primeval forest region between Kasai and Luebo he was accompanied by members of a Pygmy tribe and had become familiar with them. Some men and a woman followed him for a week. One evening there was a shortage of food and he asked the men to bring down one more antelope for him. The people looked at him in astonishment at this demand, and one blurted out in reply: they would gladly do so, but for today it was too late, since no preparation had been made; they would do it the next morning. Eager to know in what the preparations of these men would consist, the narrator crept, in the morning before sunrise, into the thicket the people had chosen the evening before for their measures. Still in the gray of dawn the men came, but not alone, rather with the woman. They crouched on the ground, plucked a small spot clear and smoothed it. One man drew something in the sand with his finger, while the others, with the woman, murmured formulas and prayers. Then waiting silence. The sun rose on the horizon. One of the men, with an arrow on the drawn bow, stepped up beside the bared spot of ground. A few more minutes, and the rays of the sun fell on the drawing on the ground. Now the following took place in a flash: the woman raised her hands to the sun and cried out some sounds unintelligible to the listener; the man shot the arrow at the drawing on the ground; then the men leapt into the bush; the woman went back to the camp afterward. Then the observer inspected the spot: on the ground was drawn the picture, some four spans long, of an antelope, in whose neck the arrow that had been shot was sticking. In the afternoon the men came back with a buck antelope shot through the neck. Then they went back to the hill with some tufts of hair and a bowl full of antelope blood, in order to smear hair and blood over the picture and to wipe it out, which later came out through the blabbing of one of the men after enjoying palm wine. Nothing could be learned of the meaning of the formulas; but he did say that the blood of the slain antelope would destroy them if they did not [p. 238] fulfill the magic rite; the wiping out, too, had to be done before sunrise. Later they stole away; evidently the leader of the party, who had betrayed the meaning of the proceeding, did not want the others, especially the woman, to learn of it.

<sup>109</sup> L. Frobenius, Das unbekannte Afrika. München 1921, p. 34.

In this simple narrative lies the key to the long-surmised meaning of the Stone Age drawings on rock and bone. Those people too belonged to a cultural soul closer to nature, more enwoven in nature, hence more primitive. What we said in the introduction: that those capacities for conjuring signify and are realities, release and master natural forces — this must probably be accepted as certain, contrary to our rationalist, crudely objectifying scientific thinking. For only from this does an understanding of that ancient paideuma result. The “daemonic” in it, however, as Frobenius explains, is accessible to the understanding only in its effects. Even for the gifted man of our own day, who in his religious and artistic vision, in the highest moments of his inner excitement, “falls prey to the daemons,” this state is afterward unfathomable to the understanding. And Frobenius rightly says: the reality of such states is, however, so eminently significant that the future will be astonished at how little attention has been paid to them until today.

Such, then, are the capacities and activities of men in the weakly nature-sighted state, in which they may partake permanently or transitorily. If now this ability takes on greater strength, there arises the type, developed in natural man, of the sorcerer and exorcist of spirits, whose greatness becomes uncanny to us already and probably dangerous as well, when he still appears at the conscious mental height of cultured man, developed intellectually though not always morally, and works here as a magician, of which the times of the Christian Middle Ages, senseless and superstitious to our enlightened world, know enough to tell; I recall Doctor Faust.

The nature-sighted is therefore something essentially other than an imagination, a figment of the brain, a ghost, a superstition; rather it is, on the one hand, the seeing of the deeper [p. 239] reality living in things — reality in the literal sense —, symbolized, say, by the nymphs and fairies, the brownies and gnomes, so far as the physical and organic nature is concerned; on the other hand it is the intimate connection with this reality, so that from it, for a conscious being such as man is, a possibility of forming and transforming a part of nature from within becomes possible, hence not by a mechanical but by a psychic way.

The question now is whether this faculty of sight, appearing in man in various degrees of intensity and consciousness as an effective natural force, does not also have other bearers, e.g. animals, or animals in general?

Ed. v. Hartmann, in his “Philosophy of the Unconscious,” turned his attention to the nature-sighted seeing of animals<sup>110</sup> and reckoned all manifestations of instinct under it; he also demonstrated it for non-individual organic formation. Bergson too, in the “Évolution créatrice,” relied especially on this side of the living natural being, in order to demonstrate the inner identity of the individual processes and individual beings, outwardly so manifoldly individualized and often present as if without connection. One of the most beautiful examples, also used by Bergson, is the laying of eggs by a sting wasp in the back of a beetle<sup>111</sup>. The beetle is paralyzed but remains alive until the larvae develop and eat it out. The wasp’s sting must instinctively have hit with the greatest precision the right spot of the beetle’s organism in order to achieve this effect, and does so. In this example the natural seeing and the nature-sighted effect thereby called forth are undoubtedly just as much in play as in the magically conjuring doing of simple natural man, since in him too much, very much that is unconscious plays a part. In the activity of the egg-laying wasp-creature there is likewise an enchanting, a binding by spell of the other being. Even if this binding is carried out very externally, namely through a crudely mechanical intervention in the body of the other, still the natural constitution from which the certainty of the mechanical intervention proceeds is no intellectual one, but an instinctively clairvoyant one, one nature-sighted in the truest sense of the word. This is the conjuring in natural man too; [p. 240] and only the individual execution in the single case remains conscious and must remain so, because each single case brings with it a somewhat different external situation. Only we see in the magic of natural man no crudely mechanical transmission. In the main both stand under the influence of the common nature-soul, and this sees, recognizes and transmits. This is the metaphysical element of instinct and of conjuring, this the nature-sighted in the single being.

<sup>110</sup> Ed. v. Hartmann, Philosophie des Unbewußten. 10th ed. Leipzig 1890, p. 68ff.

<sup>111</sup> H. Bergson, Schöpferische Entwicklung. Translated by G. Kantorowicz. Jena 1912, p. 177 ff. There it says, in extract: “Thus the different species of Hymenoptera conform entirely to the different kinds of prey with which they have to do. The sting-wasp that attacks a golden-beetle larva stings it at one single point only; but a point at which the motor ganglion cells, and these alone, are concentrated: a sting into other ganglia could bring about death and putrefaction, which are to be avoided. The yellow-winged digger wasp, which picks out the cricket as its victim, knows that the cricket possesses three nerve centers that set its three pairs of legs in motion; or at least it proceeds as if it knew this.... To be sure, it is far from true that the execution of the operation is always perfect.... But from the fact that instinct is fallible like the intellect, that it is exposed to individual deviations like the intellect, it by no means follows that the wasp’s instinct was acquired through intelligent groping. For supposing even that the wasp had, in the course of time, piece by piece, through groping over its victim, arrived at the knowledge of the points one must sting in order to render it motionless, of the special treatment one must give to the brain so that paralysis may ensue without death — how are we to assume that such specific components of so precise a knowledge were regularly inherited? If there were within our whole experience hitherto a single undoubted transmission of this kind, no one would deny the heritability of acquired characters. In reality, however, the hereditary transmission of acquired habits takes place in an imprecise and irregular form — supposing, that is, that it ever takes place. Yet the whole of this difficulty stems only from our wanting to translate the knowledge of the Hymenoptera into concepts of the understanding... In that case the digger wasp, exactly like the entomologist, would have to get to know, one after another, the position of the nerve centers of its caterpillar, would have to acquire at the very least the practical knowledge of these positions through experiment on the success of its sting. Quite otherwise, by contrast, if one assumes between the digger wasp and its victim a sympathy — in the etymological sense of the word; a sympathy that informs it, as it were, from within about the vulnerability of the caterpillar. This feeling of vulnerability need owe nothing to external perception; it would result simply from the meeting of wasp and caterpillar — both regarded no longer as two organisms, but as two activities. And this feeling would be only the concrete expression of the relation of the two. Scientific theories, to be sure, cannot appeal to considerations of this kind. They may not put action before the organ, sympathy before perception and knowledge. But, to say it again and again: either philosophy has nothing at all to seek here, or else its task begins where science ends. For no matter whether science makes of instinct a ‘compound reflex,’ or an intelligently acquired habit that has become automatic, or even a sum of small advantages accumulated by chance through selection and become fixed — it always asserts the seamless reduction of instinct, be it to intelligent modes of behavior, be it to mechanisms constructed piece by piece, like those which our understanding puts together. And that science fulfills its role in this way I do not dispute. Instead of the true analysis of an object it will always offer us only its translation into concepts of the understanding. But how then can we fail to recognize that natural science itself invites philosophy to consider things from another angle?”

In a treatise<sup>112</sup> on telepathy and clairvoyance Tischner points to the occurrence of psychic memory traces, whose working appears not bound to the brain and which, by virtue of their own lawfulness, exclude a mechanistic psycho-physiological interpretation. Thereby the scientific possibility is given in principle to infer, for other psychic processes too, an extra-cerebral, immediate working from soul to soul, a transference of ideas and thoughts from man to man. The psychistic explanation of telepathic and clairvoyant occurrences seeks from the outset to hold clearly fast that a psychic, hence also a clairvoyant or telepathic transmission is a process that is well to be distinguished from the becoming conscious of the result in the brain of the individual.

<sup>112</sup> R. Tischner, Über Telepathie und Hellsehen. Experimentell-theoretische Untersuchungen. Heft 106 der “Grenzfragen des Nerven und Seelenlebens”. München 1920. In particular section III (p. 87—125), on the theory of telepathy and clairvoyance, opens to the non-specialist a possibility of making his own the various serious theories that really grasp the problem. One is astonished, as a layman, to see how much experimental and theoretical work has already been accomplished here, of which one has hardly any idea. For one was, after all, only too much deterred by the abuse of the charlatan spiritistic-occultistic circle from seriously occupying oneself with these questions, which contribute so much of importance to the clarification and understanding of the oldest philosophies, as well as of legends and myths and of the conditions of humanity, and will perhaps still open our eyes so far that we shall gradually learn to come back from our pride in the mechanistic worldview, in this way too.

One cannot, says Tischner, ascribe to the soul in the body any organs with which it would, say, come into activity in a resolution of the will to raise the arm; in the extra-corporeal working of the soul the matter lies fundamentally no differently. As the soul knows how to find the way to bring a purely psychic memory trace to consciousness, so too in the transference of thought something passes over from soul to soul, which only then calls forth the consciousness in the recipient. Clairvoyance too is to be understood so. The soul, as a non-spatial being, has in any case a quite different relation to space, and the ideas that we make for ourselves, through our physical organization, of the spatial world, are in no way sufficient here. With the facts of clairvoyance once established, one cannot get around assuming a different relation of the soul to space and to time than corresponds to everyday waking consciousness. But then one also understands the rising up of long-past things and processes from the womb of the [p. 241] past in clairvoyant souls. And Tischner concludes, passing over into the significant general: It gives the impression as if the subconscious, expressed pictorially-spatially, were not so sharply delimited from its surroundings, but stood, as a psychic domain, in connection with a non-humanly-individual or a super-individual psychic realm. Descending from our upper consciousness, we would gradually come into subconscious psychic regions that no longer belong to the individual alone, as a water vein welling out of a mountain passes in the darkness of the mountain’s interior soon into the water surrounding and permeating everything. These deepest layers of the subconscious would then partake of a non-individual or super-individual psychic realm and therefore lead to a knowledge of things that are inaccessible, indeed incomprehensible, to individual conscious life. The rarity of this phenomenon, however, would be explained by the difficulty of bringing this knowledge from the depths of the unconscious into the light of the upper consciousness.

All this, which I have so far tried to indicate in brief strokes, is what is nature-sighted in our present physical existence. To be sure, only faint remnants of it are still there, and it no longer plays that great and in many respects determining role which it played in natural man, and so presumably also in Stone Age man, as we know from certain indications. In this, at any rate, he resembled the present-day “savage,” so long as the latter has remained original and untouched, at least in his life of soul.

But were there not, in the end, ages and human beings in whom nature-sightedness, and with it the nature-daemonic, played a greater, perhaps even a decisive role, one that almost or wholly replaced the intellect? And of what kind must such states have been?

Let us recall the law that faint images of what is past repeat themselves in later courses of organic evolution. So too in the life of soul, the overflowing of the supra-individual psychic into the individual, so clearly described by Tischner, and its meager seepage into the individual’s consciousness, may be an embryonic remnant, a faint afterglow of an older permanent state, which marked an earlier, still more nature-bound man as what was essential and as what was constantly [p. 242] lived and experienced.

There has been left over in our late humanity a memory of a state of soul that is, or was, capable of reshaping nature from within, and even one’s own body. This is the origin of the legends of the great magicians, who could do both. If this magic was practiced for selfish ends, it was diabolical, black; if it stood in the service of God, it was white, and the person from whom it proceeded was suffused with light. In the essay on animal magnetism and magic, Schopenhauer brings together some data on this view of human nature<sup>113</sup> and gives there a quotation: “Magical power enables him who possesses it to dominate and to renew creation, that is, the plant, animal and mineral kingdoms; so that, if many were to work together in one magical power, nature could be transformed into a paradisal state.”

<sup>113</sup> A. Schopenhauer, Gesammelte Werke, vol. III. Edited by A. Griesebach. Leipzig 1891 (Reclam.) p. 319.

Primordial human beings who saw nature differently may well have had fundamentally different qualities, known to us only through legend, which are physically incomprehensible to us. So, for example, the much-discussed capacity for levitation, by which they not only stood as bodies in a different relation to the gravity of the earth, perhaps able to glide over the earth in a different way, but in which they possessed a force that could be hurled outward and then, say, have stone colossi of such dimensions transported over such distances as late-age technology declares impossible, or envies, and gapes at as a mystery — the buildings of “Ur-Gilgamesh.” Indeed, as one was fond of proceeding in the last century, with our critical and also shallow late-age spirit, toward everything deep and secret in nature, it has long since been “demonstrated” here too that the gigantic buildings and giant blocks and monoliths were moved by the countless labor forces of toiling masses of people at their disposal, and through the notion of the effect of the masses an attempt has been made to explain the magical element of an earlier, legendary time as well, although it ought to have been understood otherwise: there are, to be sure, reliefs on which mass forced labor is depicted. But nonetheless: just as little as the remnants of ancient nature-sightedness in [p. 243] the latest tribes or in diluvial man can be a measure of the former greatness of this faculty in an older human being, so little probative force have the Quaternary depictions of mass forced labor for a nature-bound, magical acquisition of power by primordial human beings. The presumably gigantic Tower, localized in late times at Babel, might likewise in reality have been a primordial expression belonging here, of applications of force unknown to us, which, applied in a nature-sighted and magical manner, exerted their effect also by a way other than the external, crudely mechanical one, so to speak from within.

In the Indian legend of the origin of emeralds we meet, as in so many legends and fairy tales, the human being endowed with magical abilities, and at the same time the production of natural formations is indirectly ascribed to his activity. It runs thus: An emperor was once told that the king of the jinn had a great vessel, wrought from a very precious stone. By conjuration the emperor gained power over the jinn and commanded that the vessel be brought to him. A jinni, charged with this by his king, was flying through the air with the vessel when a daemon met him halfway and tried to wrest the vessel from him. In the struggle it fell to earth and broke into countless pieces; these are the emeralds that now exist on earth.<sup>114</sup>

<sup>114</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 334/35.

We recall the bodily forms that existed among primordial men. There, as a lower type, were above all the frontal-eyed (p. 81). This frontal eye was, as has already been explained, the characteristic organ of that primordial age. Here is the place to interpret it. It was not a simple eye for seeing; for the higher animals then, as later, always had their two well-developed normal eyes besides that frontal or parietal eye. It can therefore only have been an organ for a sense which the later animals and men lacked, or which they lost, so that they no longer possess the organ, or only in a rudimentary state — the pineal gland, overgrown by the cerebrum. The cerebrum, however, is the seat of the intellect that mechanizes the aspect of the world, in which we now especially share and, as the development of the skull shows, even more than the Stone Age man, who presumably regressed [p. 244] in this respect, although he too, like the Tertiary mammals, is still a “brain giant.” In those ancient animals with the fully developed parietal eye, however, the brain was particularly small by comparison. If, then, the cerebrum is the seat of the intellect, with whose acquisition and unfolding the daemonic-nature-sighted element more and more disappeared, the complete recession of the cerebrum and the full development of the parietal organ, now extinguished beneath it, must signify precisely the physical exercise of that sense which we most lack in favor of the reflecting intellect — that of the nature-sighted. The parietal or frontal eye was presumably the physically effective, seeing and exercising organ of this lost nature-sighted inner being.<sup>115</sup> Distant seeing and distant binding of prey or adversary may have been one of its tasks and abilities no longer perceptible to us — that is, the nature-sighted and magical on a larger scale.

<sup>115</sup> I found the thought that the pineal gland is the seat of the clairvoyant functions for the first time in A. Besant, Die uralte Weisheit. Translated by L. Deinhard. Leipzig 1898. Yet the thought is by no means original here either, but only the expression of an old conviction; for Cartesius, too, already calls the corpus pineale the workshop of the soul.

Of particular importance for this view seem to be the investigations on the pineal gland recently conducted by Reich in the Dutch East Indies.<sup>115a</sup> He has established, both anatomically and by X-ray examination, that the pineal is the ideal central organ of the whole brain, since it lies in every direction at the golden section. For the parietal opening of the Palaeozoic reptiles, too, he has demonstrated this. He suggests that the pineal is the polar counter-organ to the sphere of sex, and points to the Indian doctrine according to which the pineal not only stands in immediate relation to the sexual sphere, anatomically and secretively, but is also a brain organ through which knowledge of the phylogenetic past of the individual, and thereby of his species, can come to us, if through meditation one succeeds in shifting “consciousness” from the cerebrum into this sphere. “Perhaps the pineal, then, is less a gland with internal secretion than rather an organ of sexual mneme, arisen from the need to take descendants of the ‘germ line’ directly into the brain, so as to link ‘germ line’ and individual most intimately with one another.” It is [p. 245] to be expected that, through the pursuit of such investigations as Reich is now undertaking, important discoveries in the indicated direction will yet result.

<sup>115a</sup> H. Reich, Über die anatomische Lage der Zirbeldrüse nebst einer Bemerkung zu ihrer Funktion. Zeitschrift für gesamte Neurologie und Psychiatrie. vol. 104, p. 818, Berlin 1925.

Post-Adamite man, then, did not yet have the cerebrum in which the supra-individual psychic surges and transmissions of a clairvoyant kind could be translated into individual consciousness. If this was therefore still undeveloped, then that “subconscious” was precisely also the normal state of the life of soul, and this is just what we, with Schopenhauer, can designate as a supra- or sub-individual “nature-somnambulic” state.<sup>115b</sup> Later, however, a restratification of the states of consciousness, of the psychic state of life, presumably occurred: with the development of the cerebrum man entered the sphere of consciously reflecting, intellectual, causal and spatio-temporal thinking; he became an individual of consciousness. Now the telepathic, the clairvoyant and the magical functions that operate in such a way, the powers of enchantment, died away more and more. It was the leaving of the real land of fairy tale, of which we hear in legends and fairy tales precisely that which appears to the sympathetic hearer so exceedingly similar to those things that still meet us sparsely as clairvoyance and telepathy and the slight sorcery of primitives, and to which our learned spirit of the age has, it seems, kept eye and ear closed far too long. According to the greater or lesser degree of this restratification of psychic consciousness toward the intellectual, times and peoples have always differed, and accordingly the immediate understanding of these connections is revealed or closed to the individual or his generation in varying degree.

<sup>115b</sup> Görres already says, in his “Mythengeschichte der Asiatischen Welt” (vol. I, Hinterasiat. Mythen. Heidelberg 1810), of the myth-creating primordial man: “Man in this period is somnambulistic, as in magnetic sleep he walks about, unconscious of his consciousness, in the deeper consciousness of the world; his thinking is dreaming in the deeper nerve-tracts; but these dreams are true, for they are revelations of nature.” I discovered this highly valuable passage only after the appearance of the 2nd edition.

Noah, as the Old Testament depicts him, is the more perfected representative of the “Noachian human stock” to which we still belong, even if it may have split and perfected itself into many lines. According to the few but extraordinarily significant indications, he is the human type with the fully spreadable five-fingered hand, with the strongly developed cerebral sphere, with the parietal organ that has become rudimentary, with an already more spiritualized belief in God, already far removed from the purely daemonic-nature-sighted constitution, which [p. 246] in its unbridled, unreasoning primordial state led a purely instinctual life of nature and in its brighter side was certainly directed at anything but the economically practical. The latter, however, is according to all accounts already clearly recognizable in Noah’s being. All the same, he still has the nature-sighted capacity of foreknowing the connections of nature and the physical future: he knows when the Flood is coming. He also still understands how to influence nature strongly, for he cultivates the vine. Something like cultivating the vine we still cannot do with all our natural science and theory of heredity; we can indeed improve existing varieties of fruit and grain and make them hardy in the soil, and likewise flowers. But to breed from a wild, inedible fruit a plant being comparable to the vine is closed to us. So Noah himself is no longer merely unconsciously nature-sighted in his disposition; he is strongly individualized, he is personality and character. He speaks with God, in which he plainly reflects, no longer merely beholds and acts unconsciously, and is thereby clearly marked as a brain-being, in contrast to the frontal-eye being of older descent with its presumably nature-like somnambulic state.

Whence he came and when has been attempted to be indicated in the chapter on the legendary primordial man and his bodily characteristics: he came when the marsupial-like mammal underlying his stock began to transform itself into the late Mesozoic-Tertiary placental mammalian being, or suddenly transformed itself. But the inner “when” with which we are concerned here is grasped by our saying: he came when the intellectual, typically placental-mammalian development of the cerebrum, and with it the intellect, became the essential peculiarity of the higher animal world.

The last step to the pure, intellectual, that is Apollonian, human type, as he stands before us in the most perfected personalities of our history and as he culminates in the almost complete elimination of the nature-sighted, must then have taken place in the Tertiary, when from the mammalian man of Noachian kind more and more of the animal was cast off, until finally anthropoid apes too came from him, while at the same time, convergently, as mirror-image and counter-effect, so great a [p. 247] human likeness appeared in the ape stock. The remarkable thing is that only now does the human form show itself in the highest sense and at the same time in an original primitiveness. For only the perfectly erect gait, which no animal ever attained, only the perfect development of the intellectual cerebrum and with it of the skull, which is capable of further increase in the future, only the perfect countenance — these are the things in which he stands, one-sidedly developed, over against the whole animal world. If the palaeontologist Jaekel once said that the deeper content of progress in the organic lies not in everything merely becoming different and manifold, but in the biological task being solved best, that is with the least expenditure, then the present-day human form is precisely that wonder of organically creating nature in which the greatest simplicity appears paired with the greatest perfection. So man, as he stands before us, is indeed the crown of living nature; but not, in the line of thought of the older theory of descent, as a last twig, nor in the sense of a misunderstood religious view as a creation of his own, standing alien over against the animal world: but as both at once, in that his present perfected form, in the course of a long way of suffering, raised itself ever more purely as the expression of the entelechy living within him, realizing the idea of his being more and more like a symbol, whereby his original stock, embracing the animal as well, released from itself everything that in the course of earth-historical time has unfolded in forms beside him, and by which he himself, step by step, became poorer in animality, richer in humanity. This thought resolves the all but insurmountable contradiction that lies in the notion of a primordial form of the highest stage of development and at the same time of the highest simplicity. In this thought too rests the reconciliation between the ancient idea of creation that rescues the image of man and the modern theory of descent that only seems to oppose it.

[p. 248]

### Kulturseele und Urwelt — Cultural Soul and Primordial World

As Spengler presents it, so insistently and vividly, in order to order his world-picture for himself, each of the historically known circles of culture has its heroic-mythical early period, then its cultural period, and lastly its intellectualized late period. In the first of these three epochs there is a tremendous heroic and apocalyptic pressing and striving. Then the culture is born, in which an existence grown fast to the land and settled possession are the essential outward mark; priesthood and nobility, spirit and knighthood the socio-psychological; the shaping of religiosity and of national character the essential inner mark. Everything is “in form,” until the third epoch makes itself noticeable and with the intellectualization brings also the mechanization of existence, the detachment from the soil with all its consequences, with the rule of the masses, with an individual or group Caesarism in the life of the state, ending with the dominion of pure “mind” in thought; whereupon later, in the normal course, this culture become civilization runs out into a fellahdom. Before all cultures, however, there goes the formless existence of primordial man<sup>116</sup>

<sup>116</sup> O. Spengler, Der Untergang des Abendlandes. vol. I, chap. 2. vol. II, chap. 4. München 1922.

Frobenius<sup>117</sup> objects that for Spengler the paideuma of primordial man is a chaos, the Stone Age without style, the environment of primitive man without physiognomy and causal order. Primitive, daemonic humanity, he says, also has its ordered physiognomy, indeed it lives on yet in the later cultural dwellings and forms. They are people of the same nation and the same language, of whom some live the urban culture, others the rural flat culture. The latter still live essentially more in a nature-like daemonic world; the former in the idealistic and mechanistic one; only individually and generally does the one often flow over into the other. The people of the cities and the people of the countryside are stratified in terms of education. Among the peoples of the Hausa lands in Africa, [p. 249] daemonic feeling in the flat culture and factual feeling in the high culture show themselves insuperably separated. But the question presses itself upon us whether this “more” of a nature-like daemonic world is at all, today and even in the diluvial Stone Age, still an original one or merely a psychic rudiment from a still earlier time.

<sup>117</sup> L. Frobenius, Paideuma. Umrisse einer Kultur- und Seelenlehre. München 1921, p. 77/78; 59ff.; 66; 110ff.; 115.

Frobenius would have three main stages of cultural development distinguished: 1. that of the primitive cultures, whose soul cannot attain the stage of the pure world of facts at all; 2. that of the monumental cultures, which is marked by the detachment of the individual cultural will, of personality, from the fate of the whole being, and to which we belong; 3. the not yet realized cultural form of the future.

In the first stage, to which the so-called primordial or natural peoples belong, so far as they are original, there prevails the daemonic-genial being that in a certain respect is also proper to the child. The paideuma has here something of the spirit-world about it; an expression of Goethe’s. The world and life are filled with daemons and daemonic faculties which, as phenomena inaccessible to human reflection, move in the sphere of feeling of existence and only with the conclusion of the process of origination enter spontaneously into the life of consciousness. Characteristic of this form of culture are conjuration and sorcery.

The next, the monumental sphere of culture consists essentially in the detachment of individual consciousness and will from the typically generic. In place of the general, dominating, daemonistically regulated relations to life and nature, the ideals now take shape, in that man becomes aware of his shaping I, detaches himself from the group-formation of the We, and sets himself over against the other unit, the world and environment, fighting and creating, as a unity. From this, says Frobenius, one will understand why the Hellenes knew only their own people and had to designate all the outer world as barbarians; one also understands why “world history” could be for us hitherto only the history and prehistory of our Western paideuma. “With the ideals the paideuma enters into the spiritual life of consciousness. Its effect [p. 250] is determined by the measure of the daemons dissolving in them and by the growth of the approaching knowledge of facts of the grown man. The presence of the ideals is equivalent to the capacity for the formation of culture, provided that they are in a position to transform the world of facts as an organic unity into an equally organic paideuma, to ensoul it through and through — a creative act whose effect is then to be given the expression ‘style.’ The ideals belong to a definite, relatively short period of the life of men and of peoples. From the autumnal concern, arising from age, for the preservation of the I there arises as a third phenomenon rational causality, and with it the ‘facts’ are fully developed in their strictly spiritual rigidity. The ideals were ends in themselves, the facts have material ends. They arise in us from the mind, and therefore this phenomenon of causality is incomparably easier to recognize in its origin and in its manifold effect than that of genial creativity (daemons) and that of individuality, of personal ideals. The I no longer remains an ideal counterpart to the world of facts, but becomes, as ‘intelligence,’ a part of it. Hand in hand with it goes the dissolution of harmony.” “In this state of mechanics our culture now is, and that of all the no longer ‘primitive’ peoples of the world, whatever special forms of culture, Faustian or magical, chthonic or telluric, they belong to. The sequence of stages in the life of soul of every single man repeats itself in the paideuma of the community and of the people as barbarism, culture-hood and mechanics, and so there will also follow upon the period of formation that dies away in fully developed mechanics a last, a period of fulfillment. How the mechanistic trait extends,” — I am still speaking with Frobenius’ thoughts and words — “with the victory of the will to facts over the inclination to the daemonic and with the impossibility of the closing off of single national cultures, and how this leads toward a state in which the possibility of producing new monumental forms vanishes! What then? Reason-life will surely rise above the old existence by estates, above the mechanizing and mechanized stratification.” “The essential trait of the third period will be a harmonious interlocking of the daemonic, [p. 251] of the ideals and of the facts.” So far the world-picture as Frobenius draws it.

One might well come upon the thought that the life of the whole of humanity since the most ancient time ran in its basic features in the same sense, but in a longer-waved rhythm, than that of the individual historically comprehensible bodies of culture, and that the existence known to us back into the Ice Age is nothing other than a total late age, in which almost everything is lacking that we call, in the most original sense, nature-sighted and, when it is active and earth-bound, daemonic. There could thus precede the intellectual-civilizational late age of the whole Quaternary epoch — perhaps also of the Tertiary age with its Atlantean and early-Atlantean cultural soul — an epoch in which precisely that was, to the strongest degree, indeed exclusively, common property and spiritual-psychic temporal character, which the early period of every later culture and also the Stone Age still suggest as a rudimentary, faint image: the soul not yet intellectualized, not yet thinking purely causally, and therefore daemonic and titanic. Before this epoch a still earlier one would then have to be conceived, corresponding to an altogether inwardly nature-bound, earth-historical primordial humanity: a high-mythical time, experiencing myths and moving only in states of myth, yet without reflecting consciousness of the understanding, joined with the strongest immediate nature-sightedness and daemonism. This epoch would, in keeping with its sense, have to be placed at the end of the Palaeozoic age and in the beginning of the Mesozoic; the other roughly in the remaining main part of the Mesozoic.

It could thus be that the most ancient former-world period of the pre-Noachian human being was an epoch in which all that which distinguishes the nature-bound primordial age of a historical, Quaternary culture was present, alone and exclusively, in quite especially high, fully developed measure and determining of life, thus a nature-sightedness and daemonism of active and passive kind that put in the shade everything which later, after the conclusion of that most ancient primordial age and the arising of the intellectual Noachian human type, repeats itself only rudimentarily and embryonically each time once more at the beginning of a cultural development and among natural peoples. As in the [p. 252] embryonic development of the higher animals, formal states can come to light again, by hints and passing swiftly, according to the same organic laws of formation, which originally, fully unfolded, were there in full force and activity out in the world for hundreds of thousands of years in a living being belonging to the same species, so too the nature-sighted qualities and vital functions that appear again and again, by hints and transiently, at the beginning of all known Quaternary cultures could be merely faintly echoing embryonic images of an originally daemonic being that was once unfolded in full force as a psychic-spiritual form of existence and temporal signature of its own, embracing and pervading everything human. That would have been, as said, the genuinely myth-forming and myth-experiencing time, common to the whole of humanity; and hence it would come about that the beginning period of a historical culture in each case has a more immediate, if only still weak, understanding and experience of a certain nature-sightedness, without however penetrating once more fully to its former exclusive unfolding. The ancient naturalness and nature-sightedness, however, culminated in the highest forms of daemonism conceivable for a human being, that is of sorcery, of binding, of distant seeing, of inward action upon organic and inorganic nature.

The more this great primordial epoch of the most ancient total humanity emerged from the naturalness and became spiritualized, the more it was led — corresponding to the becoming of one of our Quaternary cultural souls — from the generally natural to a higher, freer form of existence and of soul, in which the gloomy daemonic of sorcery and of the lowest desire for natural life rose to a brighter, if untamed willing: the age of the radiant hero and of the hero myth, yet still purely in the nature-daemonic, not in the knightly-epically mirrored late sense. Accordingly the second stage of a Quaternary cultural development, where the period of high culture begins to take shape, has the most understanding and immediate, if comparatively only weak, re-experiencing of a great primordial heroism, a re-experiencing that then makes itself known as real and legendarily interwoven heroic knighthood.

[p. 253] There comes the last phase of a Quaternary culture: the intellectualist. It is a brief, embryonic late reflection of another great epoch of the former world, common to humanity, in which, however, we all still find ourselves today: that of the Noachian brain-man of the pure mammalian type, which we see unfolding exclusively from the end of the Mesozoic into the early Tertiary, after the Flood had made a clean sweep of the pre-Noachian human type.

Thus each of these soul-bearing great epochs of earth history would have had its own human type, and everything ends with the full development of the cerebral sphere, with the disappearance of the frontal eye, perhaps with a reduction in bodily size and a shortening of the individual lifespan, also with the greater short-livedness of the individual stocks themselves, and at last shows us the man of the Ice Age with a not quite so vaulted intellectual skull, yet wholly as one of our own, or only as degenerate stragglers of a cultural epoch that had long since unfolded, namely the Tertiary-Quaternary one. It would accordingly already be an epoch of total humanity standing almost completely under the full unfolding of the intellect, of which the individual historical paideumata, as well as the presumably Ice-Age ones and probably also those of the Tertiary or Atlantean age lying before them, are only species and variants. I therefore consider the old special centers of culture such as Babylon, Egypt, West Africa, Central and South America to be, in their beginnings, substantially older than the Ice Age, or at least of Ice-Age date. Around them, in the lands untouched by them, the Stone Age man of older date perhaps survived as a “savage,” as an undiscovered “Australoid Negro.” Indeed, in their like conditions of life and with the same cultural paideuma, both have such similar skull forms. Whether any bearers of the later and present-day cultures came out of the younger Diluvial Stone Age man in Europe, for instance, cultures for which the complete vanishing even of the last instinctive nature-sightedness and the ever stronger Apollonian development of the cerebrum, the seat of mechanizing intelligence, has more and more become the signature—that shall no longer be discussed here; the science of our day is on the way to clarifying it.

[p. 254] I believe, then, that I can here supply a complete foundation for those realities, reaching deep into the past, still ungrasped and unexplained, upon which Spengler’s and Frobenius’ views on the course and the primeval age of cultures rest, and which opened so many new gates. All present-day and historically known cultures, however, have probably received their great myths and legends, common in their core though recast a thousandfold in form, from those older Mesozoic and earliest late Palaeozoic ages of man. Much has run together there in the traditions and can now at best be separated again and assigned to the right epoch only by first making clear to oneself the essence of the chief ages: that of the nature-sighted-daemonic, that of the daemonic-intellectual, and that of the phase which follows, which still includes us, predominantly intellectual.

Before all humanity lies, so we may conclude or, what is the same, believe, a further great world-epoch with new possibilities of soul and body, which will emerge in the measure that we shall shed the cerebral intellectual condition. The mightiest beginnings of it are already there. In the seated, smiling figure of the Buddha with the eye of wisdom on his forehead—I am only allegorizing here—this being is indicated in advance. All human types have been one-sidedly gifted. If they were essentially nature-sighted, they probably lacked in the same measure the discursively thinking causal intellect; the later, essentially intellectual man, as he is now reaching completion, essentially lacks the great bond with nature, nature-sightedness. To ideal figures of humanity, however, such as Buddha and Christ, everything is mythically ascribed that the types of humanity possessed only singly and in succession, and perhaps may yet come to possess. Christ stands over against Satan, who shows him that he possesses daemonic powers which he could use for the conquest of the world; and yet Christ is at the same time the being endowed with full intellectual understanding. Buddha too has supernatural powers; and since he is an ideal image of the universal, hence perfect man, there is set upon his thinker’s brow, though [p. 255] nowadays in an entirely different meaning, the eye of wisdom like a symbol of the old nature-sighted vision that still penetrated nature directly. Thus he already unites outwardly in his image both poles of the development of humanity, in order to be, beyond that, the God-man wholly removed from the earthly, overcoming the world, smiling down upon all multiplicity.

Ever since the Quaternary cultural ages accessible to us historically, we see the noblest and best of our cultural circles again and again behold this ideal of the future, praise it, live and die for it: for an epoch of harmony, where ideals are fulfilled that are utopias to present-day man, who is once more one-sidedly gifted as an intellectual being. So perhaps Plato’s “Republic” too is to be understood: as the work of a seer, which to our age remained an empty chimera of an otherworldly philosopher; whereby it is immaterial that it could be called into life neither today nor then in Syracuse. It will have its future. Another spirit will create for itself its other body, just as the daemonic nature-sighted spirit created the pre-Noachian body, and the predominantly intellectual spirit the Noachian body, of which we have remained partakers, and which we, leaving the Stone Age men aside, visibly carry further to completion. Whether then, once more, in inner connection with the transformation of the human soul and with the transformation of the body and of the animal world, a transformation of the earth’s surface and of the world of stars will come? Only after an Apocalypse, after all, shall the tabernacle of God be with pacified men.

So the oldest epoch of total humanity in earth history was that of pure nature-sightedness and highest nature-daemonism, in which thinking was not yet done with the reflective sense of causality, in which men as individuals perhaps reflected as little individually as the ant or the bee in its commonwealth, but in which, like “natural somnambulists,” they lived directly together with the being of nature, saw and felt nature from within, and also co-shaped it as a consequence of, and at the same time as, the reflexes of their own being.

These trains of thought are to receive further elaboration. But this will be intelligible only to one who from

[p. 256] Draft of a Table of the Development of Humanity in the Earth-Historical Ages. (The height of the columns does not signify relative duration.)

[p. 257] within is ready to descend with us into a dark passage, where another light prevails than that of the sun of Enlightenment of decades just past. Perhaps he will find there things that, though only half consciously, are already known to him from his own nature. By that he may measure their reality-value. He must be continually ready to follow the fabulous, even in his own inner world, as something real. Then he may, with an altered view of the things of the surrounding world and of the former world, return to customary criticism. Until then patient silence, listening and beholding are demanded of him, so that the inner vision that lies before us may not be disturbed. For we are going down into the depths, to the Mothers. Whether we shall succeed in bringing back unentangled the double thread of natural history and metaphysics that is to guide us down?

[p. 258]

### Naturdämonie und Paradies — Nature-Daemonism and Paradise

It was shown in what precedes that the nature-sighted faculties of a low degree, occasionally exercised in our time, could be remnants of that natural faculty which the uncorrupted primitive peoples and the Stone Age men still possess and possessed more strongly than we. This faculty goes so far, only in its extreme degree, that bodily transformations take place, such as accelerated and surer healing of wounds. In the great-magical phenomena of which we have spoken, and which one probably still meets occasionally in history, and at which the Schopenhauerian quotation aims, there lie now rudiments of a still older time, of a still more primordial human type, far before the Stone Age man who is still fairly comprehensible to us; thus soul-rudiments of an entirely different fundamental stock of the human essence, which probably also had, just like historical-prehistoric man, its cultural or spiritual stages of development.

When in what follows the daemonic is spoken of, what is meant by it is the natural, not the genial in Goethe’s manner, nor the daimonion of Socratic foresight. Every man has by nature certain basic images of the daemonic-natural within him. The daemonic can be of two kinds: ingratiating or terrifying, lovely or gloomy and black. In no case is it to be confused with wicked in the moral sense. It can be through and through naive, and is so, as long as it is not paired with the intellect, and only then deserves to be called wicked or ruthless. Otherwise it is, like a child, cruel and guiltless, or lovely and guiltless. Nature is through and through daemonic, and because it is, in the world the lovely goes intimately bound up beside the terrible. In the same measure as man’s spirit overcomes natural, naive daemonism, ideally and physically, the divine realizes itself in him. The diabolical is [p. 259] not the daemonic nature, which is neither divine nor diabolical; rather, the diabolical is the realization of the daemonic through intellectual spirituality; the divine, however, is the overcoming of this through love that no longer seeks itself. The parable of Christ and the harlot has this meaning; and transcendently, in the ultimate ground of existence, it also becomes the redeeming meaning of Christ’s descent into hell.

The representations of demons, of devils and angels, gods of light and of darkness, have at all times and among all peoples been essentially the same, and we can understand them immediately. What is striking is that we see the daemonic, as it lives in our hearts or is beheld, symbolized and realized also in nature, in forms of living beings; the lovely as well as the terrible. Most drastically and convincingly the daemonic emerges in the late Palaeozoic-Mesozoic reptiles, the lindworms, basilisks and dragons, with whose images of remembrance literature too is full as with symbols of terrifying daemonism. Since one need not instruct any knowing man what the daemonic is and what form the daemonic has, one will be able to recognize it again even without verbal analysis, wherever it meets one in nature, human life and the primordial past. Definitions of such a thing cannot be given at all, and it is the error of our scientific age to want to define the soul-like as one defines an object. It is like the concept of race as Spengler seeks to grasp it, when he says, say, that it is not measurable corporeality but soul and constitution of mind, which reveals itself immediately to him who himself has race.

It was set forth that every larger geological span of indefinite delimitation shows its own formation of types and organs. The time from the Permian epoch to the end of the Mesozoic age is now characterized in the higher animal life by the creation of daemonic beings of the lindworm and serpent character. I give alongside a characteristic picture. As for the gloomy daemonism of expression, living also in the grotesque masks of primitive peoples, it shines forth immediately from it. Have we not also, in the oldest legends of humanity, again and again the reference to the bodily

[p. 260] Fig. 27. Dragon-worm landscape of the late Mesozoic age with the swamp-dwelling giant form Brontosaurus. Present-day man reaches only to the animal’s knee. (After a reconstruction by H. F. Osborn and C. R. Knight, modified from W. D. Matthew, Dinosaurs. New York 1915.)

[p. 261] and spiritual terribleness of the “serpent,” in whose present-day descendants we can point to the same peculiarity: then many a peculiar trait in that old fear of serpents, in the old legends of lindworms and serpents with their battles and terrors, becomes clear to us.

So we must point to the much-discussed phenomenon in the animal kingdom to which the character of conjuration unquestionably belongs, to the way in which serpents can bind their living prey in order to swallow it down alive or to crush it. With this we touch upon the truly daemonic nature, in its primordial state probably far mightier, become a symbol, the being of the “serpent nature.” Schopenhauer, who gives a description of it, already passed by this singular natural phenomenon with a shudder, and recalls the saying of Aristotle that nature is daemonic but not divine. The concept daemonic is neutral. When serpents bind, it happens not only through fear-inspiring suggestion, but also through a suggestive switching-off of fear in the prey, so that it perceives nothing threatening, does not even suspect the danger, or goes playfully to meet it<sup>118</sup>. This is all the more striking as otherwise animals do have a generic instinct against beings dangerous to them.

<sup>118</sup> The ability of snakes to charm animals is attested by all travelers who observe nature without prejudice. In Brehms Tierleben too (Lurche und Kriechtiere, vol. 2, 4th ed. Leipzig and Vienna 1913, p. 266/67) O. zur Straßen gives a vivid description, without however expressing himself in any way about the essence of the power of charming. Doflein, on the other hand, writes (Hesse-Doflein, Tierbau und Tierleben. vol. II. Leipzig and Berlin 1914, p. 147): “That many snakes... feed on birds... has led to an interesting scientific fable, the factual background of which has only recently found its interpretation. It has often been observed that birds, at the approach of the snake, instead of flying away, remain sitting as if spellbound... This was attributed to a kind of hypnotizing influence which was supposed to proceed from the shimmering eye of the snake, from the luster of its skin, from the evenly swaying movements of its head and forebody. How much has been written and mystified about this ‘magical faculty’ of snakes. According to others the sight of the snake was supposed to make the bird stiffen with fright. Investigations recently carried out in the London Zoological Garden have led to a quite different result. No species of animal, with the exception of the apes, recognizes the snake as something to be feared or gives signs of terror at the sight of it. Very many animals, above all small mammals and birds, show however, at the approach of a conspicuous object, attention, even something one might call curiosity. If the object moves slowly, deliberately and quietly, they observe it with tense attention, but without moving. If a sudden quick movement follows, they flee at once. They behave so regardless of whether the head of a snake, a ribbon, or a human finger sways slowly to and fro before them. If the snake now hurls itself at the right moment swiftly upon its victim, it has caught it. It needs no sorcery for this, but proceeds according to the natural capacities of its bodily structure and of its instincts.” This is an example of modern natural science. How unnatural are already the conditions in the “Zoological Garden,” where the animals are accustomed from the beginning of the experiment to a quite different environment and are wholly constrained in the unfolding of their instincts. Apart from the fact that the natural course of events, according to the many descriptions of the nature observers, who are far more familiar with the phenomenon, is by no means so simple and meaningless as it is presented here, and that many other animals also succumb to the spell of the snake’s gaze, this argument and demonstration is only a typical example of how one can take away the soul of nature; one then has the parts in one’s hand, only, alas, the spiritual bond is missing! This power of charming, with all its closer circumstances, has meanwhile also been described to me by a thoroughly critical colleague residing abroad, in a detailed written account, as his own experience.

Another kind of daemonism, yet in essence the same as the previous one, is the luring to destruction by the offering of taste-stimuli that one animal offers another. According to a description by the Jesuit Father Wasmann, famous as an ant-researcher, there lives in the nest of the red wood-ants a beetle that secretes a juice just agreeable to this species of ant, which they so crave that they even feed the beetle’s larvae in order to rear it again. But the beetle is dangerous to the larvae of the ant, thus harms them in their offspring, and the enjoyment of the juice leads them to bodily degeneration; the individual animal is narcotized, and through replacement-breeding imperfect females also arise.

A graceful counterpart, as an example of lovely nature-daemonism, is offered us by Weismann<sup>119</sup> in his “Lectures on the Theory of Descent,” where he reports of an insect that gathers glittering colored little stones, arranges them in a definite way, and [p. 262] so builds a little garden of love, in which it waits for its female, who, attracted by the luster and the arrangement, soon comes to it. What is that, seen from within, other than sorcery and binding in the most pleasing form, as the doing of the serpent is in the gloomiest?

<sup>119</sup> A. Weismann, Vorträge über Deszendenztheorie. 3rd ed. Jena 1913. p. 184.

Here, moreover, we have the middle thing between the use of the body-sting, described in the preceding section, by the digger wasp already mentioned (p. 239), as an instrument that is directly handled by the unconscious will of the species, and the desire of the serpent, working purely through the suggestive glance psychically—and who knows whether not also, through something or other, physiologically. The building insect uses its little stones precisely as a means of conjuration, similar to the savage with his sticks, drums or figures, or the sorcerer with his staff. But is it a difference of principle, or not rather merely a formal one, whether the serpent acts with suggestive power on its prey at a distance of a few meters, or whether the pygmy hunter does this through words of conjuration and transmission of his hypnotic power through the sun’s rays upon the prey animal which, though distant, is yet situated under the same rays, and which is afterwards for the most part struck by his arrow with the same natural certainty at the right spot of the body as the sting of the egg-laying wasp strikes the beetle? It seems to me that only different arrangements and outward forms of expression of one and the same nature-sightedness or magic lie before us here, whose effects, so bewildering for our intellect, simply stand before us as fact.

The next step toward deeper penetration is now to ask whether this nature-daemonism lives only in visible beings such as man and animal, perhaps also in plants; or whether it still leads a life in some other form, not at first bodily visible to us? Such a question, sounding quite odd at first glance, one does not pose arbitrarily; one must have a factual ground for it. This lies in the effects of invisible daemonisms, from time immemorial well known in all folk-souls not yet intellectualized, well feared and combated with corresponding magico-religious means, misunderstood among us as senseless superstition, which are or were just as “real” [p. 263] as their correlate: the daemonic in man and in animal. Those entities, designated as supersensible or supernatural, yet empirically noticeable, may nevertheless be perceptible even to us intellectualized late-age men, grown generally insensitive to their immediate perception, in all sorts of curiosities of our everyday existence. As the nearest at hand and generally known, let me point to the multiple repetition, so frequent and often so unpleasantly occurring under the expression “duplicity of cases,” of like processes not causally connected in the customary sense; besides events, this can also concern the recurrence of objects. “Misfortunes seldom come singly” is a further saying of experience taken from life, which contains a half-conscious, half-unconscious acknowledgment of that independent nature-daemonism. “The malice of the object” is known to us all; and here language itself, regarding from the depth of existence and creating its expression, has, as with a certain mockery of our mechanistic bigotry of causality, quite literally set the demon before our eyes.

Quite clearly and often overpoweringly it confronts us in the psychic formations that we call mass suggestions, mostly of religious or political coloring, on which Spengler gives so clear a characterization in the “Decline of the West”<sup>120</sup>. If such suggestive associations under the power of the daemonic are of longer duration, they probably also adopt badges or emblems or articles of clothing, and these now react in turn fanaticizingly upon the bearers or upon those at first uninvolved and standing farther off; how these effects can be reversed by holding up other symbolic signs, whether the demonized bodies can thereby be brought to rage or pacified or inspired or set in motion in a definite spatial direction or brought to a halt. It is the typical repetition of the signs of magic and conjuration of the oldest time with their concrete effects, though different insofar, that is, no longer so natural, original and effective, as in our cultural sphere they have the intellect far too much as spectator.

<sup>120</sup> O. Spengler, Untergang des Abendlandes. II. p. 22ff.

[p. 264] Such supra-personal demons and daemonisms, if they do not break in foaming and suddenly and therefore mostly pass away again just as quickly, but work themselves out slowly and intensively and spread widely, can lead to the same or very similar social, political or sectarian formations and bodies in the most varied cultural regions and countries. Indeed, seized by the same being, not only the same psychic but also the same organic-bodily formations are produced, of which the same facial expression, the same bodily posture, are perhaps still the simplest and most immediately comprehensible effects on the organism of the bearers. Is that not a perfect parallel to the law of the corresponding temporal forms in organic nature?

The old frontal-eyed men of the early age may now, as black magicians, with their daemonic power have acted terrifyingly and injuriously upon nature and the human environment, indeed perhaps themselves have produced beings of nature which, in their sense, dependent on and nourished by their nature-sighted-daemonic power, worked likewise. The producers were met by the struggle of the luminous human daemons, the terrifying images by their power of binding.
