Therefore we hear in legends and fairy tales of the great wise magician and king—in the Thousand and One Nights he is called Solomon<sup>121</sup>—who shuts the unclean spirit with the frontal eye into the bottle and sinks it in the sea or binds it on the pillar. We find the same echoes from the oldest time in the Odyssey, where, in keeping with the intellectually rationalistic spirit of Greekdom, every metaphysical reminiscence has vanished from the struggle of the heroes with the Cyclops and the legend is only utilized quite externally. Exactly the same level of conception and presentation as that of the Cyclops struggle in Homer corresponds to the transmission of the legend of the dragon fight and of the hero Siegfried in the Nibelungenlied. Here too a quite external working-in into the much later remaining legendary material, and otherwise a poem from which every metaphysically conscious sense has already been lost. As one readily notices, however, Siegfried’s figure stands before all other heroes of the Nibelungen legend in a quite singular, light-enveloped splendor, against which the others appear like late-age knights, while the figure of Siegfried [p. 265] still unmistakably has its highly mythic aureole and also commands magic powers, such as the body armor, the making-invisible and the mythic sword against the dragon.

<sup>121</sup> That he is called Solomon has no other significance than that of a transfer of name, even though in the being of the real King Solomon there may have been some occasion for the ancient notions of magicians to be transferred to him later. Thus Virgil, too, was regarded in the Middle Ages as the great sorcerer, and thus even Peter figures as the devil beside God in a creation legend of the Ukrainians (cf. Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 59). King David, then Roland and Dietrich of Bern, Gilgamesh too, play a quite similar role. And how greatly the early is transferred to the late is shown by the Psalm of Solomon 2, where the old dragon legend has been recast into the Jewish campaign to Egypt with the Romans and the end of Pompey. (Schürer, Geschichte des Jüdischen Volkes II. p. 589. Quoted after H. Gunkel, Schöpfung und Chaos in Urzeit und Endzeit. Göttingen 1895, p. 79.) In the latter, still more of the same kind (p. 73-74). Nevertheless the ancient legend-contents blend here, too, only outwardly; but in essence they are and remain ancient, like the world from which they spring.

Such primordial heroes, as they are still woven into the Parzival legend, hack off one another’s limbs in battles lasting hours, they strike out one another’s eyes, and yet afterwards they go off together as friends, free of suffering. Even if this combat and the grave injuries that fall away in it are symbols, still here too, as always, the genuine symbolism of legend has its deep, real significance. Let it be, then, that a natural process is symbolized; it is nonetheless the natural process of the daemonic wrestling of beings bound to nature; and the form, the image in which the process is clothed, points to a most ancient psychic and physical state of man, in which, as a consequence of the bond with nature, the body too was differently, more strongly flooded by formative life, and regenerated.

If we turn to the natural-historical and metaphysical side of nature-daemonism, then a struggle of man against it means a struggle in a sphere other than the intellectual or outwardly natural one. Who does not think at once of the most ancient human legend of the expulsion from Paradise, handed down not only in Moses, which is closely bound up with the Fall, to which man succumbs after he has lent his ear to the “insinuations of the serpent”? Stepping forth from the spiritual existence of light and from the All-unity, he had committed himself to nature-daemonism, and with this metaphysical step had brought about a psychic-spiritual catastrophe, which has found its imperishable expression, still shattering in its truthfulness even for the most enlightened, most unbelieving man of the late age, in the myth of the Fall, of the expulsion from Paradise, and of the duality and enmity toward nature that set in with it. Thereby the human being was torn out of the spiritual world into the physical — not merely symbolically but wholly and entirely in the manner of nature. The next consequence was first the affliction with physical corporeality, with “death” — the physical primordial man entered earthly appearance.

Upon God’s curse man fell out with the serpent: “There shall be enmity between thee and her.” [p. 266] After man in Paradise had “understood the voices of the animals,” they now become mute to him; he is estranged — in the literal sense — from the rest of living nature. He, who before needed no clothing and had no need to care for his nourishment, must now, after the Fall, clothe his body, which comes to his consciousness for the first time; “for he saw that he was naked.” He must also till the field and “eat his bread in the sweat of his face”: physical existence. Was there, and is there, in order to return to unity, anything other than death? According to the legend it then came into the human world. As individual death for the single person and at the same time as the promise of the redeemer’s death for humanity, as the books of Moses meaningfully relate.

Physical nature, as shows itself ever more clearly even to merely scientific thinking, is not only mechanically objective in the ordinary sense, but is also permeated by states of form and existence which correspond to what the so greatly misunderstood and misused domain of the occult sphere gives us to know. The scientific investigation, still in its beginnings, of, say, telepathic phenomena as bearers of those states and effects will presumably yet open to us an understanding for a physical substance veiled from the coarser use of the senses, in which the delimited individual and bodily appears bound by “invisible threads” and under a different kind of space-time state, where precisely a relation prevails that is popularly characterized by the much-invoked and much-misused “fourth dimension.” Thus one could imagine that there exist formations there which have not yet, or perhaps only at times, realized themselves in coarse physical visibility, have not materialized, as the technical language of research now has it. Such formations could not only be glimpsed visionarily, but they could, meanwhile, already be physically effective themselves, without their coming to light for the bodily eye itself.

So too in the most ancient time, before he appeared in coarsely physical form in vertebrate shape, man could already have been linked with the lower animal world, have released it out of himself, before he himself, for a coarsely physically seeing eye, entered [p. 267] earthly nature — and with this we pick up the train of thought again at the natural-historical and have answered the question to what extent even the lower animal world, prehuman in the ordinary sense, was anchored in the existence of the human stock. Only, that invisible-physical must not be confused, epistemologically falsely, with the Platonic-ideal and finally the transcendent; the connection, however, remains insoluble for the discursively proceeding understanding.

It lies in the nature of the matter, all too well known to everyone who thinks, that such grasping of inner connections is properly inexpressible. Language, the construction of thought in rational sentences, has been brought forth, and is therefore adaptively set, for the conveyance of purely physical images of existence. The psychic, or indeed the spiritual, can be conveyed through words and sentences only if on the receiving side the same inner structure is present, through whose being touched by means of the word-symbols the same inner seeing of one’s own dawns. Thus we are, each of us, after all shut up in our physical body as in a dungeon. Through the walls nothing can get out to, and across to, the other prisoner unless the way beyond the coarse sense-idea and sense-language is opened. Only if one is able to transform this mediating way of speaking into a corresponding and not coarsely objective seeing will one understand what these our expositions would awaken in the reader to living knowledge.

The most ancient becoming of man took place in a state that has long since become transcendent for our life and understanding, a state which at first did not necessarily need to be tied to a corporeality in the strictly visible physical sense. In the human being there lay beforehand, included with it, the daemonic, bright as well as dark. The dark side began to stir; in his spirit a first lighting-up of animal-physical consciousness occurred — the whispering of the serpent that was wound about the tree of life. The Fall and the expulsion from Paradise took place. This can therefore have been nothing other than the physical-bodily effect and transformation of the human being issuing from that transcendent psychic-spiritual alteration. The estrangement between man and woman set in. [p. 268] That is to say: there came about the visibly physical origin of the bisexual human being of the most primal kind and at the same time — corresponding to the metaphysical estrangement between the originally divine, hence specifically human, spiritual being and the opposite one of animality, of which he now became conscious — the creation of an animal world of daemonic character, symbolized in the serpent, which now confronted the human being, become physically visible, likewise physically objectified.

From then on man stood visibly there in organic nature; animal-like procreation prevailed in him, because the formerly transcendent unitary being had now entered physical nature in countless individuals, who were mortal and, according to the law of nature, ever renewed and multiplied themselves. With that the struggle with nature, the struggle with the animal world, the struggle for living space, and the struggle of men among themselves, of Cain with Abel, had come. Out of the ideal primordial human being there had become, objectively-physically, on the one hand the daemonic animal, on the other the Ur-Adamic human type, the man expelled from Paradise. Paradise had been nothing other than the inner living state of unity of the whole natural and human-divine existence, which had been broken by the stirring of the daemonic and its becoming conscious and thereby its physical objectification. Since then nature has been our enemy on the way back to spiritual man.

So there arose simultaneously with visible man both a higher animal world now standing over against him, which made itself known in daemonic beings of the psychic character of serpents, in daemonic shapes of terror, and bore in itself that mentality which man’s primal being had released out of itself metaphysically, which he now understood in his soul, experienced, and fought with unreconciled hatred; and on the other hand there arose the mammalian world physically rooted in the same root with him, to which all reptilian-daemonic being is alien, and which from then on partly developed further by itself, partly was ever again newly multiplied out of the main stock, man, the more of the animal-like split off from him, the more typically human he himself became. So man became, metaphysically and physically, the ancestor of an animality which had previously [p. 269] rested in him, then became free and now developed beside him, more and more, into physical existence. From this that tradition becomes explicable which says that man does not descend from the animals, but the animal from man. Ever since, the creature languishes along with man under his Fall and, like him, awaits redemption. The return to that transcendent unity, and with it the conscious deification of what before was unconscious unity, and with it the liberation from the estranged-physical: that is the longing for the lost Paradise, which even today lives, not only in the human soul, but according to ancient doctrine also, unconscious to the individual creature, in the animal soul.

But, as already touched upon, another estrangement lives in human nature from the beginning: the sexual one between man and woman. It is an uncannily shattering moment when, out of inner seeing, one comprehends for the first time in its whole depth the old saying of the secret hatred of the sexes. Woman, who according to the legend first and immediately, understandingly, lent her ear to the whispering of the serpent, is today still the real bearer of the sweetly or darkly daemonic in human life, far more than man, whose daemonic, even when it is dark as in a Napoleon, is nonetheless intellectualized and, in keeping with his whole nature, accomplishes deeds outwardly, however dreadful they may be; but it does not covet for itself the soul of man — the serpent. We have no more fitting symbol even today. In the original, transcendent state before the expulsion from Paradise both, the masculine and the feminine, must already have been there, but must have had another sense, another meaning, than later in the physical. Both must have been a reciprocal spiritual unity, that for which, beyond corporeality, we all strive as inward men, as for a lost Paradise.

The original being-united of the two poles of sex, the masculine and the feminine, is known also to Germanic theogony and anthropogony. As soon as a man-like formation rises out of the primal matter, the further development proceeds by way of natural procreation. When Ymir slept, [p. 270] he fell into a sweat, and under his left arm there grew to him a man and a woman. One of his feet begot a son with the other; from this came his descendants, the giants<sup>122</sup>.

<sup>122</sup> Golther, Germanische Mythologie, p. 514.

Here now the legends that I interpret in the sense of what was said above, namely that the entry of the human being into the physical was an expulsion from his original, transcendent-divine being, and that thereby the door was opened to daemonism in him and around him in nature, and that he created beings of nature<sup>123</sup>. From Adam’s tears of repentance, in the legend, after the Fall, all sorts of plants arise; in the Arabic narrative the narcissus, in the Indian the coconut tree, the myrobalan, and all sorts of precious spices. For his tears that fell upon the earth had such magic power because he still had within him the juices of the food of Paradise. Eve’s tears turned to pearls in the sea, and where they fertilized the dry land the most glorious flowers sprang up. But the serpent wept too; on the earth her tears brought forth the scorpion, in the sea the crab, because she had left Paradise by the gate of vexation.

<sup>123</sup> For this and the following legends: Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 220—229.

It is, to be sure, very simple and obvious to see in this only a poetry of nature. Admittedly it is that too, presumably because the last transmitters recast the ancient material so, or had already received it so, and passed it on so, whether through misunderstanding or on purpose. But considered with what the manner of intuition followed here discloses to us, there open up beneath the harmless dress which later ages threw over them mighty events in nature and in human life: the physical formation of nature through the becoming and the transformation of the human being himself, from within, metaphysically, not outwardly cultural-technical.

Adam’s bodily change, however, is reported by the Ethiopic Book of Adam. God speaks there to him: As long as you were under my command and an angel of light, you did not know water. But since you have transgressed my command, you must give your body water to drink and make it grow; for it has become animal-like. I withdrew from you the nature of light when you fell into transgression; yet I did not change you wholly into darkness, but made you this body of yours and created this skin upon it. But at that time you knew nothing of weariness and suffering.

[p. 271] Another legend from the same source, which with truly human truthfulness, deeply in mood, renders the whole poverty of physical existence bereft of Paradise: When Adam and Eve had been driven out of Paradise they lived in a cave. In the morning they went out before the cave. But their belly was weighed down with food and they did not know how to help themselves. They lamented: such troubles did not befall us in the garden, and such bad food we did not eat there. And Adam implored God: Let us not perish through what is in our belly. And God looked upon them and made openings for them through which they could relieve themselves, as it has become nature to this very hour. Adam and Eve, however, thrust out what they had in their belly, went into the cave, sad and weeping because of the excrement that had gone out of their body. And they felt that it had become different with them from that hour on, and that the hope of coming into the garden again was cut off from them, because no body that needs food, drink and evacuation is able to dwell there.

So far the legend. Can the terrible tragedy, by which humanity entered earthly existence through a guilt, be depicted more vividly and more truthfully? And here one should still ask whether something fundamentally real does not live in the ancient thoughts? Whether it happened thus or thus — it is historical reality, because it is the psychic, the metaphysical reality. There is nothing inward that does not have its outward, and conversely. That we must distinguish the two lies in our reflecting consciousness. When we look at it metaphysically and understand in it, we also know that the “outward” is symbol and parable. Yet in its being symbol it is not unreal, not detached empty fantasy, as the sober understanding of our time takes it to be, because with seeing eyes it still does not see the real.

Man’s soul was torn apart, and the body full of suffering corresponded to this. It is again thoroughly true to life and, in the metaphysical sense, real for that time, when we see in the legend Adam and Eve still wrestling for the lost Paradise. As we find the struggle with the dragons and lindworms in that [p. 272] nature-sighted and then heroic primeval age which follows, so too among the human beings expelled from Paradise, who have remained partakers of the knowledge of God, the struggle against their own bodily soul, become animal-like, goes on alongside. The Arabic text of the Book of Adam depicts for us an asceticism of abstinence which the first human pair impose upon themselves; they separate for forty days and each stand in the water in a different place. So they stood and prayed and begged of God that he forgive them and lead them back into Paradise. But on the 35th day Satan drew near and misled Eve into going to Adam — and both are again in their old sorrow.

To be sure there are other legends that contradict this: Adam and Eve hold out and rejoice over it. But do they come again to Paradise? Let each answer that for himself out of his own existence. But are the legends, in spite of such contradictions, however they may run, any the less true in the essence of the matter? Enough; man, hurled out of the transcendent state of Paradise into the physical world, afflicted with bodily misery, has so suffered and struggled. And it is with this truth alone that we are concerned. And how many a relapse into the animal-daemonic may yet have occurred — a last echo of remembrance lies perhaps still in Goethe’s uncannily shimmering ballad “The Bride of Corinth.”

To the originally so darkly daemonic in woman, however, which now in human existence raged itself out, fighting and fought against, conquering and conquered, point all the remarkable traditions of Adam’s first wife, who meets us as Lilith also on the Blocksberg in Faust’s Walpurgis Night. She was the mother of devilish children, and her figure thereby points clearly to the highly daemonic sexual struggles or states taking place in the first humanity, of which we know last faint images, probably, even in our cultural condition, and which lead a shy existence outwardly. At that time, however, they were, like the rest of the life permeated by nature-sightedness, mightily daemonic or heroic. To this the following Jewish legend: When God had created the first man and his wife, they soon began to quarrel with one another. She rose up and would not obey; for she said: we [p. 273] are both equal to one another, because created from earth. With a mysterious magic formula she rose into the air and vanished. Adam complained of it to God, and he threatened her with the death of 100 of her children daily, if she did not comply with his command to return. But she swore by the living name of God that she was there for nothing else than to destroy the young children (?) and accepted that every day 100 of them should die. Therefore a hundred evil spirits die every day. But God gave Adam Eve. A Swedish version, however, says further: Adam’s wife, Lucia, became displeasing for some reason. The marriage was dissolved and she, with her children, was condemned to remain invisible.

A wonderful embodiment in an emblem of that innermost connection between animal-like daemonism in man, Fall, and objectification of the daemonic in the animal world, which now confronted the human being as such physically-organically, is given by an Ethiopic text: After the expulsion from Paradise the serpent was cursed and had to crawl on its belly in the dust. It, which before had been beautiful above all animals, was now ugly and, through God’s curse, poisonous. When now the cursed serpent saw Adam and Eve, it made its head swell and stood up on its tail, and its eyes became like blood; and it wanted to kill them. It came first toward Eve: she ran away. Adam had no stick to beat it to death, but his heart was inflamed for Eve’s sake; he went at the serpent and seized it by the tail. Then it turned against him and said to him: “Because of you and because of Eve I have become slippery, so that I must go on my belly.” By its great strength it threw Adam and Eve to the ground and laid itself over them to kill them. Then God sent his angel; he threw the serpent off them and set it upright. But God said to the serpent: “The first time I made you only slippery, so that you must go on your belly; but the speech I did not take from you; from now on, however, you shall be dumb and no longer able to speak, you and your kind. For through you the ruin of my servants came for the first time, and now you have wanted to kill them.” And the serpent at that hour became dumb and could not speak again.

[p. 274] That after the Fall man no longer understands the language of the animals is a consistent, ancient motif, which accordingly meets us again and again in all sorts of transformations and much distorted, and at times is also transposed into a later time, long after the expulsion from Paradise. In a wholly Christianized and purely nature-poetic form, letting nothing be felt any more of the original horror still alive in the preceding legends, it is offered to us by the legend of the Estonian peasant who wanted to fell a tree in the forest and to whom the souls of the trees called out to spare them; for from of old the trees could speak. Now they do indeed have a soul too, which one recognizes from the fact that they grow and bear blossoms and fruit; but speech has been taken from them. For when the peasant went home grieved, the Lord Jesus met him, to whom he complained of his misfortune. Then the Lord answered him that from now on he would forbid the trees to speak to men. So one hears it now only as a soft rustling in the forest and the leaves moving when the trees whisper gently to one another.

In this legend the timeless moment too, that is, the purely metaphysical, becomes vividly clear. For as soon as one goes at it with the logical probe nothing remains but a flatly allegorizing fairy tale, because it is entirely unmotivated in itself that the peasant should have experienced such a thing now for the first time. He himself and his parents and forefathers must surely already have felled wood often. Why does he alone have this experience of the trees’ lament? Here, then, there shows itself an inner intuition expressing only the idea, in which the moment of time and logical causality carry no weight at all; there shows itself thus the sovereignty of the old legendary cores, strong in reality, independent of their wording, cores which one never does justice to by logical dissection in the ordinary sense, and which must rather remain eternally silent to it.

To the fallen human being, expelled from Paradise, help had come through the divinely pure spirit, and this spirit “raised him up again.” That is perhaps what the combats of the heroes with the dragons, of the wise with the dark magicians symbolize, and of which the legends of all peoples, though with [p. 275] countless variants, are yet in essence full in agreement. Here I should like to put up for consideration whether the Laocoön legend too does not contain this one old core? Homer apparently understood and presented it wholly in the Greek-hierarchical manner, perhaps already took it over so himself. But in essence it too comes to this, that the daemonic serpent meets him who represents the luminous, him and his race, with will to destruction. Since, however, the serpent in that late age of the time had already become a being of the good, it comes on the other hand not out of daemonic darkness toward Laocoön, but is sent by Athena, who herself is a goddess of light and not an evil, hate-filled daemon. Thereby, mirror-fashion, Laocoön is made into a villain — and in this crossing-over of the two psychic-moral motives the whole uncertainty becomes clear which already prevailed in the Homeric world in the handling of such most ancient mythological motives, as we had remarked on several occasions.

When Adam had come into God’s favor again, only then did he also become lord over nature, over the animal-daemonic and the will to destruction in it. A Jewish legend relates: Adam said to God: You created us for Paradise, and before I transgressed you brought all the animals to me that I might name them; and you had made them all harmless to me. But now they want to devour us; they will snatch my life away from the face of the earth. And when God perceived this he commanded the animals that they should come to Adam and should do no harm of any kind either to him or to Eve, or to the good and righteous among his descendants. Then they bowed before God’s command, with the exception of the serpent.

One sees how and where one meets such legends of most ancient origin, there can always be won from them a rational meaning for the history of man, they always yield us the same core, if only one has once found a key and applies it in the same sense. That they then so often offer what is conclusive, and in essence, for all outward diversity, again and again present a core out of primeval history — that is conversely precisely the proof of the correctness of the method, of the correctness and fruitfulness of the [p. 276] ideas brought to bear on them. That legends were quite obviously often shaped and handed on only for poetic purpose or for moral instruction must not, as already set forth, mislead us into seeing in them altogether merely symbols for such purposes, or even in the end only allegories; an erroneous interpretation which one meets with at every step, because one so rarely wants to grasp what an essential difference there is between the old inner pith and the body of wood and bark layered over it later in the manner of annual rings. If one keeps that in mind, then the garment is clearly separated from the essence, and all the manifoldness becomes a single, simple, small thing, which nonetheless discloses to us a great mystery.

It must not be allowed here, and still in what follows, where the lines of the biological and of the metaphysical view cross and a straying to the wrong side is all too possible, that the misunderstanding arise as if at any earth-historical time or anywhere at all Paradise could be sought “on earth or in heaven.” If one has undertaken to give it a flatly naturalistic interpretation, or if one, as in the Assyriological literature, wants to derive the myth of Paradise once more from the sight of the starry heaven with its rivers of the Milky Way<sup>124</sup>, then such interpretations do not rise above the long-familiar allegories of legend, whose rationalistic incapacity does not disclose the depths of human existence, which from of old appeared to the greatest minds not as an allegorical play of nature, but as an overwhelming myth of truth about guilt and atonement, about fall and redemption, and has been so experienced. The other notion too, as if we could mean that before the physically visible appearance of the human being in earthly nature, thus at some time in older earth-historical periods, paradisal happiness, peace without enmity, without struggle for existence and death had reigned — this notion too we reject. With every physical existence, as soon as it is of individual nature, thus with every single animal existence, the non-paradisal state is given. The paradisal state of existence was, if one is again to speak so space-and-time-wise of transcendently bound things, in order to bring them near to the reader’s inner seeing — it was already ended when with the [p. 277] metaphysical conception of the later physical, thus no longer purely spiritual, world in eternal creatorhood the idea of man too became alive. For precisely this is the deepest sense of being human, that in him the Creator becomes conscious of his existence, as we live it again inwardly through the millennia ever on new paths, live it in pain or in jubilation. Perhaps that too is the deepest reason why every greatest artist has to reflect on his art in torment. So creation, and the creation of man too, is not yet at an end: it is still ever the day of creation!

<sup>124</sup> F. Delitzsch, Wo lag das Paradies? Eine biblisch-assyriologische Studie. Leipzig 1881.

We have come here to the remotest problems of philosophy and of religious vision. We take from them the prospect that becoming man is, after all, the last and the first meaning of all physical-organic existence in nature, and that God sends the creatures to him, to man, that he may give them “name and essence.” Thus the old popular feeling that God created the earth for man’s sake, though untrue in its naive manner, is yet an intimation of the deepest myth of man. And for us myth is metaphysical-physical reality. We arrive at the conviction that man was already, even in the lower organic nature, the phylogenetic ground of origin; that this nature was already part of his essence when, in still older geological epochs, he himself had not yet entered into visible physical existence even as a vertebrate form.

[p. 278]

### Die Natur als Abbild des Menschen — Nature as the Image of Man

With the foregoing a bridge has been built that leads across to that other problem, central to our inquiry: the bond between the human essence and its development, and the earth-historical development of animal life.

We assume that primordial man entered into his physical existence with a daemonic, nature-sighted essence, and still comprehended within himself the higher animal kingdom, to speak in Platonic terms: its living metaphysical archetypes, that is, the genera and types that had not yet entered into physical existence. Unless one thinks metaphysically, one will be able to make no sense of all this. Accordingly man found himself, in this nature, visibly confronted by an animal world that had issued from the cleaving of his essence. To this corresponded in the physical world, above all, the antagonism between primordial man and the reptile world. This had precisely those psychic-daemonic qualities that man now felt as hostile, because he had himself experienced them in his metaphysical essence as catastrophic and ruinous, qualities that he was to cast out of himself and that were now embodied for him in the daemonic character of the reptilian beings, out of which soon arose the whole “vermin” of dragons, lindworms and serpents, of which, as something hostile to the human world and therefore continually fought by it physically and psychically, the myths speak to us down to this day, when even the Punch-and-Judy show, rooted in the dark depths of the folk soul, still knows a reptilian “Tier Piep” as the enemy of man.

With that act of spiritual evolution, which ran its course in the transcendent, played over into physical nature and there came to work itself out, and which we wish to call, in the language of myth, the “Fall,” a curse had also come into the animal world, which had now been projected, as a direct result of the spiritual tragedy of the human essence that had taken place in the transcendent [p. 279], into physical existence and objectified in it. This clarifies for us the myth of the dragging of the animal creature along into the Fall and death of man. The amphibian, or an amphibian fish-form, was perhaps the animal primal form in which his corporeality and that of the reptiles then showed itself visibly and physically; and for that reason the amphibian being may appear, in the Babylonian legend of the oldest men already told, as the friend and teacher of the first man (p. 93).

The shedding of his skin by the Adamite, whose scaly armor fell from him, mentioned in the section on the bodily characteristics of primordial man (p. 95), plays its peculiar role in legend, in that it appears connected on the one hand with the antagonism of the human and the serpent nature, and on the other with the entry of death into the human world. Thus the Melanesians of the Bismarck Archipelago relate: “The good spirit loved man and wished to make him immortal; but he hated the serpent and wished to kill it. He said to his brother: go to man and impart to him the secret of immortality, and tell him that he is to shed his skin every year; so he will be safe from death and will always renew his life. And tell the serpent that henceforth it must die. The messenger acquitted himself badly of his charge, in that he betrayed the secret to the serpent. Since then men are mortal, but the serpent, because of its shedding of skin, immortal.” The motif recurs here and there throughout the whole Malayan world. But its explanation is not so simple and external as Frazer, from whom it is taken<sup>125</sup>, thinks, when he says that an animal that sheds its skin simply gives man the impression of a rejuvenation. Rather, here too the inner thread of the legend, grasped metaphysically, is once more this: that the casting off of the daemonic reptilian nature from the essence of primordial man made it indeed humanly physical, but let it enter the physical world burdened with death.

<sup>125</sup> J. G. Frazer, The belief in immortality and worship of the dead. Vol. I. London 1913, p. 69, 74.

After the Adamic man had been created and found himself in physical dress in nature, and saw confronting him what was daemonic and had formerly been in him, now set out from himself, then the struggle with the “serpents” began, which was now necessary and meritorious in a twofold [p. 280] respect. The outer struggle with the vermin was the symbol of the inner enmity — everything transitory became here, too, a parable. In these struggles, thoroughly interwoven with nature and waged with all the means of a super- or sub-individual nature-sightedness and of a lower daemonism, it was perhaps a matter of the being or not-being of the whole species on this side and on that. Only late seers, who looked into the past partly by drawing on legendary oral tradition and partly by a clairvoyant memory, then probably first created from it the images of myth and legend, which in their turn became the foundation for the stock of tradition and poetry of still later epochs; as, for example, the solar myth or the cult of the dead may have been the late-period formation of the most ancient mythic experience of nature.

To the degree that man rose out of his deeply nature-bound and wretched daemonic initial existence, the living animal nature, corporeally co-ordinate with him, mirrored this condition too; and this both metaphysically and physically. Thus from this standpoint also we can infer, from the physical world of animal forms in the epochs of the earth, the bodily condition of man, just as, conversely, from the appearance of newly added animal types we can infer the degree to which the human essence had released ever more of the animal out of itself, in order thus mutatively to become ever purer into what we call the Apollonian human type.

The old legends suggest that the oldest man became man in the sense that he “sundered” himself from the serpent nature; so with the primal-Adamic man came the daemonic reptilian being. Later came the post-Adamic man, resembling more the Noachian, that is, the pure mammal-man, but still strongly endowed with the daemonic sense. With the Noachian man of our type, though still far from our intellectual height and bodily formation, came the intellectual being, though at first still endowed with nature-sightedness. Bound up with this new becoming-man was the physically objectified appearance of the later mammalian type, from which the human stock now “sundered” itself and which it had released out of its stock into nature. It is to be assumed that this happened early, because we have the oldest [p. 281] true mammals presumably already at the beginning of the Mesozoic era, and because already in the closing phase of the Palaeozoic era many reptiles show mammalian characteristics as a temporal signature. Man himself is, from the ground up, physically a mammal. The mammal is then, metaphysically seen, the brighter, intellectually freer, less daemonic animal phenomenon as against the reptile. If one compares the essence of the reptilian beast of prey, that is, of a lindworm with its presumably very serpentine psychic qualities, and the essence of a mammalian beast of prey such as the lion or even the insufferable hyena, we can find in these nothing more of what we have called gloomily daemonic in the serpent-beings. From this we now infer back to the meanwhile less daemonic metaphysical condition of the human essence. It is just as though a piece of animality had thereby once more been expelled from the inner essence of the human stock, and the latter itself given back to itself more freed. So here too he was once again “estranged” from the animal world; his nature had split the lower mammalian nature off from itself. This had now become a natural being for itself and from then on unfolded along its own paths with its own formal development. But man ascended further to purer spirituality, his body became freer, more upright, he drew near to Apollo.

The pictorial echo of such a conception we have in the Babylonian Gilgamesh epic. As already mentioned in another connection, I set particular value on this work as a source from which, despite the late reworking of the material and despite its being pieced together from inadequate parts, with the incongruities of sense that thereby arise, one can gain positively overwhelming insights into the most ancient relations of humanity. For the epic begins at once with a characterization of Gilgamesh that cannot possibly apply to a Quaternary man and that allows the primeval-heroic and nature-bound to shine through only too plainly: the hero who saw everything, who came to know all things, understood and saw through everything, who looked into the hidden depths of wisdom, saw what was guarded, opened what was concealed, brought tidings from the time of the Flood and raised giant [p. 282] structures. He is the representative of the primeval post-Adamic, pre-Noachian man, and beside this nature-sighted oppressor-hero Engidu is afterwards set, the Noachian mammal-man, who mingles with the old type — one should take the typical in place of the individual figure. As in the Bible the creation of Adam from earth, and with his Fall the expulsion from Paradise and the estrangement from nature, is reported, so the same motif is in the Gilgamesh epic tied to the becoming of the mammal-man Engidu, of whom we spoke above (p. 92)<sup>126</sup> and whom, according to our chronology, we regard as the Noachian. Gilgamesh, the king, has mighty buildings erected; heavily the forced labor weighs on the inhabitants of the land. The oppressed call the gods to their aid, and Aruru, the creatress, forms of clay the natural man Engidu, endowed with mighty strength. His body is covered with hair, his head-hair long, and he is clothed in skins. With the game, the cattle — the mammal, not the reptile — he lives together, goes with them to the watering place, his heart rejoices in the teeming of the waters. The men of the land ruled by Gilgamesh do not yet know him; only the nature-sighted Gilgamesh himself has already beheld him in a dream. Now Engidu penetrates into the sphere of Gilgamesh, and with that comes the transformation, which sounds like an infinitely sad farewell, yet one bound up with a new promise, from the nature hitherto so intimately bound up with him, from the “cattle”:

<sup>126</sup> Das Gilgameschepos, p. 73 (see n. 151).

“Six days and seven nights Engidu rose up, tending the harlot of love. / When he had sated himself on her charms, he turned his eyes upon his cattle: / No sooner did they see Engidu than the gazelles fled away, / The cattle of the field shrank back from him! / Then Engidu stood amazed, as though spellbound was his body; / Lamed were his knees, because his cattle went away. / Engidu grew calm, not as before was his wildness: / He, yes he listens, he opens his ear; / He turned back and sat down at the feet of the harlot...” / “The harlot says to him, to Engidu: / Beautiful art thou, Engidu, like a god art thou! / What wilt thou with the teeming herd, rushing over the field? / [p. 283] Come, I will lead thee to walled Uruk... / Where Gilgamesh dwells, unique in strength... / She persuades him, till her words please him; / Knowing his heart, he seeks a friend. / Engidu says to her: / Come then, harlot, take me with thee / To the pure holy house... / Where Gilgamesh dwells. / I alone will change fate, / Born in the field, mighty in strength, / O Gilgamesh, would that I might behold thy face! / All that shall be, I know in truth.”

Does not everything lie here, for him who can grasp the metaphysical metaphysically, that was bound up with that new decisive becoming-man, that had to be bound up with it? Everything that lives in the old sacred legends and notions of the whole of humanity recurs here, in these wonderfully tragic verses, with such an inner truthfulness that we are encouraged to draw upon them further, as hitherto.

Thus nature is for us not only an image of the human condition in the bodily respect, for times from which we as yet know no fossil human skeleton, but also an immediate gauge of what the human essence had meanwhile cast out of itself of inner and outer animality, and of what had objectified itself over against it in nature, and of how much more Apollonian it had itself become. All this is to be understood metaphysically and at the same time thoroughly physically-phylogenetically. Whoever is unwilling to behold and comprehend both in one indivisible process, let him read no further; for this is not symbolic in the banal sense, but thoroughly realistic in the physical and metaphysical sense. At the same time this manner of consideration lets us understand why earlier human types must have been built and constituted differently also anatomically and physiologically; a different soul with different contents and forces had to find a different bodily expression too, and to have other organs at its disposal<sup>127</sup>.

<sup>127</sup> It is remarkable that in a more recent work on the origin of man, which became known to me only afterward (P. Alsberg, Das Menschheitsrätsel. Versuch einer prinzipiellen Lösung. Dresden 1922), the author on the one hand adopts the absolute animal origin of man in the usual line of thought of the theory of descent, but yet emphasizes that from the moment when that ancestral man-animal (Pithek-anthropo-goneus) turned, on the one hand, into human development and, on the other, into the animal-ape one, a special principle of development came into play. “The first man was in body and mind a wholly faithful copy of his animal predecessor. Not by any special formation of organs or by any heightening of his intellect did he differ from it, but solely and exclusively by his special principle of development, which only secondarily brought about bodily and also mental changes” (p. 377). But what is that other than the fundamentally different determination of the human being as against the animal being? Here too the researcher who will not leave the ground of the theory of descent can do no other than declare man to be something “in principle” other than the animal. I do not know in what respect that would differ from all religious myths, except in manner of expression?

The duality, the rift, that runs with man’s Fall, metaphysically and physically, through nature, where now the divine and the daemonic, creature of God and creature of the devil [p. 284] stand confronting one another, of which we have already spoken, finds its echo in many legends. Well known is the old designation of flies, lice, bedbugs, rats and mice as creatures and servants of the devil. But to more than this simple abuse of vermin, ultimately comprehensible merely as an outflow of everyday vexation, this confrontation of the two types rises in the frequently recurring legend of the bee and the wasp: the former a creature of God, the latter a creature of the devil. Here we find a deeper metaphysics already. Whereas the bee in more recently worked-over legends is much brought together with the Savior, against whom the devil enviously works with the creation of the wasp; or whereas the wasps appear as disobedient children cursed by a mother, the bee is always designated as holy; she has remained over from the lost Paradise and had to leave it only through man’s sin. She is also always wise and clairvoyant and knows many a counsel against what the devil undertakes against God. At first she is said to have been white of body, and afterwards to have become blackish-brown through the devil’s work upon her<sup>128</sup>. Drawn into the trivial, one can of course easily read into the legend the sense that man, greedy by nature, will readily designate the honey-giving bee as a divine animal when he sees beside her the rapacious wasp, worthless to him. But this very opposition between the two animals has, as its physical, so also as its necessary counterpart its metaphysical sense, and that is precisely what the legend, in contrast to our everyday understanding, bears within itself and what we too wish to pursue.

<sup>128</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen. I. p. 128; 215.

If one considers that the bee is the most perfect “natural somnambulist,” then this her nature accords with her origin from Paradise mentioned in the legend and at the same time with the first human being, whose oldest condition we likewise took to be perfect nature-sightedness, that is, a quite pronounced natural somnambulism with scarcely noteworthy individual variation, as in the bee. She knows counsel against all manner of things the devil undertakes; that is a clear designation of her natural clairvoyance. Her luminous daemonism, however, manifests itself in fact in this, that she does not, like the wasps and the other vermin, plague living beings. [p. 285] She is therefore, with her essence and her nature, still the genuine representative of the original paradisal condition, to which her white bodily form symbolically corresponded. Insofar, then, the metaphysical interpretation here too illumines the sense of the physical.

I refer further to yet another remarkable parallel that can be drawn between this our manner of conception and a natural-historical phenomenon. This is the creation of the cat, which according to legend God gave to man on account of the plague of mice getting out of hand. A Finnish tradition gives the legend thus: When Jumala had created man, he showed him to the devil; the devil wanted to create something still more singular and created the mouse; then Jumala created a cat and threw it after the mouse. In the parallel legend, however, it runs: When in the beginning of the world the grain was already beginning to grow, the devil created the mouse, that it might eat up the grain of men. Still more essential, however, appears the version of the Russian Methodius: The devil changed himself into a mouse and began to gnaw the bottom of the ark. Noah prayed to God, and there came a ravening beast, a lion. From its nostrils sprang a tomcat and a she-cat; they strangled the mouse and the devil’s guile came to naught<sup>129</sup>. Is it not, then, a coincidence surprising for the primeval-historical significance of the legend, that for the true cats, to which lion and tiger also belong, we have found in palaeontology as yet no possibility of a morphological-phylogenetic connection? They appear in the Late Tertiary, but their origin is still wholly dark. The thought might therefore be pursued that they were a breeding product of Noachian man, whose skill in this direction is betrayed also by the cultivation of the vine and of the apples of the Hesperides. That would then give the last-named legend its clear sense: from a wild animal form of the older Tertiary, which has its nearest formal relatives today in Madagascar, that is, in the residual region of the former Gondwana continent, those Noachian men created the species of cats, last the house cat, as the comparatively most harmless, which had become a domestic animal. The intermediate steps of the breeding ladder may have run wild or never become tame; they appear fossil first in the [p. 286] Late Tertiary in the Atlantic region, namely in Europe, and essentially simultaneously in North America, and have there freely peopled nature. Perhaps with this the primeval-historically true kernel can also be peeled out of a tradition such as that of the prophet Daniel in the lions’ den; and finally also the use of the lion’s form as an emblem of sovereignty in coats of arms or at the gate of Mycenae?

<sup>129</sup> Dähnhardt, ibid. p. 166. That the lion is an unfinished breeding of Tertiary man is also reported by the theosophical literature on Atlantis (see n. 56).

The phenomenon touched on here at length, of an opposition of animal forms in nature, of which the one are, so to speak, the evil image of the others, and which is accompanied by such definite legendary notions, I should like to describe by way of comparison as a “complementary” conception of organic forms and of their ensoulment. As the eye, dazzled by a primary color, creates for itself in optical self-deception the complementary color after shutting out the light, so too in organic nature there often appears within individual types a complementary, one might almost say: base variety. Thus over against man the anthropoid ape; over against the forest and field insects the bedbugs, fleas and lice; over against the noble game the rats and mice; over against the bee the wasp; over against the grain-bearing fruit the empty grasses. This fact, vexatious and harmful for the human race in many ways, might, as said, be that on which that tradition of the devil’s working against God’s works rests allegorically, and which would thereby be traced back to a very simple natural, yet only naively — not profoundly — anthropocentric relation, and apparently explained. But the question is surely this: did men experiencing mythically mean that externality by it? Did they see and think like our sober late ages, clinging to the outside of things? They saw, I think, here too again an inner connection of opposed metaphysical conditions running through nature, as it also runs through the human soul; an opposition, a sundering, that lies probably deeper than the banal feeling of vexation and everyday plague that such accursed creatures cause. To this feeling or knowing, pointing to something deeper, namely something metaphysical, they gave mythic-pictorial expression, which, even if it may have been handed on ever so [p. 287] flat and external in the legends, might nonetheless have expressed an inner of nature, an essentially real and still operative something; to trace this and bring it forth again will perhaps one day belong to an explanation of organic forms just as, now, for example, the epistemological penetration of the duality of idea and being belongs to philosophy.

It would surely be an arrogant, indeed morally reprehensible conduct, were one as a human being to look down in pharisaical disdain on the animal world or on individual animals, such as the apes that so often instinctively repel us, whether one be a descent theorist or stand upon a naively Mosaic popular faith. As little as a justified unfavorable judgment of another human being entitles us morally to meet him with contempt in our innermost heart, be the loathing ever so great, so little can any loveless contempt of animal beings find room in a human being who experiences nature as the manifestation of the Godhead, albeit of the unredeemed Godhead. And yet a connection is conceivable — we maintain it here — out of which a hostile feeling toward the animal world or individual types of it can, consciously or nature-sightedly, arise and act in a special degree. If we but recall what the fear of serpents and dragons in myth meant in its strongest form, as we interpreted it in its natural physical and metaphysical aspect, we can now go further and put forward as possible that the apes appear to us as a particularly unsympathetic distorted image of our own essence and our own figure because, on the one hand, they are precisely the last stage of formation which we have just stripped off, leaving it behind us; as also, on the other hand, perhaps the possibility of a new straying into this biological stage of form-character cannot be regarded as excluded by natural science, which perhaps lives in us as a presentiment of nature and is instinctively felt. This presentiment of nature can very well, in spiritually primitive men, be translated directly into a feeling of enmity, or at least call forth a repudiating or scornful contempt of such beings. If one has ever seen with what positively insane fury people from [p. 288] the common folk destroy dangerous and harmless snakes in the forest and thereby, as I myself once experienced, in a kind of cultic primitive ecstasy, utter blasphemous imprecations, not merely abusing them in vulgar fashion — something quite different lies in it — one will be better able to appreciate the path of thought on which we set out a moment ago. Only on a more liberated spiritual level, where nature like one’s fellow man is recognized and embraced as the Tat twam asi — that art thou — do such lower instincts depart, such as one still encounters abundantly today, perhaps even in oneself. And perhaps the Mesozoic Noachite, when the earlier mammalian conditions had only just been released from the human stock, stood over against these in a similar way as we today stand toward the apes, from whom man last parted and into whose stage a slipping back, as said, is biologically not impossible. The older and more nature-bound, the less spiritualized earlier men were, so much the more strongly may they have been dominated metaphysically by such hostilities against the animal world, whose highest and most daemonic after-echo we see today still vibrating alive in the people, in the enmity toward dragons and serpents, and preserved in emblems or poems. Has not the ape, too, among some peoples been represented as the prototype of the god of death; and the scornful fury with which in the century just past the popular, Darwinistically imbued teachers of descent on the one side brutally proclaimed the derivation of the whole human race from this animal type, and against which they were on the other side indignantly repulsed from ethical or religious motives, strikes one as a psychic, not scientific, struggle resting on such instincts of nature, for which one might positively choose the figure of the dragon-slaying archangel as emblem. We will therefore not simply smile at such moods and such enmities toward animals, any more than at such legends of creatures of the devil among the animals, but ask after their metaphysically well-founded sense. How peculiarly such a train of thought illumines the ark of Noah and finally the legend of the unicorn, this section will show at the last.

The inner connection of human life and animal world becomes clear from the following legend of the Jews: Man had indeed [p. 289] committed sin, but why should the innocent cattle be punished? To this a sage replied: A king wished to celebrate the wedding of his son and prepared all manner of precious things and a feast. But then the king’s son died. Then the king arose, tore down the canopy and destroyed everything that was on it. The servants said: Lord, thy son is dead, but why hast thou destroyed the canopy also? The king said: For whom did I make all this? Only for my son. Now that he is dead, what is the canopy to me? So also the Lord, who said: All that is on earth and in the water I have made for none but for man alone; now that man is no more, what are cattle, beasts and birds for? If man has perished, let all perish; if man lives, let all live<sup>130</sup>.

<sup>130</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden. I. p. 197/98.

In keeping with this inner connection of the human being with the animal world, the legends too represent again and again how the psychic constitution of man, and precisely the moral one, shows its reaction upon organic and inorganic nature. The interpretation of the Flood as a judgment of punishment is generally known, whence indeed the designation “Sündflut.” The increasing depravity of the human race, however, made itself felt even earlier in all manner of changes in the heavenly constellations, but above all on earth as well. To this belongs the legend of the alteration of the ear of wheat, which is said to have grown smaller on account of man’s sinfulness; a legend which, to be sure, is varied, in that the event is placed partly already in Paradise, and partly imposed only as a later punishment of God upon the pre-Noachian humanity, together with the Flood. In essence this remains the same, since the inner condition of man is made the decisive motive of the occurrence. A shortened Polish version runs: In the beginning man needed only to draw a single furrow, and God made the stalks so fruitful for him that they were full of grains from top to bottom. The first man contented himself with his furrow and reaped enough from it to live on. But the devil put it into his mind to draw more furrows, in order to have more. Man succumbed to the temptation and God’s punishment came. On the day of the harvest, when the proud man wanted to bring in his gains greedily, [p. 290] God appeared, took the stalks, rubbed them in his hands, and left the grains standing only at the top, the length of one ear. But to man he said: Because you were so insatiable, I have punished you; from now on you may work as much as you like, I shall give you only as much as pleases me!<sup>131</sup> A parallel to the foregoing may be found in the Greek tale of the Aloadae. They descend from Aloeus, the planter, and the fruit-bearing earth, and learn from her the threshing of the grain and the fruit. At first they were tiny, but then grew quickly and mightily in height, so that in a short time they became giants, for the sprouting cornfield nourished them; and they became the greatest and most beautiful men that had ever been seen. They fettered the god of war, but they also became so arrogant that they wanted to force their way to the gods on Olympus. They perished of it.<sup>132</sup>

<sup>131</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen. I. p. 212. Similar in Gorion, p. 189/90.

<sup>132</sup> Preller-Robert, Griechische Mythologie. 4th ed. p. 103/04.

The constant recurrence of the assertion of the wickedness of men at the time of the Flood is indeed striking. In a Jewish legend it finds perhaps its most drastic expression, as indeed the idea of the ever-wrathful and punishing God lies at the bottom most strongly in the Jewish nature elsewhere too: Everything had corrupted its way at the time of the Flood, man and beast. Each took to himself as wives those who were fair and whom he wished; they exchanged their wives with one another. The dog took to the she-wolf, the horse to the she-ass, the cock to the peahen, the ass to the serpent, the serpent to the bird. Even the earth fornicated at that time. They cast into it the seed of wheat and it brought forth darnel; this grass, which still grows today, stems precisely from the time before the Flood.<sup>133</sup>

<sup>133</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden. I. p. 193.

Thus through all the legends we see three fundamental ideas of the connection of the spiritual-psychic with the physical recur again and again: first, an oppositeness which makes itself known in the conflict between the creations of God and of the devil; then one between man and nature, which presents itself in the concept of estrangement; finally a counter-play of the moral-spiritual conditions of humanity in organic and inorganic nature.

The daemonic animal forms, essentially reptilian and serpentine and without a large brain, had thus formerly entered physical existence with the mammalian man [p. 291] through the cleavage and estrangement of his nature, and his being, still nature-sighted through and through, lived as his moral, as his physical being, perhaps little conscious and individualized, wholly in inner and outer contact with a surrounding world that gripped him psychically and physically and was in turn directly influenced by him in return, which now forced upon him a bodily and psychic struggle for existence that, waged with daemonic somnambulic forces, was played out essentially in the mythical as well. In so far as it spilled over into the bodily, this bodily was so permeated by the nature-sighted and the inwardly nature-like that it was able to withstand even the severest injuries and to regenerate without further ado.

This now was the source, that is, the physical and psychic foundation, for the later myths of the great magicians and sages and priest-kings, of the great invulnerable heroes, or those exposed to wounds without impairment. Children of God and children of Satan, as the legend has it, stood opposed to one another, and humanity, pressed after the Fall more and more into the developmental course of intellectual spirituality, whose consummation we are now perhaps approaching, not only stood outwardly and physically over against the animal world and lost more and more the inner connection with it, no longer understood the language of the animals, of nature, but was evidently also divided within itself into two psychic streams of life.

Two fundamentally different spiritual tendencies may thus have stood against one another at that time, in that highly daemonic age, expressing themselves in the black magicians and in the bright figures of priests and kings, the few prominent individualities that existed then. Not only in the saying just cited about the children of God and of Satan does an intimation of this recur; not only in the Old Testament report that the children of God saw that the daughters of men were fair, and that they desired them and wedded them; it is also told us in other legends constantly with the moral background. So in an Estonian legend, where it runs: The Old One created heroic angels for himself, in order to avail himself of their counsel. Thereafter he slept. Another creator — there are three of them there — to whom the [p. 292] gift of art had been given, made the firmament, the stars and the course of the stars. The third, for joy, seized his harp, struck up a song of jubilation and sprang, followed by his songbirds, onto the earth. Where his foot touched them, flowers and trees sprouted forth, on which the birds settled and accompanied his song. The Old One awoke at the noise and saw everything changed and said to his heroes: I created the world as a rough block, it is your business to beautify it. Soon I shall people the world with all kinds of creatures and shall then create men. You shall mingle with men, so that they may not so easily succumb to evil.<sup>134</sup>

<sup>134</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen. I. p. 68.

To every psychic or spiritual process in humanity and in the individual being there necessarily corresponds a physical one, be it in one’s own body or in the surrounding world, in nature. And conversely. Consequently every process, be it of an outer or inner kind, is only then really adjusted to our understanding when we have learned to grasp it as physical no less than as metaphysical. Both sides of the consideration are equally necessary and stand side by side with equal rights, complementing one another, and the one without the other remaining a half-measure. But both point to the absolute unity, ungraspable by discursive thought and without space or time, and are first united in it into living spiritual possession. Thus to the psychic-spiritual unfolding of the human race there corresponds in each case another corporeality and in each case another condition of physical and organic nature. This is perhaps even the inner meaning, to be grasped one day, of the geological epochs, as it probably is the inner meaning of the development of animals and plants. Thus to the transcendent Fall of man corresponds his being burdened with physical corporeality; to his nature-sightedness, the development of the frontal eye; to the outflowing of the gloomily daemonic, the unfolding of a reptilian world so endowed; to the becoming of the intellectual condition, the becoming of the Noachian mammalian body with its unfolding sphere of the large brain, and, as its counter-play, a mammalian world of the same kind; to the moral decline of primordial man, however, also in earth-historical terms the Flood, and to such connections probably also the catastrophic disturbances of the picture of the heavens. Is it not, as shown, also told in the legend, [p. 293] that formerly the fruits of the field were more luxuriant, until man became covetous and God made the ears small as a punishment, so that he must now toil bitterly. And in the Siegfried legend too the hero loses his bodily strength and can be felled by the gloomy Hagen only after he has committed betrayal against Brunhild. Furthermore it is said already in the Vedas, to which Schopenhauer points, whose doctrine indeed rests wholly on the same thought on a grand scale, that all changes of the world are to be ascribed to the moral works of animal beings.<sup>135</sup> In any case it is immaterial in this connection whether the hero, like Adam, has his invulnerable body with God in Paradise or, like Siegfried, among men; decisive in both cases is the cause of the loss: sin or betrayal, which in both cases also end with a “death.” Each falls victim to the daemonic: the Adamite to the serpent, Siegfried to the gloomy Hagen. But then comes the great difference in the soul of the legend, which I should like to regard as something significant: the Oriental tradition preserves for its hero, the Adamite, his bodily life; he dies, to be sure, in soul to Paradise, but he retains a wretched, degenerate earthly existence; helplessly he implores God and God helps him, after his wrath has blown over. But the Nordic Siegfried dies the full death. The daemonically hostile world is in the Oriental Adamite legend a morally reprobate one, never redeemed; in the Nordic figure of Hagen, however, a heroic one, by no means evil from the ground up, indeed surrounded by the fire of a man’s fidelity to king and queen, which ends later in glorious battle, albeit unhappily. It is so, when one sets these two legends side by side, as if the Oriental soul, despite all mythic depth, stands toward life differently, more bound to it, indeed more sensuous, than the human soul in the cold, more inhospitable North — even then, in the ancient myth-creating age of primordial man! That might perhaps help toward the explanation of many a myth and legend.

<sup>135</sup> Schopenhauer, loc. cit. vol. III. p. 196 note (see n. 113).

The difference of spiritual and psychic character, perhaps with it also the difference of the transcendent origin of those primordial human natures, emerges still more sharply in the following Jewish tradition: There were in [p. 294] those times giants on earth; for since the children of God went in to the daughters of men and begot children with them, heroes and mighty ones came of them. So too in the Old Testament. Another version gives more details: With uncovered nakedness the generations of Cain went about and were man and woman like cattle. They took off their clothes and cast them on the ground and went about naked in the marketplace, practiced all manner of fornication: the man lay openly in the streets with his mother or daughter and with his brother’s wife, and all the devising and striving of their hearts was directed only to this. And the angels who fell down from the height of their holiness saw the daughters of Cain, how they went with uncovered nakedness and painted their eyebrows like harlots, and were seduced by them and took wives from among them. But the angels are, after all, flames of fire — when they join with the daughters of men, do they not burn their bodies? Not so; for since they fell down from the height of their holiness, they became like men in strength and form and were clothed in earthly bodies. From them then came the giants, who were of mighty stature and who stretched out their hand to robbery and to plunder and to bloodshed. The giants begot children and multiplied like the creeping things; six at a time were born to them.<sup>136</sup>

<sup>136</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden. I. p. 191/92.

It was still, in that most ancient time, only the daemonic reptilian and serpentine nature that had been released into the physical world and there met man again, in that it now, in forms multiplying in many ways, became what is known to us in earth history as the reptilian world of the Late Palaeozoic and Mesozoic with its immense multiplicity, and is also sufficiently handed down in legends. Man too developed his form further physically. His hand was at first still fused flat and had a form somewhat like that of Chirotherium. His mind too must have attained, out of the nature-bound daemonic, more and more to a more intellectual existence, while perhaps another part of humanity, the children of Satan, persisted in the gloomily daemonic, intensified it and became ripe for destruction. With this the second great epoch of the becoming of man drew near, which again meant a moral, [p. 295] that is, psychic-spiritual, as well as a bodily change: the Noachite came, in whom the old post-Adamite man of the daemonic age was overcome, inwardly and outwardly. This act too of the drama of humanity was bound up with mighty changes of inner and outer nature, of animal as of human, and the inorganic and cosmic surroundings too must have been in turmoil at that time — movements of the heavenly bodies of unheard-of kind and, in their train, the Flood set in.

But we must ask further. What does it mean: that the human stock released more and more of the animal out of itself and, as we assumed, let the higher animal forms arise out of itself? What can one imagine, natural-historically, by such a creation of types which, once they had become independent, unfolded within their own field of evolution and formed genera and species through shorter or longer periods of time?

According to the doctrine of descent hitherto held, it is difficult, if not impossible, to understand that new types should have appeared as if out of nowhere, instead of detaching themselves formally, step by step, outwardly visible, from one another in series. The mechanistic, unbiological explanation of living forms held that by the accumulation of the smallest chance variants species after species must in the end have arisen. But the “species” in the deeper sense — identical with “genus” — is no aggregate, but a principle of life and formation determined from within, a potency, not a conceptual abstraction. If, however, such an entelechial formation of types of a new kind is to be assumed, there still remains the theoretical difficulty of imagining a way in which this transformation could be accomplished otherwise than through gradual series of forms from generation to generation. But there are certain, seemingly remoter facts from human life which perhaps, epistemologically, for the time being indicate a way of coming somewhat closer to the problem of the unmediated appearance of new types and of the splitting-off from the still more nature-bound human stock. We recognized that all existence, and so organic nature too, is a physical and a metaphysical in one, thus body and soul in one. We were further convinced that the clairvoyance [p. 296] and the action at a distance of present-day men or of known historical periods, including the nature-men and Diluvial men, must be only a feeble remnant of a former nature-boundness in earlier ages, when these forces and capacities of formation played so great a part that the legends arose out of them of temporal and spatial far-seeing and far-working, of magical happening, of transforming outer nature from within, and under circumstances that leave everything later far behind. We can thus think that the feeble present-day forces and phenomena of telekinesis and teleplasty — now belonging no longer to superstition but to the scientifically established and critically observed phenomena from the border region of bodily and psychic life — are likewise only meagre remnants of a formative power of the species-soul, once far mightier, more enduring, supra-individual, nature-somnambulic, subordinate to the individualized waking intellect. So there could and had to well forth from it — quite literally as a physiological emanation of the more comprehensive human stock, which still contained animal formative potencies within itself — by a not grossly physical way, formations in organic nature which became the psychophysical primal forms of the new animal circles of forms that then first entered outward visibility. There would thus be a process of evolution from within that could explain the appearance of basic animal forms as if out of nowhere, that is, as if by new creation, once these things shall have been investigated more deeply than has so far been achieved.

Is it fundamentally anything else when the legend tells that God at the creation brought the animals to man, that he might give them their “name”? In Hebrew as in Greek the onoma in any case does not signify so much the outer naming, but precisely the being only outwardly designated by the name, which lives behind the individual, from which it receives life and breath, in which it “essences” (“west”). If man, then, gave the animals their onoma — what can that be other than the knowledge that they have their essence from him?<sup>137</sup> Thus man was from of old the fundamental thought of the living creation, as the whole world comes from the Logos, which indeed is also not merely the [p. 297] “thought,” not even merely the Platonic idea, but the inner living being out of which the world presents itself, from which it has its existence and essence.

<sup>137</sup> H. Holzinger, Genesis. (Kurzer Handkommentar zum Alten Testament. Edited by K. Marti. Section I.) Freiburg, Leipzig, Tübingen 1898, p. 5; 29. “The ‘naming’ in the Genesis account is no idle addition, but the old expression for calling into being.” And: “The name is to express at the same time the significance of the being in question and its position in relation to man.”

In such a backward tracing, and meaningful extension, of the psychophysical and mediumistic forces now only weakly perceptible into the earth-historical primal condition of the human stock still filled with the animal, there lies not only the theoretical but also the natural-historically one day perhaps feasible linking of the occult phenomena and forces that have occupied humanity since ancient times with the findings of the doctrine of descent and the results of palaeontology and earth history. For here may also be the source from which still other influences, on inorganic nature, may perhaps one day be understood, that is, say, the transformations and catastrophes that, according to the legend, mirror the moral essence of man.

In this way we come to yet another thought, which one can understand, to be sure, only from the preceding expositions, and only if one has a sense for the non-objective, non-spatial, thus metaphysical conception of potencies whose physical realization or non-realization is no integral moment of their subsistence; that is, for an essentiality that is real, and remains real, in a sphere other than the merely grossly physical, even if it never enters physical appearance.

Much that is allegorical has already been attached to the ark of Noah; some of it was touched upon in passing in the course of the earlier expositions. It is a symbol of reality for an evolutionary condition of Noachian man. We conjectured that the human stock brought forth more and more the Apollonian, bright, undaemonic, more nature-free spiritual being, in that it released out of itself the animal element originally enclosed in its stock, whereby this presented itself physically in beings and forms of its own, branched off from it phylogenetically. Thus the Noachian human stock that outlasted the Flood, because it then still contained the greater part of the later, Tertiary mammalian types, could in a manner have rescued them across the catastrophe of the Flood, [p. 298] in that they lay still enclosed in its stock potentially, that is, physically unrealized, as archetypes of the genera, as ideas in the Platonic sense. If, therefore, the phylogenetic evolution that had to lead to the animal splittings-off was suspended during the time of the Flood, and only later, after the restoration of corresponding environmental conditions, took its natural course, then the animal species would in this sense have been shut up with Noah during the time of the Flood in one casing, in the ark; he would have had them with him and rescued them across, of each type the pair, as the tradition fittingly says. Only after the Flood had run its course and the “casing” was reopened would the evolution of the mammalian world out of the Noachian human stock that still contained it, and its spread over new living spaces, then have taken place.
