Since, as we assumed, man became more perfect to the same degree as the animal split off from his line of descent, there would correspond to this also the post-diluvian origin, at that time, of new human stocks from the Noachian primal father, who are characterized in the Old Testament as Shem, Ham and Japheth. That would be the physical-metaphysical interpretation of the ark of Noah.

If, then, the ark was the symbolized condition of the Noachian man still harboring within himself significant animal evolutions, one can derive from this thought still a further possibility by which other legends of fabulous animal beings, which tenaciously persist and are decried as empty fantasies, become explicable, without such animals ever having been present in physical nature or having been found fossil. I think here especially of the enigmatic unicorn. It is perhaps an animal form that coexisted potentially in the Noachian primal stock, but for some inner or outer reasons could not realize itself phylogenetically in physical existence. Hence the impossibility of finding a consistent representation and tradition of the unicorn’s form, quite in contrast to the firm traditional image of the dragon and lindworm type. One understands too, in such a metaphysical way of looking, that the physically unrealized form, yet latently present in the primal stock, [p. 299] of such an animal being floated visionarily before the still strongly clairvoyant Noachian man of the Tertiary, and indeed, since he no longer bore within himself the daemonism of the older human races, like a menacing dream, a form of terror, before which his being was afraid, which it negated in that it felt and beheld it, because his nature wished to thrust it away from itself. This is not to be understood individually, but generically-metaphysically, yet certainly not as unreal. It was the formative form-soul pressing into physical existence, the species-soul of that animal kind still enclosed in the human stock, which made itself known inwardly and immediately to the Noachian human being, still gifted with nature-sight, in that it stepped out of the unconscious, nature-like latent existence across the threshold of his consciousness, thus came inwardly before his sight; visionarily, as it were, as idea, not yet as a physically objectively realized being, but pressing into physical existence, which for some outer or inner reasons did not take shape for it. So there arose a nature-sighted legend, almost a myth of terror, which later men took over and which, like the dragon and lindworm legends, has been preserved down to the time of the cultural man, almost freed from the animal, of the post-glacial age.

So a version of the unicorn legend also becomes more comprehensible, which is handed down from the Polish: When Noah let a pair of all animals into the ark, he took in the unicorn too. But this butted other animals, and Noah threw it without hesitation into the water; in the end it had to drown. A Jewish legend, handed down in rabbinic myths, likewise tells that the unicorn indeed desired to be taken into the ark, but that Noah was not able to do this. He cut it up and tied the horn on outside the chest, but the nostrils inside. Yet the unicorn does not perish, but David finds it later in the desert.<sup>138</sup>

<sup>138</sup> Cf. n. 136.

If, therefore, we take Noah as that stage of human development out of which the late-Mesozoic and Tertiary mammal with its many special stocks branched off, up to the splitting-off of the ape-man, while at the same time man, soaring ever more purely to the Apollonian type, took shape in physical nature; and if originally still in [p. 300] the “ark” the many later mammalian genera were contained as well; and if the unicorn remained a mammalian stock not visibly realized for some reasons, which was beheld by Noachian man in a nature-sighted way in his own interior — then one will certainly never attach such connections, if one wishes to pursue them, to an individual being, but will always think of the generic, which precipitated out of the naturally somnambulic general consciousness of the human stock into a legend-formation accomplished quite unconsciously, and only afterward came to consciousness in the intellectually more mature and became poetry in them; comparable, say, to the unconscious-general origin of passions or instincts in animal species or tribes of peoples and their later becoming conscious.

The arrangement here attempted of otherwise uninterpretable legendary cores, which one cannot quite grasp natural-historically, is the utmost point to which our attempt from within can for the present reach, and forms at the same time an essential link in the chain of the remaining, metaphysically oriented considerations on the evolution of the higher animals, closely bound up with the human. These have fundamentally the same bodily endowment as man, and he is thereby distinguished, together with them, anatomically from the whole remaining lower animal and plant world, to which unobjectionable bodily transitions in the time of the former world, so far as we can gather from fossil records, have not been recognized. Man stands now over against these higher animals, whom one unites with him in the stock of the vertebrates, like a primitive, comprehensive primal form — with the exception of his brain development and his spiritual being. Not as if one did not find rudiments of both in the higher animal world; they are certainly and manifestly there. But they are not the goal of development and not there in such a way that the human could be derived from them; but quite the reverse. For while all vertebrates without exception, the fossil ones of the former world as well as the living ones now, are one-sidedly specialized and therefore formally derivable from the simple human form, man, conversely, precisely because of their specialization, is not derivable from them. The higher animal, the vertebrate, is thus in all its forms the [p. 301] descendant of man, if one believes at all in a phylogenetic development of the organic world. Only with the general fish form can this theory perhaps not be reconciled. It could in fact be the foundation both of the visibly physical stock of primordial man and of the amphibian stock of Palaeozoic origin.

Thus with this conception we partly return to an old doctrine: that man is the norm of the higher animal world and that this world is rooted in him, that he potentially contains everything it shows laid out asunder. The old doctrine lacked something that now accrues to it: the idea of a real, natural descent of the higher animal types from the all-embracing primordial human stock, of which present-day man appears as the remnant, but also as the unveiled essence. Formerly, in the old doctrine, this relation of the animal world to man was meant, if not allegorically, then probably formalistically. Now it is grounded in facts and considerations of comparative anatomy and palaeontology, and, despite its apparent oppositeness, it stands the less opposed to the general theory of descent in that the latter itself, as shown earlier (p. 95), basically sought, in all real or hypothetical primordial forms of the higher animals, only the primordial form of man himself, and thereby, partly without noticing it, took the higher animal world into the stock of man, and so in fact derived it from him.

[p. 302]

### Die Quelle der Weltentstehungs- und Weltuntergangssagen — The Source of the Legends of the Origin and End of the World

As for the myths of world-creation, the cosmogonies, one might fairly ask, after all that has been set forth, whether in them too, besides what is merely symbolic, there lies a historical, in particular a natural-historical, core? And one could make my whole remaining theory ridiculous with this question, if, on an affirmative answer, one retorted whether man was then already present at the creation of the universe and of the earth? If, to be sure, in answering the question affirmatively, I took the standpoint that man was present physically in time and in space at the creation of the world — or let us say, to grasp the problem more definitely, of the earth — that would doubtless be wholly absurd and ridiculous, and would rightly cast the whole remaining theory in a false light. Nevertheless I believe I must hold fast to the objectively historical core of many cosmogonies too, those going back to very ancient times, though of course not in the sense that man, whose physical appearance as a vertebrate can at most be ascribed only a late Palaeozoic age, was present in human form.

I call attention to a profound saying of Schelling’s<sup>139</sup> which runs: “Nature knows not through science, but through its essence, or in a magical way.”

<sup>139</sup> F. W. J. Schelling, Sämtliche Werke 1805—1810. I. Abt. vol. VII. Stuttgart and Augsburg 1860, p. 246/47.

Our intellectual-mechanistic late age cannot conceive that cognition and genuine knowledge might be attained in any way other than the sensory-empirical, that is, by any path other than an outward one. It has in general no longer any immediately co-experiencing understanding for that kind of connection which obtains between the comprehending and the comprehended, the beholder and the beheld. And yet even in us men of the late age there is still present all sorts of that capacity of knowing in an inner, “magical” way. It need only be pointed [p. 303] out how, even down to our own time, there still occurs here and there, though weakly clairvoyant in comparison with former times, the capacity of some people, of which not only literature but everyday life too has many things to tell. Not to speak of the certainty and unshakable assurance of knowing between two people who are bound together in the deepest part by a common idea, a common beholding. So the essence of that immediate path of knowledge is not wholly closed to us either; we perhaps merely have a certain fear of it because our intellect cannot follow it, and positively rejoice when we are permitted to unmask as swindle or superstition and imagination whatever occasionally appears in its guise. For we have become so scientific, and have a scientific worldview too, which indeed would be very serviceable for understanding the deeper connections of existence, if only existence were an exclusively conscious-empirical one.

That inner, or, as Schelling calls it, magical path of knowledge was, however, the surer form of knowledge of older times. The more nature-sighted or merely clairvoyant life and capacity there lies in a human being, whether consciously or unconsciously, the more this more immediate path of knowledge is open to him, which in the end can amount to a naturally somnambulant state, hence not to an artificially induced and willed one. Schopenhauer once calls the bees, as already mentioned, natural somnambulists, in order thereby to demonstrate the essence of their so eminently instinctive manner of acting and collaborating, still unified even across individual distance; they behold something common, and are therefore a unity amid all individual multiplicity, so that each at every moment “knows” what it has to do and to leave undone in the service of the whole. Surely it can happen so with man too.

This inner connection need not exist only between persons, it can also exist between persons and objects; one calls it psychoscopy<sup>140</sup>. Known are the reports of persons who, from an object unknown to them, say a garment, can occasionally give information about the one who wore it. It seems indeed that of late, gradually, [p. 304] we are coming closer again to such highly significant problems through critically supervised and thoroughly worked-out experiments; and it is at any rate quite possible that in the not too distant future a new, thoroughly empirical method will open up to us here for the investigation of the former world and of the history of humanity as well. Some years ago, somewhere, without being able to find the source again, I read a description of an Ice Age landscape with a herd of mammoths, which is said to date from a time when scientifically nothing yet was known of an Ice Age, and which, as I remember, went back to a Belgian woman to whom, in the somnambulant state, a fossil mammoth tooth had been given because one wanted to know what sort of thing it was. Even if I do not wish to argue about the degree of credibility of the story, it nevertheless shows, in its outline, vividly, in view of more recent experiences, that kind of nature-sighted knowing whose faint traces still live in our cultural sphere.

<sup>140</sup> R. Tischner, loc. cit. p. 37 (see n. 112).

Such a knowing can therefore take place not only from person to person, or from person to object, but also between person and object with a wide temporal perspective into the past. But what we have before us, at most, in such relatively simple and harmless clairvoyances of today or of the centuries just past, and what for the contemporary who is still scientifically or unscientifically enlightened is already indigestible superstition, that must have been developed in far higher degree, indeed with a certainty reaching into unimaginably distant times and hidden states, in those epochs, in those men who still lived in so close an inner bond with the essence of nature that, owing to this sameness of essence, an immediate, a nature-sighted beholding could also take place of things which perhaps already lay infinitely far back, and which remain wholly and entirely inaccessible to an empirical-intellectual everyday understanding, or at most can be inferred by it only mediately, when remains or effects of them are still at hand. That is just how it goes with us in the science of the ages of the former world with their earth-historical and organically living transformations. An immediate, nature-sighted looking-into is naturally all but wholly lacking to us; only to an extremely small [p. 305] degree does our intellect still possess so much intuition that, from empirical, logically conscious inference from the physical objects, say from strata, rocks and fossils, it can advance to a faintly clairvoyant beholding kept within quite narrow limits. The ancients could do this often more than our age, wholly sterile in this respect; and for that reason such ancient doctrines as that of Democritus on the atoms, of Aristotle on the entelechies, of Plato on the ideas, even without the outward empirical foundations such as we may boast of having, have remained so astonishingly durable and, in their core and essence, genuine, serviceable and fruitful through all the centuries, while our scientific garment, despite the sufficiently heaped-up “facts,” goes out of date outside and inside every few decades.

So the insights of men in the nature-sighted age of the world, in particular probably those of the then most conscious, that is, spiritually most developed men, were of a depth inscrutable to our intellectual mind, and of an indestructible genuineness and truthfulness, of which our academic wisdom hardly understands anything any longer. In good and in evil, in the luminous as in the gloomy, in the moral as in the physical, this nature-sighted beholding and knowing moved, and from it welled all the fundamental doctrines, of a religious or cosmogonic nature, to be found with so much agreement of essence in the very ancient legends and traditions. Evidently it was then self-evident, and could not possibly be otherwise, that the individuals who disposed most consciously of the unconditionally sure natural seeing and knowing, and therefore also, practically, were equipped with enormous power, were the leaders of peoples and states as kings, priests and judges in one, to whom the others, the mass of the less strongly gifted, submitted; not, as today, by virtue of bayonets or money, but from an inner certainty, in essence likewise determined by nature-clarity, and thereby from recognition of their power and wisdom.

What was it, then, in our own time just past, that put into the hands of a man like Bismarck the enormous power, which was created almost out of nothing, if not precisely the intuitive, not to say natural certainty with which he [p. 306] instinctively saw through every situation in its real conditions, connections and possibilities of solution. Bismarck’s being has something thoroughly daemonic about it, and just as instinctively the German people, sensing and feeling this, have always compared him to the champions of ancient circles of legend. A writer has recently pointed out that his work is the only one to which the German people at once gave, in monuments, the figure of Roland — also an ancient champion-image, like David, say, in the Jewish folk-soul; Dietrich of Bern too plays this role — and straightway only a symbolic, impersonal one, in that it erected to him not monuments in the paperweight style then customary, but Bismarck towers pointing out over the land from forest and mountain heights. In this there lies manifestly a living expression of the feeling for the suprapersonal, archetypal quality of his nature and his work — he of whom an English statesman is said to have said, we must all let him have his way, for he sees through everything we undertake. That is genuine far-spelling, even if Europeanly intellectualized. Yet perhaps no German genius was poorer in eternal ideas than Bismarck; they lay remote from him, for them he had no inner organ. He stood, to be sure, in the service of ideas which realized themselves through him; but he hardly knew it, and without being filled or inspired by them in the ordinary sense of the word. His greatness was, apart from moral strength of will and fearlessness, his real, immediately grasping knowledge. That is why the being of the philosopher or the professor was so insufferable to him, and that is why his “Reminiscences,” too, as retrospective thoughts directed no longer to what is becoming but to what has historically hardened, are of an emptiness of ideas striking for German literature, whatever historical value they may have. The German folk-soul was no longer able to be kindled and inwardly deepened by them. Only where he points to the future does the uncannily sure knowing emerge again: he saw the fruits we would reap; but it is not ideas that guide him in this.

This excursus on the daemon Bismarck is meant only faintly, by way of comparison, to give a notion of wherein lay the knowledge of truth, and therefore the power, of those very ancient, still fully nature-sighted magi and sages, who yet exerted an influence extending over hundreds of thousands [p. 307] of years, and perhaps will gain it once again when the intellectualized mechanics of our days has exhausted itself and done its duty. This connection reminds me of a saying of Friedrich Ratzel’s in his “Völkerkunde,” which I read as a young man and which has not let go of me since, and which in part strengthened me in the thoughts presented here: the agreement of the fundamental doctrines of all cosmogonies and religions of the tribes of peoples, and the duration and tenacity with which they were held fast, positively force upon one the thought that great teachers and sages guided and educated the young humanity and planted these doctrines in its heart.

It has become sufficiently clear in what precedes how truly full of life and fruitful for further knowledge such a thought must be, once one has become clear about the possibility of a wholly different, primordial-worldly constitution of the human being, in contrast to the late-age, predominantly intellectual one. So we now return, fully prepared, to the, as we said, ridiculous question whether the very ancient cosmogonies too possess reality-value, that is, objective, natural-historical truth, especially since man himself certainly “was not there” at the origin of the earth. Where nature-sighted beholding is fully developed, nature recognizes itself in man as its organ and image. There man descends into the depths to the Mothers, where he finds again within himself the archetypes of formation, where, detached from the spatial-temporal of the intellect, they remain visible to his beholding unchanged and have the same truth as on the first day. I therefore believe that the chief cosmogonic legends too, so far as they agree in essence, harbor natural-historical truth, and to that extent that ancient Chinese tradition is not quite so foolish as a modern cosmogonic investigator thinks<sup>141</sup>, when they say that one of their own was there at the creation of the world; or when we hear in Greek mythology that the ancestors sat in council with the gods.

<sup>141</sup> M. B. Weinstein, Entstehung der Welt und Erde nach Sage und Wissenschaft. 2nd ed. Leipzig and Berlin 1913 (no. 223 “Aus Natur und Geisteswelt”).

In such a way it may well be that the most ancient nature-magical primordial states of humanity, say the individually unconscious, hence through and through nature-somnambulant struggle against [p. 308] the nature of the dragon- and serpent-brood, were carried over in retrospective clairvoyance into the already more developed intellectual consciousness and were now worked up into the earliest comprehensible cores of the oldest myths and legends and hero-figures, which have since remained the foundation of the whole world of legend of all later histories of humanity, even though from epoch to epoch, from race to race they were worked over again and again differently, linked to other lived experience of one’s own, and finally misunderstood and distorted to the point of flattening. Thus natural, primordial-time connections and events had been ascertained, in that the soul, penetrating backward into great temporal distance, disclosed the past again out of the superindividual memory of the world or of the species — in essence no differently from what the still faintly clairvoyant capacity of our days can accomplish upon objects.

Such an inner knowing of natural, though bygone, connections — exercised in ancient time and by nature-sighted human beings also with mightier soul-force and on a greater spatial and temporal scale — has likewise been the source of genuine astrology, which, no less degenerate and weakened, confronts us from Babylonian days merely like a flat sorcery. Does not even Ptolemy say in his work on astrology: what can be read out of the nature of things springs from the contemplation of related formations and positions. This inner community, or better said: this homology of things, which permits one to infer, from the existence and essentiality and behavior of the one, the very same in the other, or to let oneself be clairvoyantly guided by it, for whom that is possible: that is the key to all experience that goes beyond immediate, grossly sensory empiricism, yet for that reason is truly no less firmly grounded. Could not even a mind like Fechner take this path, in order to arrive at insights which of course do not lie about like the coarse paving-stones on the open highway of naively realistic science.

So it can and surely will be that very ancient traditions were not always drawn from the time of their events, but were gained anew afterward, on that path of inner beholding, of absorption, by later [p. 309] minds, and then of course shaped according to their understanding. The legend of the Flood, then, need not have been handed down either in writing or orally everywhere it later appears, and yet could have been recorded for the first time, objectively correctly, on a nature-sighted path by later seers, just like the origin of the earth itself and of the human race. Such legends and insights, with a nature-sighted ascertainment of the physical-metaphysical body of fact, will therefore be able to deliver natural-historical truth, explored with an empiricism other than ours, although their recorders and investigators certainly “were not there” at the events; just as little, incidentally, as we natural scientists, who likewise investigate the conditions of the former world from indicia and with a strong empathy that certainly cannot dispense with “fantasy.” When today we tell the scientifically undeveloped common man of our researches into the history of the former world, they certainly strike him as no less mythical than the bodies of knowledge won clairvoyantly or nature-sightedly by the most ancient seers strike us who are scientifically equipped.

In this respect an experience of a geologist of the former Boer states in South Africa, esteemed in the professional world, is instructive, which is personally vouched for to me, and which may be told here because it throws a most apt light on the state of soul from which we, men of modern times attuned solely to outward activity of the understanding, look down so haughtily on the old myths, which likewise know what is real. That geologist once met, on his solitary expeditions in the mountains of Natal, two Boers who were likewise crossing the country with their mules. They made camp together, and presently he was asked what he was doing so alone in that land, where there was after all nothing for him to look for; he seemed to them at the least peculiar, if not otherwise suspicious. At the meal prepared and eaten in common he began to tell them what he was doing, how he investigated; for two long hours he told them how the mountains there had come to be, how it had looked there in earlier, former-world times, how the ore had got into the mountains, and all sorts of things more. The peasants listened silently and attentively. After he had at last [p. 310] let his account slowly die away and they were still pensive, the one Boer nudged the other: “You — the gentleman talks as if he had been there when the Lord God created the world!” Are we scientists not just such Boers when we let ourselves be told something of the myths?

I therefore believe that the knowledge acquired on the nature-sighted path and with means other than our understanding, the knowledge of seers of primordial times, transmits truth to us, although their recorders were certainly not there in person at the events. They descended into the realm of the dead and learned there what they sought; as it says in the ancient Egyptian teaching<sup>142</sup> it is said that they “have spent the day among the great”; they “performed the opening of the face .. and the circle of darkness was opened.” That is the meaning of Odysseus' visit in the underworld to the seer Calchas; that the meaning of Gilgamesh’s journey to his ancestor Utnapishtim, who tells him the story from the days of the Flood; that too the meaning of Faust’s descent to the Mothers. From this inner beholding, knowledge of what lies back in time may have been gained by way of a self-mirroring of the being of nature in man, where it just attains consciousness of itself. Besides, if nature is of one essence in itself, which is indeed also the philosophical conviction of our science, and if human nature is rooted in universal organic nature, indeed potentially and essentially contains it within itself, then the human spirit can well, through contemplation turned inward, have recognized, nature-sightedly or clairvoyantly, also the origin, the development of organic nature on the earth-star, and then have arrived at definite results, by no means improbable even for our scientific calculation, such as that “from the water alone, at the beginning of creation, the praise of the Lord resounded,” that at first there was a Panthalassa on the earth-body, and that only later the continents emerged, which were settled from the water by animal and plant<sup>143</sup>. The geologist and palaeontologist has nothing essential to object to in this, even if, understandably, within the frame of his science he keeps aloof from such postulates, which for him, to be sure, are not borne by nature-sightedness and are therefore purely speculative, but which a nature-sighted sense may long since have beheld.

<sup>142</sup> Urkunden zur Religion des alten Ägypten. [Edited by Günth. Roeder. Jena 1915, p. 273.

<sup>143</sup> Gorion, Sagen der Juden. I. p. 199-200. Likewise: Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 13.

[p. 311] Also in the Aztec Maya manuscript already mentioned several times I suspect the tradition of myths which relate to conditions and events of the history of the earth and of humanity, without it being asserted thereby that the recording of the manuscript itself was in any way written by seers in the sense just characterized. Yet here a doubtless very ancient tradition of this kind seems to be recorded, which even calls to mind pictorially many things that the Gilgamesh epic too contains, to which I ascribe originally the same nature-sighted character. In particular the figure reproduced in Fig. 13a (p. 87), which belongs in a series with the two others in the original as well, makes precisely the impression that it was meant to symbolize the inner beholding of daemonic-natural-historical things. The human being of divinely luminous nature experiences, nature-sightedly, visionarily, its ancient serpent-nature, which indeed once led to the expulsion from Paradise. Or else they are intimations of the stages of corporeal human becoming, which is revealed to the seer: out of the primordial state still bound to the water, symbolized by the fish, there became later that of the at first amphibious land-animal, which thus emblematically swallows the fish. All such knowledge, beheld only nature-sightedly, is contained in the essence of man, wherefore it is represented as issuing from the head and not as an image-effect opposite him, outside his body. The journey of the perfected one with the daemonic man further makes the impression that the journey of Gilgamesh with the ferryman of Utnapishtim into the realm of the dead were described there, where he obtains his knowledge of the Flood and the forefathers. He learns there that the old daemonic race perished with the Flood; for the picture-field clearly shows the raining from heaven; and later comes the scene of drowning, of which there was already mention in the chapter on the dating of the Flood (p. 178).

Quite special interest is offered, on the presupposition of nature-sighted ancient ascertainment of truth, also by the Nordic-Germanic legend of the origin of the world. In the beginning of the world there was neither land nor sea, neither earth nor heaven; only a yawning chasm. The essenceless primal matter condensed in the north and south into dark mist, into water and ice and fire. Many years before [p. 312] the creation of the earth Niflheim had arisen; in the midst of it lies the kettle-shaped, roaring spring Hwergelmir; from it many rivers pour forth. The region of the world Muspelheim came to be, bright and hot. Now these and the cold region of mist stood facing each other — fire and water as the primal elements of the world-structure. In the twelve rivers, which carry cold moist layers of air with them, the water flows forth from the spring, the primal source, and condenses into ice. These rivers had gone far from their origin, so that the poisonous liquid contained in them, the bitter-cold flood, froze to ice like the slag in the forge. This ice stood still and advanced no further. Rime settled upon it, the wetness of the poison-flood also froze to rime, and layer upon layer pushed into the yawning abyss. The north of the abyss filled with thick, heavy masses of ice and rime, which produced drizzling rains and winds. The southern part became milder through the sparks which flew in from Muspelheim; for there it was hot and bright. The hot air reached the rime, which began to drip, and a being arose, shaped like a human being — not the human being. It was the mightily roaring giant Ymir, the forefather of the rime-giants. Then the giants arose, who dwell in the inhospitable regions<sup>144</sup>.

<sup>144</sup> Golther, Germanische Mythologie, p. 511-14.

This doctrine is like a complete anticipation of the glacial-cosmogonic doctrine of the origin of the world, as it stands in detail in the section on the cosmological explanation of the Flood. We marvel at the likeness penetrating to the details; so, for example, the standing still of the congealed ring of ice, which with the glacial cosmogony we regard as the nebular Milky Way, far outrun and penetrated into the yawning world-space. So it is not, “the total picture of the primordial world filled with water, mist and ice, whose night receives from the south gradually light and milder air, is clearly enough taken from Nordic nature,” as Golther thinks; it is rather the theory of world-formation itself, as we with new mature scientific means of thought cannot create it better and more clearly. Here great truths must lie open to the day — no matter whether it is early legend or late science — which for that very reason were the same in all epochs of humanity, even if represented with [p. 313] another beholding and therefore with other images. And so here too the sun has seen nothing new.

A cosmogonic, earth-historical motif that confronts us with surprising frequency in many myths concerns the growth of the body of the earth. The older scientific doctrine, taken over from Kant, believes, to be sure, that after its attainment of independence as a planetary mass, and after absorbing the matter surrounding it, the earth has since circled the sun as a rounded stellar individual and, initially molten, gradually surrounded itself with a crust of rock as it cooled. More recently a theory has been discussed according to which the bodies of our solar system were not flung off as a whole from a primal solar body, but were built up out of planetary bodies rushing together. If we consider, moreover, the possibilities, opened up by the glacial cosmogony already set forth, of a still later accretion that the earth underwent, then an ancient Iranian legend gains interest, about a divine ruler under whose happy reign the population of the earth grew so much that it had to widen its circumference: first by a third, then once more by two thirds, and finally once more by a third.

Perhaps such a nature-sighted, astronomical knowledge of the capture and clustering of masses around given centers of force underlies the many legends of the creation of the earth, which in essence offer the same thing again and again, and which all agree in this, that at first there was no “land” on which human beings and animals could have dwelt. Then God went about on the wide world-ocean, and usually with him the devil, and had a handful of earth brought up from the bottom. For even if it were only a few grains, that would still suffice to let a whole earth eventually come of it. After many vain attempts it succeeds, barely, and now “the land” begins to grow and to enlarge itself, so that it becomes habitable for human and animal. One need only translate the legend into the planetesimal theory of the American Chamberlin, which of late has been gaining greater probability, according to which the body of the earth streamed together from cosmic bodies, in order to have a similar modern doctrine.<sup>145</sup>

<sup>145</sup> Th. C. Chamberlin and R. D. Salisbury. Geology. Vol. II. Earth History. New York 1906, p. 1—81.

[p. 314] Among the Algonquins, an Indian tribe in southern eastern Canada, this legend runs roughly as follows: All was water before the earth was created. On this great expanse of water floated a great wooden raft; on it were all the different kinds of animals that exist on the earth. The great Hare, their leader, looked for a firm place to land, but did not find one. He had the Beaver dive in order to fetch up some earth from the bottom of the water; in vain. The Otter too came up again in vain. Finally the Muskrat offered herself. But she stayed down a very long time, until she came up as if dead and had to be drawn onto the raft. Disappointed, the animals at last found a little grain of sand in her fourth paw, between the claws. The great Hare, who had resolved to create an immensely large earth, took the grain of sand and let it fall on the raft. Then it grew larger. Of this he took a part again and sowed it out. Thereby the earth grew more and more. When it was as large as a mountain, he wanted to walk around it. But the more he walked, the more the earth grew. When it already seemed quite large to him, he commanded the Fox to inspect his work and to enlarge it still further. But the Fox recognized that it was roomy enough and reported to the Hare that the earth could hold and sustain all the animals. Then the great Hare himself went once more around the earth and continued to enlarge it. That is why the savages say, when they hear it rumbling in the mountains, that the great Hare is enlarging the world.<sup>146</sup>

<sup>146</sup> Dähnhardt, Natursagen I. p. 82.

How clearly it is told here, if one ventures the extended astronomical interpretation, that the first planetesimal clustering of the earth and its later enlargement from outside, through the accretion of bodies circling around it, came about. The ceaselessly circling Hare could have been a planetoid sister body which the primal earth drew to itself and which gradually united with the earth amid quaking effects, though on the whole without catastrophe — precisely as the planetesimal theory or, in part, the Hörbiger doctrine of the origin of the world is able to present it to us. This is also shown in an extension of the same legend, according to which the Fox ran around the earth for a time and enlarged it, but grew tired, whereupon another, the Michapu, took the work from him. It is especially valuable [p. 315] for the interpretation that the Algonquins append to their legend the conclusion that the great Hare is enlarging the world whenever it rumbles in the mountains; for great earthquakes and volcanic paroxysms must have been preparing themselves and coming to a head if such circlings and increases in the mass of the earth’s body were taking place.

Of late there is also, as was already mentioned in passing, a theory recognized in principle by many researchers, though still contested by others, according to which continental surfaces, resting on the deeper plastic-magmatic zone of the earth, can slowly shift over it. Thus Hither India is supposed to have moved up against Asia. If such movements of individual continental portions of the crust, pushing together, perhaps in connection with shiftings of the poles, took place within an earth-historical span of time not exceeding the lifetime of a primal people, then the following legend about the diminution, instead of the enlargement, of the earth could arise, as it is handed down from Bulgaria, and which runs: When the earth had been created, it did not fit under the vault of heaven. Where was one to put so large a disk? Then the Hedgehog came and asked what was the matter. One would have to press the disk together a little, then it would go all right. Then God pressed the disk together, and now it let itself be moved quite easily under the sky. But in the pressing together folds arose here and there; those are the present mountains and valleys.<sup>147</sup> So far the legend. It is natural-historically especially significant that the formation of the Alpine mountain ranges is traced with certainty to a folded thrusting together of portions of the earth’s crust, in which horizontal displacements of continental extent must have taken place, because by smoothing the folds of the mountains out again one can show by calculation the former breadth of the surface of the earth occupied by their material to have been many hundreds of kilometers.

<sup>147</sup> Dähnhardt, ibid., p. 128.

That clairvoyance with respect to very early human or even pre-human conditions of the world and the earth, such as we assume here for the source of cosmogonies, may now equally hold also for future events and developments. Thus it would be conceivable in the same sense that myths of the end of the world could come into being, such as the Norse Twilight of the Gods [p. 316] or the oriental Apocalypse of John offer us, which in the essence of their content are probably identical and are likewise likely to be grounded in a clairvoyant disposition of mind. Sheerly astronomical knowledge and calculation certainly cannot underlie this; it is all rooted in transcendent nature-sightedness. I recall here again the word of Schelling given above.

It is an immense amount that can be brought out of the myths in the course of time, especially also out of our Nordic-Germanic ones, once research has first turned its attention to it and found thorough scientific methods. The methods are certainly not at my disposal; here only a foreboding is at work, and only sketchy samples could be given, attempts at interpretation made, and the general direction indicated. The Völuspa and the Ragnarök are complexes of knowledge of ancient seers, containing an immense amount that awaits being opened up in the sense pursued here.<sup>148</sup> Only one point of principle shall still be pointed out.

<sup>148</sup> Ax. Olrik, Ragnarök. Die Sagen vom Weltuntergang. Translated by W. Ranisch. Berlin and Leipzig 1922.

Once one acknowledges that mental dispositions such as we sought to characterize a moment ago are at work in the origin of mythical traditions out of the past or in anticipations of the future, then it can no longer be felt as disturbing if, say, the legends of the end of the world frequently contradict one another in content. An end of the world by fire, as the Norse Twilight of the Gods gives it, or one by ice, as appears alongside it in the Fimbulwinter of Norse mythology and brings about the annihilation of the human race, seem indeed in and of themselves to stand in mutually exclusive opposition. However, it must be considered that, just like the Flood event, any other earth catastrophe of cosmic or telluric origin must, according to the cause and manner of its course, produce quite different phenomena in the different zones. Thus, for example, the capture of a cosmic body will bring with it in the tropics, apart from floods of the sea, also an especially violent fall of meteorites and with it possibly devastating effects of heat, perhaps also directly a scorching and burning like the chariot of Phaëthon. In other places, where the earth’s crust was already under strong tension [p. 317] beforehand, mighty earthquakes will set in, and in mountain regions landslides and rockfalls of unheard-of magnitude; at the edge of the oceans lands will sink into the sea; in unstable regions of the earth’s crust, traversed by fissures, great volcanic paroxysms occur, “the bound beast of prey” or “the subterranean ox” emerge; for a long time the sun is darkened by the fine and coarser ashes and the violent thunderstorms with which the sky is full; eclipses of the sun are produced incessantly, in rapid succession, for the most various regions by the cosmic bodies and fragments whizzing around the earth; “tailed stars, evil stars, glaring and dark stars” threaten the earth and torment all life upon it; in the polar regions, with us therefore in the north, but in the southern hemisphere in the south, the phenomenon of lasting snowy precipitation, perhaps of an ice cover, that is, of the Fimbulwinter, will appear through the suction of air toward the equator — and so all the mythical seers will prove right despite the apparently most contradictory results of their foresight, which must be different according to the region of the world on which they directed their nature-sighted gaze, or according to the moment they had in view. Let one read a work such as the “Ragnarök” of Axel Olrik, and one will find all these fundamentally different and yet perhaps identically meaning legends together, and be able in part to resolve their contradictions.

Just as the world, humanity as a whole, has its apocalypse, its Twilight of the Gods, its prophesied downfall, so it would be possible that for individual tribes or races, too, such future-seeing prophecies exist, obtained in very ancient times in a nature-sighted manner. For Germanic folk I should like to find such a soul-mirroring, opening up the future, in the core of the Nibelungen legend, where the bright German Siegfried-being is murdered by Hagen, the daemonic spirit, loyal to his liege though it be, of the naturelike sense of reality, which leads to the downfall of the whole Nibelung people.

[p. 318]

### Seelenwanderung, Tod und Erlösung — Transmigration of Souls, Death, and Redemption

The mysterious relation of the human soul to the animal kingdom, as the penultimate section sought to make recognizable, is sufficiently expressed in the old, widespread idea, originally shared probably by all peoples, of a transmigration of souls of the deceased. Since cultivated peoples versed in the sciences, such as the Egyptians and the wisdom-filled Indians, always preserved this doctrine, acted and lived by it, and lost it only with the rise of the naively realistic and rationalistic Greek and Jewish outlook, one will do well here too not to adopt, toward the question, the bloodless standpoint of the modern enlightened man as unassailable, but to attempt a more reverent solution, one that grasps the problem in its depth. This too will be a twofold one, a physical-metaphysical one.

I recall Fechner. No scholar of nature knew as he did how to show what one might call the essence of spiritual and psychic communion. Immerse yourself, he says, for instance, in the work of a philosopher, forget yourself in it and look at the things around you and at the eternal ideas with his sense. Thus you will share and live in his spirit, in communion with him. Likewise you also enter into living communion with the great dead, when you grasp their thoughts livingly, live their teaching out of the heart. Or you love your child deeply and tenderly. And all with which you surround it, all your care and goodness and kindly strictness, does not efface the terrible feeling that you are separate, are two beings, and are far away from the power to hold it, protect it, and make it proof against all misery. Then the child dies. And now all at once you learn how intimately you have it, how it lives in you and you in it, in unhindered, ever full-sounding interaction, unseparated and with the feeling that its being is secured in you and made proof against everything. And in spite of the immense pain, the transfigured peace lives in you.

[p. 319] This is the living communion of spirits. Whoever does not experience it knows nothing of it, for all words. To whom these words are only words, empty mental images, the following will be unable to yield any sense. It is a different knowing from that which is directed outward and nourished from outside. In every profound science one must experience, otherwise only formulas, words, definitions remain.

Such a spiritual or psychic communion is entered into also by him who thinks thoughts, gives himself up to feelings, and carries on a being with the lower nature that he finds daemonically within himself. It may be apelike sensuality, catlike cruelty, or snakelike falseness, to name extremes for the sake of clarity, that he realizes. This being has a share in the universal being of nature that expresses itself in those surgings, and so also created them and gave them the same spirits of life. Take these expressions as symbolically as you like — everything symbolic can in the last resort correspond only to something essential, hence again real and therefore effective. And what is indicated is real and effective enough. But if this real thing lives in apes, cats, and snakes, procures reality for itself in them, why should it not, where it lives and breathes in a human individual and creates its reality in him, radiate back from there, return, and find itself again in animal nature, feel itself one with those other forms of existence, unconsciously to the individual, to be sure, and there, especially after bodily death, continue to be active not living, that is, be reincarnated? To whom these are empty words, he should — I repeat it — read nothing of this. He may also call it nonsense, but only not symbol; for symbols signify something real, and to him it would indeed not be real.

On the same real sense and content of existence rests also the possession of lower, daemonically disposed natures. And on this too their healing by the sun god, the god of light, the Savior. The light-spiritual in man overcomes this lower and fettering naturalness as well, of which everyone probably still senses one thing or another in himself, and redeems him from the lowly natural.

If now, as we had reason to assume, in earlier [p. 320] ages human nature was in general less permeated by the bright spirit and was still more strongly and more immediately devoted to the daemonic-natural and to little individualization, then the channels too that joined the soul’s stirrings and surgings and formations with the regions of the animal- and plant-like creating life were probably not yet so narrowed or even closed, but still easily passable roads. Thus being flowed to and fro — human soul to animal soul and animal soul to human soul; transmigration of souls in death and possession in life, in the good sense as in the bad.

Even he among us to whom it does not appeal to think in such a world of ideas of something real and to see something real will yet be able to agree with us that the origin of individuals in animal and man has a producing, livingly effective cause, a multitude of antecedent, tangibly real conditions. Now if these conditions, these causes, create outwardly corresponding bodily beings, as the individuals of a species with all their variability simply are, then the source of each single one must have been a very similar or corresponding one; hence also the cause that leads to the same psychic and spiritual habitus, to beings of the same kind of soul and spirit. The same constitution of soul and the same spirit, even if bound to different bodies and perhaps the expression of what lives and weaves in bodily nature, signifies and is nothing other than: being of the same essence. As this essence is One in different bodies, so too it returns as the Same in new bodies, quite real and actual; and so the soul even of the human individual, which came to consciousness in him himself, weaves as essence also in other bodies, new or already existing. If human nature, spiritually undeveloped, spiritually still unpenetrated, not yet sharply and firmly personally stamped, once contained “essentially” more of animal nature, then, with the formerly less firm boundaries between the two, it passed more easily from the one into the other — and hence the ancient tradition, hallowed in legends and religious ideas, of the transmigration of souls, which was once real, only too real.

Was? No longer is? There can be no doubt that the [p. 321] unfolding of the bright spirit that fixes the boundary between the animal and the human kingdom lies in evolution and thereby in the destiny of the human essence. Even if we do not look into the distances of earth-historical pre-humanity opened up here before, but wish to think only of the ideal ascent from the being of the Stone Age to the sage of classical antiquity, the increasing dominion of the spiritual-luminous over the slavish-daemonic becomes clear to us. In the same measure as the former grew and the latter declined, there vanished in human beings nature-sightedness, nature-daemonism, that is, sorcery, natural healing, far-seeing, and other things characteristic of that state. Might not the transmigration of souls too have died out thereby, and the human essence have been anchored more firmly within the boundary-posts of its humanity? It would be once more the way of Engidu from the mountain rocks and from communion with nature down into the city of Gilgamesh, the dismissal of the animal element from the human stock.

When the spirit of man and of the generations changes, world and reality have become different too. Not only subjectively, but also objectively, naturally. For he himself is reality, and his becoming-other lies in the becoming-other of nature and vice versa. So it comes about that truths, cognitions, sciences, kinds of faith can for a long time see, know, create something real, and later become, and have become, only empty form and thereby superstition. If the enlightened European of the soulless last century has stepped over all the ancient traditions of such truths, cognitions, and kinds of faith, because they had become for him objectless, lifeless, unreal, and ineffective, he was quite right insofar as they are superstition for him, because they are no longer alive for him and his own nature, like his world, has become another, to which other visions are available and in which other truths hold, other cognitions flow, another faith lives. But he is wrong if he rejects the reality and efficacy of those ancient things altogether. The times of the past are for us a book with seven seals.

From this reflection we can, I believe, incidentally also come closer to an answer to the question of the sense and the [p. 322] justification of everyday superstition. We indeed often experience that even intellectually developed people — men no less than women — are caught in superstitious ideas, and even direct their conduct in various ways accordingly. Perhaps one laughs at oneself or is annoyed with oneself about it — no reflective person is wholly free of it, if he confesses it honestly to himself. Least troubled by it are probably those who are not too much shackled to their personal existence, but live in pure great ideas and walk selflessly over the earth. We have all come to know certain facts and experiences, our own or handed down from our families or other circles, that can bring us to an “There is something in it.” If a gypsy woman prophesies death from the lines of the hand or from the cards, then — that is indeed a truism — superstitious fear may suffice to bring it about on the appointed day. And if it does not come, then it simply shows that he to whom it was prophesied made no scruples about it, that it was no psychic shock that gripped the marrow of life, but that with bright, untormented spirit and healthy nature he remained indifferent or, consciously and strong-willed, swung himself over it. Therein lies, even in our days, which have remained so little naturally-daemonic, the key to the solution: superstition is a daemonically grounded and therefore effective power for souls that surrender themselves to it as individuals or as masses; it loses its binding force when a healthy bright spirit confronts it. Superstition is true and effective or ineffective and untrue, according to the human being who has to do with it.<sup>148a</sup> This holds equally for the disputed experiencing of miracles; it has a subjective and an objective side. According to the soul of the human being, according to the context in which it perceives the outer event, what it makes of it from itself, the essence of things and events takes shape for it. Insofar everyone is the smith of his own fate and determines for himself the place where his soul stands and what it experiences as the essence of things — let the Others experience and do and say what they can according to their constitution of soul. The philosopher’s stone is of use to no one who is not the sage [p. 323]; but the sage has it, even if the Others declare that there is no such thing. Only the stone looks different from what the less wise imagine, and its effects are different from what he expects who is not wise.

<sup>148a</sup> On this too the psychologically so deeply penetrating views of Danzel (quotation, see n. 7) give indirect information, when he shows how, for instance, conjurations or interpretations of signs are anchored in the state of belief of the subject.

That furnishes us the greater key to the greater gate of the past. There were times and dispositions of mind in which conjuration worked, in which signs foretold, in which miracles happened, because the essence of man was another, more naturelike, more clairvoyant, more entwined in the weaving of the elements, and could give to them and receive from them, and therefore stood in connections that we do not notice. When one tries, without the misleading spirit of negation, to feel one’s way purely soulfully into the old cults with their “superstition” and their revelations, as far as one still can as a child of our age, one becomes aware all at once from what dreadfully earth-bound depths the wisdom and love of great spirits and religions have led us more and more into the light and into true freedom, and are still to lead us, and what an immense event a deeply lived Christianity, for all modern denial, must have been for the Nordic-Germanic man whose forefathers, in the raving orgies of the daemonic worship of the gods, thrust the knife ecstatically into their own throats.<sup>149</sup>

<sup>149</sup> Moreover, wounding as a means to ecstasy still appears occasionally even among the Old Testament prophets, though not among the greatest, truly God-gifted ones, but only among those of lesser degree, and there leads to visions which in the pure, lofty natures, such as an Isaiah, flow wholly and entirely from the revelation of a divinely artistic seeing itself. On the nature of ecstatic visions of both kinds, W. Jacobi instructs, “Die Ekstase der alttestamentlichen Propheten”. (In Grenzfragen des Nerven- und Seelenlebens, no. 108.) München-Wiesbaden 1920.

Yet that too had its deep metaphysical meaning and was of an immense expiating effect, banning the nature-daemons. To the degree that the natural-daemonic has been eliminated from our life of soul and senses, much too became more harmless with us which in the legendary state bound to nature could become dreadful or heroic and both at once. So too the sexual drives and the soul-images and surgings metaphysically effective in them must have been of a daemonism unimagined and unattainable for us, whose relaxation only death itself could follow, death under processes appalling to us, yet wholly corresponding to the being of that time. It was the daemonically great redemption for the daemonically great desiring and acting. How right Spengler was when he saw death standing behind the myth!

When, from the time of an immense heroism bound to nature, we read [p. 324] of a willing and a devotion to aims that appear to us late-born, but also redeemed, as the spawn of an uncanny fantasy, we all too easily forget that our world is not that of that time, neither within nor without. Heroes or heroines of the daemonic, as in late echo still Tristan and Isolde, could suffer the love-death in the most immense states of being beside oneself — here too not only as redemption, nor only as expiation in the simply moral sense — there was here precisely nothing “immoral” that by our morality would have had to be atoned for — but death as the sole way of continuing precisely a primal-daemonic life, feeling, willing, because it alone burst the individual and lifted it, with its daemonic burden and lust of willing no longer bearable for a single being, out into the transcendent realm of the species, into the womb of the Mothers, where all this — willing and silence, desiring and fulfilling, dissolving oneself and finding oneself, life and death — lies enclosed, eternally alive.

“All that ever happens / In our day, / Is a sad echo / Of glorious days of the forefathers.”

How wonderfully, after all, what these primordial men of heroic measure were and experienced and had to suffer, and freely suffered, shines forth precisely from the Germanic hero- and god-legends which they beheld and created in their spirit. The heroism, intimately akin to death and living in it, which probably pervades the legends from most ancient times and finds its echo in the earliest period still reachable from the heroic songs handed down, can probably not be described more beautifully and gravely than with the words of a German legend-scholar,<sup>150</sup> which he says, though only of the late Germanic heroic figures:

<sup>150</sup> Fr. von der Leyen, Deutsches Sagenbuch. Die Götter und Göttersagen der Germanen. München 1909.

“The kings and warriors of the Germanic migration of peoples who step before us in our songs will horrify many and be forever an abomination for an age of humanity. For they are hard and merciless; to gain beautiful armor they kill their enemies; lustfully they covet lands and treasures, keep faith only with the king who is [p. 325] generous to them and rewards them as they wish. They call revenge their supreme commandment and are cunning and treacherous when they want to reach their goals. Successful betrayal and cruel assault fill them with proud satisfaction, and for the sake of their revenge and their greed and their lust for war they fall upon one another, brother against brother, son against father, people against people. And yet what heroes! Overwhelming in their courage and their indomitable strength; in order to be able to live, they seek out the wildest dangers and adventures and let only the hardest tests of heroism count as tests. Iron, unbending, implacable, like the forces of nature, they are in their action whenever it concerns the highest commandments of heroism, honor and revenge, hospitality and loyalty. They sacrifice to the categorical imperative of their custom, without asking, their happiness, their love, their whole existence, they keep faith beyond life and death; this power of sacrificing themselves gives their heroism its greatness. The old heroes are taciturn, victorious over themselves in the annihilating conflict of feelings, and more glorious than ever when they fight with sure death before their eyes, when they obey reverently and quietly the almighty fate that has swept them all away, and above which the power of their heroism yet rises gleaming and imperishable.”

Let us set against such untamed late heroism the downright feeble lamenting and cursing of the two Babylonian heroes of the Gilgamesh epic in the face of the death that has dawned upon them, even if it dies away at last in collected, sublime calm. Here a poet of a late age lets an ancient legendary material end in a mood almost sentimentally resigned, a splendid passage in itself, which I set down here in Ungnad’s wonderful translation<sup>151</sup>, but which falls short of what the Germanic warriors experience in death:

<sup>151</sup> A. Ungnad, Die Religion der Babylonier und Assyrer. Gilgamesch-Epos und Sintflutsage, p. 101. (In: Religiöse Stimmen der Völker, vol. III. Jena 1921.)

“Raging is death, no mercy does he know. / Do we build an eternal house? Do we seal eternally? / Do brothers divide eternally? / Does begetting go on eternally upon the earth? / Does the river rise eternally, bearing the flood away?... / From of old there is no permanence: / The sleeper and the dead, how alike they are! / One cannot give back the image of death... / [p. 326] The great gods assemble .. / The fate-shaping goddess determines their destinies. / They lay down death and life, / Without determining the days of death!”

So ends the tragedy of the mighty Gilgamesh and of Engidu, and so will the tragedy of the peoples and of humanity end. But threefold can be what the human soul makes of the inescapable doom: quiet submission, loud despair, or defiance and creative heroism! The divine — lies beyond all of this.

That lived experience of death, as the highest expression of life in the daemonically opened world, has an ever-recurring, deep, truly real meaning. Now we also understand why, in the legends<sup>152</sup>, together with the Fall and the expulsion from Paradise, seen metaphysically-transcendently, death enters nature. If the entry of the human essence into physical nature was, as we sought to grasp it metaphysically, a descent from a unified, luminous state, from a paradisal existence in which it lived in peace with nature, in which it understood the language of the animals, in which the wolf lay down with the lamb, then this could only have been a transcendent state in which the death of the individual did not exist, because the individual was still wholly absorbed in the species — species in the sense of the metaphysical-living, which is the foundation of all natural formations. As soon as the physical individual state set in for the human essence with the Fall, and with it for the whole organic nature rooted in kinship with it, individual death too had to set in. For this alone, over against that falling, is the atonement, it alone the renewed liberation, the leading back into the superindividual and therefore pure, into the perfect and ideal of the species-soul. So with the expulsion from Paradise death came, but at the same time also the promise of the Redeemer who overcomes death, into the human world.

<sup>152</sup> Many such legends are collected in J. G. Frazer, The belief in immortality (see n. 125).

In part death in the myths and legends is gloomy, full of pain and despair, tragic, or an atonement, a being absorbed in the pure species; in part it is sublime and luminous, full of freedom and a mighty surpassing of the all too narrowly bound [p. 327] individual personality; but daemonic through and through, not yet divine. Again and again it becomes, for the daemonically living and heroic man, a glorious fulfillment, a pressing forward into the one truly single life, however gloomy it may appear to our physically oriented, late-age sense of this world. Whoever bears a great fate or is himself a great fate for others makes, in his innermost being, no longer any distinction between life and death. He lives as it were in far-reaching death. His spirit is attuned alike to death and life, is the equal of death, because it also embraces life. Therefore heroes have not shunned death and have unhesitatingly brought it upon peoples as well. Hence the praising of death in battle and the entry into Valhalla, in contrast to the “death in bed”; hence also the symbolic death-sacrifice of a youth standing just in the fullness of his strength, Isaac in the Old Testament; or of a maiden just come into bloom, Iphigenia before the Trojan War; hence the sacrificing of kings and of the best of the people; hence the whole ancient daemonism of human sacrifice generally, which, wholly degenerate and meaningless, still appears to us in a repulsive form among the Semites, as an aberration from a purer, higher religion, in the cult of Moloch, whereas all the earlier death-sacrifices have nothing of this about them whatever. For in the genuine, high death-sacrifice even we men of the late age still feel the deeply mystical, the liberating, that which introduces the high power of a best individual personality into the whole species and therefore infinitely enlarges the species, that which makes one truly a partaker of a higher stream of life, where the individual being, the individual personality, first finds its fulfillment and full significance, because it bursts its fetters and thereby also becomes, for the others, a great fate, a symbol, a myth, an effective hero. And while we can at most think this out for ourselves and re-feel it to a higher or lesser degree, for the men of that time it was a real life, death was an immense, infinitely rich lived experience, a wandering of the soul in the greatest sense, a living transformation of the soul.

Step by step something else becomes intelligible to us again and again, when we see death light up in this light of a genuinely daemonic-heroic [p. 328] age. How intelligible and transparent the cult of the graves and the cult of souls of the ancients becomes to us. The dead had visibly passed into the total life of the species, in which every single individual is transcendently rooted and weaves and lives, which one symbolizes with striking objects. Whereas we can make this community of the species clear to ourselves only still through philosophical acts of thought or moral impulses, the ancient nature-sighted, daemonically living human beings saw immediately this connection of their existence with that of the dead in the species — hence the cult of the dead and of ancestors, the manner of burial, the altars, among these transmigrating times, living and communing nature-sightedly with one another and with their dead, in which they shyly and reverently venerated and saw the group-soul.

So all this becomes living and true and warm and full of flesh and blood. It was a misfortune for our understanding of these things that they became known to us only in the distorted tradition and cultic practice, itself already dead to true understanding, among the thoroughly intellectualized men of antiquity or the primitive peoples discovered only lately<sup>153</sup>. There the highly daemonic ancient spirit of death is already extinguished, and only the traditional, often hollow forms are still present. From this standpoint too I see in the Diluvial man already, through and through, the late-age man with the almost perfectly intellectual orientation toward existence, even if he still had as much of daemonic magic as primitive peoples have been wont to have even in our own millennia, which however no longer bears even approximate comparison with the ancient, original nature-sighted-daemonic. The only gloomily daemonic thing that we still experience of death, apart from rare cases in individual existence and strongly shot through with intellectual reflection, is the war of states against one another or revolutions, like the French at the end of the 18th and the Russian at the beginning of the 20th century. There too, beside an immense night of blood and distress, heroism of singular greatness becomes visible in groups or in individuals; but it is dictated by iron, bitter self-conquest, no longer sought and experienced as daemonic ecstasy. It is only as sad [p. 329] as it is fraught with consequences that this heroism nowhere emerged among those who in the World War were nominally the leaders of the peoples and armies. None of them knew or suspected that from him, from him precisely, instead of flight a hero’s death was demanded, which would have unleashed immense daemonic powers when peoples and armies broke down in their heroism. Had the leaders at the last, at the head of all, fallen heroically, the ancient luminous daemonism that lies in the sacrificial death would have become free again, a great faith seizing all would have arisen, a myth would have become alive and would have produced in the soul of the people a spiritual and psychic surge, which would have brought about a German revolution of quite another kind than the one that ran its course in the mire. But of this transcendence of facts and of life we no longer have any knowledge; our princes and leaders too have become technicians and scientists.

<sup>153</sup> Cf. the account in Preller-Robert (Griech. Mythologie III, p. 1095 ff.), where model examples seem positively to have been collected of the rationalistic manner in which, already in Greek antiquity, a mythical material was smothered in flat pictures of everyday life. One feels exactly as if one stood in the age of our pseudo-scientific efforts at enlightenment, when one reads how a clumsy love affair of Agamemnon serves to elucidate that sacrificial legend.

The highest, historically clearest living symbol of the sacrificial death, creating great reality, appears before our eyes in the historical figure of Jesus. If the tradition through the Gospels betrays, as one now wants to establish, however much, image by image, the symbolism of the ancient solar myth<sup>154</sup>, this by no means proves, as was supposed, the unreality and unhistoricity of the life of Christ in the person of Jesus of Nazareth, but only proves that the wholly immense spiritual and spirit-liberating heroism of that Son of Man of mythical greatness — of a being sent by God in the truest sense — which reached decisively into the transcendent and set it living into this world, was of such measure and such height that one had precisely to reach for the ancient solar myths, which offer the same gigantic dimensions, in order to pour that immense content into a vessel which one could no longer create with one’s own late-age thoughts and one’s own late-age language: the whole man, as he was beheld and willed by the Creator.

<sup>154</sup> P. Jensen, Das Gilgamesch-Epos in der Weltliteratur. I. Band. Straßburg 1906, p. 811 ff.

How true this “solar myth” is after all! Should a similar or identical knowledge dwell in the older solar myths? And should this, beside the natural as outer symbol, be their spiritually foreseeing last, highest content? Should it already have been beheld in the spirit by the most ancient God-favored sages and seers [p. 330] what only late became reality as a Christ and took on flesh and blood? Should what the solar myths speak have been already an ancient, ever-repeated yearning lived experience in the spirit? One could believe it; for already Adam, at the expulsion from Paradise, beholds this promise of the Redeemer, of the holy, God-born man.

Christ, as he meets us in the Gospels, was a soul of the luminous, the equal of the highest natural-daemonic, in which, as the story of the Tempter teaches, the deeply dark daemonism too rose up at the start. He beheld how man can become a dark just as well as a holy-luminous conqueror of the world. With his great luminous soul he was capable of the spiritual, not only of the physical sacrificial death — and the latter would have released the same gigantic power toward the dark side in the world as later toward the luminous, had he then, in the “wilderness” and on the “pinnacle of the temple,” followed the tempter within himself like the first man. But as it was, he made the turn toward the light. His sacrificial death — seen spiritually — has overcome all the daemonism of the world, of the human essence driven out of Paradise; he has become the archetype of the Redeemer of the human soul.

In all great human natures, indeed, these two poles lie. Even in a Bismarck — to return once more to this man of will of the greatest measure as a uniquely intelligible example for us — those two possibilities lived on side by side. The king’s remark about him, that he would even as a revolutionary mount the barricades if he thereby reached his goal, shows that duality of his nature, which in men of small format appears criminal or ridiculous, as all over-great men have it in some way, who can become just as well dark-daemonic as luminous-holy — quite unlike the many, many others with their never disturbed concept of right and wrong, in whom it is not nature, not daemonism that wrestles with divinely liberated breathing; who do not suspect that he can be good and holy who brings guilt-laden bitter suffering upon the others; for whom he is upright and decent to whom one owes good manners, good fortune and well-being; who do not [p. 331] want to understand that the tax collector stands closer to the heart of God than the righteous — the many, many, in whom the struggle of Michael with the dragon has not yet begun at all and whose rectitude therefore still lies on this side of good and evil. They do not feel at all the darkness in the human essence, and therefore neither suspect nor know that every great man, as a Heracles, not only once, comes to the crossroads, from where he can become just as well a daemonic servant of Satan as a luminous messenger of God. And hence the myth, coming from the beginning of the world, of the fallen angel, and the later one of Christ in the wilderness and on the pinnacle of the temple, where he sees the glory of the kingdoms of the world, and that of Heracles, whom the goddess lets choose the way to the right or to the left, have remained as often uncomprehended as — the mystery of death.

When a Buddha, a Christ gives up his spirit in God, divests himself wholly of his own spirit and enters living into God’s All-unity, the immense luminous and heroic power that was chained to him and lived in him becomes free, and pours itself into others who can receive it. A true transmigration of souls, grounded on living faith. Therefore the Redeemer appears afterward to his disciples; therefore a Redeemer can say: I am with you to the end of the world. From such mighty figures, whose existence and whose spiritual death is a mighty myth, turnings of the world proceed. From the heroism of Jesus too a turning of the world proceeds; not only for the Occident, but also for the Orient, as the Byzantine-Arabic cultural circle shows. Judaism itself, from which it sprang, was no longer able to take up wholly into itself this new, luminous, liberating spirit. The “kingdom” was taken from the children, and the Lord went out on the broad highway and invited cripples and beggars to the meal. The “magian” soul had already once become a redemption in the ancient world through the living grasp of monotheism over against the anciently living daemonic magic. This Chaldeandom raised itself, monotheistically specified at last in Judaism, to the height, in order then to release the event of Christ out of itself and against itself. Since it could no longer appropriate what here broke forth, we see in Judaism, as it goes today as a race of the spirit through all the [p. 332] world, still plainly the unresolved linkage of the ancient-daemonic with the later light-monotheistic. All the spiritual heights of the latter and all the psychic depths of the former we find in it still living together, and from time to time lighting up, sympathetic or uncanny to us, and making themselves effective. One thinks of its two younger counter-poles: the Baal Shem<sup>155</sup> and the political revolutionaries. Yes, here the two primordial worlds, even if intellectualized, are still set before our eyes alive through the centuries in dreadful tragic linkage — and that is the misery and misfortune of this torn people, driven out not only from its earthly but also from its originally beheld spiritual homeland, to which only few, it seems, have found their way home.

<sup>155</sup> M. Buber, Die Legende des Baalschem. Frankfurt a. M. 1918.

Turning from there our gaze directly, let us consider the spiritual-psychic situation of Christianity — I mean Christianity as inner lived experience, not its hieratic forms — so we see here too again death as the highest symbol, as the highest idea, as the entry into the highest reality. But what sort of death? What form, what content has it assumed in comparison with the ecstatic death of daemonic times or with the blood-sacrifices?

Christ sealed with bodily death the spiritual upheaval of the “reconciliation of God with man” outwardly as well. He thereby still stands in the daemonic world of the ancient myth, where the best, the king, suffers the sacrificial death in order to reconcile the gravely angered god. From then on the upheaval was accomplished for our spiritual world, a “new covenant” with the divine being was created, which revealed itself definitively and decisively only with him, even if before him and in his contemporary world it was prepared and expected through “prophets” or “baptists” and was already seeking to break through there.
