But now the living idea of the spiritual sacrificial death stood there as a new demand of reality. Christ made this demand and its fulfillment the decisive world-event for the oriental and occidental human soul, as history proves. With that the “old covenant was dissolved,” “the veil in the Holy of Holies rent,” the daemonic-ecstatic sacrifice of the body as sole God-reconciling act in the religious overcome, “hell [p. 333] vanquished,” the ancient natural daemonism overthrown. That is the world-event or, if one will: the living, effective myth of Christianity, seen against the background of earth-historical distance. What does the Gospel criticism matter to the truly believing, the truly knowing! It can give him nothing, nothing at all, nor take anything away.

Yet the life of Christ itself did not end with the cry of jubilation of the victorious conqueror, as the optimistic shallowness of modern religiosity misunderstands it. Christ, standing on the water as hero of the solar myth, may indeed be the image of the triumphant god of light; it is a symbol of inner truthfulness. But the man Christ in Gethsemane and on the cross knows no miracles, but passes away humanly. His life closes with the words, descended into the deepest spiritual poverty: “My God, my God! Why hast thou forsaken me?” That first was the entrance gate to the divine life, was the highest fulfillment of life, was the victory of the existence of Christ. The way into the last redemption became free: the complete spiritual dying-out, beyond which God first is found. That which redeems is infinitely poor, but also infinitely overcoming — like death. There God lives all-alone. The human spirit recognized at its highest summit this goal as its best life, as a life leading to the highest liberation. It is not the will to live that is abolished, as Schopenhauer, looking only at nature, understands it, or as one misunderstands the Indian doctrine of salvation: only the will to the natural, to the daemonic, to the selfish will give way to the divine. Behind it stands not nothingness, but the ego-free love and joy of creation in God. By desire for one’s own right, by requital of like with like, as human law only is, the daemonic-natural is never abolished, never transfigured; it is always only fed anew and heaped up. But the renouncing, forgiving overcoming of the world with the love that does not know itself is the revelation of the divine in the human soul, is the overcoming of nature, set upon right and struggle and out of it ceaselessly stamping its renewed forms. Wishing and fulfilling or denying calls forth new desire — that is the world of causality, of nature, of daemonism. [p. 334] In the divine, however, all desire dies, all over-againstness. Ungodly is even the desire for one’s own spiritual perfection, if it does not happen in order to become poor in God. The measure is called not good and evil, but love of God or of self.

But of this we need speak no more; one finds it better among the mystics of the Middle Ages. The most consummate bloom of this transfiguring seeing and dying, which reconciles God with the man driven out of Paradise, bears its purest fruit in the spiritual figure of Master Eckhardt. Here death is praised as conscious giving up of the creaturely essence and thereby, after the model of Christ, as entry into the life of God, into the surrender that desires nothing more and no longer seeks itself, into a spiritual dying. To physical, like causal, thinking, to the “world,” this wisdom is insoluble, for behind it stands, for it, nothingness. But the divine being is infinitely rich because it is infinitely poor. Credo quia absurdum! The deepest and most consummate word of Eckhardt’s symbolizes it: Great as death is love.

If one asks whether there is in humanity as a whole, or within races and world-ages, an ascent, a consummation, then the way from the gloomy, self-desiring, ecstatic daemonism up to the luminous Madonna, forgetting herself in quiet beholding, who bears the perfectly poor, naked Redeemer in her lap, can be a measure for us; or, what is in essence the same: the knowledge and the lived experience of ‘death.’ What death ever was, and what became of it in the highest spirits — this perhaps gives an answer to the question of humanity’s progress toward its promised goal.

[p. 335]

## Anmerkungen — Notes

[p. 336]

[p. 337]

### Vorbemerkungen zur 2. bis 4. Auflage — Preliminary Remarks on the Second through Fourth Editions

The Preface remained the same for all editions; it keeps its original date, 1924. The second edition was an almost unaltered reprint of the first; only a few improvements of wording and sentence structure, as well as some notes, were added; besides this, at the end of the chapter “Primordial Man and Legendary Animals,” a section on the question to what extent animal primordial forms are pure products of fantasy or generic memories of humanity. This supplement was occasioned by corresponding questions that had reached the author, although a detailed answer to such questions, in many directions, had already been given in the metaphysical part. Furthermore, the brief remarks on the oldest stages of the epiphysis and paraphysis in the brain (in the chapter on “The Earth-Historical Age of the Human Stock”) were exposed to so many misunderstandings that the text was altered already in the second edition and more thoroughly in the third; the sense of the remarks has remained the same. The latter holds also for some changes in the explanation of the genealogical-tree figure 1 in the chapter “Type-Circles and Biological Temporal Character.” There the new figure 2 was also added. In the third edition, furthermore, some textual expansions were made in the chapter “The Earth-Historical Age of the Human Stock,” because since the appearance of the book important advances on this question had come to the author’s knowledge, which gratifyingly supported his theory but were won quite independently of it. This also finds expression in a quotation added on page 94 concerning a presumably aquatic-animal primal condition of the human being. A brief supplement was also given to the end of the chapter on the “Dating and Spatial Delimitation of the Noachian Flood.” Of the greatest importance, finally, are the references, given in the third edition, to the views of the Hamburg ethnologist Th. W. Danzel on myths and sorcery, which were added to note 7; here lie powerful beginnings of new research. Notes 30 and 64 were likewise supplemented; the struggle over the glacial cosmogony is now in full swing, and one does best to wait and see, if one cannot oneself intervene as a specialist in some direction, which has not been attempted anew in the third edition.

Almost all notes marked with an added a) belong to the second and third editions. The text of the “Introduction” and of the “Metaphysics” has remained the same in all editions, apart from small additions, improvements of wording, and changes of form.

The fourth edition has been altered above all in this: that in the section on “The Earth-Historical Age of the Human Stock” the Permian-Triassic hand-animal trackways were placed in a different context, following the fundamental investigations of W. Soergel, and attention was drawn to the still higher age of the opposable first toe-joint and to the conclusions that follow from it all the more strongly, for a very high age of the human type. Likewise the important investigations of H. Reich on the pineal gland of man were added in the chapter on nature-sightedness. Notes 19 a), 57a) and 115a) were added as supplements. For the rest, in the new edition too, occasional unclear or sense-disturbing words and unessential details have been improved throughout the text.

[p. 338]

### Textanmerkungen — Notes on the Text

#### A. Allgemeines — A. General Works

The skeleton of earth history and the external course of the history of life in the geological epochs, according to the doctrines now valid, are given in an intelligible form by roughly the following works, in which one can inform oneself about everything touched on in the text that belongs to earth history:

Joh. Walther, Entwicklungsgeschichte der Erde und des Lebens. Leipzig 1908.

M. Neumayr, Erdgeschichte. 3rd ed. by F. E. Sueß. 2 vols. Leipzig and Vienna 1920.

Oth. Abel, Lebensbilder aus der Tierwelt der Vorzeit. Jena 1922.

The geographical conditions of the former world are given in survey and furnished with palaeogeographical maps by:

Th. Arldt, Handbuch der Paläogeographie. Berlin 1917—1921.

The fossil Diluvial man is treated at length in:

E. Werth, Der fossile Mensch. Grundzüge einer Paläoanthropologie. Berlin 1921 ff.

A. Heilborn, Der Mensch der Urzeit. (Aus Natur- und Geisteswelt No. 62.) Leipzig, Berlin 1918.

Those moments in particular that demand a high age of the human stock are treated by the posthumous work of:

H. Klaatsch, Der Werdegang der Menschheit und die Entstehung der Kultur. Ed. by A. Heilborn. Berlin 1920.

General palaeontology, as well as the palaeobiological and phylogenetic basic concepts on the basis of the fossil animal material, are presented in:

Oth. Abel, Grundzüge der Paläobiologie der Wirbeltiere, Stuttgart 1912.

—, Allgemeine Paläontologie. (Sammlg. Göschen No. 460.) Berlin-Leipzig 1914.

E. Dacqué, Biologische Formenkunde der fossilen niederen Tiere. Berlin 1921.

—, Biologie der fossilen Tiere (Sammlg. Göschen No. 861), Berlin and Leipzig 1923.

[p. 339] Brief diagnoses and further details on the general skeletal anatomy of the fossil animals frequently named in the text will be found in:

K. A. v. Zittel, Grundzüge der Paläontologie. Recent editions ed. by F. Broili and M. Schlosser. Part 1: Invertebrata, 6th ed. Munich 1924. Part 2: Vertebrata. 4th ed. Munich 1923.

The palaeobotanical material in: H. Potonié, Lehrbuch der Paläobotanik. 2nd ed. by W. Gothan, Berlin 1921.

As sources for most of the legends and myths, insofar as other works are not also cited, the better-known collections and reference works were used:

D. Dähnhardt, Natursagen. Eine Sammlung naturdeutender Sagen, Märchen, Fabeln und Legenden. 4 vols. Leipzig, Berlin 1903—1912. (Vol. IV also by A. v. Löwis of Menar.)

A. Pauly, Realenzyklopädie der klassischen Altertumswissenschaft. New revision by G. Wissowa. Stuttgart 1893ff. Continued by W. Kroll and K. Witte.

W. H. Roscher, Lexikon der griechischen und römischen Mythologie. Leipzig 1884ff.

L. Preller, Griechische Mythologie. 4th ed. by C. Robert. Berlin 1894. Vol. 2. Die Heroen. Berlin 1920, 1921. Vol. 3. Die griechische Heldensage. 1923.

W. Golther, Handbuch der germanischen Mythologie. Leipzig 1895.

R. Andree, Die Flutsagen. Brunswick 1891.

H. Usener, Die Sintflutsagen. Religionsgeschichtliche Untersuchungen III. Bonn 1899.

J. G. Frazer, The golden bough. A study in Magie and Religion. 3. Edit. 12 Vols. London 1911—1920.

A. Jeremias, Handbuch der altorientalischen Geisteskultur. Leipzig 1913.
